### [1] [Beyond Textual Chain-of-Thought: A Survey on Action-Grounded Reasoning in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01659)
*Zhengxu Tang,Xiaozhou Zhang,Guofeng Cui,Ziyu Gong,Zi Wang,Yunfei Shi,Ruifeng Deng,Chengzhi Qi,Ke Chen,Sachin Patil,Tianjun Xiao,Langechuan Liu,Pichao Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This survey examines the application of Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning in autonomous driving, proposing a representation-centered taxonomy that categorizes 130 methods into four types: language-based, visual-spatial, latent-dynamic, and externalized reasoning, with 13 subtypes. The study analyzes 171 papers to address the challenge of adapting textual CoT to action-grounded reasoning in safety-critical driving systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional CoT reasoning in generative models produces intermediate steps leading to textual answers. However, autonomous driving requires continuous actions rather than text outputs, necessitating reasoning that shares the same spatiotemporal structure as the physical world. This shift from textual CoT to action-grounded reasoning represents a fundamental challenge in the field.

Method: The survey employs a representation-centered taxonomy, using the form of intermediate state as the organizing axis. It systematically reviews 171 papers, comprising 130 method papers and 41 benchmarks, datasets, surveys, and analysis papers, organizing the methods into four main categories further divided into 13 subtypes tied to distinct regions of interests.

Result: The research identifies four categories of reasoning in driving agents: language-based, visual-spatial, latent-dynamic, and externalized reasoning. The synthesis reveals that the open frontier lies in developing intermediate representations that can be grounded in the real world, coupled to real-time action, and verified under safety-critical systems.

Conclusion: The future of reasoning in driving agents requires intermediate representations that satisfy three key criteria: real-world grounding, real-time action coupling, and safety-critical verification. This survey provides a comprehensive framework for understanding the current landscape and future directions of CoT reasoning in autonomous driving.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning powers generative models by eliciting intermediate steps before producing an answer. In autonomous driving, the answer is a continuous action. Thus its reasoning must share the same spatiotemporal structure as the physical world. This survey studies the resulting shift from textual CoT to action-grounded reasoning. Surveying 171 papers, including 130 method papers and 41 benchmarks, datasets, surveys, and analysis papers, we propose a representation-centered taxonomy that treats the form of the intermediate state as the organizing axis. We systematize the 130 methods into four categories: language-based, visual-spatial, latent-dynamic, and externalized reasoning, further divided into 13 subtypes tied to distinct regions of interests. Our synthesis shows that the open frontier of reasoning in driving agents lies in intermediate representations that can be grounded in the real world, coupled to real-time action, and verified under safety-critical systems. Project page: https://github.com/tangzhengxu/awesome-av-cot.

</details>


### [2] [FORGE: Forward-Only Test-Time Adaptation for Integer-Only Vision Models on Microcontrollers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01683)
*Muhammad Rehan,Haider Ali,Muhammad Ali Munir,Moaz Amjad*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a forward-only Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) method for deployed, BN-folded, integer-only convolutional networks on microcontrollers. The key innovation is re-normalizing folded convolution outputs to clean training statistics using only forward-pass estimates, enabling adaptation without backpropagation or BN layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision models deployed on microcontrollers are quantized to integer-only arithmetic and run in inference-only runtimes that lack backpropagation capability. Existing forward-only TTA methods either require server/edge-GPU class models or rely on batch-normalization (BN) layers that are fused away during integer deployment. The distribution shift (sensor noise, blur, lighting) in real-world deployment necessitates adaptation, but standard methods cannot work on true microcontroller integer execution.

Method: The key observation is that fusing BN into the preceding convolution (mandatory for integer inference) destroys the statistics that normalization-based adaptation relies on. The proposed method restores adaptation by re-normalizing each folded convolution's per-channel output to its clean training statistics, using only forward-pass estimates. The method selects only 3 of 21 layers for adaptation (without seeing test corruptions) and uses batch-size-scaled momentum for single-sample streaming.

Result: The method (i) recovers most of gradient-based TENT's accuracy gain (+20.9 vs. +24.9 points) and matches forward-only BN adaptation, while being the only method that runs on a folded integer-only model; (ii) needs to adapt only 3 of 21 layers to recover 93% of the benefit; (iii) survives single-sample streaming with a batch-size-scaled momentum; and (iv) generalizes across three datasets (up to 200 classes) and two architectures. On ESP32-S3, the forward-only adaptation costs only 8.3 mJ (6.8% of inference energy) and 21.9 ms, demonstrating practical deployability.

Conclusion: The paper presents the first forward-only TTA method that works on deployed, BN-folded, integer-only convolutional networks. By re-normalizing folded convolution outputs to clean training statistics, the method achieves near-complete recovery of gradient-based TTA accuracy while being computationally lightweight enough for real microcontroller deployment. The method is practical, generalizable, and represents a significant step toward adaptive edge AI.

Abstract: Vision models deployed on microcontrollers (MCUs) are quantized to integer-only arithmetic and run in inference-only runtimes that do not carry the machinery backpropagation needs: the standard tool for adapting a model to the distribution shift (sensor noise, blur, lighting) it meets in the field. Existing forward-only test-time adaptation (TTA) methods either run only on server- or edge-GPU-class models (not true microcontroller integer execution), or require the batch-normalization (BN) layers that integer deployment fuses away. We present a forward-only TTA method that operates on deployed, BN-folded, integer-only convolutional networks. The key observation is that fusing BN into the preceding convolution, a mandatory step for integer inference, destroys the statistics that normalization-based adaptation relies on. We restore adaptation by re-normalizing each folded convolution's per-channel output to its clean training statistics, using only forward-pass estimates. The method (i) recovers most of gradient-based TENT's accuracy gain (+20.9 vs. +24.9 points) and matches forward-only BN adaptation, while being the only method that runs on a folded integer-only model; (ii) needs to adapt only 3 of 21 layers (selected without seeing the test corruptions) to recover 93% of the benefit; (iii) survives single-sample streaming with a batch-size-scaled momentum; and (iv) generalizes across three datasets (up to 200 classes) and two architectures. We validate bit-exact int8 convolution execution and deploy on an ESP32-S3, where, measured with a Nordic PPK2 power profiler, the forward-only adaptation (a lightweight fp32 recalibration around the int8 convolutions) costs only 8.3 mJ (6.8% of inference energy) and 21.9 ms on the deployed SIMD-optimized model: forward-only adaptation is cheap on a real microcontroller.

</details>


### [3] [FairLens: Benchmarking Fairness in Vision-Language Models for High-Stakes Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01691)
*Vahid Reza Khazaie,Ahmed Y. Radwan,Shaina Raza*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FAIRLENS is a benchmark and evaluation framework for measuring fairness and validity of Vision-Language Models (VLMs) in high-stakes domains (hiring, legal, healthcare). It uses 100K+ image-question pairs with real face images across demographic groups and evaluates responses from four complementary views: demographic parity, soundness, demographic association, and bias in free-text generation. The key finding is that the primary failure is unwarranted inference rather than unequal treatment - models routinely make unsupported inferences from faces instead of abstaining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models are increasingly deployed for high-stakes decision-making, but there lacks a comprehensive framework to evaluate both fairness and validity of their responses. Existing metrics focus on demographic parity but miss critical issues like unwarranted inference and soundness - whether models follow evidence or appropriately abstain when input cannot support an answer.

Method: FAIRLENS pairs real face images spanning gender, race, and age groups with closed- and open-ended questions, generating 100K+ image-question pairs per model. Responses are evaluated from four complementary views: (1) demographic parity over adverse outcome rates, (2) soundness (whether responses follow evidence and abstain when image cannot support answer), (3) demographic association over unsupported roles and statuses, and (4) bias in free-text generation. Eight VLMs were evaluated.

Result: The primary failure mode is unwarranted inference rather than unequal treatment. Models routinely infer qualifications, threat, illness, or professional role from faces instead of abstaining. The weakest model makes such inferences on 99% of questions its input cannot answer. Failures are most severe in legal and healthcare domains. Disparity metrics alone miss these failures: parity gaps are small in absolute terms, but when baseline adverse rates are low, the same gap means one demographic group receives adverse labels several times as often as another. Bias in free-text responses is only loosely coupled to multiple-choice accuracy.

Conclusion: Fair high-stakes VLM behavior requires both similar treatment across demographic groups AND refusal to infer high-stakes attributes from appearance alone. Current models fail on the second requirement. Parity metrics alone are insufficient; soundness and validity evaluation are essential. The FAIRLENS question suite can transfer to any face corpus with demographic annotations, making it a generalizable evaluation framework.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used to make decisions from visual inputs. We introduce FAIRLENS, a benchmark and evaluation framework for measuring both the fairness and the validity of VLM responses in three high-stakes domains: hiring, legal, and healthcare. FAIRLENS pairs real face images spanning gender, race, and age groups with closed- and open-ended questions, giving more than 100K image-question pairs per model, and evaluates responses from four complementary views: demographic parity over adverse outcome rates, soundness, demographic association over unsupported roles and statuses, and bias in free-text generation. Soundness is the central validity criterion: a response is sound when it follows the evidence stated in the question and abstains when the image cannot support an answer. Evaluating eight VLMs, we find that the primary failure is unwarranted inference rather than unequal treatment. Models routinely infer qualifications, threat, illness, or professional role from a face instead of abstaining, and the weakest model does so on 99% of the questions its input cannot answer. These failures are most severe in legal and healthcare, where recognizing insufficient evidence matters most, and disparity metrics alone would miss them: parity gaps are small in absolute terms, yet when baseline adverse rates are low the same gap means one demographic group receives adverse labels several times as often as another, and a small gap can equally reflect a model that treats every group unsafely. Bias in free-text responses is only loosely coupled to multiple-choice accuracy, so correct structured answers do not imply safe generation. FAIRLENS shows that fair high-stakes VLM behavior requires similar treatment across groups and refusal to infer high-stakes attributes from appearance, and its question suite transfers to any face corpus with demographic annotations.

</details>


### [4] [From Visual Cues to Spoken Narration: Rethinking Audio Description](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01725)
*Akshita Gupta,Aditya Arora,Federico Tombari,Marcus Rohrbach,Anna Rohrbach*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Cue2Narrate is a two-stage pipeline for generating Audio Descriptions (AD) in movies, addressing both what visual events to narrate and when to insert them in long untrimmed video clips. It uses a dual-head audio-visual localizer to predict visual cue and narration windows, combined with a LoRA-adapted VLM for AD generation trained with a Description Ranking Loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work treats audio description as simple video captioning of pre-segmented clips, ignoring the critical temporal aspect of when to insert narration during dialogue gaps. This limits real-world applicability for helping visually impaired audiences.

Method: The method consists of: (1) A dual-head audio-visual localizer predicting two temporally distinct windows per AD utterance - a visual cue window and a spoken narration window; (2) A LoRA-adapted Vision-Language Model (VLM) generating concise ADs from predicted visual evidence; (3) Training with a Description Ranking Loss that ranks negative caption samples lower than ground truth ADs.

Result: On the new LongLSMDC benchmark (8-min movie clips, ~6.5min average), Cue2Narrate outperforms video-only and audio-only localization baselines by 5-12 points in average mAP. It also improves AD generation over fine-tuned base VLM under both predicted- and GT-window evaluation.

Conclusion: This work establishes the first benchmark for multi-segment AD generation on long-form clips, introducing a more realistic problem formulation that jointly addresses what to narrate and when to insert descriptions in movies.

Abstract: Audio Description (AD) provides spoken narration of visual events during dialogue gaps, making movies accessible to visually impaired audiences. The problem requires determining both what (which visual event) and when (position for inserting the AD) to narrate, to achieve the best user experience. Prior work has largely reduced the problem to video captioning of pre-segmented video clips, i.e., what is largely predefined and when is ignored entirely. We propose Cue2Narrate, a two-stage pipeline that jointly predicts what and when to narrate in longer untrimmed movie clips. A dual-head audio-visual localizer predicts two temporally distinct windows per AD utterance: a visual cue window and a spoken narration window. A LoRA-adapted VLM then generates concise ADs from the predicted visual evidence, trained with a Description Ranking Loss that ranks captions (negative samples) of the same frames lower than the GT AD. To benchmark this new problem statement, we introduce the LongLSMDC benchmark with up to 8-min movie clips (~6.5min on average). On LongLSMDC, Cue2Narrate outperforms video-only and audio-only localization baselines by 5--12 points in avg. mAP. Under both predicted- and GT-window evaluation, Cue2Narrate improves AD generation over the corresponding fine-tuned base VLM. These results establish the first benchmark for multi-segment AD generation on long-form clips. Data & Code: https://github.com/multimodal-ai-lab/Cue2Narrate

</details>


### [5] [Multi-Tool Image Editing Attribution in Facial Forgery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02751)
*Sheng Liu,Qiang Sheng,Danding Wang,Yu Li,Chenming Zhou,Juan Cao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces Multi-Tool Image Editing Attribution (MIEA), a new task to identify multiple editing tools used in facial image editing, and proposes DPEC method with a new dataset (MultiEdit, 500k+ images) that outperforms nine existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing image editing attribution methods only handle single-tool editing scenarios, but real-world editing often involves multiple tools whose artifacts are composite and overlapped. There was a need to address this more complex multi-tool attribution problem.

Method: DPEC captures distinguishable, locality-aware editing tool traces from both spatial and frequency domains, using an error-based curriculum learning strategy to handle multi-step edited images.

Result: DPEC outperforms nine existing methods for facial images edited in at most five steps.

Conclusion: The proposed MultiEdit dataset and DPEC method successfully address the multi-tool image editing attribution problem, achieving superior performance over existing approaches.

Abstract: As generative AI tools become increasingly powerful and easy to use, people can easily edit portrait images with a prompt, necessitating the task of image editing attribution, which predicts the involved editing tools from the given image. Existing attribution methods hold the single-tool assumption and can only attribute a specific editing tool, but struggle to handle the more complex and increasingly common multi-tool editing scenarios, where artifacts left by different editing tools are composite and overlapped. To address this gap, we explore Multi-Tool Image Editing Attribution (MIEA), which aims to identify multiple editing tools involved in a multi-tool edited facial image. To simulate the real-life editing operations on facial images, we then construct a new dataset, MultiEdit, which contains 500k+ edited facial images and covers six types of editing tools that support face swapping (Deepfake) and various facial enhancements. Inspired by the findings from data analysis, we design DPEC, a multi-tool attribution method that can capture distinguishable, locality-aware editing tool traces from both spatial and frequency domains with the support of an error-based curriculum learning strategy. Experiments show \Method\ outperforms nine methods for facial images edited in at most five steps.

</details>


### [6] [UAV Thermal Imagery for Inert Ordnance Screening: Multi Campaign Dataset Development,Object Detection, and Practical Recommendations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01738)
*Chad Melton,PhD.,Annabelle Kelton*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study developed a UAV-based thermal imaging system with object detection models (YOLOV11l and RT-DETR-R50) to detect unexploded ordnance (UXO) for humanitarian mine action. The dataset contains 5,855 thermal image pairs collected across four field campaigns under various conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UXO contamination restricts civilian access, agriculture, infrastructure, and environmental remediation globally. Current detection methods are time-consuming and dangerous, necessitating automated detection solutions for humanitarian demining operations.

Method: Data collection: Four field campaigns in Tennessee under summer/winter conditions using inert ordnance on varied surfaces (short grass, tall vegetation, gravel, mulch, rock, compost, compacted surfaces). Thermal imagery captured at 33m and 15m flight altitudes. Dataset: 5,855 thermal image label pairs (918 positive, 4,937 background). Training: YOLOV11l and RT-DETR-R50 algorithms trained on downsampled datasets (33m: 420 training/106 validation; 15m: 629 training/157 validation).

Result: Developed a multi-campaign UAV thermal image dataset with labeled ordnance. Trained and evaluated YOLOV11l and RT-DETR-R50 object detection models. Practical recommendations include: collecting thermal and RGB imagery together, incorporating varied surfaces, considering post-solar exposure periods, balancing coverage against pixel representation, calibrating with local data, and retaining human review.

Conclusion: The developed system is intended for screening and prioritization for follow-on technical survey or EOD assessment, not standalone clearance. The approach provides practical recommendations for humanitarian mine action applications, emphasizing the need for combined sensor modalities and human oversight.

Abstract: Unexploded ordnance (UXO) continues to restrict civilian access, agricultural activity, infrastructure recovery, and environmental remediation in contaminated areas around the world. This study created a multi campaign UAV thermal image data set of inert ordnance, developed a labeled image set from collected imagery, tested object detection models, and identified practical considerations for humanitarian mine action and demining applications. Data were collected during four field campaigns in Tennessee under summer and winter conditions using inert mines, munitions, and other ordnance placed in short grass, tall vegetation, gravel, mulch, rock, compost, and compacted surfaces. Thermal imagery was collected under flight altitutes of 33 m and 15 m. The final source inventory contained 5,855 thermal image label pairs, including 918 positive images and 4,937 background images. After retaining all positive images and downsampling background images, the 33 m dataset contained 420 training and 106 validation images, while the 15 m dataset contained 629 training and 157 validation images. YOLOV11l and RT-DETR-R50 algorithms were trained and evaluated to develop an automated candidate detection model. Practical recommendations include collecting thermal and RGB imagery together, incorporating varied surfaces and background only imagery, considering periods following changes in solar exposure, balancing survey coverage against target pixel representation, calibrating models with representative local data, and retaining qualified human review. The intended use is screening and prioritization for follow on technical survey or EOD assessment, and not a standalone clearance.

</details>


### [7] [ZipTok3D: High-Fidelity 3D Tokenization with Compact Token Prefixes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01740)
*Mingda Lin,Weijie Wang,Zeyu Zhang,Bowen Cui,Yefei He,Haoyu Zhao,Yuanyu He,Donny Y. Chen,Feng Chen,Bohan Zhuang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ZipTok3D is a 3D tokenizer that achieves high-fidelity 3D reconstruction from extremely compact token sequences (1 token on ShapeNet, 4 tokens on TRELLIS) by organizing geometry into progressive global-token prefixes and using iterative decoding with parameter-shared Transformers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D tokenizers suffer sharp reconstruction degradation when compressed to extremely low token budgets. Current approaches organize latents over spatial regions or fixed-size global tokens, both failing at high compression.

Method: 1) Nested dropout randomly truncates latent sequences during training, requiring each retained prefix to reconstruct the complete object, thereby prioritizing essential geometric information in leading tokens. 2) Parameter-shared Transformer block iteratively decodes fine-grained geometry from each prefix without separate generative sampling stage.

Result: With same token dimension, ZipTok3D achieves reconstruction quality comparable to 32-token COD-VAE baseline using only 1 token on ShapeNet (32× reduction) and 4 tokens on TRELLIS (8× reduction).

Conclusion: ZipTok3D demonstrates that progressive token organization combined with iterative decoding enables high-fidelity 3D reconstruction from extremely compact representations, significantly advancing compression ratios for 3D tokenizers.

Abstract: Compact token sequences are essential for efficient 3D generation. However, existing 3D tokenizers typically organize latent representations either over spatial regions or as fixed-size sets of global tokens, both suffering sharp reconstruction degradation when compressed to extremely low token budgets. In this paper, we present ZipTok3D, a 3D tokenizer designed for high-fidelity reconstruction from extremely short token sequences. Its key idea is to organize object geometry into progressively informative global-token prefixes and unfold these compact representations through iterative decoding. Specifically, nested dropout randomly truncates the latent sequence after encoding during training and requires each retained prefix to reconstruct the complete object, thereby prioritizing essential geometric information in the leading tokens. The decoder then repeatedly applies a parameter-shared Transformer block to recover fine-grained geometry from each prefix without a separate generative sampling stage. With the same token dimension, ZipTok3D achieves reconstruction quality comparable to the 32-token COD-VAE baseline using only one token on ShapeNet and four on TRELLIS, yielding $32\times$ and $8\times$ shorter token sequences, respectively.

</details>


### [8] [Evidential Deep Learning for Multi-Modal Anti-UAV Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01742)
*Dmitry Golovchits,Seyed Sahand Mohammadi Ziabari,Ali Mohammed Mansoor Alsahag*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper evaluates whether evidential deep learning (EDL) heads, Dempster-Shafer evidence fusion, and uncertainty-driven temporal sensor gating can improve anti-UAV multi-sensor detection systems. The study conducts controlled ablations on three benchmarks (AntiUAV600, TRIDENT, MM-UAV). Results show EDL training objective improves accuracy (+5.9pp in E1, +4.8pp in E2), but the remaining components (DS fusion, Dirichlet vacuity, temporal gating) do not support their respective hypotheses. The benefit arises from the training objective rather than the uncertainty estimate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Anti-UAV systems increasingly fuse multiple sensors, yet their detection heads provide no per-modality reliability signal. The authors aim to evaluate whether EDL uncertainty estimation, DS evidence fusion, and temporal sensor gating can improve detection reliability and performance.

Method: Controlled ablation study on three benchmarks: (1) AntiUAV600 for thermal tracking, (2) TRIDENT for RGB-audio-RF classification, (3) MM-UAV for RGB-IR tracking. Evaluated EDL training objective vs. retrained sigmoid baselines, DS fusion vs. probability averaging, Dirichlet vacuity vs. predictive entropy, and temporal gating for latency saving.

Result: EDL training objective improves accuracy by +5.9 percentage points in E1 (with tripled tracker-on-absent rate) and +4.8pp in E2 (surviving bootstrap, p=0.011), with better classification error ranking (entropy UAUC ~0.94 vs. 0.51). DS fusion does not outperform probability averaging. Dirichlet vacuity adds no ranking power beyond predictive entropy and inverts at detection level due to extreme background imbalance. Temporal gating preserves accuracy only when nearly inactive and yields no realized latency saving on shared-backbone hardware.

Conclusion: The benefit of evidential learning arises primarily from its training objective rather than its uncertainty estimate. The detection-level breakdown is localized to anchor-level evaluation rather than the learned representation. Future work should focus on the training objective mechanism rather than uncertainty estimation for anti-UAV detection improvements.

Abstract: Anti-UAV systems increasingly fuse multiple sensors, yet their detection heads provide no per-modality reliability signal. This study evaluates whether evidential deep learning (EDL) heads, Dempster-Shafer (DS) evidence fusion, and uncertainty-driven temporal sensor gating improve anti-UAV detection through a controlled ablation on three benchmarks: thermal tracking (AntiUAV600), RGB-audio-RF classification (TRIDENT), and RGB-IR tracking (MM-UAV). The EDL training objective improves accuracy over retrained sigmoid baselines (+5.9 percentage points in accuracy and a tripled tracker-on-absent rate in E1; +4.8 percentage points in classification accuracy in E2, surviving a clip-clustered bootstrap, p = 0.011) and ranks classification errors substantially better (entropy UAUC approximately 0.94 vs. 0.51). The remaining components do not support their respective hypotheses. DS fusion does not outperform simple probability averaging. Dirichlet vacuity adds no ranking power beyond predictive entropy and inverts at the detection level, where extreme background imbalance causes it to encode class membership rather than error likelihood, a failure also observed for entropy and sigmoid confidence. Temporal gating preserves accuracy only when nearly inactive and yields no realised latency saving on shared-backbone hardware. The benefit of evidential learning therefore arises primarily from its training objective rather than its uncertainty estimate; a crop-level control further localises the detection-level breakdown to anchor-level evaluation rather than the learned representation.

</details>


### [9] [SCULPT: Training Edge Vision Models for Post-Training Quantization Readiness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01743)
*Bharadwaj Kavuri,Sourav Babu-PK,Varadhraj Ellapan,Pullarao Maddu,Prasad Deshpande*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SCULPT is a training-time method that improves post-training quantization readiness of edge vision models by using a topology-aware activation regularizer to suppress quantization-hostile skewness/kurtosis combined with a stable percentile-based clipping mechanism to learn deployment-ready activation bounds. It enables direct export to standard PTQ workflows for low-bit deployment (INT8, W4A8) without runtime transformations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge vision models are difficult to deploy on resource-constrained hardware due to quantization challenges. Standard FP32 training produces heavy-tailed activation distributions with outliers that destabilize activation quantization: preserving full range wastes quantization bins on rare extremes, while aggressive clipping causes information loss. Existing solutions like QAT add training complexity and bit-width coupling, while post hoc outlier-repair methods require runtime activation transformations.

Method: SCULPT combines two components: (1) A topology-aware activation regularizer that suppresses quantization-hostile skewness and kurtosis, and (2) A stable percentile-based clipping mechanism that learns deployment-ready activation bounds. Unlike QAT, it does not simulate quantization during optimization; it operates during ordinary FP32 fine-tuning.

Result: The method learns clipping bounds that can be exported directly into standard PTQ workflow for low-bit deployment, including INT8 and lower-bit settings such as W4A8.

Conclusion: SCULPT provides a training-time solution that improves PTQ readiness without the complexity of QAT or the runtime overhead of post hoc outlier-repair methods. It addresses the heavy-tailed activation distribution problem by learning appropriate activation bounds during training that can be directly used in standard PTQ deployment.

Abstract: Edge vision models are difficult to deploy on resource-constrained hardware, making low-bit post-training quantization (PTQ) attractive. In practice, standard FP32 training often produces heavy-tailed activation distributions whose outliers destabilize activation quantization: preserving the full range wastes quantization bins on rare extremes, while aggressive clipping causes information loss. Existing solutions typically rely on quantization-aware training (QAT), which adds training complexity and bit-width coupling, or advanced PTQ procedures that repair the model after training.
  We present SCULPT (Statistical Clipping and Uniform Loss for Post-Training), a training-time method that improves PTQ readiness during ordinary FP32 fine-tuning. SCULPT combines a topology-aware activation regularizer that suppresses quantization-hostile skewness and kurtosis with a stable percentile-based clipping mechanism that learns deployment-ready activation bounds. Unlike QAT, SCULPT does not simulate quantization during optimization; unlike post hoc outlier-repair PTQ methods, it does not require runtime activation transformations. The learned clipping bounds can be exported directly into a standard PTQ workflow for low-bit deployment, including INT8 and lower-bit settings such as W4A8.

</details>


### [10] [Swin Meets EfficientNet: Lightweight Architectures for GAN-Based Face Forensics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01749)
*Sejuti Basu,Ashima Sood,Vijay Kumar,Sahil Sharma*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A hybrid EfficientNet-B0 + Swin Transformer model achieves 99% accuracy and 99.44% recall for detecting GAN-generated synthetic faces, outperforming pure Swin variants and CNN-only baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern generative models (GANs, diffusion, autoregressive) produce highly realistic fake facial images, making detection increasingly difficult and raising concerns about identity theft, fraud, and misinformation. The research specifically targets GAN-generated synthetic faces which are foundational to face-centric deepfakes.

Method: Evaluated three Swin-Transformer-based architectures: (1) compact Swin Transformer trained from scratch, (2) ImageNet-1K pre-trained Swin-Tiny and Swin-Small adapted for binary classification, (3) novel hybrid combining EfficientNet-B0's convolutional processing with Swin Transformer backend. Models were tested on the 140K Real and Fake Faces dataset (StyleGAN-generated fakes + authentic Flickr/DFDC images) with balanced train/validation/test splits.

Result: The EfficientNet-B0 + Swin hybrid achieved 99% accuracy and 99.44% recall on 5,000 test images, outperforming both pure Swin variants and the previous CNN-only baseline.

Conclusion: Combining hierarchical CNN features with shifted-window self-attention provides an efficient and computationally lightweight method for detecting GAN-generated synthetic faces, leveraging CNNs' local texture features and Transformers' global contextual understanding.

Abstract: Modern generative models, such as GANs, diffusion architectures, and autoregressive systems, now produce facial images that are nearly indistinguishable from authentic photographs. This capability makes detecting forged images increasingly difficult, raising serious concerns about identity theft, fraud, and misinformation campaigns. Our research focuses specifically on GAN-generated synthetic faces, which underpin many face-centric deepfakes, and investigates efficient detection approaches using image analysis alone. Existing detection systems rely heavily on either convolutional neural networks (CNNs) or global vision transformers. While CNNs excel at identifying texture-based local features, they struggle with broader contextual understanding. Traditional Vision Transformer (ViT) models can capture long-range structures effectively, but demand substantial computational resources. Our work explores Swin-Transformer-based architectures across three implementations: a compact Swin Transformer trained from the ground up, ImageNet-1K pre-trained Swin-Tiny and Swin-Small models adapted for binary classification, and a novel hybrid combining EfficientNet-B0's convolutional processing with a Swin Transformer backend. We evaluated all models using the 140K Real and Fake Faces dataset, which includes StyleGAN-generated fake faces alongside authentic images from Flickr and DFDC, with balanced splits for training, validation, and testing. The EfficientNetB0+Swin hybrid achieved 99% accuracy and a 99.44% recall on 5,000 test images, outperforming both pure Swin variants and a previous CNN-only baseline on this dataset. Our results suggest that combining hierarchical CNN features with shifted-window self-attention provides an efficient and computationally lightweight method for detecting GAN-generated synthetic faces.

</details>


### [11] [AlphaRAD: Grounded Zero-Shot Classification in Chest Radiology via $α$-Corrected Binary Cross Entropy and Factorized Latent Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01757)
*Jianzhong You,Yuan Gao,Chris McIntosh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AlphaRAD is a vision-language pretrained model for open-vocabulary chest radiology understanding that addresses two key challenges: reducing in-batch noise during contrastive learning using structured medical concepts from LLM-parsed reports, and enhancing spatial grounding through a simple cross-modal fusion module called FLaS (Factorized Latent Supervision).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-Language Pretrained Models (VLPMs) offer scalable open-vocabulary chest radiology understanding, but two aspects remain underexplored: (1) how structured clinical semantics from medical reports can reduce in-batch noise during contrastive learning, and (2) how cross-modal fusion can produce more faithful spatial grounding without added complexity.

Method: Two main contributions: (1) Constructing a large-scale structured medical concept space from medical reports parsed by a Large Language Model, training AlphaRAD as a medical concept discriminator via α-Corrected Binary Cross-Entropy to mitigate in-batch learning noise and remove heuristic pair matching. (2) Proposing FLaS (Factorized Latent Supervision), which factorizes VLPM representations into independent subspaces with dedicated alignment supervision to enhance spatial grounding without introducing additional model parameters.

Result: AlphaRAD shows strong zero-shot generalization across diverse chest radiology tasks. It achieves state-of-the-art average performance across 16 classification benchmarks, and achieves individual state-of-the-art results on 7 grounding/phrase grounding and 3 segmentation datasets.

Conclusion: AlphaRAD demonstrates that leveraging structured medical concepts from LLM-parsed reports for contrastive learning, combined with a simple factorized latent supervision approach for cross-modal fusion, can effectively advance the state-of-the-art in chest radiology understanding with zero-shot generalization capabilities.

Abstract: Vision-Language Pretrained Models (VLPMs) offer a scalable path to open-vocabulary chest radiology understanding, yet two aspects remain underexplored: how structured clinical semantics extracted from medical reports can reduce in-batch noise during contrastive learning, and how cross-modal fusion can be designed to produce more faithful spatial grounding without added complexity. We introduce AlphaRAD, addressing these opportunities through two contributions. First, we construct a large-scale structured medical concept space from medical reports parsed by a Large Language Model for training, thereby mitigating in-batch learning noise and removing heuristic pair matching in contrastive learning, and thus naturally positioning AlphaRAD as a medical concept discriminator trained via $α$-Corrected Binary Cross-Entropy. Second, we propose FLaS (Factorized Latent Supervision), an extremely simple yet effective cross-modal feature fusion module that factorizes VLPM representations into independent subspaces, using dedicated alignment supervision to enhance the expressiveness of spatial grounding without introducing additional model parameters. Through extensive empirical validation, AlphaRAD shows strong zero-shot generalization across diverse chest radiology tasks. Notably, it establishes state-of-the-art average performance across 16 classification benchmarks, while achieving individual state-of-the-art results via distinct gains on 7 grounding/phrase grounding and 3 segmentation datasets.

</details>


### [12] [Allocate Before You Embed: Adaptive Visual Input Allocation for Video Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01778)
*Song Jin,Zhongtao Jiang,Chenglei Shen,Huanxuan Liao,Haozhe Chi,Zhiwei Wang,Kun Xu,Yong Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AllocEmbed is a novel allocate-then-embed framework for large-scale video retrieval that reallocates a fixed visual-input budget across more frames by using a lightweight allocator to assign frame-wise resolutions before the embedding backbone, guided by Retrieval-Driven Policy Optimization (RDPO).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video retrieval methods sample a small, fixed set of frames at original resolution, limiting temporal coverage and ignoring frame importance. The authors empirically found that expanding temporal coverage improves retrieval under fixed visual-input budget, with larger gains when preserving original per-frame resolution.

Method: AllocEmbed uses a lightweight allocator with low-cost previews to assign frame-wise resolutions before the embedding backbone. It introduces Retrieval-Driven Policy Optimization (RDPO) that learns the allocator directly from retrieval feedback using a rank-validated similarity gap and a confidence-guided efficiency incentive. The framework operates entirely before the backbone, allowing integration with existing retrieval systems without modifying the embedding model.

Result: AllocEmbed achieves the best overall retrieval performance among evaluated budget-matched methods on MMEB-V2 V-QA and V-RET tasks and the LongRet benchmark. It also demonstrates transferability across different embedding backbones.

Conclusion: The proposed allocate-then-embed paradigm effectively balances temporal coverage and spatial fidelity within fixed visual-input budgets. By reallocating budget based on frame importance learned through retrieval feedback, AllocEmbed outperforms existing methods while maintaining compatibility with existing retrieval systems.

Abstract: Large-scale video retrieval requires embedding models to encode long and diverse videos under tight visual-input and inference budgets. Existing methods typically sample a small, fixed set of frames at their original resolution, limiting temporal coverage and ignoring frame importance. Our empirical analysis shows that expanding temporal coverage improves retrieval even under a fixed visual-input budget. Gains are larger when the original per-frame resolution is preserved, highlighting the complementary roles of temporal coverage and spatial fidelity. Motivated by this finding, we propose AllocEmbed, an allocate-then-embed framework that reallocates a fixed visual-input budget across more frames. A lightweight allocator uses low-cost previews to assign frame-wise resolutions before the embedding backbone, preserving more detail where it most benefits retrieval while reducing visual cost elsewhere. We further introduce Retrieval-Driven Policy Optimization (RDPO), which learns the allocator directly from retrieval feedback using a rank-validated similarity gap and a confidence-guided efficiency incentive. Operating entirely before the backbone, AllocEmbed integrates with existing retrieval systems without modifying the embedding model or downstream pipeline. Experiments on the MMEB-V2 V-QA and V-RET tasks and our LongRet benchmark show that AllocEmbed achieves the best overall retrieval performance among the evaluated budget-matched methods and transfers across embedding backbones. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/jinsong8/AllocEmbed.

</details>


### [13] [Ten Architectures, One Error: Shared Failure Modes in Hyperspectral Classification under Spatially Disjoint Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01786)
*Ehsan Faghih,Fatemeh Ashrafi,Marguerite Moore,Zahra Saki*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper reveals that hyperspectral image classification benchmarks suffer from data leakage due to random pixel splits, causing inflated accuracy scores. The authors propose a leakage-free evaluation protocol based on spatial separation linked to model receptive fields, and demonstrate that using this protocol across 10 architectures leads to significant performance drops (0.147 average Macro-F1 drop) and changes in model rankings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hyperspectral image classification relies on random pixel splits within a single scene, which causes test pixels to be immediately adjacent to training pixels, artificially inflating accuracy. The Salinas dataset is widely used but its random split protocol creates misleading evaluation results.

Method: The authors introduce a leakage-free evaluation protocol that links spatial separation to the model's receptive field. They test this protocol across ten different architectures spanning multiple families: classical methods, spectral-only methods, spectral-spatial methods, transformer-based methods, vision-backbone methods, and state-space methods.

Result: Applying the leakage-free protocol shows that Macro-F1 drops by 0.147 on average across all architectures. Model rankings change by as many as five places. The study also reveals that all ten architectures misclassify largely the same pixels, indicating a spectral ambiguity in the data that none of the methods can resolve.

Conclusion: Leakage-free evaluation is essential for fair comparison in hyperspectral image classification. The protocol limits which architectures can be tested on a given benchmark based on the finite patch radius supported by each partition. The authors conclude that reporting the receptive field alongside the patch radius is essential for fair comparison. Additionally, the finding that all architectures fail on the same pixels suggests a fundamental limitation in the data that current approaches cannot overcome.

Abstract: Hyperspectral image classification still relies heavily on random pixel splits within a single scene. The Salinas dataset, randomly split, is among the most widely used datasets for comparing different architectures. However, under a random split method, a large fraction of test pixels fall immediately adjacent to a training pixel, which inflates reported accuracy. This work introduces a leakage-free evaluation protocol linking spatial separation to the model's receptive field. Applying this protocol across ten different architectures, including classical, spectral, spectral-spatial, transformer, vision-backbone, and state-space families, shows that Macro-F1 drops by 0.147 on average and model rankings change by as many as five places. Furthermore, leakage-free evaluation limits which architectures can be tested on a given benchmark. Since each partition supports patches only within a finite radius, reporting this radius alongside the receptive field is essential for fair comparison. In addition, this study reveals that all ten architectures misclassify largely the same pixels, pointing to a spectral ambiguity in the data that none of them resolves.

</details>


### [14] [CoViT: Instance-Correspondence Contrastive Learning for Vision Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01787)
*Yisen Wang,Zhirong Wu,Limin Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CoViT is a self-supervised learning framework that enables Vision Transformers to discriminate between object instances through geometry-guided contrastive learning. It uses attention-guided masking to generate instance masks and hardest contrastive mining to select positive/negative pairs, achieving 2+ AP improvements across instance-level perception tasks without extra decoders or labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision Transformers excel in semantic understanding but fail to discriminate between object instances - they produce identical embeddings for visually similar objects (e.g., two dogs), limiting their use in instance-level tasks such as object detection and instance segmentation.

Method: CoViT uses two key components: (1) Attention-guided masking - refines multi-head attention via adaptive thresholding and morphological operations to generate instance masks, identifying foreground anchors; (2) Hardest contrastive mining - computes pairwise embedding similarities to select intra-instance hardest positive (least similar patch within its mask) and inter-instance hardest negative (most similar patch from other instances), with intra-instance regions masked during negative search. These triplets drive a contrastive loss that compresses intra-instance variance and expands inter-instance margins.

Result: Consistent stable performance gains of over 2 AP points across multiple instance-level perception tasks using ViT as backbone architecture.

Conclusion: A pure ViT can learn instance-aware representations via inherent attention priors and targeted contrastive constraints, without requiring extra decoders or labels. This demonstrates that ViT can be extended to instance-level tasks by leveraging its attention mechanisms and carefully designed contrastive learning.

Abstract: Vision Transformers (ViT) excel in semantic understanding but fail to discriminate between object instances (e.g., identical embeddings for two dogs), limiting their use in instance-level tasks such as object detection and instance segmentation. We propose Contrastive Vision Transformer (CoViT), a self-supervised learning framework that injects instance-awareness into ViT through geometry-guided contrastive learning. CoViT uniquely coordinates ViT's attention maps and embeddings by constructing triplets: (1) Attention-guided masking: Refine multi-head attention via adaptive thresholding and morphological operations to generate instance masks, identifying foreground anchors; (2) Hardest contrastive mining: For each anchor, computing pairwise embedding similarities to select the intra-instance hardest positive (least similar patch within its mask) and inter-instance hardest negative (most similar patch from other instances), with intra-instance regions masked during negative search. These triplets drive a contrastive loss that simultaneously compresses intra-instance variance and expands inter-instance margins, forcing ViT to discern subtle geometric and appearance differences between instances. CoViT consistently achieves stable performance gains of over 2 AP points across multiple instance-level perception tasks by using ViT as backbone architecture. Notably, CoViT requires no extra decoders or labels, demonstrating that a pure ViT can learn instance-aware representations via inherent attention priors and targeted contrastive constraints. Code and models will be released.

</details>


### [15] [DESA-TTA: Dynamic EMA and Source Anchoring for Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01795)
*Atif Belal,Lilian Hollard,Marco Pedersoli,Eric Granger*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DESA-TTA addresses vulnerability of vision-language object detectors to distribution shifts during deployment by combining dynamic temporal averaging (which estimates teacher uncertainty from pseudo-label confidence and box density to select sample-wise EMA coefficients) with source anchoring (which partially restores student parameters toward pretrained values based on student drift). The method achieves 14.5 AP50 improvement over zero-shot on VOC-C while being 55% faster than prior TTA methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language object detectors (VLODs) achieve strong zero-shot performance but are vulnerable to distribution shifts during deployment. Existing mean-teacher test-time adaptation (TTA) methods suffer from two key limitations: (1) fixed exponential moving average (EMA) coefficient for teacher updates is suboptimal, and (2) repeated optimization with noisy pseudo-labels causes cumulative student drift.

Method: DESA-TTA uses two complementary mechanisms: (1) Dynamic temporal averaging estimates teacher uncertainty from pseudo-label confidence and box density to select sample-wise EMA coefficients within bounds determined by teacher parameter drift; (2) Source anchoring partially restores updated student parameters toward pretrained values, with anchoring strength increasing according to student drift.

Result: DESA-TTA achieves consistent improvements across diverse distribution shifts and two VLOD architectures. On VOC-C, it improves AP50 by 14.5 points over zero-shot inference while achieving 55% higher inference throughput than the previous state-of-the-art TTA method for YOLO-World.

Conclusion: DESA-TTA provides a low-overhead solution for improving VLOD robustness to distribution shifts through dynamic temporal averaging and source anchoring, effectively addressing both fixed EMA sensitivity and student drift problems with improved accuracy and efficiency.

Abstract: Vision-language object detectors (VLODs) achieve strong zero-shot performance but remain vulnerable to distribution shifts during deployment. Mean-teacher methods for test-time adaptation (TTA) can improve robustness by updating a student model using teacher-generated pseudo-labels. However, mean-teacher TTA is highly sensitive to the choice of a fixed exponential moving average (EMA) coefficient for teacher updates, and repeated optimization with noisy pseudo-labels can cause cumulative student drift. We propose Dynamic EMA and Source Anchoring for TTA (DESA-TTA), a low-overhead method that jointly regulates teacher updates and student drift through dynamic temporal averaging and source anchoring. Dynamic temporal averaging estimates teacher uncertainty from pseudo-label confidence and box density and uses it to select a sample-wise EMA coefficient within bounds determined by teacher parameter drift. Source anchoring partially restores the updated student parameters toward their pretrained values, with the anchoring strength increasing according to student drift. Experiments across diverse distribution shifts and two VLOD architectures show consistent improvements over existing TTA methods. On VOC-C, DESA-TTA improves AP$_{50}$ by 14.5 points over zero-shot inference while achieving 55\% higher inference throughput than the previous state-of-the-art TTA method for YOLO-World. Our code: https://github.com/imatif17/DESA-TTA

</details>


### [16] [Improved Automatic Target Recognition in Synthetic Aperture Sonar Imagery Using Large Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01800)
*C. J. Moore,Alex Hurt,Jordan Malof*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper compares CNN and transformer-based deep neural networks for Automatic Target Recognition (ATR) in Synthetic Aperture Sonar (SAS), investigating how architecture, network size, pretraining, data augmentation, and regularization affect performance to establish best practices for state-of-the-art SAS-ATR models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While deep neural networks dominate SAS-ATR, CNN architectures are predominantly used despite transformers being state-of-the-art in general computer vision. Additionally, limited labeled training data presents challenges, with data augmentation and pretrained weights showing mixed results in prior work. This motivates a systematic comparison of modern CNN and transformer architectures to identify optimal configurations for SAS-ATR.

Method: The authors conduct comprehensive experiments comparing modern CNN and transformer-based DNNs on SAS-ATR tasks. They systematically investigate multiple factors: network size, architecture type, pretraining method (from various imaging modalities), data augmentation strategies, and other regularization techniques. The goal is to identify which configurations yield the highest performance.

Result: The abstract does not provide specific quantitative results. It states the aim is to determine which architecture and training configurations elicit the highest performance in SAS-ATR, with a focus on producing the highest-performing model and providing a practical roadmap for training state-of-the-art SAS-ATR models.

Conclusion: The paper aims to provide empirical guidance and a roadmap for training state-of-the-art SAS-ATR models by identifying optimal combinations of architecture (CNN vs transformer), network size, pretraining approaches, and regularization strategies.

Abstract: Automatic Target Recognition (ATR) in Synthetic Aperture Sonar (SAS) is a task largely dominated by deep neural networks (DNNs). Most SAS-ATR models use convolutional neural network (CNN) architectures whereas transformer-based architectures have had much less representation in the literature despite being state of the art in general computer vision (CV) research. Additionally, researchers have had mixed results in attempting to overcome challenges presented by a scarcity of labeled training data by using methods such as data augmentation and the use of pretrained weights from a variety of imaging modalities. In this work, we compare the performance of modern CNN and transformer-based DNNs to determine which architecture and training configurations elicit the highest performance in SAS-ATR. We investigate how network size, architecture, pretraining method, data augmentation and other forms of regularization affect SAS-ATR performance with a focus on producing the highest-performing model and providing a roadmap for training state-of-the-art SAS-ATR models.

</details>


### [17] [Consistency as Regularization for Unsupervised Shadow Removal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01806)
*Anh-Kiet Duong,Petra Gomez-Krämer,Jean-Michel Carozza*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ShadowCLR is an unsupervised shadow removal framework that learns directly from shadow images without requiring paired data, shadow masks, or shadow-free references. It leverages consistency across multiple shadow observations of the same scene to regularize the learning process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing shadow removal methods rely on either supervised learning with paired shadow/shadow-free images or unsupervised approaches that still need shadow masks or reference images. This limits their applicability in real-world scenarios where such auxiliary information is unavailable.

Method: ShadowCLR uses consistency across shadow observations as regularization. The key insight is that shadows vary while underlying scene content remains consistent. The framework employs global and local consistency to: (1) explore visually related images, (2) learn from imperfectly aligned observations, and (3) focus representation on shared scene information while suppressing shadow-specific variations.

Result: Experiments on multiple benchmarks show that ShadowCLR achieves competitive and often superior performance over state-of-the-art unsupervised methods.

Conclusion: Consistency across shadow observations can provide effective regularization for shadow removal without requiring shadow masks or shadow-free images, enabling fully unsupervised learning directly from shadow images.

Abstract: Shadow removal is an important preprocessing step for many vision tasks, yet existing supervised methods require paired shadow and shadow-free images, while unsupervised approaches often still rely on shadow masks or shadow-free references. We propose ShadowCLR, an unsupervised framework that learns shadow removal directly from shadow images. Our key observation is that shadows vary across observations while the underlying scene content remains largely consistent. We therefore use consistency across shadow observations as regularization, encouraging the model to recover scene-consistent appearance while suppressing shadow-specific variations. Global and local consistency further enable us to explore visually related images, learn from imperfectly aligned observations, and focus the representation on shared scene information. Experiments on multiple benchmarks show that ShadowCLR achieves competitive and often superior performance over state-of-the-art unsupervised methods, demonstrating that consistency can provide regularization for shadow removal without shadow masks or shadow-free images.

</details>


### [18] [Integrated Laser Scanning and Image-Based Topology Optimization Techniques for Detection and Quantification of Visible and Subsurface Structural Defects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01808)
*Mehrdad Shafiei Dizaji,Devin Harris*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study presents two non-contact, vision-based methods for detecting and quantifying both visible surface damage and invisible subsurface defects in structural components. The first uses laser scanning to create 3D point clouds for comparative analysis, while the second combines 3D-DIC with finite element model updating and topology optimization to infer subsurface damage from surface deformation patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable characterization of structural defects requires methods capable of resolving both directly observable surface damage and damage that is not visible from the inspected surface. Current methods fail to adequately detect subsurface abnormalities.

Method: Two complementary approaches: (1) High-resolution laser scanning to generate 3D point clouds, comparing measured and reference point clouds to localize damaged regions, quantify geometric loss, and transfer defect geometry to finite element models. (2) 3D-DIC combined with finite element model updating and topology optimization, using measured surface response to infer subsurface abnormalities through their influence on spatial distribution of structural response.

Result: Both methods were evaluated on experimental steel-beam specimens with controlled smooth defects and randomly distributed defects. Comparisons with milling-based ground-truth measurements demonstrate that both methods can identify and quantify defect geometry, providing complementary information for visible and subsurface damage assessment.

Conclusion: The combined framework establishes a pathway toward high-fidelity, non-contact structural condition assessment and model updating for components with complex and irregular damage.

Abstract: Reliable characterization of structural defects requires methods capable of resolving both directly observable surface damage and damage that is not visible from the inspected surface. This study presents two complementary non-contact, vision-based approaches for the detection and quantitative characterization of defects in structural components. The first approach employs high-resolution laser scanning to generate three-dimensional (3D) point clouds of damaged steel specimens. Comparative processing of measured and reference point clouds is used to localize damaged regions, quantify geometric loss, and transfer the measured defect geometry to a finite element representation. The second approach combines full-field surface deformation measurements obtained using three-dimensional digital image correlation (3D-DIC) with finite element model updating and topology optimization. In this inverse framework, measured surface response is used to infer subsurface abnormalities through their influence on the spatial distribution of structural response. Experimental steel-beam specimens containing controlled smooth defects and randomly distributed defects are used to evaluate the approaches. Comparisons with milling-based ground-truth measurements demonstrate that both methods can identify and quantify defect geometry, while providing complementary information for visible and subsurface damage assessment. The combined framework establishes a pathway toward high-fidelity, non-contact structural condition assessment and model updating for components with complex and irregular damage.

</details>


### [19] [Video2Reaction: Training Foundation Video Models to Predict Audience Reaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01816)
*Sidong Zhang,Trang Nguyen,Shiv Shankar,Gauri Jagatap,Deepak Chandran,Andrea Fanelli,Madalina Fiterau*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Video2Reaction is a new multimodal dataset that maps short movie segments to viewers' emotional reactions expressed through social media comments. It models emotional labels as distributions over categorical emotions to capture the subjective nature of emotional perception. Two vision-language models (VLMs) fine-tuned with LoRA were benchmarked, showing effective learning and superior performance over specialized baselines. The pre-finetuned model also demonstrates strong transfer learning capabilities to another dataset (VCE) with limited training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to capture the natural diversity of emotional responses to videos by aggregating reactions from online comments at scale, addressing the subjective and ambiguous nature of emotional perception that traditional single-label approaches fail to capture.

Method: The researchers created Video2Reaction dataset by collecting social media comments on short movie segments and modeling labels as distributions over categorical emotions. They fine-tuned two vision-language models (VLMs) using LoRA adapters, then evaluated transfer learning on VCE dataset with different taxonomy and video domain, using only 1% of training data.

Result: VLMs fine-tuned on Video2Reaction outperform specialized baselines on dominant reaction prediction. LLaVA-NeXT-Video-7B pre-finetuned on Video2Reaction and adapted on only 1% of VCE training data achieves top-3 accuracy of 0.682, on par with the best reported VCE performance trained on the full dataset.

Conclusion: Video2Reaction effectively enables VLMs to learn emotional reactions from video content. The pre-finetuning approach demonstrates strong transfer learning capabilities across different emotional taxonomies and video domains, achieving competitive performance with minimal target domain data, suggesting the dataset has good generalization properties.

Abstract: We introduce Video2Reaction, a multimodal dataset that maps short movie segments to the induced emotional reactions of viewers in the wild, as expressed through social media comments. Video2Reaction captures the natural diversity of emotional responses by aggregating reactions from online comments at scale, modeling labels as distributions over categorical emotions to better reflect the subjective and ambiguous nature of emotional perception. We benchmark two vision-language models (VLMs) finetuned with LoRA, showing that VLMs learn effectively from Video2Reaction and outperform specialized baselines on dominant reaction prediction. We further demonstrate that VLMs pre-finetuned on Video2Reaction transfer effectively to VCE, another induced emotion dataset with a different taxonomy and video domain. Notably, LLaVA-NeXT-Video-7B pre-finetuned on Video2Reaction and adapted on only 1% of VCE training data achieves a top-3 accuracy of 0.682, on par with the best reported VCE performance trained on the full dataset. The dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/infofusionlab/Video2Reaction

</details>


### [20] [Kirin: Animal Motion Generation from In-the-Wild Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01823)
*Brian Nlong Zhao,Zhuoyang Pan,James M. Rehg,Jiajun Wu,Shangzhe Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Kirin is a framework that addresses the scarcity of animal motion data by reconstructing 3D motion from in-the-wild videos, creating AiM3D (the first large-scale video-text-motion dataset for quadrupeds), and enabling text/image-conditioned motion generation for animating 3D animal meshes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Animal motion research lags behind human motion research due to the impracticality of capturing animal motion in controlled environments, resulting in small, domain-limited datasets that restrict downstream applications like animation.

Method: 1) Reconstruct 3D motion from large collections of in-the-wild animal videos; 2) Pair reconstructed motion with captions to create AiM3D dataset; 3) Develop a visual-guided motion generation model conditioned on both text and image; 4) Use off-the-shelf image-to-3D model for automatic rigging and animation of 3D meshes.

Result: AiM3D: the first large-scale dataset offering aligned video-text-motion tuples for quadruped animals; a visual-guided motion generation model capable of generating realistic motion across diverse animal species; ready-to-render animated animals through automatic rigging and animation.

Conclusion: Kirin and AiM3D establish a new foundation for large-scale, text and image conditioned animal motion generation and animation, addressing the data scarcity problem that has historically limited progress in animal motion research.

Abstract: Understanding animal motion is fundamental to modeling animal behavior and biomechanics, yet progress in this area lags far behind human motion research due to the scarcity of high-quality motion data. While human motion can be captured in controlled environments, it is impractical for most animal species, resulting in small, domain-limited datasets that restrict downstream applications such as animation. To address this challenge, we introduce Kirin, a framework that reconstructs motion from video, learns motion priors at scale, and generates realistic motion that can be directly applied to animated assets. Using large collections of in-the-wild animal videos, we reconstruct 3D motion sequences and pair them with captions to create AiM3D, the first large-scale dataset offering aligned video-text-motion tuples for quadruped animals. Building on this dataset, we develop a visual-guided motion generation model that conditions on both text and image to guide the generation of realistic motion across diverse animal species. Finally, by leveraging an off-the-shelf image-to-3D model, we automatically rig and animate 3D meshes using generated motion, producing ready-to-render animated animals. Together, our dataset and framework establish a new foundation for large-scale, text and image conditioned animal motion generation and animation. Project page: https://kirin-ani.github.io/.

</details>


### [21] [SliceBridge: context-consistent repair of corrupted slice intervals in T1-weighted MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01827)
*Jiheng Li,Michael E. Kim,Trent Schwartz,Gaurav Rudravaram,Derek B. Archer,Timothy J. Hohman,the Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative,Lianrui Zuo,Bennett A. Landman*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SliceBridge is a deep learning framework that restores corrupted slice intervals in T1-weighted brain MRI using rectified flow matching, conditioned on surrounding intact slices and slice positions. It maintains through-plane consistency by coupling slices within the interval through correlated noise, shared flow time, and synchronized sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structural MRI images can have corrupted slices due to acquisition, motion, hardware, or reconstruction issues, which can bias downstream morphometric analysis. Current solutions either discard the entire image or attempt slice-wise restoration without considering the interval as a cohesive unit, leading to inconsistencies.

Method: The proposed SliceBridge framework uses rectified flow matching conditioned on surrounding intact slices and their relative slice positions. Through-plane consistency is achieved by: (1) interval-correlated initial noise, (2) shared flow time, and (3) synchronized sampling across all slices within the corrupted interval. The restored interval is then inserted back while preserving all other slices.

Result: SliceBridge reduced error in slice-to-slice changes within repaired intervals by 32.9%-41.3% across interval lengths compared to independent reconstruction. It achieved higher SSIM at every interval length. In controlled-corruption cases, it reduced median error in regional brain volume estimates from 1.95% (corrupted volumes) to 1.05%.

Conclusion: SliceBridge effectively restores corrupted slice intervals in T1-weighted MRI while maintaining through-plane consistency. The method significantly improves downstream morphometric analysis accuracy, reducing volume estimation errors by nearly 50% compared to corrupted data.

Abstract: Structural magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) images are sometimes corrupted over a contiguous set of slices, where acquisition, motion, hardware, or reconstruction effects leave a single slice or short interval inconsistent with its neighbors while the rest of the image remains usable. Such localized corruption can bias downstream morphometric analysis, yet discarding or reacquiring an otherwise usable image is costly. We formulate this as an image restoration problem: given the location of the affected interval, reconstruct those slices from the surrounding anatomical and imaging context. We propose SliceBridge, a framework for restoring corrupted slice intervals in T1-weighted MRI using rectified flow matching conditioned on the surrounding intact slices and their relative slice positions. Through-plane consistency is encouraged by coupling the slices within the interval through interval-correlated initial noise, a shared flow time, and synchronized sampling. The restored interval is then inserted back, leaving all other slices unchanged. We trained and validated the model on 9,877 T1-weighted brain MRI volumes from four datasets and evaluated it on 581 external subjects using clean interval withholding and controlled corruptions. Compared with a matched model that reconstructed target slices independently, SliceBridge reduced error in slice-to-slice changes within repaired intervals by 32.9%-41.3% across interval lengths and achieved higher SSIM at every interval length. In controlled-corruption cases, SliceBridge reduced the median error in regional brain volume estimates produced by a downstream segmentation model from 1.95% in corrupted volumes to 1.05%.

</details>


### [22] [Cross-Model Distillation of a Human-Pose Foundation Model from Unannotated Infant Video for Markerless 3D Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01840)
*R. James Cotton,Divya Joshi,Colleen Peyton*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of applying adult-trained pose estimation models to infant video analysis. The authors use cross-model distillation to transfer knowledge from Sapiens 2 (strong 2D performance) into SAM 3D Body (3D mesh recovery), achieving significant improvements in both 2D keypoint accuracy (PCK@10px: 0.22→0.42) and 3D position error (25.5mm→22.2mm) on infant data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spontaneous movement assessment in infants is a validated early predictor of cerebral palsy risk, but current clinical instruments require specially trained raters, are time-consuming, and have inter-rater variability. Existing foundation models for markerless motion capture are trained almost entirely on adults, and prior research showed no single model is jointly best for infants (trade-off between 2D keypoint accuracy and 3D body recovery).

Method: Cross-model distillation from Sapiens 2 pose model (frozen teacher supplying dense pseudo-labels) into SAM 3D Body model, using unannotated infant video alone. A differentiable renderer aligns the predicted mesh to pseudo-labels in the training loop. Evaluation on 11 held-out infants (18 sessions, 173 recordings) under multi-view protocol.

Result: Fine-tuning improves same-view 2D keypoint agreement with Sapiens reference: median body PCK@10px improved from 0.22 to 0.42, face PCK@10px from 0.22 to 0.42. Procrustes-aligned mean per joint 3D position error improved from 25.5mm to 22.2mm.

Conclusion: Cross-model distillation using unannotated infant video improves SAM 3D Body model performance on infants, demonstrating an effective approach to adapt adult-trained foundation models for infant motion capture.

Abstract: Spontaneous movement is one of the earliest windows onto an infant's neuromotor health, and structured clinical instruments that score it are validated early predictors of cerebral-palsy risk. However, they require specially trained raters, are time-consuming, and carry inter-rater variability. This motivates automated, video-based markerless assessment, especially as marker-based motion capture is impractical in infants. Yet the foundation models that make markerless capture possible are trained almost entirely on adults: our recent multi-view infant study found that no single model is jointly best, with strong 2D keypoint accuracy and direct 3D body recovery split across different models. While that study identifies this trade-off, it does not resolve it. Here, we perform cross-model distillation from the Sapiens 2 pose model into the SAM 3D Body model, using unannotated infant video alone. A frozen teacher supplies dense pseudo-labels, and a differentiable renderer aligns the predicted mesh to them in the training loop. On eleven held-out infants (18 sessions, 173 recordings) under our prior study's multi-view protocol, fine-tuning improves same-view 2D keypoint agreement with the Sapiens reference (median body percentage of correct keypoints @ 10px 0.22 -> 0.42, face 0.22 -> 0.42) and Procrustes-aligned mean per joint 3D position error (25.5 -> 22.2 mm). This demonstrates how cross-model distillation improves SAM 3D Body model performance on infants.

</details>


### [23] [RAFT-DVC: Resolution-Aware Machine Learning-Based Digital Volume Correlation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01876)
*Zixiang Tong,Lehu Bu,Jin Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RAFT-DVC is a resolution-aware deep learning framework for digital volume correlation that uses recurrent all-pairs field transforms with encoder downsampling factors (s=2, 4, 8). It achieves displacement localization accuracy of ~0.017 feature-grid voxels with error scaling of ~0.017s voxels, performs comparably to classical DVC under fine-texture conditions, and becomes advantageous under coarse-texture/large-displacement scenarios. The work also identifies and corrects coordinate-order inconsistencies in 3D RAFT correlation sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital volume correlation (DVC) lacks systematic understanding of how internal model resolution affects accuracy and operating range. Existing ML-based DVC methods don't adequately characterize these trade-offs. This work aims to develop a resolution-aware DVC framework that systematically explores different downsampling factors and their effects on displacement measurement accuracy.

Method: The researchers developed RAFT-DVC, a family of RAFT-based DVC solvers with matched design using encoder downsampling factors s=2, 4, and 8. They used synthetic benchmarks, frequency-swept tests, tiled inference for dense estimation, and evaluated on confocal volumetric images during indentation and micro-CT images of elastomeric foam. They also identified coordinate-order inconsistencies in 3D RAFT correlation sampling and used a non-cubic impulse test to verify sampler geometry independently of network training.

Result: The three solvers achieved displacement localization of ~0.017 feature-grid voxels, with empirical raw-volume error scaling of ~0.017s voxels. RAFT-DVC achieves errors comparable to tuned classical DVC under fine-texture, small-to-moderate-displacement conditions and becomes competitive/advantageous under coarse-texture, large-displacement conditions. The sampler correction improved native-input accuracy and generalization to unseen volume dimensions. Cross-texture transfer was demonstrated from synthetic particle-labeled data to real micro-CT foam images.

Conclusion: RAFT-DVC establishes a fast, resolution-aware framework for dense DVC with characterized accuracy and operating regimes.
