The resolution (downsampling factor) directly controls the accuracy-displacement reach trade-off, enabling users to select appropriate solvers based on deformation magnitude and image texture. The work provides practical guidance for matching solver operating regime to specific experimental conditions.

Abstract: Digital volume correlation (DVC) provides three-dimensional full-field displacement measurements from volumetric images, but how the internal resolution of a machine-learning-based DVC model affects accuracy and operating range remains poorly understood. Here, we present RAFT-DVC, a resolution-aware family of recurrent all-pairs field transforms (RAFT)-based DVC solvers with encoder downsampling factors s = 2, 4, and 8. Using a matched design, we find that the three solvers localize displacement to approximately 0.017 feature-grid voxel, giving an empirical raw-volume error scaling of approximately 0.017s voxel. The solvers exhibit complementary operating regimes governed jointly by displacement reach and volumetric-texture compatibility. Synthetic benchmarks show that RAFT-DVC achieves errors of the same order as tuned classical DVC under fine-texture, small-to-moderate-displacement conditions and becomes competitive or advantageous under coarse-texture, large-displacement conditions. Frequency-swept tests quantify deformation spatial resolution, while tiled inference enables dense estimation on large volumes. Evaluation on confocal volumetric images acquired during indentation illustrates the importance of matching solver operating regime to deformation magnitude and image texture. Tests on micro-CT images of elastomeric foam, despite training only on particle-labeled synthetic data, provide evidence of cross-texture transfer. We also identify coordinate-order inconsistencies in three-dimensional RAFT correlation sampling and introduce a non-cubic impulse test to verify sampler geometry independently of network training. Correcting the sampler improves native-input accuracy and generalization to unseen volume dimensions. Together, these results establish RAFT-DVC as a fast, resolution-aware framework for dense DVC with characterized accuracy and operating regimes.

</details>


### [24] [SignMatch: Matching Dictionary Signs to Continuous Sign Language Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01886)
*Ryan Wong,Youngjoon Jang,Liliane Momeni,Gül Varol,Andrew Zisserman*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a sign language matching method that learns a prototype-structured embedding space from continuous signing videos, enabling dictionary sign videos to be matched to corresponding signs in continuous signing based on visual similarity (handshape and motion). The method generalizes across American, British, and Spanish Sign Languages without dataset-specific supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core challenge is matching isolated dictionary sign videos to their corresponding instances in continuous signing videos, which is essential for dictionary retrieval, sign annotation, and understanding continuous sign language. Existing methods struggle with generalization across different sign languages and require heavy supervision.

Method: The method learns a prototype-structured sign embedding space from continuous video annotated with signs, where each learnable prototype corresponds to a sign class. Isolated dictionary videos are mapped into this sign space, enabling matching through embedding similarity. This design supports direct dictionary-guided sign matching and can extend to unseen signs using only dictionary exemplars.

Result: The method demonstrates strong generalization across three datasets/tasks: ASL-Citizen dictionary retrieval, ChaLearn OSLWL dictionary-to-continuous sign matching, and BOBSL's CSLR2 evaluation for automatic sign annotation. It outperforms prior methods on all three benchmarks without benchmark-specific supervision, effectively transferring across American, British, and Spanish Sign Languages.

Conclusion: The proposed prototype-structured embedding approach successfully enables dictionary-to-continuous sign matching with excellent cross-lingual generalization. By learning from continuous signing videos and mapping dictionary exemplars into the same space, the method achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple sign languages and datasets without requiring dataset-specific supervision.

Abstract: The objective of this paper is to match dictionary sign videos to corresponding signs in continuous signing videos, where a match is defined by the visual similarity alone - the handshape and motion relative to the body. To achieve this, we learn a prototype-structured sign embedding space from continuous video annotated with signs, where each learnable prototype corresponds to a sign class. Isolated dictionary videos are then mapped into this sign space, enabling the matching between dictionary exemplars and continuous sign instances. This design supports direct dictionary-guided sign matching through embedding similarity and naturally extends to unseen signs using only dictionary exemplars. Experiments on ASL-Citizen dictionary retrieval, ChaLearn OSLWL dictionary-to-continuous sign matching, and using BOBSL's CSLR2 evaluation for automatic sign annotation demonstrate strong generalisation across datasets, tasks and sign languages. Without benchmark-specific supervision, the learned representation transfers effectively across American, British, and Spanish Sign Languages, outperforming prior methods on all three benchmarks. Project page: https://www.robots.ox.ac.uk/~vgg/research/signmatch/

</details>


### [25] [Does Playing it Safe Count as Faithfulness? Reassessing LVLM Hallucination Mitigation Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01888)
*Mehrdad Fazli,Sina Mansouri,Mohit Marvania,Ziwei Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Current hallucination mitigation methods for LVLMs may create a false sense of progress by rewarding conservative generation rather than true multimodal grounding; improvements on hallucination benchmarks fail to transfer to broader capabilities, revealing a need for multi-dimensional evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether reported hallucination reductions in LVLMs reflect genuine improvements in multimodal grounding or merely conservative generation strategies, as existing evaluation protocols may overestimate progress.

Method: Evaluated six inference-time hallucination mitigation methods across three LVLMs and four benchmarks, including hallucination-focused evaluations and the diverse capability benchmark MMStar.

Result: Two consistent patterns emerged: (1) hallucination reduction is coupled with reduced informativeness (lower object recall, visual coverage, and response detailedness); (2) improvements on hallucination benchmarks do not reliably transfer to broader multimodal capabilities, showing inconsistent or degraded performance on fine-grained perception and reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Current evaluation protocols overestimate progress by rewarding conservative generation. Hallucination mitigation should be evaluated as a faithfulness-informativeness-capability trade-off rather than through hallucination scores alone.

Abstract: Recent inference-time hallucination mitigation methods for large vision-language models (LVLMs) report strong gains on hallucination benchmarks. However, it remains unclear whether lower hallucination scores reflect improved multimodal grounding or more conservative generation. We evaluate six mitigation methods across three LVLMs and four benchmarks, including hallucination-focused evaluation and the diverse capability benchmark MMStar. Our analysis reveals two consistent patterns. First, hallucination reduction is often coupled with reduced informativeness: methods that lower hallucination rates also reduce object recall, visual coverage, or response detailedness. Second, improvements on hallucination benchmarks do not reliably transfer to broader multimodal capabilities, with methods showing inconsistent or degraded performance on fine-grained perception and reasoning tasks. Our findings suggest that current evaluation protocols may overestimate progress by rewarding conservative generation. We argue that hallucination mitigation should be evaluated as a faithfulness--informativeness--capability trade-off rather than through hallucination scores alone.

</details>


### [26] [TAPVid-MV: A Benchmark for Tracking Any Point in 3D Across Multiple Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01899)
*Skanda Koppula,Frano Rajic,Abdullah Faiz Ur Rahman,Yi Yang,Ignacio Rocco,Jeet Thakwani,Rishabh Kabra,Andrew Zisserman,Joao Carreira,Siyu Tang,Carl Doersch,Gabriel Brostow*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TAPVid-MV is the first benchmark for long-term 3D point tracking across multiple synchronized camera views, containing 284 sequences with 1,142 camera streams and 109,769 point tracks. The benchmark reveals that no existing method performs well, and geometry recovery (not point correspondence) is the major bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing point-tracking benchmarks focus on single videos or static multi-camera rigs, but none test long-term 3D point tracking across several synchronized views under camera motion. This gap limits progress in robotics, AR/VR, and autonomous driving applications that rely on multi-camera systems.

Method: The dataset was created using various auxiliary modalities (sensor depth, LiDAR, SLAM, SfM, human meshes, posed object meshes, and simulation) to obtain ground truth trajectories. Every sequence and trajectory was visually verified by human annotators. Over 30 baselines were evaluated.

Result: No method comes close to solving the task. Surprisingly, existing multi-view point trackers do not consistently outperform monocular point trackers. Geometry recovery was identified as a major bottleneck, not point correspondence errors.

Conclusion: The benchmark addresses a critical gap in multi-view 3D point tracking evaluation. The released annotations support not only multi-view 3D point tracking but also monocular 2D/3D point tracking, future-trajectory prediction, and 4D reconstruction. The key insight is that improving geometry recovery methods is more impactful than improving correspondence matching.

Abstract: Multi-camera systems are increasingly practical for robotics, AR/VR, and autonomous driving because complementary views reduce depth ambiguity and preserve visibility under occlusion. Existing point-tracking benchmarks, however, focus on a single video or static multi-camera rigs. None test long-term 3D point tracking across several synchronized views under camera motion. We introduce TAPVid-MV (Tracking Any Point in Video across Multiple Views), the first benchmark for this setting. It contains a curated set of 284 sequences, 1,142 calibrated camera streams, and 109,769 point tracks across seven subsets spanning indoor and outdoor domains, from robotics and human activity to driving and synthetic procedural scenes. We obtain these trajectories using dataset-specific auxiliary modalities: sensor depth, LiDAR, SLAM and SfM points, human meshes, posed object meshes, and simulation. Every sequence and trajectory is visually verified by human annotators.
  Across more than 30 baselines, no method comes close to solving the task. Surprisingly, existing multi-view point trackers do not consistently outperform monocular point trackers. By evaluating reconstruction and point tracking on the same datasets, TAPVid-MV helps distinguish errors in recovered geometry from errors in point correspondence. Through this joint analysis, we identify geometry recovery as a major bottleneck for accurate 3D point tracking. Beyond multi-view 3D point tracking, our released annotations support monocular 2D and 3D point tracking, future-trajectory prediction, and 4D reconstruction.

</details>


### [27] [Automated Maize Ear Phenotyping Using 3D Reconstructions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01921)
*Ritwesh A. Kumar,Som Tripathi,Peja Matthews,Srikar Reddy,Talukder Zaki Jubery,Patrick Schnable,Adarsh Krishnamurthy,Baskar Ganapathysubramanian*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents an automated pipeline for extracting maize kernel traits (row number, kernels per row, kernel size) from 3D point clouds of corn ears using computer vision techniques including COLMAP, NeRF, and Cellpose-SAM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual measurement of maize kernel traits cannot keep pace with the volume of maize generated in breeding programs, despite these traits being important for yield prediction and being genetically determined.

Method: The pipeline uses: 1) video-to-point-cloud platform (COLMAP and NeRF), 2) density-based separation for ear isolation, 3) distance calibration to physical units, 4) Z-axis alignment via PCA, 5) cylindrical unwrapping to 2D image, 6) contrast enhancement, 7) zero-fine-tuning instance segmentation using Cellpose-SAM, and 8) a triple-juxtaposed unwrap strategy to prevent double-counting.

Result: The pipeline achieved kernel count R² = 0.921 (MAPE = 10.33%) and kernel row number within ±2 rows for 95.2% of ears (MAE = 0.75 rows) on a 168-ear held-out test set from a 268-ear labeled dataset.

Conclusion: The developed pipeline enables high-throughput, automated extraction of maize kernel traits from 3D point clouds, producing a multi-trait dataset with known genotype identity for phenotype-to-genotype association analyses.

Abstract: Maize kernel traits such as row number, kernels per row, and kernel size vary largely for genetic reasons and are consistently associated with regions of the genome that influence yield. Manual measurement of these traits, however, cannot keep pace with the volume of maize generated in a breeding program. To address this, we developed and validated a fully automated pipeline for extracting these traits from 3D point clouds of corn ears, built on a recently developed video-to-point-cloud platform. Raw video frames are processed through COLMAP and NeRF, the ear is isolated via density-based separation, and the point cloud is distance-calibrated to physical units. The calibrated ear point cloud was Z-axis aligned via PCA and cylindrically unwrapped to a 2D image. We enhanced contrast and performed zero-fine-tuning instance segmentation using Cellpose-SAM. A triple-juxtaposed unwrap strategy was used to prevent double-counting at the seam. The pipeline achieved kernel count R^2 = 0.921 (MAPE = 10.33%) and kernel row number within +-2 rows for 95.2% of ears (MAE = 0.75 rows) on a 168-ear held-out set from the 268-ear labeled dataset. The resulting multi-trait dataset has known genotype identity for each ear, positioning it for phenotype-to-genotype association analyses.

</details>


### [28] [Learning with Volterra Neural Networks: A System Theoretic Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01928)
*Haoyu Yun,Hamid Krim,Yufang Bao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: kVNN is a learnable kernelized Volterra Neural Operator that enables compact higher-order filtering for signal, image, and video modeling by combining Volterra filtering with learnable polynomial-kernel atoms, achieving an order-decoupled representation that avoids explicit high-order tensor parameterization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Higher-order interaction components are important for signal, image, and video modeling, but explicit high-order operators suffer from rapidly increasing parameter and computational costs. The paper aims to improve the efficiency of Volterra-type neural operators through kernelization while providing structured interpretation of higher-order components.

Method: The proposed kVNN combines the order-wise structure of Volterra filtering with learnable polynomial-kernel atoms. Different interaction orders are represented by separate learnable centers and coefficients, creating an order-decoupled representation that can be implemented as a CNN-compatible layer.

Result: Experiments on representative vision tasks demonstrate that kVNN achieves a favorable accuracy-efficiency trade-off.

Conclusion: Kernelization is an effective approach to make Volterra-type neural operators more practical for higher-order modeling, offering a good balance between representational power and computational efficiency.

Abstract: Higher-order interaction components are important for signal, image, and video modeling, but explicit high-order operators often suffer from rapidly increasing parameter and computational costs. This paper presents kVNN, a learnable kernelized Volterra Neural operator for compact higher-order filtering. The motivation is to use kernelization to improve the efficiency of Volterra-type neural operators while providing a structured interpretation of their higher-order components. The proposed formulation combines the order-wise structure of Volterra filtering with learnable polynomial-kernel atoms, allowing different interaction orders to be represented by separate learnable centers and coefficients. This order-decoupled representation avoids explicit high-order tensor parameterization and can be implemented as a CNN-compatible layer. Experiments on representative vision tasks show that kVNN achieves a favorable accuracy--efficiency trade-off.

</details>


### [29] [Aggregating Neighbor Embedding Projection and Rank-Based Manifold Learning for Image Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01963)
*Vinicius Atsushi Sato Kawai,Gustavo Rosseto Leticio,Lucas Pascotti Valem,Daniel Carlos Guimarães Pedronette*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a novel framework that combines neighbor embedding projections (UMAP) with rank-based manifold learning through Borda Count aggregation to improve content-based image retrieval. The approach addresses the challenge of combining projection-based and rank-based strategies to exploit their complementary properties for better retrieval performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses three key challenges in CBIR: 1) effectively ranking similar images in high-dimensional feature spaces where pairwise distances fail, 2) capturing contextual relationships among images, and 3) bridging the semantic gap between visual features and high-level concepts. The motivation is to combine complementary strategies - manifold learning for improved feature representations and rank-based refinement for exploiting contextual information from ranked lists.

Method: The proposed framework combines: 1) Uniform Manifold Approximation and Projection (UMAP) for generating alternative low-dimensional feature representations, and 2) Borda Count aggregation strategy to combine ranked lists from UMAP projections and rank-based re-ranking methods. Experiments used deep learning features from ResNet152, Swin Transformer, and DINOv2 models on multiple public datasets.

Result: The proposed approach improves retrieval effectiveness in several scenarios, particularly when baseline representations struggle to achieve high precision. The aggregation strategy improves quality of top-ranked positions, achieving competitive Mean Average Precision (MAP) and Precision values across different datasets and feature extractors.

Conclusion: Combining projection-based and rank-based manifold learning strategies through rank aggregation can provide complementary contextual information for image retrieval tasks, offering a promising direction for improving CBIR systems.

Abstract: Content-based image retrieval (CBIR) has advanced significantly with deep learning, yet effectively ranking similar images remains challenging, particularly in high-dimensional feature spaces, where pairwise distances often fail to capture contextual relationships and the semantic gap between visual features and high-level concepts persists. Manifold learning and rank-based refinement methods have emerged as complementary strategies, respectively improving feature representations and exploiting contextual information embedded in ranked lists, such as neighborhood relationships among images. However, combining these projection-based and rank-based strategies to exploit their complementary properties remains a challenging research problem. To address this, we propose a framework that combines neighbor embedding projections with rank-based manifold learning through rank aggregation. Uniform Manifold Approximation and Projection (UMAP) generates alternative low-dimensional feature representations, and ranked lists obtained from UMAP projections and rank-based re-ranking methods are combined using the Borda Count aggregation strategy. Experiments were conducted on several public datasets using deep learning features extracted from ResNet152, Swin Transformer, and DINOv2 models. Results show that the proposed approach improves retrieval effectiveness in several scenarios, particularly when the baseline representation struggles to achieve high precision. The aggregation strategy also often improves the quality of top-ranked positions, leading to competitive Mean Average Precision (MAP) and Precision values across different datasets and feature extractors. These findings suggest that combining projection-based and rank-based manifold learning strategies through rank aggregation can provide complementary contextual information for image retrieval tasks.

</details>


### [30] [Morphology signal in whole slide image foundation models can automatically triage slides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01987)
*Ayushi Sinha,Shashank Yadav,Benjamin Holmes,Pravat Das,Aaron W. Bogan,James S. Lewis,Santiago Romero-Brufau,Andrew Y. K. Foong,Scott H. Kaufmann,Kathryn M. Van Abel,David M. Routman,Michael R. Lucas*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a pipeline using WSI foundation models for zero-shot classification to automatically rank and identify tumor-containing slides from cancer patient exams, eliminating the need for manual pathologist curation. The method successfully identifies tumor slides in the top-2 positions for patients with up to 43 slides.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the challenges in training models on WSI data: the artificial assumption of 1 slide per patient in many datasets, or the signal dilution when using all slides per patient. The goal is to automatically identify slides containing tumor or diagnostic biomarkers without requiring tedious manual pathologist curation.

Method: The proposed pipeline uses zero-shot classification with publicly available WSI foundation models (FMs) to rank whole slide images based on their predicted tumor content. A ranked evaluation framework was also developed to benchmark FM performance in slide triage tasks.

Result: The evaluations demonstrate that ranking WSIs based on zero-shot classification predictions using WSI FMs accurately identifies slides with the most tumor. On multiple datasets, tumor slides are identified in the top-2 ranked slides for patients with up to 43 slides, indicating that WSI FMs contain sufficient morphology signal to automatically triage slides.

Conclusion: The pipeline demonstrates that WSI foundation models can effectively automate slide triage, identifying tumor slides in the top-2 positions for patients with up to 43 slides. This approach eliminates the need for tedious manual curation by pathologists and provides a practical solution for handling multiple slides per patient in cancer diagnosis and staging.

Abstract: Patient exams in the cancer diagnosis and staging process typically generate several whole slide images (WSIs). One of the initial steps in training models on WSI data is identifying one or a few slides containing tumor or other diagnostic biomarkers necessary for downstream prediction tasks such as estimating recurrence risk or progression-free survival. This step requires tedious manual curation by experienced pathologists. Many published datasets make the artificial assumption of 1 slide per patient. Alternatively, all slides per patient may be used for model training, which may dilute the signal from the few slides containing tumor or other relevant information. In this paper, we present a pipeline to overcome these challenges using publicly available WSI foundation models (FMs). Our evaluations show that ranking WSIs based on predictions from zero-shot classification using WSI FMs accurately identifies slides with the most tumor, indicating that WSI FMs contain sufficient morphology signal to automatically triage slides. We also present a formulation for ranked evaluation to benchmark FM performance in slide triage. We show, on multiple datasets, that tumor slides are identified in the top-2 ranked slides for patients with up to 43 slides.

</details>


### [31] [Linear Fusion MultiDiffusion for Fast Training-Free Spherical Panorama Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01997)
*Akio Hayakawa,Yusuke Mukuta,Tatsuya Harada*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LF-MultiDiffusion is a training-free panorama generation method that extends MultiDiffusion by reformulating latent aggregation as a regularized least-squares problem, solved with a Krylov-based iterative solver inside the denoising loop. This enables denser, more natural mappings with fewer perspective views while significantly improving inference efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training-free panorama generation methods often require many perspective views and suffer from unstable generation. The goal is to improve inference efficiency while maintaining or enhancing visual quality and panoramic consistency.

Method: The method extends MultiDiffusion to support linear projections between target and reference image spaces. Key technical contributions include: (1) reformulating latent aggregation as a regularized least-squares problem, and (2) solving it efficiently with a Krylov-based iterative solver embedded inside the denoising loop.

Result: LF-MultiDiffusion achieves better visual quality, text alignment, and panoramic consistency compared to the strongest training-free baseline, while providing a 15.36× speedup in inference time.

Conclusion: The proposed approach demonstrates that treating latent aggregation as a regularized least-squares problem with Krylov-based solvers enables more efficient and higher-quality panorama generation without training, significantly reducing computational overhead while improving output quality.

Abstract: We propose LF-MultiDiffusion, a training-free panorama generation method that extends MultiDiffusion to support linear projections between target and reference image spaces. Our key idea is to reformulate latent aggregation as a regularized least-squares problem and solve it efficiently with a Krylov-based iterative solver inside the denoising loop. This formulation enables denser and more natural mappings than prior training-free methods, yielding more stable generation with far fewer perspective views. As a result, LF-MultiDiffusion reduces the number of image generator evaluations during denoising and significantly improves inference efficiency. Experiments show that LF-MultiDiffusion achieves better visual quality, text alignment, and panoramic consistency than the strongest training-free baseline, while providing a 15.36$\times$ speedup. Our project page is available at: https://ahykw.github.io/lfmd.

</details>


### [32] [Who Drives the Probability Game of VLMs? A Temporal Causal Drive Evaluation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02000)
*Shuyao Xiao,Shengling Wang,Haoyu Niu,Ke Chao,Changwei Xu,Xinran Duan,Chaoyong Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a causal and temporal evaluation framework for Vision-Language Models (VLMs) that traces how different information sources (visual input, question text, and generated prefixes) contribute during autoregressive decoding. The framework uses Structural Causal Models with interventions and backdoor adjustment to derive three metrics: Visual Causal Drive (VCD), Question Causal Drive (QCD), and Prefix Causal Drive (PCD).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional metrics for evaluating VLMs only assess final-answer quality and fail to reveal how different information sources (visual input, question text, generated prefixes) contribute to the generation process. There's a need to understand the causal roles of these different inputs during autoregressive decoding.

Method: The authors propose a causal and temporal evaluation framework grounded in a Structural Causal Model (SCM). They use interventions and backdoor adjustment to derive three step-indexed causal-drive metrics: Visual Causal Drive (VCD), Question Causal Drive (QCD), and Prefix Causal Drive (PCD). These metrics characterize source-specific generation patterns without requiring reference answers. Experiments were conducted on Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct across multiple datasets (MAVIS, LLaVA-Video-178K, MiraData) with cross-validation on InternVL2-8B.

Result: The study reveals a consistent transition from stronger early question and visual guidance toward increasing reliance on generated prefixes during decoding. Randomized-intervention validation shows that QCD and PCD reduce recovery error over observational PMI baselines by 34.8% and 47.1%, respectively. On VLMBias, the prefix-visual imbalance score achieves 0.767 AUROC and 0.873 AUPRC for distinguishing prior-driven from visually grounded generations.

Conclusion: Causal-drive trajectories provide complementary source-level diagnostics for multimodal generation, offering insights into how different information sources contribute at different stages of the generation process.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly evaluated on complex image and video understanding tasks, yet conventional metrics primarily assess final-answer quality and reveal little about how different information sources shape the generation process. We propose a causal and temporal evaluation framework that traces the evolving roles of visual input, question text, and generated prefixes during autoregressive decoding. Grounded in a Structural Causal Model, we use interventions and backdoor adjustment to derive three step-indexed causal-drive metrics---Visual Causal Drive (VCD), Question Causal Drive (QCD), and Prefix Causal Drive (PCD)---for characterizing source-specific generation patterns without requiring reference answers. Experiments on Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct across MAVIS, LLaVA-Video-178K, and MiraData, together with cross-model validation on InternVL2-8B, reveal a consistent transition from stronger early question and visual guidance toward increasing reliance on generated prefixes. Randomized-intervention validation shows that QCD and PCD reduce recovery error over observational PMI baselines by 34.8\% and 47.1\%, respectively. On VLMBias, the prefix--visual imbalance score achieves 0.767 AUROC and 0.873 AUPRC for distinguishing prior-driven from visually grounded generations. These results show that causal-drive trajectories provide complementary source-level diagnostics for multimodal generation.

</details>


### [33] [InsightSeg: Reusing Correction Insights for Guideline-Consistent Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02002)
*Vanshika Vats,Ashwani Rathee,James Davis*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: InsightSeg introduces an episodic memory mechanism for semantic segmentation that converts successful correction episodes into reusable, visually grounded insights. It anchors these insights to image regions using patch-level visual concept vectors and retrieves them for new images to prevent errors before they occur, rather than correcting them after the fact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multi-agent refinement systems for semantic segmentation are stateless - they discard feedback from critiquing agents, causing the same guideline-specific mistakes to be repeatedly rediscovered and corrected across the dataset. This leads to inefficient refinement and redundant error correction.

Method: InsightSeg uses a meta-analyzer to distill successful correction episodes into directive natural-language insights and anchors them to local image regions using patch-level visual concept vectors. On subsequent images, these concepts are matched against dense patch embeddings to retrieve relevant insights, which condition the segmenting agent before making its first prediction.

Result: Across Waymo and Cityscapes datasets, InsightSeg improves both first-pass and final guideline-consistent segmentation performance while requiring fewer refinement steps.

Conclusion: Multi-agent refinement can become more accurate and efficient by drawing on past correction experience. The episodic memory mechanism successfully shifts the system from correcting recurring errors to preventing them, improving segmentation quality before any refinement occurs.

Abstract: Guideline-consistent semantic segmentation requires more than category recognition, as real-world labeling policies demand fine-grained, task-specific decisions. Recent multi-agent refinement systems improve compliance with such textual guidelines by detecting and correcting errors. However, they are stateless: feedback from the critiquing agent is discarded, causing the same guideline-specific mistakes to be repeatedly rediscovered and corrected across the dataset at the cost of additional refinement. We introduce InsightSeg, an episodic memory mechanism that converts successful correction episodes into reusable, visually grounded insights. A meta-analyzer distills each qualifying episode into directive natural-language insights and anchors them to the local image regions that caused the error using patch-level visual concept vectors. On subsequent images, these concepts are matched against dense patch embeddings to retrieve relevant insights, which condition the segmenting agent before making its first prediction. This shifts the system from correcting recurring errors to preventing them, improving segmentation quality before any refinement occurs. Across Waymo and Cityscapes, InsightSeg improves both first-pass and final guideline-consistent segmentation performance while requiring fewer refinement steps, demonstrating that multi-agent refinement can become more accurate and efficient by drawing on past correction experience.

</details>


### [34] [InstEditSeg: Instruction-Driven Image Editing for Polyp and Skin Lesion Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02004)
*Ziquan Liu,Zhewei Zhu,Xuyang Shi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: InstEditSeg is a unified generative framework that reformulates medical segmentation (polyps and skin lesions) as an instruction-driven image editing problem using latent diffusion models, achieving competitive accuracy with better cross-domain generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing polyp and skin lesion segmentation methods struggle with low contrast, ambiguous boundaries, and cross-domain distribution discrepancies. Discriminative networks and diffusion-based approaches predict standalone binary masks without leveraging visual priors from large-scale pretrained generative models.

Method: InstEditSeg uses a latent diffusion model that renders color-coded overlays on original images conditioned on textual instructions. It incorporates DINOv3 as an auxiliary visual encoder with a DINO Feature Guidance Block building a multi-scale feature pyramid fused into the diffusion U-Net via channel concatenation and zero-initialized convolution. A dual-branch classifier-free guidance strategy requires only two forward passes per denoising step.

Result: On polyp and skin lesion benchmarks, InstEditSeg achieves accuracy competitive with strong discriminative baselines. It demonstrates better cross-domain generalization on unseen data, more complete multi-lesion segmentation, instruction-conditioned task control, and sampling flexibility. Limitations include color sensitivity and unsupported attribute-conditioned selection.

Conclusion: The generative formulation for medical segmentation is viable and offers advantages over discriminative approaches, particularly in cross-domain generalization. The paradigm shows promise but has limitations regarding color sensitivity and attribute-conditioned selection that need further investigation.

Abstract: Accurate segmentation of polyps and skin lesions is pivotal for clinical diagnosis, yet existing methods struggle with low contrast, ambiguous boundaries, and cross-domain distribution discrepancies. Discriminative networks and most diffusion-based segmentation approaches predict standalone binary masks, leaving the visual priors of large-scale pretrained generative models largely unexploited. We propose InstEditSeg, a unified generative framework that reformulates medical segmentation as an instruction-driven image editing problem. Instead of emitting a mask, the model renders a color-coded overlay on the original image, conditioned on a textual instruction, so that the edited output aligns with the natural image distribution learned by latent diffusion models and mitigates the domain gap between natural and medical imagery. To recover fine anatomical structures, we introduce DINOv3 as an auxiliary visual encoder and a DINO Feature Guidance Block that builds a multi-scale feature pyramid. The pyramid is fused into the diffusion U-Net by channel concatenation and zero-initialized convolution so that hierarchical discriminative priors can be injected without perturbing the pretrained weights. A dual-branch classifier-free guidance strategy requiring only two forward passes per denoising step reduces inference cost. On polyp and skin lesion benchmarks the framework achieves accuracy competitive with strong discriminative baselines, and it further demonstrates concrete advantages of the generative formulation: notably better cross-domain generalization on unseen data, more complete multi-lesion segmentation, instruction-conditioned task control, and sampling flexibility. We also analyze the strengths and limitations of the paradigm, including its color sensitivity and unsupported attribute-conditioned selection. Code is available at: https://github.com/wincharm001/InstEditSeg.

</details>


### [35] [GeoStore: Finding Small Storefronts in Large Scenes -- A Fine-Grained POI Localization Benchmark with Global-to-Local Asymmetric Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02012)
*Lu Han,Xiting Sun,Hao Wang,Zhiqiang Cao,Ruihuan Du,Ziquan Zeng,Chunlong Lv*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GeoStore is the first benchmark for POI localization - matching close-up storefront photos against wide street-view imagery. The paper shows existing VPR methods fail on this asymmetric task and proposes GLAM, which combines global retrieval with local asymmetric matching, achieving superior performance with lower computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing visual place recognition (VPR) methods assume symmetric, whole-image matching at comparable scales. However, POI localization requires matching close-up queries (where target fills the frame) against wide references (where same POI occupies small, off-center region among similar shops), creating a significant capture-domain gap that existing methods cannot handle.

Method: The paper proposes GLAM (Global-to-Local Asymmetric Matching) with two components: (1) A retrieval-anchoring global descriptor for initial candidate retrieval, and (2) An asymmetric local pathway where each reference is stored as compact pooled region tokens matched against a single query probe through learnable soft late interaction. Inference uses mutual-nearest-neighbor re-ranking with the same tokens.

Result: GLAM outperforms strong global and two-stage baselines on Recall@1/5/10 and mAP metrics. It achieves ~5x smaller re-ranking features and ~two orders of magnitude lower per-pair matching cost compared to prior local re-ranking methods.

Conclusion: The asymmetric, fine-grained nature of POI localization requires specialized methods like GLAM rather than repurposed VPR approaches. The GeoStore benchmark enables systematic evaluation of this practical problem. Code and benchmark will be publicly released.

Abstract: Point-of-interest (POI) localization -- matching a user's close-up storefront photograph against large-scale geo-tagged street-view imagery -- underpins map construction, POI verification, and location-based services. Its closest existing paradigm, visual place recognition (VPR), assumes symmetric, whole-image matching of the same scene at a comparable scale; POI localization instead must match a close-up query, in which the target fills the frame, against wide references in which the same POI occupies only a small, off-center region among visually similar shops, under a substantial capture-domain gap. We introduce GeoStore, to our knowledge the first benchmark dedicated to this asymmetric, fine-grained, open-set formulation, and show that global-descriptor methods tuned for symmetric VPR are systematically limited on it, since a single global vector dilutes the small target. We further propose GLAM (Global-to-Local Asymmetric Matching), which couples a retrieval-anchoring global descriptor with an asymmetric local pathway: each reference is kept as a compact set of pooled region tokens and matched against a single query probe through a learnable soft late interaction; at inference, the same tokens enable a lightweight mutual-nearest-neighbor re-ranking. GLAM surpasses strong global and two-stage baselines on Recall@1/5/10 and mAP, with ~5x smaller re-ranking features and ~two orders of magnitude lower per-pair matching cost than prior local re-ranking. The benchmark and code will be publicly released.

</details>


### [36] [Detecting Object Hallucinations in Large Vision-Language Models via Cross-Modal Attention Drifts and Mask-Based Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02028)
*Xuanbing Wen,Boxu Chen,Le Yang,Jiakai Wang,Zhengyu Zhao,Chenhao Lin,Chao Shen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CADMP is a lightweight framework for detecting object hallucination in large vision-language models (LVLMs) by combining adjacent-layer cross-modal attention drift analysis with prediction sensitivity to visual masking. The method identifies hallucinations by measuring stability of visual grounding across layers and verifying predictions truly depend on visual evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Object hallucination remains a major barrier to reliable deployment of LVLMs. Existing detection methods focus on attention from individual layers without exploring how visual grounding evolves across layers, leaving a gap in understanding the dynamics of cross-modal attention during decoding.

Method: CADMP quantifies distributional changes between consecutive cross-modal attention maps to capture abrupt transitions in visual grounding. It selects the transition with largest drift, locates visually relevant regions, and measures prediction probability changes after masking these regions. A lightweight detector integrates both signals (attention drift and probability variation) to identify hallucinated predictions.

Result: CADMP achieves consistently competitive detection performance across multiple benchmarks and representative open-source LVLMs. Ablation studies confirm the complementary contributions of adjacent-layer drift modeling and mask-based grounding verification.

Conclusion: The proposed CADMP framework successfully combines internal attention dynamics (attention drift) with external grounding verification (mask-based sensitivity) to detect object hallucinations in LVLMs. The two signals provide complementary evidence: attention drift characterizes stability of internal visual grounding, while probability variation verifies whether predictions truly depend on identified visual evidence.

Abstract: Despite recent advances in large vision-language models (LVLMs), object hallucination remains a major barrier to their reliable deployment. Existing detection methods often characterize visual grounding using attention from individual layers, leaving its evolution across layers underexplored. We propose CADMP, a lightweight object hallucination detection framework that combines adjacent-layer cross-modal attention drift with prediction sensitivity to targeted visual masking. During decoding, CADMP quantifies distributional changes between consecutive cross-modal attention maps to capture abrupt transitions in visual grounding. It then selects the transition with the largest drift, locates the corresponding visually relevant regions, and measures the change in prediction probability after masking these regions. These two signals provide complementary evidence: attention drift characterizes the stability of internal visual grounding, while probability variation verifies whether a prediction truly depends on the identified visual evidence. A lightweight detector integrates both signals to identify hallucinated predictions. Experiments on multiple benchmarks and representative open-source LVLMs demonstrate that CADMP achieves consistently competitive detection performance. Ablation studies further confirm the complementary contributions of adjacent-layer drift modeling and mask-based grounding verification.

</details>


### [37] [SelfLift: Accelerating Few-Step Diffusion via Self-Recovering Resolution Transition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02036)
*Tingyan Wen,Chenqian Yan,Xurui Peng,Xiazhang Fang,Shuai Wang,Xueqian Wang,Songwei Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SelfLift is a self-recovering progressive-resolution framework for few-step diffusion models that reduces inference latency by enabling reliable late transitions from low to high resolution. It uses artifact-aware consistency lift (training-free) and on-policy self recovery to achieve 41-44% latency reduction while maintaining quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Few-step diffusion models compress temporal computation, making spatial cost dominant. Progressive-resolution inference reduces cost by early denoising at low resolution, but existing methods suffer from distribution mismatch artifacts in the few-step regime due to limited recovery budget. The goal is to enable later transitions (more efficient) without artifacts.

Method: SelfLift introduces two components: (1) SelfLift-zero - a training-free Artifact-Aware Consistency Lift that uses disagreement between direct latent lifting and pixel-VAE re-encoding as artifact-risk signal and correction direction; (2) SelfLift-rich - On-Policy Self Recovery that transfers dense high-resolution guidance from an internal self-teacher to student-visited states while remaining aligned with altered progressive-resolution dynamics.

Result: SelfLift reduces end-to-end latency by 41.5% (FLUX.2-Klein) and 44.1% (Z-Image-Turbo). Combined with timestep distillation, it achieves 29.61x and 19.21x speedups over 50-step models respectively, while preserving competitive generation quality.

Conclusion: SelfLift establishes a stronger speed-quality frontier for few-step diffusion by enabling reliable late transitions without external super-resolution, extra denoiser evaluations, or sampling-schedule modifications. The self-recovering mechanism effectively handles distribution mismatch artifacts.

Abstract: Few-step diffusion models substantially compress temporal computation, making the spatial cost of each model evaluation an increasingly dominant source of inference latency. Progressive-resolution inference reduces this cost by performing early denoising at low resolution and reserving high-resolution computation for refinement. However, existing methods typically lift intermediate latents directly and rely on subsequent steps to absorb the induced distribution mismatch. In the few-step regime, the limited recovery budget leaves these errors as visible artifacts, constraining how late the transition can occur and, consequently, how efficiently it can be performed. We introduce SelfLift, a self-recovering progressive-resolution framework that derives both transition-repair signals and trajectory-aligned supervision from the generative model itself. SelfLift-zero proposes a training-free Artifact-Aware Consistency Lift, using disagreement between direct latent lifting and pixel-VAE re-encoding as both a localized artifact-risk signal and a model-native correction direction. It enables reliable late transitions without external super-resolution, extra denoiser evaluations, or sampling-schedule modifications. Building on this robust transition, SelfLift-rich performs On-Policy Self Recovery on student-visited states, transferring dense high-resolution guidance from an internal self-teacher while remaining aligned with the altered progressive-resolution dynamics. Across FLUX.2-Klein and Z-Image-Turbo, SelfLift reduces end-to-end latency by 41.5% and 44.1%, respectively. Combined with timestep distillation, it delivers overall speedups of 29.61x and 19.21x over the corresponding 50-step models while preserving competitive generation quality, establishing a stronger speed-quality frontier for few-step diffusion.

</details>


### [38] [Test-Time Logit Prompting for Source-Free Missing Modality Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02039)
*Taixi Chen,Nancy Guo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes Test-Time Logit Prompting (TLP), a source-free test-time adaptation framework that enables VLMs to handle missing-modality inputs efficiently at test time without accessing original training data, achieving up to 8% performance improvement with only hundreds of tunable parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models suffer significant performance degradation when encountering missing-modality inputs in real-world deployment. Existing methods require source training data for modality compensation, which is often unavailable due to privacy, storage, or accessibility constraints (e.g., clinical applications, personalized AI services). This raises the need for source-free test-time adaptation.

Method: TLP optimizes logit prompts using two key mechanisms: (1) uncertainty-aware adjustment to address missing-induced prediction shifts, and (2) modality-complete consistency regularization to preserve semantic consistency. The framework requires only hundreds of tunable parameters and a few test-time optimization steps.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse vision-language benchmarks demonstrate that TLP consistently enhances recognition performance under missing-modality scenarios, achieving up to 8% improvements.

Conclusion: TLP provides an efficient solution for adapting VLMs at test time for visual recognition with missing modalities without requiring access to source training data, offering a practical approach for real-world deployments where original data is inaccessible.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved remarkable performance by leveraging complementary information from large-scale image-text pairs. However, missing-modality inputs are commonly encountered during real-world deployment, often leading to significant performance degradation. Existing methods primarily enhance model robustness by learning modality compensation strategies from source training data. However, their reliance on source training data makes them difficult to apply when original data are unavailable due to privacy, storage, or accessibility constraints, such as clinical applications and personalized AI services. This raises an important yet underexplored question: can VLMs be efficiently adapted at test time for visual recognition with missing modalities without accessing source training data? To this end, we propose Test-Time Logit Prompting (TLP), a lightweight source-free test-time adaptation framework for visual recognition with missing modalities. To address missing-induced prediction shifts, TLP optimizes logit prompts with uncertainty-aware adjustment and modality-complete consistency regularization, adaptively adjusting prediction confidence while preserving semantic consistency. Extensive experiments across diverse vision-language benchmarks demonstrate that TLP consistently enhances recognition performance under missing-modality scenarios, achieving up to 8\% improvements while requiring only hundreds of tunable parameters and a few test-time optimization steps.

</details>


### [39] [LaST-SR: Laplace-Inspired Steady-Transient Complex-Frequency Decomposition for Single Image Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02063)
*Linhao Li,Zhaojie Pan,Langkun Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LaST-SR proposes a complex-frequency decomposition approach for single-image super-resolution, combining global Fourier modeling with local complex-frequency analysis to handle both periodic and aperiodic image features, achieving state-of-the-art PSNR/SSIM on ×2 and ×4 benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fourier operators used for global context modeling in SISR have periodic spectral bases that cannot effectively represent localized aperiodic variations, limiting recovery of irregular structures and fine details.

Method: 1) Derive approximate steady-transient decomposition for 2D feature maps; 2) Design Complex-Frequency Decomposition module with two branches: global full-spectrum Fourier branch for image-wide dependencies and long-range structural consistency, plus window-conditioned local complex-frequency branch for localized aperiodic variations; 3) Develop Steady-Transient Collaborative Aggregation module for cross-branch feature fusion and joint aggregation.

Result: LaST-SR achieves the best PSNR/SSIM among compared methods for ×2 and ×4 SISR on five benchmark datasets.

Conclusion: The proposed complex-frequency decomposition with coupled global-local branches and collaborative aggregation effectively addresses the limitation of periodic Fourier bases in representing localized aperiodic variations, significantly improving super-resolution quality.

Abstract: Single-image super-resolution (SISR) requires global context modeling for structurally consistent reconstruction. Fourier operators are increasingly adopted for global feature modeling. However, their periodic spectral bases constrain the representation of localized aperiodic variations, limiting the recovery of irregular structures and fine details. In dynamical systems, the Laplace neural operator extends Fourier modes to complex frequencies and decomposes the output signal into complementary steady-state and transient responses to jointly model periodic and aperiodic information. We derive, for the first time, an approximate steady-transient decomposition for two-dimensional feature maps, providing an analytical basis for the proposed complex-frequency decomposition. Accordingly, we propose LaST-SR, centered on a Complex-Frequency Decomposition module that couples a global full-spectrum Fourier branch for image-wide dependencies and long-range structural consistency with a window-conditioned local complex-frequency branch for localized, content-dependent aperiodic variations. To fuse the resulting features, we further design a Steady-Transient Collaborative Aggregation module for cross-branch interaction and joint aggregation. Experiments on five benchmarks show that LaST-SR achieves the best PSNR/SSIM among the compared methods for $\times2$ and $\times4$ SISR. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of the proposed architecture and its key modeling mechanisms.

</details>


### [40] [DPA: Decoupling Product-Agnostic Anomaly Representations for Zero-shot Anomaly Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02075)
*Hang Yao,Yansheng Fu,Ming Liu,Zifei Yan,Yanli Ji,Hongzhi Zhang,Wangmeng Zuo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes DPA, a diffusion-based zero-shot anomaly generation framework that transfers real anomalies from source products to target products for industrial anomaly detection. It uses anomaly type filtering, product-irrelevant anomaly embeddings learned via mismatched data pairs, adaptive mask-guided generation, and training-free pixel-level annotation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial anomaly detection requires anomaly samples, but newly deployed products only have normal images. Existing zero-shot methods rely on texture images or text descriptions, producing unrealistic anomalies. The key insight is that similar anomalies recur across different products, enabling anomaly transfer from source to target products.

Method: 1) Anomaly type filtering selects plausible source anomaly types for target product. 2) DPA framework learns product-irrelevant anomaly embeddings through training with mismatched data pairs (normal target + anomaly source), enabling transferable anomaly concept learning. 3) Adaptive mask-guided pipeline uses adaptive masks to control positional and geometric plausibility of generated anomalies. 4) Training-free anomaly labeling module produces pixel-level annotations aligned with generated anomalies.

Result: Extensive experiments on MVTec-AD, VisA, and a dedicated anomaly-transfer benchmark demonstrate that the proposed setting and DPA generate more realistic anomalies and significantly improve downstream anomaly detection performance under both zero-shot and few-shot settings.

Conclusion: The anomaly transfer-based zero-shot generation paradigm is feasible and effective. DPA successfully transfers real anomalies across products, generating realistic anomalies that improve downstream detection performance in both zero-shot and few-shot settings.

Abstract: Industrial anomaly detection benefits from anomaly samples, yet newly deployed products typically provide only normal images, making anomaly samples difficult to collect. Zero-shot anomaly generation offers a promising solution which avoids collection of target-product anomalies. However, existing methods mainly rely on texture images or text descriptions as anomaly sources, which often produce unrealistic anomalies. Observing that similar anomalies can recur across different products, we propose anomaly transfer-based zero-shot generation, which reuses real anomalies from existing source products, making target-product anomalies no longer necessary to generate realistic anomalious samples for unseen target products. Since not every anomaly type suits the target product, an anomaly type filtering mechanism first selects plausible source types. To transfer selected anomaly, we propose DPA, a diffusion-based framework that decouples product-agnostic anomaly representations. Instead of directly extracting anomaly representations, DPA learns product-irrelevant anomaly embeddings through training with the mismatched data pair, enabling transferable anomaly concept learning across products. Furthermore, we design an adaptive mask-guided pipeline that leverages adaptive masks to control the positional and geometric plausibility of generated anomalies during generation. A training-free anomaly labeling module is further introduced to produce pixel-level annotations aligned with generated anomalies. Extensive experiments on MVTec-AD, VisA, and a dedicated anomaly-transfer benchmark demonstrate that the proposed setting and DPA generate more realistic anomalies and significantly improve downstream anomaly detection performance under both zero-shot and few-shot settings. Source code and models will be released.

</details>


### [41] [KSG-Net: Key-Sparse and Global-Context Learning for Maritime 3D Ship Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02077)
*Zhouyuan Huai,Meiqi Wan,Yan Yang,Minshi Chen,Xin Yuan,Wei Wang,Xiao Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: KSG-Net is a novel fully sparse 3D ship detection network for maritime LiDAR perception that addresses two key challenges: weak feature representation for small/sparse vessels and insufficient global structural modeling for large vessels. It uses Key Sparse Multi-scale Aggregation (KSMA) to enhance small vessel features and Global Context Aggregation (GCA) to capture long-range dependencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from critical challenges in maritime 3D ship detection: (1) large-scale vessel variations, (2) sparse point clouds of small vessels, and (3) severe sea-clutter interference. Existing methods either rely on 2D features/dense representations (struggling with accuracy-efficiency tradeoff) or sparse 3D detectors designed for road scenes that generalize poorly to maritime scenarios.

Method: The proposed KSG-Net employs two core modules within a unified fully sparse detection framework: (1) Key Sparse Multi-scale Aggregation (KSMA) module - selects informative key voxels and aggregates cross-scale neighborhood features to enhance representation of small and sparse vessels; (2) Global Context Aggregation (GCA) module - captures long-range geometric dependencies through scene-level context modeling with gated residual interactions to improve large vessel representation.

Result: Extensive experiments on the Thames River vessel dataset and simulated datasets demonstrate that KSG-Net consistently outperforms existing methods in multi-scale vessel detection and exhibits strong robustness in complex maritime environments.

Conclusion: KSG-Net effectively addresses the challenges of maritime 3D ship detection by jointly enhancing local discriminative features and global structural awareness within a fully sparse framework. The method shows superior performance across different vessel scales and robustness in complex maritime environments.

Abstract: Accurate 3D ship detection in maritime environments is critical for autonomous navigation, yet remains challenging due to large-scale vessel variations, sparse point clouds of small vessels, and severe sea-clutter interference. Existing methods, primarily based on 2D features or dense representations, struggle to balance detection accuracy and computational efficiency, while sparse 3D detectors designed for road scenes generalize poorly to maritime scenarios. This paper focuses on two key challenges in maritime LiDAR perception: weak feature representation for small and sparse vessels, and insufficient global structural modeling for large vessels due to the limited receptive field of local sparse convolutions. To address these issues, we propose KSG-Net, a Key-Sparse and Global-Context learning network for maritime 3D ship detection. The core idea is to jointly enhance local discriminative features and global structural awareness within a unified fully sparse detection framework. Specifically, a Key Sparse Multi-scale Aggregation (KSMA) module is designed to enhance the representation of small and sparse vessels by selecting informative key voxels and aggregating cross-scale neighborhood features. Furthermore, a Global Context Aggregation (GCA) module is introduced to capture long-range geometric dependencies through scene-level context modeling with gated residual interactions, thereby improving the representation of large vessels. Extensive experiments on the Thames River vessel dataset and simulated datasets demonstrate that KSG-Net consistently outperforms existing methods in multi-scale vessel detection and exhibits strong robustness in complex maritime environments.

</details>


### [42] [Rendering-in-the-Loop: An Execution-Driven Agent for Interactive Web Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02088)
*Yilong Guo,Hanqi Chen,Zixiao Ye,Guanzhong Wang,Chen Yu,Zeyu Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RILA is an execution-driven agent for front-end web development that uses browser rendering feedback to iteratively optimize generated code, improving both interactive functionality and visual fidelity over existing approaches that only evaluate visual metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work on multimodal LLMs for web development focuses on visual metrics (aesthetics, layout similarity) while neglecting interactive functionality validation, which is more critical for real-world applications.

Method: RILA introduces: (1) Action Interaction Verification (AIV) module that replays reference interaction trajectories on generated webpages to collect execution-aware observations; (2) Execution-aware Rendering Score (ERS) that jointly measures interaction correctness and visual fidelity; (3) Execution-verified data synthesis pipeline for diverse, high-quality training data.

Result: On IWR-Bench, RILA consistently improves both interaction and visual fidelity across foundation models. The compact Qwen3.5-9B backbone improved from 40.40% to 57.52%, surpassing larger one-shot generators including Kimi-K2.6 (55.61%) and GPT-5.5 (55.74%).

Conclusion: Putting browser rendering in the loop with execution-driven feedback enables significant improvements in generated web pages' interactive functionality, with training data synthesis providing complementary gains to inference-time optimization.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models have achieved remarkable progress in front-end web development, generating interactive webpages from multimodal references such as screenshots and interaction videos. However, existing work largely emphasizes visual metrics such as aesthetics and layout similarity, while overlooking the more critical validation of interactive functionality. We present RILA, an execution-driven agent that puts browser rendering in the loop, iteratively editing generated code from runtime interaction feedback. RILA introduces an Action Interaction Verification (AIV) module that replays the reference interaction trajectory on the generated webpage to collect grounded execution-aware observations, and an Execution-aware Rendering Score (ERS) that jointly measures interaction correctness and visual fidelity to guide iterative optimization. We further build an execution-verified data synthesis pipeline that produces diverse, high-quality training data, offering gains complementary to inference-time optimization. On IWR-Bench, RILA consistently improves both interaction and visual fidelity across foundation models. Notably, with our training pipeline, RILA lifts the compact Qwen3.5-9B backbone from 40.40% to 57.52%, surpassing far larger one-shot generators, including the 1T-parameter Kimi-K2.6 (55.61%) and the proprietary GPT-5.5 (55.74%).

</details>


### [43] [Evidence-Guided Detection, Localization and Explanation for Text-Centric Image Forensics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02097)
*Peifeng Liu,Bin Li,Qingsong Zhang,Yangxin Yu,Leqing Chen,Xiaoye Qiu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A system for detecting and explaining text-centric AI-generated image manipulations, combining a detector, localizer, and MLLM-based reasoner to provide both authenticity detection and evidence-based forensic reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid progress of AIGC has made text-centric image manipulation increasingly accessible, creating new forensic challenges that require not only authenticity detection but also spatial grounding and evidence-based explanation.

Method: Proposed an evidence-guided detector-localizer-reasoner system with cascaded evidence flow: (1) image-level detector provides global authenticity prior; (2) dedicated localizer extracts tampered regions as spatial grounding evidence; (3) MLLM-based reasoner generates structured forensic reports. Key techniques include iterative difficulty-aware mining for improved localization and report-mask consistency post-processing.

Result: Achieved a final score of 0.638 and ranked second in the GenText-Forensics Challenge at ACM Multimedia 2026 on the official hidden test set.

Conclusion: The proposed evidence-guided system validates the effectiveness of combining detector, localizer, and MLLM reasoner for comprehensive image forensics with spatial grounding and evidence-based explanations.

Abstract: The rapid progress of AIGC has made text-centric image manipulation increasingly accessible, creating new forensic challenges that require not only authenticity detection but also spatial grounding and evidence-based explanation. This paper presents our solution to the GenText-Forensics Challenge at ACM Multimedia 2026. We propose an evidence-guided detector-localizer-reasoner system, where an image-level detector provides a global authenticity prior, a dedicated localizer extracts tampered regions as spatial grounding evidence, and an MLLM-based reasoner generates structured forensic reports grounded in this expert forensic evidence. These modules are connected through a cascaded evidence flow: the detector gates the subsequent localization and prompting process, the localizer converts tamper responses into grounding boxes, and the reasoner is trained to synthesize the detector decision and localized evidence into the final report. As a key part of our method, we introduce iterative difficulty-aware mining to improve localization quality and apply report-mask consistency post-processing to align report grounding with predicted masks. On the official hidden test set, our system achieves a final score of 0.638 and ranks second in the challenge, validating the effectiveness of the proposed evidence-guided system. The code is available at https://github.com/peifengLiu42/ACMMM26-evidence-guided-detector-localizer-reasoner-system.

</details>


### [44] [Disease Burden over Skin Tone: Decomposing the Dermatology-AI Generalization Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02111)
*Nirajan Kunwor,Sanjaya Poudel,Quoc-Huy Trinh,Jahidul Arafat,Sunil Kumar Gaire*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Disease-distribution shift, not skin-tone underrepresentation, is the primary cause of poor generalization in dermatology AI models deployed in resource-constrained settings. Dermatology foundation models with lightweight adaptation (∼10 labeled examples per category) can recover most performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dermatology AI models are trained on light-skinned, cancer-focused datasets but deployed in diverse settings where patients differ in both skin tone and disease distribution. This study investigates which factor primarily causes poor generalization.

Method: Evaluated a cancer-trained baseline (ResNet-50 on HAM10000/ISIC 2019), two dermatology foundation models (DermLIP, MONET), and a general vision model (DINOv3) as frozen feature extractors. Tested on tone-stratified disease-matched dataset (DDI) and disease-shifted tone-diverse dataset (SCIN). Used label-free representation analysis (kNN purity) to assess representational quality.

Result: Disease-distribution shift contributes more to performance decline than skin tone. Cancer baseline balanced accuracy drops from 0.62 to 0.21 when transferred to unfamiliar conditions. Within-disease skin-tone gap is smaller (0.10-0.18) and inconsistent. Cancer-specialized features poorly cluster unfamiliar conditions (kNN purity lift +0.06), while dermatology-pretrained features retain stronger structure (+0.23). Starting from dermatology foundation models, ~10 labeled examples per clinical category recover most attainable performance.

Conclusion: Disease distribution shift is the dominant challenge for dermatology AI generalization. Dermatology foundation models with lightweight adaptation (∼10 examples per category) can effectively recover performance, making them promising for deployment in resource-constrained settings. The evaluation protocol and code are released for reproducible auditing.

Abstract: Dermatology artificial intelligence (AI) models are predominantly trained on light-skinned, cancer-focused image collections, yet they are increasingly proposed for deployment in resource-constrained settings where patients differ from training populations along two confounded axes: skin tone and disease distribution. We investigate whether poor generalization is primarily caused by skin-tone underrepresentation or disease-distribution shift. We evaluate a cancer-trained baseline (ResNet-50 fine-tuned on HAM10000 and ISIC 2019), two dermatology foundation models (DermLIP and MONET), and a general-purpose vision model (DINOv3) as frozen feature extractors. Models are evaluated on a tone-stratified disease-matched dataset (Diverse Dermatology Images, DDI) and a disease-shifted tone-diverse dataset (Skin Condition Image Network, SCIN). Our results show that disease-distribution shift contributes more than skin tone in the evaluated settings. The cancer baseline decreases from 0.62 to 0.21 balanced accuracy when transferred to unfamiliar clinical conditions, while the within-disease skin-tone gap is smaller (0.10-0.18) and inconsistent. Label-free representation analysis shows that this failure reflects a representational limitation rather than only missing output labels: cancer-specialized features poorly cluster unfamiliar conditions (kNN purity lift +0.06 over chance), whereas dermatology-pretrained features retain stronger transferable structure (+0.23). Finally, we show that representation quality predicts recoverable performance under lightweight adaptation. Starting from dermatology foundation models, approximately ten labeled examples per clinical category recover most attainable performance. We release the evaluation protocol and code to support reproducible auditing of dermatology AI generalization.

</details>


### [45] [Synergistic Information Disentanglement for Omni-modal Slide Representation Learning in Computational Pathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02118)
*Mingxin Liu,Chengfei Cai,Anwen Lu,Pengbo Xu,Jun Li,Jinze Li,Depin Chen,Jun Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Φ-Omni is a novel synergistic information disentanglement framework for omni-modal self-supervised learning in computational pathology. It addresses modality collapse in multi-modal learning (histology, genomics, clinical reports) by using Partial Information Decomposition (PID) theory to maximize irreducible synergy while suppressing marginal redundancy, enabling better transfer learning for whole slide image representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SSL approaches force heterogeneous modalities into a uniform latent space through contrastive alignment, causing modality collapse where unique diagnostic signals (Φ) are discarded in favor of trivial redundancy. The authors hypothesize that the strongest task-agnostic SSL training signal stems from distilling synergistic interactions over merely aligning shared redundancy.

Method: Φ-Omni employs a Synergistic Information Bottleneck (SIB) regulated by the proposed ΦID objective, which explicitly suppresses marginal redundancy while maximizing irreducible synergy. This approach distills high-order cross-modal interactions. The model was pretrained on breast (n=1031) and lung (n=919) cohorts.

Result: Φ-Omni demonstrates superior few-shot performance across five independent external datasets spanning eight tasks compared to supervised and SSL baselines.

Conclusion: The framework successfully proves that maximizing synergistic information from multi-modal data leads to better task-agnostic representations than standard contrastive alignment approaches, with strong generalization across diverse external datasets.

Abstract: In computational pathology (CPath), developing omni-modal self-supervised learning (SSL) models that integrate histology, genomics, and clinical reports enables transferable representation learning for whole slide images (WSIs). Existing approaches implicitly force heterogeneous modalities into a uniform latent space by contrastive alignment, causing modality collapse where unique, synergistic diagnostic signals (termed as $\mathrmΦ$) are discarded in favor of trivial redundancy. We hypothesize that the strongest task-agnostic SSL training signal stems from distilling the synergistic interactions over merely aligning shared redundancy. To this end, we introduce \textsc{$\mathrmΦ$-Omni}, a synergistic information disentanglement framework grounded in Partial Information Decomposition (PID) theory for slide representation learning. Unlike standard contrastive approaches, \textsc{$\mathrmΦ$-Omni} employs a Synergistic Information Bottleneck (SIB) regulated by the proposed $\mathrmΦ\text{ID}$ objective, which explicitly suppresses marginal redundancy while maximizing irreducible synergy, thereby distilling high-order cross-modal interactions. Following pretraining on breast ($n$=1031) and lung ($n$=919) cohorts, \textsc{$\mathrmΦ$-Omni} demonstrates superior few-shot performance across five independent external datasets spanning eight tasks compared to supervised and SSL baselines. Source code is available here.

</details>


### [46] [World-Coherent Decoding: Self-Verifying Test-Time Planning for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02159)
*Chuhan Zhang,Seiji Ito,Kenta Hoshino,Satoshi Ikehata,Ikuro Sato*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: World-Coherent-Decoding (WCD) is a test-time planning framework for World Action Models (WAMs) that improves action selection by ranking sampled futures using visual plausibility and action stability metrics, then using execution feedback to train an online predictor—all without updating the backbone model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: WAMs stochastically generate visual futures and decode actions, but results strongly depend on which future is selected. Current methods lack a reliable way to select among multiple candidate futures before execution.

Method: WCD samples multiple candidates from a frozen WAM and ranks them using two internal generative signals: (1) flow-based video surprisal for visual plausibility, and (2) action path effort for action-generation stability. After execution, realized observations audit selected imaginations, and the mismatch trains a lightweight online predictor for future candidate selection.

Result: On RoboTwin 2.0, WCD improves Hard success from 55.80% to 60.90% (+5.1%), with +16.43 gains on Horizon-3 tasks, and shows qualitative robustness on real Franka visual-shift tests.

Conclusion: Test-time scaling for WAMs depends less on sampling more futures than on selecting reliable ones. The key insight is converting delayed self-verification into pre-execution reliability estimation without updating the backbone model.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) aim to control robots by stochastically generating visual futures and then decoding actions, but empirical observations indicate that the results can strongly depend on which future is selected. We propose World-Coherent-Decoding (WCD), a self-verifying test-time planning framework that treats WAM rollouts as falsifiable future--action hypotheses. At each decision step, WCD samples multiple candidates from a frozen WAM and ranks them using internal generative signals: flow-based video surprisal for visual plausibility and action path effort for action-generation stability. After execution, the realized observation audits the selected imagination, yielding an imagination--reality mismatch that trains a lightweight online predictor for future candidate selection. Thus, WCD converts delayed self-verification into pre-execution reliability estimation without updating the backbone model.
