On RoboTwin 2.0, WCD improves Hard success under limited randomized-scene supervision from $55.80\%$ to $60.90\%$, with a $+16.43$ gains on Horizon-3 tasks, and shows qualitative robustness on real Franka visual-shift tests. These results highlight a simple principle: test-time scaling for WAMs depends less on sampling more futures than on selecting reliable ones.

</details>


### [47] [Progressive Pseudo-Label Optimization for Point-Supervised Change Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02171)
*Hailong Ning,Hao Wang,Yimeng Wang,Tao Lei,Renwei Dian,Asoke K. Nandi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A two-stage framework that leverages SAM2 priors for point-supervised change detection (PS-CD), incorporating bi-temporal mask selection and a teacher-student self-training mechanism to generate high-quality pseudo-labels from sparse point annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Point-supervised change detection reduces labeling costs but produces incomplete and noisy pseudo-labels due to limited spatial coverage of point annotations.

Method: Stage I: SAM2 generates object-aware candidate masks from point annotations on bi-temporal images, with a bi-temporal mask selection strategy to produce reliable change pseudo-labels, followed by a lightweight CNN refinement module with uncertainty-aware loss. Stage II: Teacher-student self-training framework with exponential moving average teacher that periodically refreshes pseudo-labels, creating a closed-loop optimization process.

Result: Outperforms previous weakly supervised approaches on most benchmarks (WHU-CD, LEVIR-CD, SYSU-CD) and remains competitive with several fully supervised methods.

Conclusion: The proposed two-stage framework effectively addresses the limitations of point supervision in change detection by leveraging SAM2 priors and self-training, achieving strong performance with minimal annotation effort.

Abstract: Point-supervised change detection (PS-CD) aims to identify pixel-level changes between bi-temporal images using only sparsely annotated points. Although point annotations substantially reduce labeling costs, their limited spatial coverage often results in incomplete and noisy pseudo-labels. To address this issue, we propose a two-stage framework that introduces SAM2 priors into PS-CD and progressively adapts them to the target task. In Stage I, SAM2 generates object-aware candidate masks from point annotations on the bi-temporal images, and a bi-temporal mask selection strategy is designed to convert generic segmentation responses into more reliable change pseudo-labels. Subsequently, a lightweight CNN refinement module with an uncertainty-aware loss is employed to improve boundary quality and local structural consistency. In Stage II, we construct a teacher-student self-training framework in which the teacher is updated by exponential moving average and periodically refreshes the pseudo-labels. This design establishes a closed-loop optimization process that alternates between pseudo-label refinement and model re-optimization. Experiments on three benchmark datasets, including WHU-CD, LEVIR-CD, and SYSU-CD, demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms previous weakly supervised approaches on most benchmarks and remains competitive with several fully supervised methods.

</details>


### [48] [CC-4DGS: Computational Deformation and Point-Cloud Compression for Storage-Efficient Dynamic Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02184)
*Kyungdae Park,Chae Eun Rhee*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CC-4DGS is a storage-efficient framework for dynamic 4D Gaussian Splatting that reduces total storage to 20-30 MB per scene while maintaining real-time rendering and comparable reconstruction accuracy to state-of-the-art methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing dynamic 4D Gaussian Splatting methods require tens to hundreds of megabytes per scene due to heavy reliance on large multi-resolution hash tables and high-dimensional Gaussian attributes, limiting scalability and practical deployment.

Method: The paper proposes two components: (1) Computational Deformation Field (CDF) using deterministic dense hash encoding and compact neural decoders instead of large hash tables; (2) Compression of Canonical Point-cloud Attributes (CCA) pipeline using conditional autoencoding, selective quantization, and residual codebooks to compress spherical harmonic appearance terms and auxiliary Gaussian attributes.

Result: Deformation storage reduced to 1-3 MB per scene; 3-5× point-cloud reduction with negligible quality loss; total storage of 20-30 MB; preserves real-time rendering performance; achieves reconstruction accuracy comparable to Swift4D.

Conclusion: CC-4DGS successfully balances storage efficiency, reconstruction quality, and runtime performance, offering a scalable solution for dynamic 4D scene representation with favorable trade-offs compared to existing methods.

Abstract: Dynamic four-dimensional (4D) Gaussian Splatting has emerged as a powerful explicit representation for high-quality view synthesis, yet existing methods still require tens to hundreds of megabytes per scene due to their heavy reliance on large multi-resolution hash tables and high-dimensional Gaussian attributes. This paper presents CC-4DGS, a storage-efficient and scalable framework that rethinks both deformation modeling and canonical attribute storage. First, we introduce a computational deformation field (CDF) that replaces large multi-resolution learnable hash tables with deterministic dense hash encoding and compact neural decoders, enabling on-the-fly synthesis of deformation features while reducing deformation storage to only 1--3 MB per scene. Second, we propose a compression of canonical point-cloud attributes (CCA) pipeline that compresses high-dimensional spherical harmonic appearance terms and auxiliary Gaussian attributes via conditional autoencoding, selective quantization, and residual codebooks, achieving 3--5$\times$ point-cloud reduction with negligible quality loss. Together, these components yield a unified representation that preserves real-time rendering performance while reducing total storage to 20--30 MB. Extensive experiments across the N3DV and Technicolor Light Field datasets demonstrate that CC-4DGS achieves reconstruction accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art methods such as Swift4D, while offering significantly improved storage efficiency and favorable runtime-memory trade-offs.

</details>


### [49] [Lightweight Adaptation of General-Purpose VLMs for Multispectral and SAR Image Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02187)
*Shanji Liu,Kelu Yao,Junxiao Xue,Chenghui Lv,Xiangyang Miao,Yekai Huang,Yaying Chen,Chao Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes a lightweight method to adapt general-purpose vision-language models (VLMs) for multispectral and SAR (synthetic aperture radar) remote sensing tasks without training new foundation models. The approach renders observations as multiple optical/SAR views and uses LoRA adaptation on the language network and visual transformer blocks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose VLMs are built for three-channel natural images and cannot directly accommodate multispectral or SAR observations. Existing approaches require dedicated encoders and domain pretraining, which slows the reuse of stronger general-purpose checkpoints. The authors aim to find a lightweight alternative.

Method: The method uses the multi-image interface of VLMs: (1) render each observation as five optical views and one SAR view, named in prompts; (2) adapt the language network and selected visual transformer blocks with LoRA; (3) for land-cover recognition, use structured supervision coupling predicted classes with sensor evidence; (4) construct preference pairs where true labels are omitted while supporting evidence is retained, encouraging complete predictions consistent with observations.

Result: On a balanced six-class land-cover benchmark from BigEarthNet-v2, adapted Qwen3-VL achieves 0.8275 micro F1. The same input and adaptation protocol improves all four tested VLM architectures and transfers to Sen1Floods11 flood verification and BigEarthNet.txt captioning. Image removal and mismatch controls confirm the adapted models use the supplied sensor observations.

Conclusion: VLMs can be repurposed for multispectral and SAR tasks through rendered inputs and compact LoRA adaptation, eliminating the need to train new foundation models. This provides a lightweight,通用 solution for applying powerful VLMs to remote sensing domains.

Abstract: General-purpose vision-language models (VLMs) now support strong visual recognition, instruction following, and generation. However, most pretrained visual encoders are built around three-channel natural images and do not directly accommodate observations such as native multispectral measurements or synthetic aperture radar (SAR). Adapting VLMs to these sensors typically requires dedicated encoders and domain pretraining, slowing the reuse of stronger general-purpose checkpoints. We show that the multi-image interface of general-purpose VLMs offers a lightweight alternative. Our protocol renders each observation as five optical views and one SAR view, names them in the prompt, and adapts the language network and selected visual transformer blocks with LoRA. This exposes band composites, spectral indices, and radar backscatter through an existing visual interface. For land-cover recognition, structured supervision couples predicted classes with sensor evidence. We further construct preference pairs in which a true label is omitted while its supporting evidence is retained, encouraging complete predictions that remain consistent with the observations. On a balanced six-class land-cover benchmark derived from BigEarthNet-v2, the adapted Qwen3-VL reaches 0.8275 micro F1. The same input and adaptation protocol improves all four tested VLM architectures and transfers to Sen1Floods11 flood verification and BigEarthNet.txt captioning. Image removal and mismatch controls show that the adapted models use the supplied sensor observations. Together, these results demonstrate that VLMs can be repurposed for multispectral and SAR tasks through rendered inputs and compact LoRA adaptation, without training a new foundation model.

</details>


### [50] [TAME: Temporal-Aware Mixture-of-Experts for Text-Video Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02204)
*Uicheol Jung,Juyoung Hong,Hojung Kwon,Yukyung Choi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TAME addresses temporal modeling limitations in CLIP-based text-video retrieval by introducing: 1) sparse MoE layers with frame-consistent routing for vision-language alignment, 2) Frame-Temporal tokens for long-range temporal dependencies, and 3) Cross-Temporal Interaction and Aggregation module for refining frame-wise similarities. Achieves 4.0 R@1 improvement over CLIP4Clip on MSR-VTT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extending image-text models like CLIP to videos is fundamentally limited by lack of temporal modeling. Videos have frame-wise heterogeneity in appearance and motion, and compressing all frames into a single representation obscures temporal structure and semantic transitions.

Method: Three key technical contributions: 1) Integrate sparse MoE layers into both CLIP encoders with frame-consistent routing on vision branch for expert specialization while preserving vision-language alignment; 2) Introduce Frame-Temporal (FT) tokens that aggregate global cross-frame information and feed back to each frame for long-range temporal dependencies without harming local details; 3) Design Cross-Temporal Interaction and Aggregation (CTIA) module that refines frame-wise sentence-video similarities through staged temporal filtering and fusion.

Result: On MSR-VTT, TAME improves R@1 by 4.0 over CLIP4Clip. Also achieves consistent gains on DiDeMo, MSVD, LSMDC, and ActivityNet benchmarks.

Conclusion: TAME provides an effective solution for temporal-aware text-video retrieval by jointly modeling frame-level structure and temporal relations through MoE layers, FT tokens, and CTIA module, showing consistent improvements across multiple standard benchmarks.

Abstract: Text-Video Retrieval (TVR) retrieves videos that match a natural-language query, but extending image-text models such as CLIP to videos is fundamentally limited by the lack of temporal modeling. Videos exhibit frame-wise heterogeneity in appearance and motion, and compressing all frames into a single representation often obscures temporal structure and semantic transitions. To address this, we propose Temporal-Aware Mixture-of-Experts for Text-Video Retrieval (TAME), a CLIP-based framework that jointly models frame-level structure and temporal relations. First, we integrate sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) layers into both CLIP encoders and apply frame-consistent routing on the vision branch so that experts specialize according to frame-level visual patterns while preserving the original vision-language alignment. Second, we introduce Frame-Temporal (FT) tokens that aggregate global cross-frame information and feed it back to each frame, enabling the visual encoder to capture long-range temporal dependencies without harming local details. Third, we design a Cross-Temporal Interaction and Aggregation (CTIA) module that refines frame-wise sentence-video similarities through staged temporal filtering and fusion. Experiments on standard TVR benchmarks show that TAME consistently improves over CLIP-based baselines. On MSR-VTT, it improves R@1 by 4.0 over CLIP4Clip, and also achieves consistent gains on DiDeMo, MSVD, LSMDC, and ActivityNet. The code is available at https://github.com/sejong-rcv/TAME.

</details>


### [51] [LeakageBench: Document-Level Leakage Risk for Redacting Personally Identifiable Information in Document Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02207)
*Vishnu Prasad Vijaya Kumar,Santhosh Venkatesh,Ivan P. Yamshchikov*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LeakageBench is a benchmark for evaluating PII redaction in document images, addressing the gap between text-centric benchmarks and real-world document-level redaction risks. It contains 500 document images with 11,954 GDPR-aligned PII annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PII benchmarks are text-centric and fail to measure document-level redaction risk—a page remains unsafe if even one identifier is missed. Real-world document images have OCR errors, layout structure, and visual noise that affect redaction effectiveness.

Method: The authors created LeakageBench with 500 document images and 11,954 GDPR-aligned PII annotations spanning direct identifiers, linkage keys, and contextual re-identification surfaces. They evaluated multiple approaches: generic OCR pipelines, commercial and task-adapted OCR-dependent detectors, and OCR-free vision-language models using entity-level F1, group-wise leakage, and document-level leakage metrics.

Result: Code Interpreter raises GPT-5.5 localization F1 from 0.090 to 0.249, but critical page-level leakage remains at 0.968. Stronger detection and tool assistance improve localization but do not make most pages safe for release.

Conclusion: LeakageBench provides a diagnostic benchmark for high-recall, spatially grounded PII redaction in document images. Current approaches still have significant document-level leakage, indicating that entity-level metrics alone are insufficient for ensuring document safety.

Abstract: Real-world personally identifiable information (PII) redaction often operates on document images---scans, screenshots, and PDF renderings---where OCR errors, layout structure, and visual noise determine whether sensitive information is actually removed. Existing PII benchmarks are mostly text-centric and do not measure document-level redaction risk: a page remains unsafe if even one identifier is missed. We introduce LeakageBench, a challenge set of 500 document images with 11,954 GDPR-aligned PII annotations spanning direct identifiers, linkage keys, and contextual re-identification surfaces. We evaluate generic OCR pipelines, commercial and task-adapted OCR-dependent detectors, and OCR-free vision-language models using entity-level F1, group-wise leakage, and document-level leakage metrics. Code Interpreter raises GPT-5.5 localization F1 from 0.090 to 0.249, but critical page-level leakage remains 0.968. These results show that stronger detection and tool assistance improve localization without making most pages safe for release. LeakageBench provides a diagnostic benchmark for high-recall, spatially grounded PII redaction in document images.

</details>


### [52] [Asymmetric Paired-Annotation Learning for Multi-Structure ULF Pediatric Brain MRI Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02210)
*Ha-Hieu Pham,Dang P. M. Cao,Minh Hoang Pham,Khanh Nguyen Vo Ngoc,Thanh-Huy Nguyen,Ulas Bagci,Huy-Hieu Pham*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AURA is an nnU-Net-based asymmetric supervision strategy for pediatric brain segmentation in portable ultra-low-field MRI (0.064 T). It handles the challenge of two non-equivalent annotations (high-field-derived and low-field-edited masks) by treating them as distinct observations rather than interchangeable ground truths, using a bounded reliability gate based on multiple factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Portable ULF MRI can expand access to pediatric neuroimaging, but segmentation at 0.064 T is challenging due to weakly delineated anatomical boundaries, partially visible small structures, and local misregistration from high-field references. The LISA 2026 Challenge provides two non-equivalent annotations: highfield-derived (HF) masks as scored targets and low-field-edited (LF) masks aligned with visible ULF anatomy.

Method: AURA uses an nnU-Net-based asymmetric supervision strategy. It anchors training to the HF mask and incorporates the LF mask through a bounded reliability gate based on: (1) label disagreement, (2) boundaries, (3) predictive uncertainty, (4) class reliability, and (5) training stage.

Result: On a 16-case development split: HF-supervised baseline achieved Dice 0.7984; AURA achieved Dice 0.7950; ensemble achieved Dice 0.7988, HD95 of 1.8892, and ASSD of 0.7855.

Conclusion: AURA provides a preliminary evaluation within the LISA 2026 Challenge. Results motivate further assessment on the hidden test set and external ULF cohorts.

Abstract: Portable ultra-low-field (ULF) MRI can expand access to pediatric neuroimaging, but segmentation at 0.064 T remains challenging because anatomical boundaries are weakly delineated, small structures may be only partially visible, and high-field references can be locally misregistered. The LISA 2026 Challenge provides two non-equivalent annotations reflecting different sources of anatomical evidence: a highfield-derived (HF) mask defining the scored target and a low-field-edited (LF) mask aligned with visible ULF anatomy. In this challenge report, we describe AURA, an nnU-Net-based asymmetric supervision strategy that treats these annotations as distinct observations rather than interchangeable ground truths. AURA anchors training to the HF mask and incorporates the LF mask through a bounded reliability gate based on label disagreement, boundaries, predictive uncertainty, class reliability, and training stage. On a 16-case development split, the HF-supervised baseline, AURA, and their ensemble achieved Dice scores of 0.7984, 0.7950, and 0.7988, respectively, while the ensemble achieved an HD95 of 1.8892 and an ASSD of 0.7855. These results provide a preliminary evaluation of AURA within the LISA 2026 Challenge and motivate further assessment on the hidden test set and external ULF cohorts. Our code and pretrained models are available at https://github.com/minhdang050806/ A-nnU-Net-based-asymmetric-supervision-strategy.

</details>


### [53] [FuDU: A Fuzzy Dual-dimensional Uncertainty Framework for Streaming Active Learning in Industrial Defect Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02212)
*Zhaoyang Wang,Haiyong Chen,Binyi Su,Xinwei Lyu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes FuDU, a streaming active learning framework for real-time industrial defect detection. It quantifies uncertainty at both image-level (via prototype-based feature comparison) and box-level (via dual-entropy evaluation), then uses fuzzy inference to fuse these uncertainties for adaptive sampling of uncertain samples, enabling expert knowledge-driven label acquisition to improve model reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ensuring reliability of deep learning models in real-time industrial defect detection is critical for high-stakes quality inspection. The key challenge is to effectively mine uncertain samples within continuous industrial media streams to enhance detection system reliability.

Method: The method has three main components: (1) PGUQ (Prototype-based Global Uncertainty Quantification) module on the backbone to evaluate image-level uncertainty via normal/defective feature prototypes; (2) DeUE (Dual-entropy defect Uncertainty Evaluator) integrated into the detection head to quantify box-level uncertainty; (3) Fuzzy dual-dimensional uncertainty-aware strategy that models uncertainty as systematic error and uses fuzzy inference to fuse dual-dimensional uncertainties for expert knowledge-driven adaptive sampling decisions.

Result: Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that FuDU is efficient and flexible, making it well-suited for challenging industrial inspection tasks such as nuclear fuel rod defect detection.

Conclusion: The FuDU framework effectively enables reliable deep learning for real-time industrial defect detection through streaming active learning, with the fuzzy fusion of dual-dimensional uncertainties providing an effective mechanism for adaptive sampling in continuous industrial media streams.

Abstract: Ensuring the reliability of deep learning models in real-time industrial defect detection is critical for high-stakes quality inspection. To mine uncertain samples within continuous industrial media streams, thereby enhancing the reliability of the detection system, this paper proposes a streaming active learning method based on the Fuzzy Dual-dimensional Uncertainty (FuDU) framework. Specifically, we first design a Prototype-based Global Uncertainty Quantification (PGUQ) module on the backbone to evaluate image-level uncertainty via normal/defective feature prototypes. A Dual-entropy defect Uncertainty Evaluator (DeUE) is then integrated into the detection head to quantify box-level uncertainty. Finally, by modeling uncertainty as systematic error, we propose a fuzzy dual-dimensional uncertainty-aware strategy that leverages fuzzy inference to fuse dual-dimensional uncertainties, enabling expert knowledge-driven adaptive sampling decisions. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that FuDU is efficient and flexible, making it well-suited for challenging industrial inspection tasks such as the detection of nuclear fuel rod defects. Our code is publicly available at: https://github.com/wangzhaoyang-508/FuDU.

</details>


### [54] [Signal or Noise? Auditing Rotation-Induced Saliency Drift in Medical and Aerial Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02224)
*Khawaja Murad ul Hassan,Mehran Ebrahimi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper addresses rotation instability in Grad-CAM saliency maps, which drift even when predictions remain unchanged. The authors discover that channel weights are rotation-stable (due to GAP+linear head making gradient field spatially constant), while the spatial activation tensor moves. They propose EquiGrad-CAM, a training-free wrapper that takes multiple rotated views, inverse-rotates saliencies to a common frame, and averages. This achieves significant equivariance improvements (+36% to +247% across models) without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In domains with no canonical orientation (histopathology, aerial imagery), saliency maps drift when input is rotated despite unchanged predictions, undermining their use as evidence. The authors investigate whether this drift is faithful signal or noise introduced by the CAM operator.

Method: 1) Measure equivariance at every stage of the CAM operator (channel weights vs spatial activation tensor)
2) Identify that channel weights are exactly stable on ResNet-50 due to GAP+linear head making class gradient field spatially constant
3) Discover that movement comes from spatial activation tensor, which is discarded by classifier's pooling
4) Propose EquiGrad-CAM: training-free wrapper taking T rotated views, inverse-rotating each saliency to canonical frame, and averaging
5) PEUM by-product ranks explanations by reproducibility

Result: - On ImageNet-1K validation: +36.0% equivariance (ResNet-50), +87.5% (VGG-16), +247% (ViT-B/16)
- Ablation confirms alignment before averaging, not aggregation locus, is the driver
- Outperforms rotation-augmented training without retraining
- Improves zero-shot CLIP by +145%
- Works on PatchCamelyon and RESISC45 datasets
- Causal test confirms: occluding drifting pixels costs less than random pixels

Conclusion: The drift is carried by degrees of freedom the classifier discards (spatial activation tensor), making removal faithful rather than destructive. EquiGrad-CAM is a simple, training-free solution that achieves rotation-consistent explanations across multiple architectures and datasets. The by-product PEUM provides reproducibility-based ranking at no additional cost.

Abstract: Post-hoc saliency maps such as Grad-CAM are increasingly used to audit why a deployed vision model made a decision, yet the heatmap drifts when the input is rotated, even when the prediction is unchanged. In domains with no canonical orientation, such as histopathology and aerial imagery, this undermines using saliency as evidence. We ask whether that drift is faithful signal or noise introduced by the CAM operator, and answer it by measuring equivariance at every stage of the operator rather than inferring it from the network's output. The instability is not where one would guess: the channel weights are the most rotation-stable stage, and on ResNet-50 exactly stable, because a GAP+linear head makes the class gradient field spatially constant. What moves is the spatial activation tensor, and the classifier's own pooling discards that movement. A causal test confirms the consequence: occluding the pixels whose saliency drifts costs the model less than occluding random pixels, at either orientation. The drift is carried by degrees of freedom the classifier throws away, which is what makes removing it faithful rather than destructive. EquiGrad-CAM is a training-free wrapper that takes T rotated views, inverse-rotates each view's saliency into a common canonical frame, and averages. On the full ImageNet-1K validation set it raises equivariance over single-view Grad-CAM by +36.0% (ResNet-50), +87.5% (VGG-16) and +247% (ViT-B/16); a scale-matched ablation isolates alignment before averaging, not the locus of aggregation, as the driver. It beats rotation-augmented training without retraining, lifts zero-shot CLIP by +145%, and yields rotation-consistent explanations on PatchCamelyon and RESISC45. Its by-product PEUM ranks explanations by how reproducible they are, at no cost beyond the views already taken. Code: https://github.com/Khawaja-Murad/EquiGrad-CAM

</details>


### [55] [InfraPatch: Cross-Task Targeted Grayscale Patch Attacks on Infrared-Adapted Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02233)
*Chengyin Hu,Dingyi Lu,Jiaju Han,Xiang Chen,Weiwen Shi,Jiahuan Long,Yiwei Wei,Jiujiang Guo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents InfraPatch, a white-box adversarial attack framework targeting infrared vision-language models (IR-VLMs). The method optimizes small single-channel grayscale patches (~5% image area) to induce specific target behaviors across three vision-language tasks. Evaluation on 10 IR-VLM variants using 300 synthetic infrared images shows targeted attack success rates ranging from 86% to 100%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Infrared vision-language models (IR-VLMs) are emerging for perception under low-visibility conditions, but their robustness to adversarial attacks remains poorly understood. Existing adversarial patch methods primarily target RGB-based models or single downstream tasks, and it was unclear whether localized perturbations could induce intended semantic targets in IR-VLMs. This work addresses this gap by systematically evaluating targeted attacks against IR-VLMs.

Method: The researchers developed InfraPatch, a white-box per-instance framework that optimizes a compact single-channel patch within approximately 5% local-area budget. The framework combines proxy-guided patch placement with task-adaptive semantic objectives. They evaluated ten infrared-adapted model variants on 300 synthetic infrared-style images generated by applying DiffV2IR to a 30-category COCO subset, using clean-conditioned targeted success criteria across three tasks: image classification, image captioning, and binary visual question answering.

Result: InfraPatch achieves targeted attack success rates from 86.00% to 100% across all ten IR-VLM variants. Proxy location search improves attack success by 6.67 percentage points on CLIP and 10.33 percentage points on BLIP-2 compared to optimized random placement. LLaVA-1.5 remains saturated near 100% under both placement strategies. Patch-area and objective ablations reveal substantial differences in vulnerability across architectures and task formats.

Conclusion: Small grayscale patches can successfully inject chosen target semantics across IR-VLM families under a controlled digital threat model. The findings highlight significant security vulnerabilities in infrared multimodal systems and motivate the need for stronger robustness evaluation protocols for IR-VLMs in safety-critical applications.

Abstract: Infrared vision-language models (IR-VLMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for multimodal perception under low-visibility conditions, yet their robustness to targeted adversarial attacks remains poorly understood. Existing adversarial patch methods mainly study RGB-based models or a single downstream task and do not characterize whether localized perturbations can induce an intended semantic target in IR-VLMs. We propose InfraPatch, a white-box, per-instance framework for targeted digital grayscale patch attacks against IR-VLMs. InfraPatch optimizes a compact single-channel patch within an approximately 5% local-area budget, combines proxy-guided placement with task-adaptive semantic objectives, and induces target behaviors in image classification, image captioning, and binary visual question answering. We evaluate ten infrared-adapted model variants on 300 synthetic infrared-style images generated by applying DiffV2IR to a fixed 30-category COCO subset, using clean-conditioned targeted success criteria. InfraPatch achieves targeted attack success rates from 86.00% to 100% across the ten variants. On CLIP and BLIP-2, proxy location search improves success by 6.67 and 10.33 percentage points over optimized random placement, respectively; LLaVA-1.5 remains saturated near 100% under both settings. Patch-area and objective ablations further expose substantial differences in vulnerability across architectures and task formats. These results show that small grayscale patches can inject chosen target semantics across IR-VLM families under a controlled digital threat model, motivating stronger robustness evaluation for infrared multimodal systems.

</details>


### [56] [SAUF-Net: Structure--Appearance Representation Learning with Uncertainty Feedback for Semi-Supervised Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02247)
*Qin Lu,Zheyang Jing,Yujie Yang,Jianwang Li,Chen Yi,Shaofeng Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SAUF-Net is a semi-supervised medical image segmentation method that addresses unreliable pseudo labels by decoupling structural and appearance representations, using uncertainty feedback to improve segmentation accuracy with limited annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing semi-supervised methods mainly exploit unlabeled data through prediction-level consistency while overlooking the reliability of internal feature representations. In medical images, target-related structural cues are entangled with unstable appearance variations, leading to unreliable pseudo labels and error accumulation during training.

Method: SAUF-Net proposes several key components: (1) Structure--Appearance Decomposition Module (SADM) to separate bottleneck features into structural and appearance representations; (2) Disentangled Guidance Module (DGM) to inject these representations into decoding for structure-aware segmentation; (3) Auxiliary Decoder for branch-specific predictions and appearance-swapped consistency; (4) Appearance-Swapped Consistency branch to maintain structural stability under appearance variations; (5) Reliability-map-guided dual-head discriminator with Validity Head and Uncertainty Head for feature-level uncertainty feedback.

Result: Extensive experiments on ISIC-2016 (skin lesion) and Kvasir-SEG (gastrointestinal polyp) datasets demonstrate that SAUF-Net outperforms state-of-the-art semi-supervised methods, especially under low-label settings.

Conclusion: The proposed SAUF-Net effectively addresses the challenge of unreliable feature representations in semi-supervised medical image segmentation by decoupling structural and appearance features and incorporating uncertainty feedback, achieving superior performance with limited annotations.

Abstract: Semi-supervised learning has shown great potential for reducing annotation costs in medical image segmentation. However, most existing methods mainly exploit unlabeled data through prediction-level consistency, while the reliability of internal feature representations is often overlooked. In medical images, target-related structural cues are easily entangled with unstable appearance variations, which may lead to unreliable pseudo labels and error accumulation during training. To address these issues, we propose SAUF-Net, a Structure--Appearance Representation Learning with Uncertainty Feedback Network for semi-supervised medical image segmentation. SAUF-Net uses the Structure--Appearance Decomposition Module (SADM) to separate bottleneck features into structural and appearance representations. The Disentangled Guidance Module (DGM) injects these representations into the decoding process to enhance structure-aware segmentation. Meanwhile, the Auxiliary Decoder produces branch-specific predictions for reliability estimation and a fused prediction for appearance-swapped consistency. Furthermore, we introduce an Appearance-Swapped Consistency branch to encourage structural representations to remain stable under appearance variations. We also introduce a reliability-map-guided dual-head discriminator with a Validity Head and an Uncertainty Head to provide feature-level uncertainty feedback. Extensive experiments on ISIC-2016 and Kvasir-SEG demonstrate that SAUF-Net outperforms state-of-the-art semi-supervised methods, especially under low-label settings.

</details>


### [57] [Handwriting Trajectory Recovery via Autoregressive Ordered Stroke Instance Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02251)
*En-Guang Wang,Yan-Ming Zhang,Fei Yin,Cheng-Lin Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a two-stage framework for recovering handwriting trajectories from static images. The first stage recovers ordered stroke instances through autoregressive prediction, while the second stage reconstructs continuous within-stroke motion. The method outperforms existing baselines on Chinese handwriting and demonstrates generalization to English and Tamil scripts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline handwriting preserves only the final spatial ink pattern, losing temporal information (stroke order, writing direction, pen-tip motion). Existing learning-based methods directly predict complete character trajectories without explicitly exploiting stroke-level organization. The authors argue that recovering the writing process should follow the natural writing process itself.

Method: The proposed two-stage framework includes: (1) First stage - integrates stroke extraction and stroke-order recovery through autoregressive ordered stroke prediction, with direction-related structural cues supporting within-stroke trajectory generation; (2) Second stage - reconstructs continuous within-stroke motion. The approach uses ordered prediction rather than post-hoc stroke ordering.

Result: The proposed ordered prediction is more effective than post-hoc stroke ordering. Even without trajectory simplification, the full-point model achieves numerically better results than all compared baselines. Trajectory sampling density substantially affects measured recovery performance. The method generalizes to unseen Chinese character categories and cross-language extensibility to English and Tamil handwriting.

Conclusion: The two-stage framework that follows the natural writing process is more effective for handwriting trajectory recovery than direct prediction methods. The approach demonstrates strong performance on Chinese handwriting and good generalization across different scripts (Chinese, English, Tamil), suggesting the methodology is broadly applicable.

Abstract: Handwriting trajectory recovery aims to infer the dynamic writing process hidden behind a static handwritten image. Since offline handwriting preserves only the final spatial ink pattern, temporal information such as stroke order, writing direction, and pen-tip motion is lost, making recovery inherently ambiguous. Existing learning-based methods often directly predict the complete character trajectory without explicitly exploiting the stroke-level organization of handwriting. We argue that recovering the writing process should follow the writing process itself. Accordingly, we propose a two-stage framework that first recovers ordered stroke instances and then reconstructs continuous within-stroke motion. The first stage integrates stroke extraction and stroke-order recovery through autoregressive ordered stroke prediction, while direction-related structural cues further support within-stroke trajectory generation. Experiments on Chinese handwriting show that the proposed ordered prediction is more effective than post-hoc stroke ordering. Even without trajectory simplification, our full-point model achieves numerically better results than those reported by all compared baselines, while a controlled analysis shows that trajectory sampling density substantially affects measured recovery performance. Additional experiments demonstrate generalization to unseen Chinese character categories and cross-language extensibility to English and Tamil handwriting.

</details>


### [58] [T2LSC-Bench: Benchmarking Localized Semantic Control in Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02255)
*Yan Wang,Xinyi Hou,Weiguo Lin,Junjun Si,Siwei Ma*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces T2LSC-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark to evaluate "semantic leakage" in text-to-image models - where target text semantics inadvertently appear in non-textual visual content beyond the designated text area. The benchmark tests 6 models across 7,160 images using a dual-branch evaluation protocol combining OCR-VLM and structured VLM assessments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text rendering benchmarks only evaluate readability, spelling, and layout, but ignore a critical issue: target-text semantics leaking into surrounding visual content (e.g., generating a "pizza" text might cause pizza-like visual elements to appear in the background). This semantic leakage can compromise subject identity and scene semantics in applications like product labeling and signage design.

Method: T2LSC-Bench uses a factorized design with 50 seed subjects and 1,200 prompt cases per model, varying semantic relation, scene openness, prompt mode, and language. A dual-branch protocol combines: (1) OCR-VLM for text verification, and (2) structured VLM for semantic judgments. Four metrics are measured: Text-at-Anchor Accuracy (TAA), Semantic Subject Preservation (SSP), Semantic Leakage Rate (SLR), and Conditional SLR (cSLR).

Result: Under stress-test conditions: SLR increases from 1.2% to 18.1%, cSLR from 1.3% to 18.2%, while TAA decreases only slightly from 91.4% to 90.9%. Anti-leakage prompting reduces SLR from 16.6% to 8.4% without degrading rendering accuracy. Human validation on 420 images shows strong agreement with automatic annotations.

Conclusion: Accurate text rendering does not guarantee local containment of target-text semantics. Even when text is correctly rendered (high TAA), semantic leakage can still occur (high SLR). This reveals a previously overlooked failure mode in text-to-image models that existing benchmarks fail to capture.

Abstract: Recent text-to-image models have become increasingly capable of rendering explicit text, but reliable localized text control requires more than generating the correct string. In applications such as product labeling, signage, and interface design, target text should be rendered within a designated text-bearing region without altering the predefined subject identity or surrounding scene semantics. We refer to violations of this requirement as target-text-associated semantic leakage, in which target-text semantics are expressed through non-textual visual content beyond the designated anchor. Existing visual-text benchmarks primarily evaluate readability, spelling accuracy, and layout, leaving this form of semantic leakage largely unexamined. We introduce T2LSC-Bench, a controlled diagnostic benchmark comprising 50 seed subjects and 1,200 prompt cases per model, yielding 7,160 evaluated images across six models. Its factorized design varies semantic relation, scene openness, prompt mode, and language. A dual-branch protocol combines OCR-VLM text verification with structured VLM semantic judgments to measure Text-at-Anchor Accuracy (TAA), Semantic Subject Preservation (SSP), Semantic Leakage Rate (SLR), and Conditional Semantic Leakage Rate (cSLR). Under stress-test conditions, SLR increases from 1.2% to 18.1% and cSLR from 1.3% to 18.2%, whereas TAA decreases only from 91.4% to 90.9%. Anti-leakage prompting reduces SLR from 16.6% to 8.4% without degrading rendering accuracy. Human validation on 420 images shows strong agreement between automatic and adjudicated annotations. These results show that accurate text rendering does not guarantee local containment of target-text semantics.

</details>


### [59] [MAOL: Morphology-Aware Ordinal Learning for Fine-Grained Industrial Defect Severity Grading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02266)
*Zhaoyang Wang,Haiyong Chen,Binyi Su,Kun Liu,Kun Wang,Xianen Zhou,Atik Shahariar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MAOL (Morphology-Aware Ordinal Learning) is a two-stage framework for fine-grained industrial defect severity grading that addresses ordinal labels, morphological feature incorporation, class-adaptive thresholds, and robustness to noisy predicted instances from two-stage pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-grained defect severity grading is challenging due to: (1) ordinal nature of severity labels, (2) strong dependence on morphology-related cues, and (3) train-test discrepancy between clean annotated instances and noisy predicted instances in two-stage pipelines.

Method: The framework has four key components: (1) Formulates severity grading as instance-level ordinal learning, (2) Incorporates explicit morphological features to enhance representation learning, (3) Introduces class-conditional adaptive ordinal thresholds to model defect-specific grading boundaries, (4) Employs prediction-aware training via localization perturbation to improve robustness to imperfect predicted instances.

Result: MAOL consistently outperforms rule-based methods, nominal classification models, and existing ordinal baselines in both clean-ROI and predicted-instance settings, with particularly strong performance in the predicted-instance setting.

Conclusion: The proposed approach ranked third in the IDA 2026 Challenge on Fine-Grained Severity Grading for High-Precision Manufacturing, demonstrating its practical effectiveness for industrial inspection applications.

Abstract: Fine-grained defect severity grading is essential for industrial inspection, yet remains challenging due to the ordinal nature of severity labels, the strong dependence on morphology-related cues, and the train-test discrepancy between clean annotated instances and noisy predicted instances in two-stage pipelines. We propose MAOL, a Morphology-Aware Ordinal Learning framework for fine-grained industrial defect severity grading. MAOL formulates severity grading as an instance-level ordinal learning task, incorporates explicit morphological features to enhance representation learning, introduces class-conditional adaptive ordinal thresholds to model defect-specific grading boundaries, and employs prediction-aware training via localization perturbation to improve robustness to imperfect predicted instances. Extensive experiments under both clean-ROI and predicted-instance settings demonstrate that MAOL consistently outperforms rule-based methods, nominal classification models, and existing ordinal baselines, especially in the predicted-instance setting. The proposed approach ranked third in the IDA 2026 Challenge on Fine-Grained Severity Grading for High-Precision Manufacturing.

</details>


### [60] [RouteGraph-Mona: Confusion-Aware Routing Fine-Tuning for Mineral Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02282)
*Jierui Li,Zhiyuan Qi,Hao Zhu,Yufan Liu,Jixian Liu,Shaojie Jiang,Jianda Wang,Yaqi Liu,Xiaotong Li,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses mineral image classification challenges using a novel adapter-based method called RouteGraph-Mona, which replaces static multi-scale aggregation in the Mona adapter with sample-adaptive routing and route-space regularization to improve classification accuracy for visually similar mineral categories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mineral image classification is important for geological exploration and resource development but faces two key challenges: (1) substantial intra-class variations in appearance, and (2) high inter-class visual similarity between different mineral categories. The existing Mona adapter has a limitation: it statically aggregates responses from multiple scales, which cannot accommodate sample-specific scale preferences and causes model confusion among visually similar mineral categories.

Method: The proposed RouteGraph-Mona is a lightweight route-space regularization method built on Mona. It has two main components: (1) Sample-adaptive routing: Replaces Mona's static multi-scale aggregation with dynamic branch selection that adapts to each image's scale preferences, defining a compact routing space. (2) Route-space regularization: Regularizes routing signatures with class-wise route anchors (encouraging class-consistent routing patterns) and confusion-weighted margins (promoting greater separation between visually similar categories in the routing space).

Result: Experiments on three public mineral image datasets with two visual backbones show that RouteGraph-Mona consistently outperforms Mona in mean accuracy and remains competitive with representative fine-tuning methods and mineral image classification baselines.

Conclusion: RouteGraph-Mona successfully addresses the limitations of Mona by introducing sample-adaptive routing and route-space regularization, achieving improved classification performance for mineral images while maintaining parameter efficiency through the adapter-based approach.

Abstract: Mineral image classification is important for geological exploration and resource development, but it remains challenging due to substantial intra-class variations in appearance and high inter-class visual similarity. Multi-cognitive Visual Adapter (Mona) is a vision-oriented parameter-efficient adapter that adapts pre-trained visual models by tuning only a few parameters. However, Mona statically aggregates responses from multiple scales, limiting its ability to accommodate sample-specific scale preferences and model confusion among visually similar mineral categories. To address this issue, we propose \textbf{RouteGraph-Mona}, a lightweight route-space regularization method built on Mona. Specifically, we replace Mona's static multi-scale aggregation with sample-adaptive routing. The resulting branch-selection behavior defines a compact routing space that captures each image's scale preferences. We then regularize the resulting routing signatures with class-wise route anchors and confusion-weighted margins. The route anchors encourage class-consistent routing patterns, while the margins promote greater separation between visually similar categories in the routing space. Experiments on three public mineral image datasets with two visual backbones show that RouteGraph-Mona consistently outperforms Mona in mean accuracy and remains competitive with representative fine-tuning methods and mineral image classification baselines.

</details>


### [61] [Diffusion-Encoding Gaussian Field for Joint k-q dMRI Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02288)
*Zhibo Chen,Yajuan Huang,Yu Guan,Qiuyun Fan,Dong Liang,Qiegen Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A novel self-supervised joint k-q diffusion MRI reconstruction method using subject-specific spatial-angular Gaussian fields with 3D Gaussian primitives carrying q-conditioned tensor-residual responses, enabling reconstruction without fully sampled targets or held-out-direction supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion MRI acquisition time is limited by spatial and angular sampling requirements. Existing joint k-q methods either fix directional parameters to voxels or separate spatial from angular reconstruction, failing to fully exploit the complementarity between shared anatomy and direction-dependent signal variation. This leads to residual spatial errors being misinterpreted as genuine angular variation.

Method: Proposed a subject-specific spatial-angular Gaussian field using shared 3D Gaussian primitives for local spatial support. Each primitive carries a continuous q-conditioned tensor-residual response. Signals are synthesized from multiple overlapping primitive responses, coupling neighboring spatial regions and diffusion directions. The field is progressively optimized from undersampled k-space measurements in a self-supervised manner without fully sampled targets or held-out-direction supervision.

Result: Experiments on three HCP diffusion shells under multiple acceleration settings demonstrated consistent improvements in: (1) missing-direction DWI reconstruction, (2) tensor-derived metrics, and (3) principal diffusion orientation estimation.

Conclusion: The proposed Gaussian field approach effectively couples spatial and angular domains, enabling robust self-supervised joint k-q reconstruction that improves reconstruction quality across multiple diffusion MRI metrics under various acceleration factors.

Abstract: Diffusion MRI requires repeated k-space acquisitions over multiple diffusion-encoding directions, making acquisition time dependent on both spatial and angular sampling. Existing joint k-q methods either associate directional parameters with fixed voxels or separate spatial reconstruction from angular completion. However, diffusion-weighted images acquired under different directions share the same anatomical organization, while their local signal intensities vary with diffusion encoding. Existing formulations do not fully exploit the complementarity between shared anatomy and direction-dependent signal variation. Consequently, residual spatial errors may be misinterpreted as genuine angular variation and propagated to unobserved directions.
  We propose a subject-specific spatial-angular Gaussian field for self-supervised joint k-q dMRI reconstruction. Shared 3D Gaussian primitives provide local spatial support, with each primitive carrying a continuous q-conditioned tensor-residual response. The signal at each location is synthesized from multiple overlapping primitive responses, coupling neighboring spatial regions and diffusion directions. The field is progressively optimized from undersampled k-space measurements of observed directions, without fully sampled targets or held-out-direction supervision.
  Experiments on three HCP diffusion shells under multiple acceleration settings demonstrated consistent improvements in missing-direction DWI reconstruction, tensor-derived metrics, and principal diffusion orientation estimation.

</details>


### [62] [If It Moves, Radar Knows: A Physics-Aware Radar Transformer for Class-Agnostic Moving-Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02289)
*Yinghao Sun,Shuguang Li,Jinliang Shao,Tieshan Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PART is a lightweight (1.1M parameters) radar-only detector that addresses the closed-set limitation of traditional detectors by predicting class-agnostic object existence, surface points, and 2D velocity. It uses novel Doppler-Aware Query Initialization and Physics-Guided Cross-Attention mechanisms to handle sparse radar data, achieving 0.8827 CA-AP and strong generalization to rare categories and adverse conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current detectors trained on closed-set annotations fail to detect rare moving objects outside the training taxonomy. While automotive radar provides category-independent Doppler motion cues and is robust to adverse conditions, sparse and noisy returns hinder class-aware 3D box detection. The key insight is that surface location and velocity remain useful for motion reasoning even when full box geometry is difficult to recover.

Method: The proposed PART framework includes three key innovations: (1) Doppler-Aware Query Initialization (DAQI) replaces scene-independent learned queries with input-dependent proposals by clustering radar returns in position and velocity; (2) Physics-Guided Cross-Attention (PGCA) incorporates radial-Doppler consistency and radar cross section (RCS) into query-point association; (3) Uncertainty-aware supervision randomly masks ground-truth objects and assigns soft existence targets to ambiguous radar-supported queries, reducing reliance on exhaustive annotations.

Result: PART achieves CA-AP of 0.8827, mASTE of 0.3188 m, and mAVE of 0.8084 m/s on nuScenes. It attains 0.9203 recall on rare and safety-relevant categories excluded from standard evaluation. The method remains effective at night, in rain, and under severe occlusion. Analysis shows some "false positives" actually correspond to moving objects missing from nuScenes annotations.

Conclusion: The physics-aware transformer approach successfully enables class-agnostic moving object detection from sparse radar data. The small model (1.1M parameters) demonstrates strong performance while generalizing well to rare categories and challenging conditions. The work suggests that radar-only detection can be practical for safety-critical applications, with the bonus of potentially discovering objects missed in existing datasets.

Abstract: Detectors trained on closed-set annotations can miss rare moving objects outside the training taxonomy. Automotive radar provides category-independent Doppler motion cues and is less affected by adverse illumination and weather, but sparse, noisy returns hinder class-aware 3D box detection. Surface location and velocity remain useful for motion reasoning and collision avoidance when full box geometry is difficult to recover. We present the Physics-Aware Radar Transformer (PART), a fully sparse radar-only detector that predicts existence confidence, a representative surface point, and 2D ground-plane velocity for each moving-object hypothesis. Doppler-Aware Query Initialization (DAQI) replaces scene-independent learned queries with input-dependent proposals by clustering radar returns in position and velocity, easing query-object assignment in sparse scenes. Physics-Guided Cross-Attention (PGCA) incorporates radial-Doppler consistency and radar cross section (RCS) into query-point association. Uncertainty-aware supervision randomly masks ground-truth objects and assigns soft existence targets to ambiguous radar-supported queries, reducing reliance on exhaustive annotations. With only 1.1 million parameters, PART achieves a class-agnostic average precision (CA-AP) of 0.8827, a mean average surface translation error (mASTE) of 0.3188 m, and a mean average velocity error (mAVE) of 0.8084 m/s on nuScenes. It attains 0.9203 recall on rare and safety-relevant categories excluded from the standard evaluation and remains effective at night, in rain, and under severe occlusion. Inspection of apparent false positives shows that some predictions correspond to moving objects absent from the nuScenes annotations. Code and pretrained model weights will be publicly available at https://github.com/sunyinghao-uestc/PART.

</details>


### [63] [VoRTeC: Taming Foundation Flow for One-step Real time Video Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02291)
*Yichong Xia,Qinhong Wu,Qinhong Wu,Jinpeng Wang,Zeyuan Chen,Haoqian Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VoRTeC is a novel ultra-low bitrate video compression framework built on a foundational flow model (Wan2.1) that achieves one-step decoding with high perceptual fidelity. It addresses the trade-off between blurring artifacts in traditional neural compression and excessive latency in diffusion-based methods by using latent encoding, flow trajectory prediction, and multi-scale priors, while maintaining temporal consistency through tail-frame reuse and prior caching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ultra-low bitrate video compression faces two critical challenges: traditional neural video compression introduces blurring artifacts, while diffusion-based generative video compression suffers from excessive decoding latency and poor temporal consistency. There is a need for a method that achieves high perceptual fidelity without the computational overhead of multi-step diffusion processes.

Method: VoRTeC uses three key techniques: (1) compactly encoding latent video representations, (2) predicting positions of compressed representations along flow trajectories, and (3) integrating multi-scale priors. The framework achieves one-step decoding without accessing parameters or gradients of flow matching networks. Temporal consistency is maintained via tail-frame reuse and prior caching across frame groups.

Result: VoRTeC reduces bit consumption by 58% compared to prior diffusion-based approaches, with decoding speed boosted by 3 to 197 times. It achieves 13 FPS at 720p and 32 FPS at 480p decoding speeds.

Conclusion: VoRTeC successfully bridges the gap between neural and diffusion-based video compression by leveraging generative video flow priors through a flow-based framework. The method achieves excellent perceptual quality with minimal computational overhead, making ultra-low bitrate video compression more practical for real-world applications.

Abstract: Ultra-low bitrate video compression still faces critical challenges: traditional neural video compression inevitably introduces blurring artifacts, while diffusion-based generative video compression suffers from excessive decoding latency and poor temporal consistency. To address these issues, we propose $\mathtt{VoRTeC}$, a Video Compression framework built upon a foundational flow model (Wan2.1). By compactly encoding latent video representations, predicting the positions of compressed representations along flow trajectories, and integrating multi-scale priors, $\mathtt{VoRTeC}$ enables the compressor to harness generative video flow priors effectively. Without accessing the parameters or gradients of flow matching networks, our framework achieves one-step decoding and reconstructions with high perceptual fidelity. Meanwhile, we maintain consistency across frame groups via tail-frame reuse and prior caching. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method reduces bit consumption by 58\% compared to prior diffusion-based approaches, with decoding speed boosted by 3 to 197 times: $\mathtt{VoRTeC}$ achieves a decoding speed of 13 FPS at 720p and 32 FPS at 480p.

</details>


### [64] [Domain shift-robust object detection with GenAI image editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02299)
*Isabel D. Stein,Thijs A. Eker,Sebastiaan P. Snel,Ella P. Fokkinga,Klamer Schutte,Luca Ambrogioni,Friso G. Heslinga*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates using diffusion-based generative image editing to improve object detector robustness under domain shifts, specifically for camouflaged military vehicle detection. The approach synthetically adds camouflage to training data to bridge the gap between source (uncamouflaged) and target (camouflaged) domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Object detectors degrade under domain shifts (lighting, weather, occlusion) because they rely on visual shortcuts learned from training data that fail to generalize. Traditional data collection is impractical for capturing rare domain variations. The authors hypothesize that generative editing can simulate controlled domain shifts to improve out-of-domain robustness.

Method: The study uses camouflaged military vehicle detection as a challenging domain shift scenario. Training data consists of uncamouflaged vehicles, while test data contains real imagery with foliage, netting, and multi-spectral camouflage across 15 vehicle classes. Three generative approaches were tested: Qwen Image Edit 2509, Flux.2 Dev, and a LoRA fine-tuned Qwen. A black-bar occlusion baseline served as a non-generative comparison. A GroundingDINO detector was trained on combinations of real and synthetically augmented data.

Result: Generative camouflage augmentation yielded substantial improvements: foliage camouflage +20.1 mAP, netting camouflage +14.4 mAP. Multi-spectral camouflage was more challenging to generate, but LoRA fine-tuning improved performance by +4.4 mAP over the uncamouflaged baseline.

Conclusion: Diffusion-based generative image editing is effective for improving out-of-domain robustness by simulating domain shifts in training data. This approach successfully bridges the source-target domain gap for camouflage scenarios, with foliage and netting being more tractable than multi-spectral camouflage. The LoRA fine-tuning approach shows promise for handling harder domain shifts.

Abstract: Object detectors often degrade under domain shifts such as changes in lighting, weather, or occlusion. These shifts alter object appearance and expose a reliance on visual shortcuts learned from the training distribution that do not generalize across domains. Acquiring sufficient real-world samples to capture such domain variation is particularly difficult in specialized, low-data settings. Recent advances in diffusion-based generative image editing have shown promise for improving the in-domain performance of object detectors through synthetic data augmentation. However, their potential to improve out-of-domain robustness remains largely unexplored. We hypothesize that generative image editing can simulate a controlled domain shift in training data, effectively bridging the gap between source and target domains. To test this, we studied camouflaged military vehicle detection as a challenging domain shift scenario. Detectors trained on uncamouflaged data demonstrate substantial degradation on real test imagery containing foliage, netting, and multi-spectral camouflage across 15 vehicle classes in close-up, ground-level imagery. We used two diffusion-based editing models, Qwen Image Edit 2509 and Flux.2 Dev, to synthetically add camouflage to the training data, alongside a LoRA fine-tuned version of Qwen. A non-generative black-bar occlusion baseline served as a lower bound on augmentation quality. Using a GroundingDINO detector trained on real and synthetic data, generative camouflage augmentation yielded substantial mAP improvements for foliage (+20.1) and netting (+14.4) camouflage. Generating multi-spectral camouflage proved more challenging, but LoRA fine-tuning improved performance by 4.4 mAP over the uncamouflaged baseline.

</details>


### [65] [YesTrack: Referring Multi-Object Tracking via MLLM-based Yes/No Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02318)
*Quansheng Hu,Qin Sun,Qiansen Dai,Jin Ding,Wan Zhang,Xue Zhou,Jianxiao Zou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: YesTrack is a novel two-stage referring multi-object tracking (RMOT) method that leverages multimodal large language models (MLLMs) as discriminative Yes/No verifiers rather than caption generators. It introduces two temporal consistency constraints (TCP and TRP) to enhance reliability and efficiency. A variant YesTrack-MOT is also proposed for generic MOT. The method achieves state-of-the-art performance on Refer-KITTI and Refer-KITTI-V2 while maintaining high efficiency, even with the smallest Qwen3-VL variant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RMOT methods predominantly use MLLMs as caption generators, requiring external modules for final decision-making. This paradigm introduces extra latency and severely underutilizes the vision-language alignment capabilities of MLLMs. The authors aim to address these limitations by directly leveraging MLLMs for discriminative verification.

Method: YesTrack proposes a two-stage approach: (1) Reformulates referring as a discriminative task, using MLLMs for Yes/No verification without explicit text generation; (2) Introduces two lightweight temporal consistency constraints: Temporal Confidence Prior (TCP) and Temporal Reference Propagation (TRP). Additionally, YesTrack-MOT is proposed as a straightforward instantiation for generic multi-object tracking.

Result: YesTrack significantly outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods on Refer-KITTI and Refer-KITTI-V2 while maintaining high efficiency, even when implemented with the smallest variant of Qwen3-VL.

Conclusion: The discriminative paradigm (using MLLMs for Yes/No verification) is effective and generalizable for both referring tracking and generic MOT. This approach better utilizes MLLMs' vision-language alignment capabilities compared to caption-generation paradigms.

Abstract: Referring multi-object tracking (RMOT) aims to track every instance in a video that matches a given language expression. Despite the recent integration of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to enhance generalization, existing methods predominantly relegate them to the role of caption generators, necessitating external modules for final decision-making. This paradigm not only introduces extra latency but also severely underutilizes the inherent vision-language alignment capabilities of MLLMs. To address these limitations, we propose YesTrack, a novel two-stage RMOT method that reformulates referring as a discriminative task, directly leveraging MLLMs for Yes/No verification without explicit text generation. To further enhance the reliability and efficiency of this MLLM-based verification, we introduce two lightweight temporal consistency constraints: Temporal Confidence Prior (TCP) and Temporal Reference Propagation (TRP). We further validate the generality of this discriminative paradigm by proposing YesTrack-MOT, a straightforward yet highly effective instantiation for generic multi-object tracking (MOT). Experiments on Refer-KITTI and Refer-KITTI-V2 show that YesTrack significantly outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods while maintaining high efficiency, even when implemented with the smallest variant of Qwen3-VL. Code is released at https://github.com/ggbondrighthere24/YesTrack.

</details>


### [66] [ORB-SVM : An Innovative Hybrid Framework for Efficient Brain Tumor Detection from MRI Scans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02333)
*Amirhosein Azarpour*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a hybrid framework for brain cancer detection from MRI images using ORB algorithm for feature extraction and SVM for classification, achieving 97.5% accuracy while reducing data by 99.5%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address challenges in brain cancer early diagnosis using MRI, including subjective variability among practitioners, image noise, and the computational overhead of deep learning approaches that require extensive data and parameters.

Method: Hybrid framework combining ORB (Oriented FAST and Rotated BRIEF) algorithm for precise feature extraction with SVM (Support Vector Machine) using a robust kernel-based classifier for classification.

Result: The proposed approach achieves 97.5% classification accuracy on the Br35H dataset while reducing data by approximately 99.5%, effectively minimizing non-informative background data while preserving critical diagnostic patterns.

Conclusion: The integration of localized feature representation (ORB) with optimized classification (SVM) provides a reliable, resource-efficient alternative for medical image analysis that maintains high diagnostic integrity without extensive computational overhead, addressing limitations of over-parameterized deep learning systems.

Abstract: Brain cancer remains one of the most significant challenges in modern medicine, where the accuracy of early stage diagnosis is a decisive factor in patient survival and treatment efficacy. Although Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) is the established gold standard for visualizing neurological structures, the interpretation of these high dimensional scans is often complicated by subjective variability among practitioners and the inherent noise present in complex medical images. While contemporary approaches frequently rely on high parameter deep learning architectures, such models often involve significant computational costs and require extensive data for effective training. This study introduces a hybrid framework that utilizes the Oriented FAST and Rotated BRIEF (ORB) algorithm for precise feature extraction and a Support Vector Machine (SVM) for classification [1], [2]. The proposed approach achieves a sub- stantial data reduction of approximately 99.5%, which effectively minimizes the influence of non informative background data while preserving critical diagnostic patterns essential for tumor identification. By balancing feature sparsity with a robust kernel based classifier, this methodology addresses the limitations of over parameterized systems while maintaining high diagnostic integrity. Experimental evaluations conducted on the Br35H dataset demonstrate that the framework attains a classification accuracy of 97.5%. The findings suggest that the integration of localized feature representation and optimized classification provides a reliable and resource efficient alternative for medical image analysis, offering a structured solution that maintains per- formance without the need for extensive computational overhead.

</details>


### [67] [Towards Zero-Shot Transfer Across Embodiments For Driving VLAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02341)
*Caio Azevedo,Stefano Sabatini,Sascha Hornauer,Fabien Moutarde*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses limitations in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models for autonomous driving, specifically the lack of multi-dataset training and zero-shot transfer evaluation. The authors propose BEV-Forcing, an auxiliary objective that transfers Bird's-Eye-View spatial information into the VLA backbone to improve both in-distribution and out-of-distribution performance. However, they find that as training diversity increases, the auxiliary task's benefits diminish.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLAs in autonomous driving are: (1) trained on individual datasets rather than multiple datasets, (2) rarely evaluated for zero-shot transfer to unseen datasets/camera rigs, and (3) do not necessarily benefit from simply adding more datasets to training. The authors aim to study multi-dataset training and determine whether auxiliary objectives can improve performance across different embodiments.

Method: The authors propose: (1) Multi-dataset training approach for driving tasks, and (2) BEV-Forcing - an auxiliary objective that transfers ground-plane object-layout information from a specialized Bird's-Eye-View model into the VLA backbone, encouraging object position representation through a shared BEV spatial interface.

Result: BEV-Forcing improves both in-distribution and out-of-distribution performance when training on a small number of camera rigs. However, as the number of training embodiments increases, the benefits of the auxiliary task are reduced.

Conclusion: New techniques like BEV-Forcing may see their benefits diminish when simply scaling up training diversity. This motivates presenting results taking into account data scaling, suggesting that researchers should consider training diversity when evaluating novel auxiliary objectives for VLAs in autonomous driving.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) have shown strong potential in autonomous driving by leveraging multimodal pretraining for instruction following, visual reasoning, and scene-level generalization. In robotic manipulation, scaling VLA fine-tuning across multiple robot setups--especially when unifying representations across embodiments--has been shown to improve in-dataset performance and cross-embodiment generalization; in autonomous driving, however, VLAs remain largely trained on individual datasets and are rarely evaluated for zero-shot transfer to unseen datasets and camera rigs; furthermore naively adding more datasets to the training data does not necessarily lead to better performance within seen embodiments. To address these problems, we study multi-dataset training for the driving task and BEV-Forcing, an auxiliary objective that transfers ground-plane object-layout information from a specialized Bird's-Eye-View model into the VLA backbone. By encouraging the model to represent object position through a shared BEV spatial interface, we show that an auxiliary task such as BEV-Forcing can improve both in-distribution and out-of-distribution performance when training on a small number of camera rigs. As the number of training embodiments increases, however, the benefits of the auxiliary task are reduced; we present this as evidence that new techniques in the literature may see their benefits diminish when simply scaling up training diversity, which motivates presenting results taking into account data scaling.

</details>


### [68] [Structured-Prior-Guided Diffusion Inpainting with Physical Consistency for Traffic Sign Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02348)
*Luo Li,Chongchong Huang,Jun Jia,Qiang Gao,Xinlong Liu,Gui Yang,Liang Cao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper addresses the long-tailed data distribution problem in traffic sign detection by proposing a structured-prior-guided diffusion inpainting framework. The key innovation is using three orthogonal pathways (text prompt, front-view vector template via IP-Adapter, and affine-aligned vector template via ControlNet) to inject semantic, appearance, and geometric priors. Two physical consistency losses (CIELAB chromaticity and Sobel gradient) ensure color and edge fidelity. The method achieves 91.1% OCR exact-match rate (vs 44.2% for FLUX.1) with 1/14 inference time, and significantly improves rare class detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traffic sign detection suffers from long-tailed data distribution where rare signs have very few samples but are still critically important from a regulatory standpoint. Existing general-purpose inpainting models distort digits, deform geometry/perspective, and shift colors when applied to sign regions because their conditioning signals are too abstract for the physical composition of signs.

Method: The proposed framework uses a Stable Diffusion 1.5 backbone (~1.4B parameters) with three orthogonal prior pathways: (1) JSON-formatted text prompt for semantic prior, (2) front-view vector template with measured dominant colors via IP-Adapter for appearance prior, and (3) affine-aligned vector template via ControlNet for geometric prior. Two physical consistency losses constrain color (CIELAB chromaticity L1 term) and edge structure (Sobel gradient term). Training is self-supervised reconstruction on AMAP in-house data, evaluated zero-shot on TT100K-2021.

Result: The method outperforms seven competitors on all metrics of reconstruction fidelity, physical consistency, and semantic controllability. OCR exact-match rate reaches 91.1%, compared to 44.2% for the 12B industrial model FLUX.1 Fill [dev], while requiring only 1/14 of that model's inference time. In downstream detection, synthetic data raises group-pooled AP50 of rare classes by 1.23× to 7.40× over real-data-only baseline. Leave-one-out ablations confirm each component contributes independently.

Conclusion: The structured-prior-guided diffusion inpainting framework effectively addresses the long-tailed data problem in traffic sign detection by maintaining physical consistency (color, geometry, digits) through multi-modal prior injection and dedicated loss functions. The approach demonstrates superior performance over large industrial models while being computationally efficient, making it practical for data augmentation in real-world traffic sign detection systems.

Abstract: Traffic sign detection faces a long-tailed data distribution. Many rare signs matter as much as common ones from a regulatory standpoint, yet they have very few samples. Generative data augmentation is one way out. General-purpose inpainting models, however, distort digits, deform geometry and perspective, and shift colours when applied directly to sign regions. We trace this to a single gap: the conditioning signal is too abstract for the physical composition of a sign. We propose a structured-prior-guided diffusion inpainting framework with physical consistency. It injects the semantic, appearance and geometric priors of a sign through three orthogonal pathways: a JSON-formatted text prompt, a front-view vector template rendered with measured dominant colours (via IP-Adapter), and an affine-aligned vector template (via ControlNet). Two physical consistency losses constrain colour with a CIELAB chromaticity $L_1$ term and edge structure with a Sobel gradient term. We train by self-supervised reconstruction on a large set of images collected in-house at AMAP, then evaluate zero-shot on the public TT100K-2021 dataset, a different source. Our method uses a Stable Diffusion 1.5 backbone of about 1.4B parameters. It beats seven representative competitors on every metric of reconstruction fidelity, physical consistency and semantic controllability. Its OCR exact-match rate reaches 91.1\%, against 44.2\% for the 12B industrial model FLUX.1 Fill [dev], and it needs only $1/14$ of that model's inference time.
