- Soybean3D (20-shot): 89.78% IoU, 90.27% mWCov
- Soybean3D (10-shot): 83.19% IoU, 83.23% mWCov
- HR3D (10-shot transfer to tobacco, tomato, sorghum): 78.41% IoU, 79.42% mWCov
- SYAU-Maize (22-shot): 92.75% IoU, 93.51% mRec
- ShapeNet Part: 85.0% category-averaged mIoU

Conclusion: PlantC2USeg reduces overall adaptation effort under distribution shifts, enabling scalable plant phenotyping and transferable 3D representation learning beyond agriculture. The framework demonstrates strong few-shot generalization capabilities across different plant species and sensing conditions, with versatility shown through successful application to general 3D shape segmentation.

Abstract: Modern crop breeding demands precise organ-level analysis for trait quantification, making plant point cloud segmentation (PPCS) increasingly important. However, conventional deep learning approaches rely heavily on densely annotated datasets that are labor-intensive to acquire. Unified PPCS adaptation from distribution-shifted examples with minimal additional training remains challenging. To address this, we propose PlantC2USeg, a deep transfer learning framework featuring cross-scale consistency learning to explicitly align features across spatial scales and an information-restricted decoding strategy that prevents reconstruction shortcuts and promotes robust adaptation. The resulting pre-training enables stable few-shot generalization across species and sensing conditions, while unified fine-tuning with inherited thresholds further reduces adaptation overhead. Under full supervision on Soybean3D, PlantC2USeg achieves the highest semantic IoU and instance mWCov among compared methods, at 91.91% and 94.62%. With 20 labeled samples, it leads both metrics at 89.78% and 90.27%; with only 10 samples, it retains the highest mWCov of 83.23% while achieving 83.19% IoU. Across HR3D, 10-shot transfer to tobacco, tomato, and sorghum averages 78.41% IoU and 79.42% mWCov, while 22-shot transfer to SYAU-Maize achieves the highest IoU and mRec at 92.75% and 93.51%. Furthermore, a leading category-averaged mIoU of 85.0% on ShapeNet Part demonstrates the framework's capability to handle diverse shape variations beyond agricultural domains. These results demonstrate that PlantC2USeg reduces overall adaptation effort under distribution shifts, enabling scalable plant phenotyping and transferable 3D representation learning beyond agriculture.

</details>


### [117] [Thinking in Pictures: A Systematic Benchmark for Reasoning-driven Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02864)
*Yutong Liu,Nan Huang,Xu Cao,James M. Rehg*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RIG-BENCH is a benchmark for evaluating Reasoning-driven Image Generation (RIG), addressing the gap between surface-level event alignment and high-level visual reasoning in unified generative models (UGMs). The benchmark covers four domains with 2000 samples and reveals significant reasoning-generation gaps in state-of-the-art models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current UGMs and world simulators primarily rely on surface-level event alignment, leaving high-level visual reasoning capacity underexplored. True visual generative intelligence requires "Reasoning-to-Generation" - the ability to infer latent rules from visual inputs and produce logically constrained visual outcomes.

Method: RIG-BENCH systematically evaluates RIG across four cognitively demanding domains: Concept-based, Transformation-based, Pattern & Structure, and Scenario-based. The benchmark contains 2000 curated samples as a rigorous stress test for RIG capabilities.

Result: Extensive evaluations of state-of-the-art UGMs and image/video generation models reveal a significant reasoning-generation gap. Models frequently produce locally plausible but globally illogical outputs.

Conclusion: RIG-BENCH provides a vital diagnostic framework to guide the development of next-generation, logically grounded UGMs and world simulators.

Abstract: Recent advancements in unified generative models (UGMs) and world simulators have achieved unprecedented results in visual perception and synthesis. However, these models primarily rely on surface-level event alignment, leaving the capacity for high-level visual reasoning underexplored. True visual generative intelligence demands "Reasoning-to-Generation", an ability to infer latent rules from visual inputs and manifest solutions through precise, logically constrained visual outcomes. We introduce RIG-BENCH, a novel comprehensive benchmark that systematically evaluates Reasoning-driven Image Generation (RIG) across four cognitively demanding domains: Concept-based, Transformation-based, Pattern & Structure, and Scenario-based. Featuring 2000 curated samples, RIG-BENCH serves as a rigorous stress test for RIG. Our extensive evaluations of state-of-the-art UGMs and image/video generation models reveal a significant reasoning-generation gap, wherein models frequently produce locally plausible but globally illogical outputs. RIG-BENCH provides a vital diagnostic framework to guide the development of next-generation, logically grounded UGMs and world simulators.

</details>


### [118] [SolarWM: Open Data and Scalable Training for Long-Horizon Video World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02886)
*Junchao Huang,Guian Fang,Shengju Qian,Xianghao Kong,Zhuoran Zhao,Wei Huang,Yihua Du,Zixin Zhang,Justin Cui,Yuchao Gu,Yukang Chen,Xinting Hu,Tianyu He,Shaoshuai Shi,Zhuotao Tian,Xin Wang,Mike Zheng Shou,Li Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SolarWM is an open-source framework for building interactive video world models, featuring a unified data engine processing 1.43M clips from 10 datasets, and backbone-native adaptation for 4 models (5B-33B) using Wan2.2, LTX-2.5, and MiniMax-H3 architectures, enabling real-time long-horizon inference from short training sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The challenges in training video world models across heterogeneous data sources and video backbones: datasets differ in temporal scale, camera geometry, visual quality, motion, and captioning styles, while video generators use distinct representations and architectures. Current approaches with naive data mixing and model-specific implementations produce inconsistent supervision and make results difficult to reproduce and compare.

Method: SolarWM addresses these challenges through: 1) A reconfigurable multi-source data engine that converts 1.43 million canonical clips from 10 datasets into a unified, frame-aligned contract covering visual observations, metric camera geometry, captions, quality metadata, selection decisions, and provenance; 2) A backbone-native adaptation framework with shared camera-conditioning, training, and inference interfaces; 3) A unified three-stage recipe combining bidirectional adaptation, teacher-forced autoregressive initialization, and distribution matching distillation.

Result: The resulting causal models enable real-time interaction over rollouts ranging from minutes to hours after being trained on only 5s sequences. Four models (5B-33B) were instantiated based on Wan2.2, LTX-2.5, and MiniMax-H3 while preserving their native representations and objectives.

Conclusion: SolarWM provides a reproducible and extensible foundation for interactive world-model research by releasing the resulting data, pipeline, recipes, weights, and framework. It addresses the coupling problem between data sources and model architectures through a unified approach that enables consistent supervision and comparison across different implementations.

Abstract: We introduce SolarWM, a fully open foundation for building interactive video world models from data preparation through long-horizon inference. Training across heterogeneous data sources and video backbones is challenging: datasets differ in temporal scale, camera geometry, visual quality, motion, and captioning styles, while video generators use distinct representations and architectures. Naive data mixing and model-specific implementations therefore produce inconsistent supervision and make results difficult to reproduce and compare. SolarWM addresses this coupling with a reconfigurable multi-source data engine and a backbone-native adaptation framework. The engine converts 1.43 million canonical clips from 10 datasets into a unified, frame-aligned contract covering visual observations, metric camera geometry, captions, quality metadata, selection decisions, and provenance, while decoupling source processing from mixture construction. Under shared camera-conditioning, training, and inference interfaces, we instantiate four 5B--33B models based on Wan2.2, LTX-2.5, and MiniMax-H3 while preserving their native representations and objectives. A unified three-stage recipe combines bidirectional adaptation, teacher-forced autoregressive initialization, and distribution matching distillation. The resulting causal models enable real-time interaction over rollouts ranging from minutes to hours after being trained on only 5s sequences. By releasing the resulting data, pipeline, recipes, weights, and framework, SolarWM provides a reproducible and extensible foundation for interactive world-model research.

</details>


<div id='cs.GR'></div>

# cs.GR [[Back]](#toc)

### [119] [WildFab: Multi-Axis 3D Printing from Models in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02413)
*Jiasheng Qu,Zhikai Shen,Chenyu Xu,Hailin Sun,Chengkai Dai,Yuhu Guo,Junpeng Wang,Yeung Yam,Guoxin Fang*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: WildFab is a computational framework for multi-axis 3D printing that directly computes spatial toolpath and collision-free motion from complex input models (non-manifold, solid-shell combinations, non-watertight meshes) without requiring time-consuming geometry repair.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-axis 3D printing enables support-free fabrication and improved part quality, but processing real-world geometries remains challenging. Models from design workflows or direct data acquisition often contain solid-shell combinations and non-manifold structures that require time-consuming geometry repair, which may alter the intended geometry.

Method: The pipeline uses a hybrid query representation combining a neural unsigned distance field (UDF) with a regularized generalized winding number field (reg-GWN). The UDF provides differentiable surface-distance and direction queries, while reg-GWN resolves near-surface ambiguity for reliable surface localization and solid-void indication. A high-precision spatial toolpath computation algorithm iteratively projects points between optimized guidance-field level sets and reg-GWN gradient-magnitude ridges. Collision checking uses a coarse-to-fine scheme: UDF-based rejection for potential collisions, followed by time-varying reg-GWN verification for accurate resolution.

Result: WildFab successfully computes toolpaths from diverse inputs including non-manifold parametric surfaces, voxelized topology-optimization results, implicit models, raw scanned point clouds, and non-watertight meshes. The method demonstrates successful fabrication results advancing end-to-end design-to-3DP workflows.

Conclusion: The proposed WildFab framework enables direct processing of complex real-world geometries for multi-axis 3D printing without geometry repair, advancing the state of design-to-fabrication workflows by handling non-manifold, solid-shell, and non-watertight input models.

Abstract: Multi-axis 3D printing enables support-free fabrication and improved part quality, but robustly processing real-world geometries remains challenging. Models from design workflows or direct data acquisition often contain solid--shell combinations and non-manifold structures. Handling such models in the wild typically requires time-consuming geometry repair, which may alter the intended geometry. In this work, we present WildFab, a computational framework for multi-axis 3D printing that directly computes spatial toolpath and global collision-free motion from input models. Our pipeline builds on a hybrid query representation that combines a neural unsigned distance field (UDF) with a regularized generalized winding number field (reg-GWN). The UDF supplies differentiable surface-distance and direction queries, while the reg-GWN resolves near-surface ambiguity in the fitted UDF by providing reliable surface localization and a solid-void indicator. Based on this representation, we introduce a high-precision spatial toolpath computation algorithm that iteratively projects points between optimized guidance-field level sets and reg-GWN gradient-magnitude ridges. Subsequently, we develop an efficient and robust coarse-to-fine collision checking scheme for motion planning: UDF-based rejection first identifies potential collisions, while time-varying reg-GWN verification accurately resolves collision pairs for both solid and shell components. We validate WildFab on diverse inputs, demonstrating successful computation from non-manifold parametric surfaces, voxelized topology-optimization results, implicit models, raw scanned point clouds, and non-watertight meshes. The fabrication results highlight our method's ability to advance end-to-end design-to-3DP workflows.

</details>


### [120] [Telligram: Text-Driven Calligram Generation via Diffusion-Guided Skeleton Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02511)
*Tianci Shi,Pengfei Xu*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: Telligram is a training-free, two-stage framework for generating word-level semantic calligrams directly from text prompts without requiring an input contour. It addresses the conflict between semantic shape formation and letter readability through a two-stage approach: Semantic Occupancy Prior Formation (using VSD with structured skeleton optimization) and Readability-Constrained Glyph Realization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing calligram methods are shape-conditioned and require an input contour, solving only the downstream letter layout problem. The text-only setting is challenging because semantic shape formation and letter readability strongly interfere with each other when optimized in a single stage - pushing the word toward a clear figure damages glyph structure, while preserving readable letters weakens the target shape.

Method: The proposed two-stage framework: (1) Semantic Occupancy Prior Formation uses Variational Score Distillation (VSD) with structured skeleton optimization and hierarchical gradient projection to produce a semantic occupancy prior. (2) Readability-Constrained Glyph Realization converts this occupancy prior into per-letter regions and reconstructs readable glyph layouts through lightweight geometric processing.

Result: The framework generates coherent and creative word-level semantic calligrams directly from text prompts, balancing semantic shape formation with letter readability.

Conclusion: Telligram successfully addresses the challenging task of text-only calligram generation by decoupling shape formation and letter readability into two stages. The training-free, low-tuning approach enables creative semantic calligram generation without requiring input contours or extensive training.

Abstract: Compact calligram generation aims to form a semantic shape while keeping letters recognizable. Most existing methods are shape-conditioned and mainly solve downstream letter layout inside a given contour. We study text-only calligram generation without an input contour. This setting is difficult because semantic shape formation and letter readability strongly interfere with each other when optimized in a single stage. Pushing the word toward a clear figure can easily damage glyph structure, while preserving readable letters can weaken the target shape. To address this difficulty, we present Telligram, a training-free, low-tuning, two-stage framework composed of Semantic Occupancy Prior Formation and Readability-Constrained Glyph Realization. The first stage uses Variational Score Distillation (VSD) with structured skeleton optimization and hierarchical gradient projection to produce a semantic occupancy prior. The second stage converts this occupancy prior into per-letter regions and reconstructs readable glyph layouts through lightweight geometric processing. The framework generates coherent and creative word-level semantic calligrams directly from text prompts.

</details>


### [121] [LightBridge: Feed-Forward Generative Relighting for 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02543)
*Hezhi Cao,Panhao Cheng,huangsheng du,Qibiao Li,Youcheng Cai,Ligang Liu*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: LightBridge is a feed-forward generative framework for single-pass relighting of 3D Gaussian Splatting assets, addressing the limitation that 3DGS has baked-in illumination and cannot be easily relit. It uses a novel Latent Bridge Relighting Diffusion model and Gaussian Propagation Transformer trained on a new Multi-Illumination Relighting Dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) achieves high-quality real-time novel view synthesis but produces assets with baked-in illumination that cannot be easily relit. Existing inverse rendering methods optimize simplified reflectance and illumination models per scene, limiting efficiency and quality. Generative approaches using diffusion models require additional per-scene optimization to bake edited appearances into the representation.

Method: LightBridge introduces three key innovations: (1) A large-scale Multi-Illumination Relighting Dataset with paired source and target observations of the same scenes; (2) Latent Bridge Relighting Diffusion that models relighting as source-to-target transport in latent space, enabling one-step extraction of 2D visual tokens without iterative diffusion sampling; (3) A Gaussian Propagation Transformer using point transformer with sparse image-to-point self-attention followed by point-to-image cross-attention to efficiently propagate relighting cues across complete 3DGS while avoiding full attention over all image and Gaussian tokens.

Result: Experiments validate the designs, demonstrating competitive relighting quality and efficient single-pass prediction of complete relit 3DGS assets without scene-specific optimization.

Conclusion: LightBridge enables feed-forward controllable relighting of complete 3DGS assets in a single pass, eliminating the need for per-scene optimization required by previous approaches. The method achieves a balance between relighting quality and computational efficiency through the proposed latent space transport and efficient Gaussian propagation mechanisms.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) achieves high-quality, real-time novel view synthesis, but the resulting assets have baked-in illumination and cannot be easily relit. Inverse rendering methods optimize simplified reflectance and illumination models for each scene, limiting efficiency and relighting quality. Recent generative approaches leverage large diffusion models for realistic lighting edits, but applying them to 3DGS typically requires an additional per-scene optimization stage to bake the edited appearance into the representation. We present LightBridge, a feed-forward generative framework for controllable relighting of complete 3DGS assets in a single pass. To enable feed-forward training, we construct a large-scale Multi-Illumination Relighting Dataset with paired source and target observations of the same scenes. Latent Bridge Relighting Diffusion models relighting as source-to-target transport in latent space, enabling one-step extraction of 2D visual tokens without iterative diffusion sampling. A Gaussian Propagation Transformer uses a point transformer with sparse image-to-point self-attention followed by point-to-image cross-attention to efficiently propagate these cues across the complete 3DGS, while avoiding full attention over all image and Gaussian tokens. Experiments validate these designs, demonstrating competitive relighting quality and efficient single-pass prediction of complete relit 3DGS assets without scene-specific optimization. The code and dataset will be made publicly available upon acceptance.

</details>


### [122] [Projective Affine Body Dynamics for Multibody Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02675)
*Zimeng Ye,Xiaowei He,Yuzhong Guo,Yin Yang,Chenfanfu Jiang,Hongan Wang*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: This paper presents a stable and highly parallel GPU algorithm for solving constrained multibody dynamics with nonlinear constraints within the affine body dynamics framework. The method reformulates multibody dynamics into a variational form using affine bodies connected with peridynamic bonds, enabling real-time simulation of complex interactions involving rigid and flexible bodies with contact, joint constraints, and friction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multibody systems are widely used in robotics, entertainment, and animation for modeling complex interactions among interconnected bodies. However, challenges arise from non-smooth contact handling, nonlinearity in flexible joints, and parallelization difficulties, limiting their practical applicability.

Method: The proposed method reformulates constrained multibody dynamics into a variational form, treating the system as affine bodies connected with peridynamic bonds. This provides a unified model for affine body dynamics, constraints, and contact within peridynamics. The semi-implicit successive substitution method is integrated to solve nonlinear optimization in a global-local iterative manner, eliminating the need to assemble a global Hessian.

Result: The method achieves efficient GPU implementation suitable for real-time simulation of intricate interactions involving both rigid and flexible bodies, encompassing contact, joint constraints, and friction.

Conclusion: The proposed GPU algorithm enables real-time simulation of complex multibody systems with nonlinear constraints while maintaining stability and high parallelization efficiency, making it highly suitable for applications in robotics, entertainment, and animation.

Abstract: Multibody systems have widespread applications in diverse fields such as robotics, entertainment, and animation. Their capability to model and simulate intricate interactions among interconnected bodies makes them invaluable in various domains. However, complexity arises with factors like non-smooth contact handling, nonlinearity in flexible joints, and parallelization challenges. We introduce a stable and highly parallel GPU algorithm within affine body dynamics for solving constrained multibody dynamics with nonlinear constraints.Our innovation involves reformulating constrained multibody dynamics into a variational form, treating the system as a set of affine bodies connected with peridynamic bonds. This formulation provides a unified model for affine body dynamics, constraints, and contact within the peridynamics framework.It also facilitates the integration of the semi-implicit successive substitution method to solve nonlinear optimization in a global-local iterative manner. The proposed method obviates the necessity of assembling a global Hessian, rendering it highly suitable for efficient implementation on GPUs.This allows real-time simulation of intricate interactions involving both rigid and flexible bodies, encompassing contact, joint constraints, and friction.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [123] [WMLLM: Self-Evolving Optimization Agents via Predict-Then-Act World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01608)
*Zhongzheng Li,Qingsong Ran,Shikun Feng,Nian Ran,Wenhao Li,Xiaoyuan Zhang,Yue Wang,Xiaoguang Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: WMLLM is a self-evolving optimization-agent framework that uses large language models as world models to predict promising optimization directions before candidate evaluation, combined with agentic multi-turn refinement, population-based search, and reinforcement learning to improve sample efficiency in black-box optimization tasks, particularly multi-objective molecular optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Black-box optimization problems are challenging due to large, weakly structured, and high-dimensional search spaces. Existing methods suffer from poor sample efficiency because they rely on direct candidate generation or trial-and-error refinement. The key insight is that large language models can predict outcomes of candidates with nontrivial accuracy due to their implicit knowledge, enabling identification of promising optimization directions before costly evaluations.

Method: WMLLM employs a predict-then-act world modeling approach: (1) The agent first uses LLM to predict promising directions, then acts to generate candidates. This is combined with (2) agentic multi-turn refinement, (3) population-based search, and (4) reinforcement learning to refine both the implicit world model and optimization strategy during search.

Result: Experiments on black-box optimization tasks, especially multi-objective molecular optimization, show that WMLLM improves sample efficiency and final optimization performance. On the multi-objective molecular optimization benchmark, WMLLM achieves state-of-the-art results under a limited evaluation budget.

Conclusion: The predict-then-act paradigm with LLM-based world modeling, when combined with agentic refinement and population-based search, effectively addresses the sample efficiency challenge in black-box optimization. WMLLM demonstrates superior performance on multi-objective molecular optimization benchmarks, establishing a new approach for LLM-driven optimization in high-dimensional, weakly structured search spaces.

Abstract: Black-box optimization problems remain challenging because of large, weakly structured, and high-dimensional search spaces. Existing methods often suffer from poor sample efficiency because they rely on direct candidate generation or trial-and-error refinement. A natural way to improve search efficiency is to use world modeling, which can help identify promising optimization directions before costly evaluation. Large language models can predict the outcomes of these candidates with nontrivial accuracy because of their implicit knowledge. Motivated by this observation, we propose WMLLM, a self-evolving optimization-agent framework based on predict-then-act world modeling. The agent first predicts promising directions and then acts to generate candidates. Combined with agentic multi-turn refinement, population-based search, and reinforcement learning, WMLLM refines both its implicit world model and its optimization strategy during search. Experiments on black-box optimization tasks, especially multi-objective molecular optimization, show that WMLLM improves sample efficiency and final optimization performance. On the multi-objective molecular optimization benchmark, WMLLM achieves state-of-the-art results under a limited evaluation budget.

</details>


### [124] [DiDrive: A Risk-Aware Hierarchical Diffusion Framework for Safe Offline Reinforcement Learning in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01609)
*Qisong Guo,Jingtang Chen,Zhilin Chen,Pei Xu,Mingjian Fu,Wenxi Liu,Yuanlong Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DiDrive is a distribution-guided offline diffusion framework for autonomous driving that addresses distribution shift, OOD action generation, and high-dimensional state redundancy through two synergistic components: Risk-Aware Hierarchical Diffusion (RHDif) for state space processing and 3DICE policy optimization for action space guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While diffusion models effectively capture multimodal behavioral priors for autonomous driving, offline RL policies remain susceptible to distribution shift, heavy-tailed risk signals, out-of-distribution (OOD) action generation, and high-dimensional state redundancy. These challenges limit the reliability of autonomous driving decision-making in complex scenarios.

Method: DiDrive proposes two synergistic components: (1) RHDif architecture uses a low-level risk-gated encoder and high-level contextual modulator to filter environmental redundancy and focus on safety-critical threats in the state space; (2) 3DICE policy optimization mitigates OOD overestimation and gradient oscillation through in-sample calibrated guidance, spatiotemporal optimization, and ensemble-based candidate ranking in the action space.

Result: On the CARLA benchmark, DiDrive outperforms baselines including IQL, CQL, and Diffusion-QL. In complex, high-density traffic scenarios with 60 vehicles, it achieves an 85% success rate and 4295.68 average reward.

Conclusion: DiDrive provides a robust pathway for safe autonomous driving decision-making by effectively addressing distribution shift, OOD action generation, and state redundancy challenges through hierarchical risk-aware encoding and calibrated policy optimization.

Abstract: While diffusion models effectively capture multimodal behavioral priors for autonomous driving, offline reinforcement learning (RL) policies remain susceptible to distribution shift, heavy-tailed risk signals, out-of-distribution (OOD) action generation, and high-dimensional state redundancy. To address these challenges, we propose DiDrive, a distribution-guided offline diffusion framework featuring two synergistic components: the Risk-Aware Hierarchical Diffusion (RHDif) architecture and the 3DICE policy optimization paradigm. In the state space, RHDif utilizes a low-level risk-gated encoder and a high-level contextual modulator to filter environmental redundancy and focus on safety-critical threats. In the action space, 3DICE mitigates OOD overestimation and gradient oscillation through in-sample calibrated guidance, spatiotemporal optimization, and ensemble-based candidate ranking. Evaluations on the CARLA benchmark demonstrate DiDrive's superiority over baselines like IQL, CQL, and Diffusion-QL, particularly in complex, high-density traffic scenarios with 60 vehicles, where it achieves an 85% success rate and a 4295.68 average reward, providing a robust pathway for safe autonomous driving decision-making.

</details>


### [125] [Prompt-Space Meta-Learning Does Not Transfer Across Users: A Frozen-LLM Negative Result](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01615)
*Liam Byrne,David Dylan,Orla Fitzgerald,Eoin Doyle,Ciara Nolan,Padraig Lynch,Sinead Gallagher*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether meta-learning in prompt space can effectively personalize frozen LLMs to individual users. The authors find that their method (Muse) fails to outperform simple baselines, attributing this to "meta-objective collapse" - the meta-validation objective is statistically invariant to genuine user-support correspondence, thus cannot be optimized into transferable adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field frames LLM personalization as a meta-learning problem in prompt space, but rarely tests whether the optimized meta-objective encodes transferable cross-user adaptation rather than generic instruction quality. This paper seeks to answer this fundamental question.

Method: Muse uses reflective prompt evolution to evolve a single shared adaptation prompt over a meta-train user population, then applies it zero-shot to held-out users. The authors employ matched controls: (1) seed prompt (un-evolved), (2) structure-broken control (meta-trains on mismatched user-support pairs), and (3) invariance oracle to isolate learning from confounds. Experiments on LaMP-2 (categorization) and LaMP-3 (rating) with 200 held-out users each.

Result: Muse does not significantly improve on its un-evolved seed prompt or on the structure-broken control. It is dominated by plain few-shot retrieval on the rating task (Delta MAE +0.175, p < 0.001). The meta-validation objective is statistically invariant to whether user-support correspondence is genuine (p=0.555 on LaMP-2, p=0.622 on LaMP-3).

Conclusion: The meta-objective collapses - it cannot be optimized into transferable adaptation and instead rewards instruction polish and validation overfitting. The seed-prompt, wrong-support, and invariance-oracle controls form a reusable protocol that separates learned adaptation from these confounds. The framing of LLM personalization as meta-learning in prompt space may be fundamentally flawed.

Abstract: Personalizing a frozen large language model (LLM) to individual users is often framed as a meta-learning problem in prompt space: each user is a task, and one seeks a shared natural-language adaptation policy that, given a handful of the user's labeled interactions, configures the frozen model for that user. The framing is attractive because it is backbone-agnostic and reuses the machinery of prompt optimization, yet the field rarely tests whether the optimized meta-objective encodes transferable cross-user adaptation rather than generic instruction quality. We study this question with Muse (Meta-learned User-adaptation via Shared Evolution), which evolves a single shared adaptation prompt over a meta-train user population by reflective prompt evolution, freezes it, and applies it zero-shot to held-out users; matched controls isolate learning from confounds of phrasing and selection. On two standard personalization benchmarks (LaMP-2 categorization and LaMP-3 rating) over 200 held-out users each, Muse does not significantly improve on its own un-evolved seed prompt or on a structure-broken control that meta-trains on mismatched user-support pairs, and is dominated by plain few-shot retrieval on the rating task (Delta MAE +0.175, p < 0.001). We attribute these outcomes to a single mechanism, meta-objective collapse: the meta-validation objective is statistically invariant to whether the user-support correspondence is genuine (p=0.555 on LaMP-2, p=0.622 on LaMP-3), so it cannot be optimized into transferable adaptation and instead rewards instruction polish and validation overfitting. The seed-prompt, wrong-support, and invariance-oracle controls form a reusable protocol that separates learned adaptation from these confounds.

</details>


### [126] [Efficient Context-Limited Telescope Bibliography Classification for the WASP-2025 Shared Task Using SciBERT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01647)
*Madhusudhana Naidu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a SciBERT-based automatic classification system for telescope bibliographies, categorizing scientific papers into four categories (science, instrumentation, mention, not telescope), achieving a macro F1 score of 0.89 and topping the WASP-2025 leaderboard despite strict 512-token constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The creation of telescope bibliographies is currently manual and resource-intensive, yet it is crucial for assessing scientific impact of observatories and ensuring reproducibility in astronomy. The motivation is to automate this classification process efficiently.

Method: The approach uses SciBERT (a domain-specific BERT model) for automatic classification of scientific papers into four categories. The method addresses the challenge of strict context-length constraints (max 512 tokens) and limited compute resources. The study analyzes the effect of truncation and compares different strategies (truncation, chunking, long-context models).

Result: The approach achieved a macro F1 score of 0.89, ranking at the top of the WASP-2025 leaderboard. Even with half the samples exceeding the token limit, SciBERT's domain alignment enables robust classification, demonstrating the model's effectiveness despite text truncation.

Conclusion: SciBERT's domain alignment enables robust classification for telescope bibliography curation, even under challenging constraints. The study provides insights into trade-offs between truncation, chunking, and long-context models, establishing an efficiency frontier for scientific text curation in astronomy.

Abstract: The creation of telescope bibliographies is a crucial part of assessing the scientific impact of observatories and ensuring reproducibility in astronomy. This task involves identifying, categorizing, and linking scientific publications that reference or use specific telescopes. However, this process remains largely manual and resource intensive. In this work, we present an efficient SciBERT-based approach for automatic classification of scientific papers into four categories - science, instrumentation, mention, and not telescope. Despite strict context-length constraints (maximum 512 tokens) and limited compute resources, our approach achieved a macro F1 score of 0.89, ranking at the top of the WASP-2025 leaderboard. We analyze the effect of truncation and show that even with half the samples exceeding the token limit, SciBERT's domain alignment enables robust classification. We discuss trade-offs between truncation, chunking, and long-context models, providing insights into the efficiency frontier for scientific text curation.

</details>


### [127] [CliffRank: A Dual-Branch Framework for Activity-Cliff Ranking Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01673)
*Kewei Li,Rongying Zhang,Peiyu Yang,Zhongjian Wang,Qiuchen Zhao,Lan Huang,Fengfeng Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CliffRank combines absolute-activity regression with ranking-consistency learning using dual predictors with MSE, thresholded listwise loss, and Pairwise Preference Consistency (PPC) to improve activity-cliff ranking in antimicrobial peptides and small molecules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Activity-cliff ranking remains challenging because minor structural changes can cause large activity differences, while high-quality mechanistic data is limited. This work aims to use available activity labels more effectively to improve ranking performance.

Method: CliffRank trains two parallel predictors with three loss components: mean squared error for absolute activity, a thresholded listwise loss for ranking, and Pairwise Preference Consistency (PPC) to align relative ordering in preference-probability space. The method was tested on three antimicrobial peptide datasets (with ESM2-t12 embeddings) and three small-molecule datasets (with PNA and MolCLR-GIN embeddings), with experiments on asymmetric initialization and delayed PPC activation.

Result: On antimicrobial peptides, CliffRank with ESM2-t12 achieved highest mean Spearman correlation (0.5393) and mean Recall@50 (21.4). On small molecules, CliffRank with PNA achieved highest mean Spearman correlation (0.6890) and matched ACANet-PNA's Recall@50 (30.4). Asymmetric initialization improved MolCLR-GIN averages; delayed PPC improved some metrics for PNA without pretrained weights, but no schedule was optimal for both metrics.

Conclusion: CliffRank effectively improves activity-cliff ranking but PPC has practical limits. Future work should evaluate more targets, develop adaptive PPC schedules, and incorporate protein or membrane context when available.

Abstract: Activity-cliff ranking remains difficult because local structural changes can cause large activity differences, while high-quality data that resolve the underlying mechanisms remain limited. To use available activity labels more effectively, we combine absolute-activity regression with ranking-consistency learning. CliffRank trains two parallel predictors with mean squared error, a thresholded listwise loss, and Pairwise Preference Consistency (PPC), which aligns relative ordering in the preference-probability space. On three antimicrobial peptide datasets, CliffRank with ESM2-t12 achieved the highest mean Spearman correlation of 0.5393 and mean Recall@50 of 21.4, although the leading method varied across individual datasets. On three small-molecule datasets, CliffRank with PNA, where PPC was activated after 120 epochs, achieved the highest mean Spearman correlation of 0.6890, while its mean Recall@50 of 30.4 matched that of ACANet-PNA. The PPC results also define its practical limits. Asymmetric initialization improved the MolCLR-GIN averages but did not improve every target. For PNA without pretrained weights, delayed PPC improved selected metrics, but no schedule was best for both mean Spearman correlation and mean Recall@50. Future work should evaluate more targets and antimicrobial peptide systems, develop adaptive PPC schedules, and incorporate protein or membrane context when available.

</details>


### [128] [Sim2Signal: Sim-to-Real Benchmarks for Traffic Signal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01676)
*Ferdous Al Rafi,Susrik Mukherjee,Latika Liladhar Dekate,Jennifer Yawa Lavoe,Huaiyuan Yao,Shlok Mohanty,Longchao Da,Xuesong Zhou,Hua Wei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sim2Signal is a benchmark that decomposes the Sim-to-Real gap in RL-based traffic signal control into four MDP components (observation, action, transition, and reward gaps), and evaluates 18 mitigation methods across 33 gap settings and 10 real-world-calibrated networks. The study finds that direct transfer consistently degrades performance, but mitigation effectiveness is highly context-dependent rather than predictable from gap severity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL achieves strong performance for traffic signal control in simulation but fails when deployed in the real world (Sim-to-Real gap). The relative impact of different gap sources and the reliability of existing mitigation methods remain poorly understood. The field lacks a standard benchmark to systematically measure this gap and evaluate mitigation approaches.

Method: The authors present Sim2Signal, a benchmark that decomposes the Sim-to-Real gap into observation, action, transition, and reward gaps corresponding to mismatches in the four components of the underlying MDP. Each gap is induced in isolation under a shared protocol. The benchmark evaluates 18 mitigation methods on 2 base controllers, across 33 gap settings and 10 calibrated networks built from 5 real-world locations.

Result: Direct transfer consistently degrades performance across all four gap sources. However, the severity of degradation does not predict the effectiveness of mitigation methods. Mitigation effectiveness depends strongly on the network and gap setting: outside the action gap, a method that helps in one case may fail in another. The most effective methods generally estimate what the gap changes, rather than making the policy insensitive through domain randomization or invariant representations.

Conclusion: The Sim-to-Real gap in traffic signal control is complex and context-dependent. No single mitigation method works universally across different gap sources and network configurations. The most promising approaches are those that explicitly estimate and compensate for the gap changes, rather than relying on policy robustness through domain randomization or invariant representations. This work provides a valuable benchmark for systematically studying Sim-to-Real transfer in traffic signal control.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning achieves strong traffic signal control performance in simulation, yet policies trained in simulators often fail once deployed in the real world, a failure known as the Sim-to-Real gap. When RL is applied to traffic signal control, this gap arises from several sources: sensing, action execution, traffic dynamics, and the control objective. Their relative impact and the reliability of existing Sim-to-Real mitigation methods remain insufficiently understood, and the field lacks a standard benchmark for systematically measuring the gap and evaluating mitigation methods. We present Sim2Signal, a benchmark that decomposes the Sim-to-Real gap into observation, action, transition, and reward gaps, corresponding to mismatches in the four components of the underlying MDP, and induces each gap in isolation under a shared protocol. We evaluate 18 mitigation methods on 2 base controllers, across 33 gap settings and 10 calibrated networks built from 5 real-world locations. We find that direct transfer consistently degrades performance across all four gap sources, but the severity of the degradation does not predict the effectiveness of mitigation. Instead, mitigation effectiveness depends strongly on the network and gap setting: outside the action gap, a method that helps in one case may fail in another. The most effective methods generally estimate what the gap changes, rather than make the policy insensitive through domain randomization or invariant representations. Our code is available at https://github.com/Red-Pheonix/Sim2RealTSCBenchMark

</details>


### [129] [A Survey on Self-Improving Test-Time Intelligence: Feedback-Driven Adapting, Learning, and Scaling at Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01679)
*Shuaicheng Niu,Guohao Chen,Yaofo Chen,Zhiquan Wen,Jinwu Hu,Zeshuai Deng,Deyu Chen,Shuhai Zhang,Renjie Chen,Zihao Lian,Shoukai Xu,Gang Dai,Yunbei Zhang,Wei Luo,Yifan Zhang,Mingkui Tan,Cheng Deng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This survey presents a unified framework called "feedback-driven Test-Time Intelligence (TTI)" to understand how AI systems can improve their behavior during deployment. The paper connects two previously separate research directions: methods that modify model state using test-time signals, and methods that improve predictions through extra inference-time resources. The survey covers test-time adaptation, test-time learning, and test-time scaling, examining their distinctions and overlaps in hybrid systems across vision, language, multimodal learning, generative models, robotics, and healthcare domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The ability of AI systems to improve during deployment is becoming increasingly important as inference moves beyond static execution of fixed trained models. Two main research directions have evolved separately: (1) methods that modify model state using test-time signals, and (2) methods that improve predictions through extra inference-time resources like sampling and tool use. These communities use different terminology, making connections harder to see. This fragmentation lacks a unified conceptual foundation for studying inference-time self-improvement.

Method: The paper proposes feedback-driven Test-Time Intelligence (TTI) as a unified perspective to connect previously fragmented ideas. It reviews major methodological paradigms, representative applications, and open challenges across multiple domains including vision, language, multimodal learning, generative models, robotics, and healthcare. The framework highlights both the distinctions and growing overlaps between test-time adaptation, test-time learning, and test-time scaling in hybrid systems.

Result: The survey provides a coherent foundation and research roadmap for studying self-improving AI systems at test time. It identifies three main directions under the TTI framework: (1) test-time adaptation - modifying model behavior based on test-time signals, (2) test-time learning - learning new capabilities at inference time, and (3) test-time scaling - leveraging additional computational resources during inference. The paper reveals growing overlap between these directions in hybrid systems.

Conclusion: This unified TTI framework helps connect previously fragmented ideas and provides a clearer conceptual foundation for understanding and studying inference-time self-improvement. The survey offers a research roadmap for future work in this area across diverse application domains.

Abstract: The ability of AI systems to improve their behavior during deployment is becoming increasingly important. As inference moves beyond the static execution of a fixed trained model, a growing body of work studies how models can refine their behavior on the fly by exploiting test-time information and additional computation. These developments have largely evolved along two directions: methods that modify the model's state using test-time signals, and methods that improve predictions through extra inference-time resources such as more sampling and tool use. However, these directions are often studied in separate communities with different terminology, making their connections harder to see. In this survey, we present feedback-driven Test-Time Intelligence (TTI) as a unified perspective for understanding such deployment-time improvement. We use this view to relate test-time adaptation, test-time learning, and test-time scaling, highlighting both their distinctions and their growing overlap in hybrid systems. This unified framework helps connect previously fragmented ideas and provides a clearer conceptual foundation for studying inference-time self-improvement. We review major methodological paradigms, representative applications, and open challenges across vision, language, multimodal learning, generative models, robotics, and healthcare. Our goal is to provide a coherent foundation and research roadmap for the study of self-improving AI systems at test time.

</details>


### [130] [Reinforcement Learning and Rule-Based Peer-to-Peer Pricing in Residential PV-BES Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01680)
*Pablo Benalcazar,Maciej Kalka,Wilian Guamán,Jacek Kamiński*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares rule-based and reinforcement learning-based pricing mechanisms for peer-to-peer electricity trading in residential photovoltaic communities. Rule-based methods (bill-sharing, mid-market rate, SDR pricing) outperform the best Deep Q-Network RL policy in the base PV-only configuration. However, with battery storage, RL savings increase from EUR 734.23 to EUR 978.52. SDR-shaped pricing consistently outperforms multiplier-based pricing across all learning-based modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate whether learning-based pricing mechanisms can outperform traditional rule-based approaches for P2P electricity trading in residential PV communities, and to assess the impact of battery energy storage on trading outcomes.

Method: The study implemented rule-based benchmarks (bill-sharing, mid-market rate, supply-demand-ratio pricing) and a Deep Q-Network RL formulation. RL was evaluated under multiplier-based and learnable SDR-shaped pricing, with a fixed-parameter SDR as control. Performance was assessed using community savings and complementary financial/operational indicators.

Result: In the base PV-only configuration, rule-based benchmarks outperform the best RL policy. With battery storage, community savings under the best RL policy increase from EUR 734.23 to EUR 978.52. SDR-shaped pricing consistently outperforms multiplier-based parameterization across learning-based modes and configurations. The distribution of benefits remains heterogeneous across households.

Conclusion: Rule-based pricing remains highly competitive compared to learning-based approaches. Battery storage substantially improves learning-based outcomes. The study suggests that simpler rule-based mechanisms may be preferable where direct comparison is possible, while RL with storage can achieve significant improvements in specific scenarios.

Abstract: This paper compares rule-based and learning-based pricing mechanisms for peer-to-peer (P2P) electricity trading in residential photovoltaic communities. The rule-based benchmarks comprise bill-sharing as an ex post allocation mechanism, the mid-market rate, and supply-demand-ratio pricing. The reinforcement-learning (RL) formulation is implemented through a Deep Q-Network and evaluated under multiplier-based and learnable SDR-shaped pricing, with a fixed-parameter SDR variant as a non-learning control. Performance is assessed through community savings together with complementary financial and operational indicators. In the base PV-only configuration, the rule-based benchmarks outperform the best RL policy. With battery energy storage, evaluated for the RL policies only, community savings under the best RL policy increase from EUR 734.23 to EUR 978.52. Across the learning-based modes and in both configurations, SDR-shaped pricing outperforms the multiplier-based parameterization considered. The results indicate that rule-based pricing remains highly competitive wherever the two families are compared directly, and that storage substantially improves the learning-based outcomes under this accounting, while the distribution of benefits remains heterogeneous across households.

</details>


### [131] [Median-of-Means as an Extremal Convex Estimator and a Nonconvex Route to the Trimmed Oracle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01689)
*Angshul Majumdar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper develops a family of block-Lp estimators for robust learning with heavy-tailed and adversarially corrupted data, revisiting median-of-means estimation from a deterministic optimization perspective. It establishes fundamental limits of convex block M-estimators and shows that nonconvex block-Lp methods can achieve near-optimal robustness constants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address robust learning under heavy-tailed and adversarially corrupted data by developing a unified optimization-based framework for analyzing block-Lp estimators, and to understand the fundamental limits of convex versus nonconvex approaches in achieving optimal robustness.

Method: The authors employ a deterministic optimization viewpoint, analyzing block contamination models with at least 1-ε fraction of good blocks. They derive finite-sample robustness bounds for global minimizers of nonconvex block-Lp objectives (with p ∈ [0,1]) and examine the landscape properties of these objectives.

Result: Key results include: (1) Convex block M-estimators have worst-case robustness constant at least 1/(1-2ε), matching classical median-of-means bounds; (2) The trimmed-block oracle constant 1/(1-ε) cannot be achieved within the convex class; (3) Nonconvex block-Lp estimators continuously approach the oracle constant as p→0; (4) For small p, global minimizers coincide with oracle under mild separation; (5) The block-Lp objectives have benign landscape with all local minima close to truth; (6) Sub-Gaussian deviation bounds under finite 2+δ moments and high-dimensional extensions to robust mean estimation and sparse regression.

Conclusion: Nonconvex block-Lp methods provide a principled approach to achieving near-optimal robustness in heavy-tailed and adversarial settings, with theoretical guarantees that continuously interpolate between convex and oracle estimators as the parameter p varies.

Abstract: We revisit median-of-means estimation from a deterministic optimization viewpoint and develop a family of block-Lp estimators for robust learning with heavy-tailed and adversarially corrupted data. In a block contamination model with at least a fraction 1 minus epsilon of good blocks, we first show that every convex block M-estimator has worst-case robustness constant at least 1 divided by 1 minus 2 epsilon. This matches the classical median-of-means bound and proves that the trimmed-block oracle constant 1 divided by 1 minus epsilon cannot be attained within the convex class. We then introduce a nonconvex block-Lp family for p between 0 and 1 and derive finite-sample deterministic robustness bounds for all global minimizers. As p decreases from 1 toward 0, these bounds continuously approach the trimmed-block oracle constant. For sufficiently small p, the global minimizers coincide with those of the oracle under a mild separation condition. We also show that the block-Lp objectives have a benign landscape, with all local minima remaining close to the truth and no bad basins. Combining these results with block-level concentration yields sub-Gaussian deviation bounds under finite 2 plus delta moments and high-dimensional extensions to robust mean estimation and sparse regression.

</details>


### [132] [Tri-Band Channel Measurement-Enabled Multi-Layer Digital Twin for Terahertz Wireless Data Centers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01699)
*Mingjie Zhu,Ziming Yu,Guangjian Wang,Chong Han*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A measurement-driven multi-layer digital twin (DT) framework is proposed for THz wireless data centers, integrating physical, channel, evaluation, and manipulation layers to enable efficient planning and real-time optimization of terahertz communications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid growth of AI computing has created increasing demands for flexible and high-capacity data-center interconnections. THz communication offers ultra-wide bandwidth and high spatial reuse capability, making it promising for future wireless data centers. However, effective planning and optimization tools are needed.

Method: The framework proceeds in layers: (1) Channel measurements at 140, 220, and 300 GHz to characterize frequency-dependent propagation; (2) A measurement-calibrated physical twin optimizing geometry, material, antenna, and hybrid propagation models; (3) A line-of-sight (LoS)-aware implicit neural field AI channel twin for efficient channel reconstruction; (4) A system-level evaluation layer for coverage and interference analysis.

Result: The proposed AI twin achieves lower power reconstruction error than existing neural-field baselines while maintaining real-time inference capability. Ceiling-mounted AP deployment achieves over 90% coverage under a 10 dB SINR threshold.

Conclusion: The DT framework effectively enables THz wireless data-center planning and optimization, demonstrating the value of measurement-driven digital twins for next-generation communication infrastructure.

Abstract: The rapid growth of AI computing has driven increasing demands for flexible and high-capacity data-center interconnections. Owing to its ultra-wide bandwidth and high spatial reuse capability, terahertz (THz) communication has emerged as a promising solution for future wireless data centers, while digital twins (DTs) enable efficient wireless planning and real-time optimization. In this work, a measurement-driven multi-layer DT framework is proposed for THz wireless data centers, where the physical, channel, evaluation, and manipulation layers are progressively constructed from bottom to top. First, extensive channel measurements are conducted at 140, 220, and 300 GHz to characterize frequency-dependent propagation behaviors. Based on the tri-band measurements, a measurement-calibrated physical twin is established by jointly optimizing the geometry, material, antenna, and hybrid propagation models. On top of the physical twin, a line-of-sight (LoS)-aware implicit neural field is developed to construct an AI channel twin for efficient channel reconstruction. The proposed AI twin learns location-dependent channel statistics from the calibrated twin, enabling real-time prediction of received power and LoS probability. Building upon the reconstructed channel field, a system-level evaluation layer is derived to analyze coverage and interference for both AP-to-rack and rack-to-rack communications. Experimental results show that the proposed AI twin achieves lower power reconstruction error than existing neural-field baselines while maintaining real-time inference capability. Moreover, the ceiling-mounted AP deployment achieves over 90% coverage under a 10 dB signal-to-interference-plus-noise ratio (SINR) threshold, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed DT framework for THz wireless data-center planning and optimization.

</details>


### [133] [Generative Diffusion Surrogates with Analytical Variance Schedule](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01705)
*Patrick Reichherzer,Gianluca Gregori,David N. Hosking,Subir Sarkar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a method to calibrate generative diffusion models for stochastic transport by using the time derivative of known variance (mean-square displacement) as the forward noising rate, creating a "physical clock" for the diffusion process. This approach enables calibrated emulation and likelihood-based inference without requiring intermediate-time physical data or heuristic noise schedule tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stochastic transport systems require probabilistic, time-resolved surrogates that can represent non-Gaussian distributional structure. However, generative diffusion models typically use heuristically chosen noise schedules unsuitable for physical systems where variance (mean-square displacement) is often known from macroscopic theory or empirical scaling. The authors seek to embed this physical variance information into the diffusion process.

Method: The authors prescribe the forward noising rate as the time derivative of the known variance path, which turns generative time into a calibrated transport clock. The variance path is enforced by construction, while the learned score field represents how non-Gaussian structure from entrance data is smoothed along that path, requiring no intermediate-time physical transport data.

Result: For ballistic-to-diffusive transport in turbulent plasmas, the surrogate matches test-particle distributions, reproduces the laboratory-measured variance scale, and tracks the simulated kurtosis evolution without any schedule tuning. The method enables both calibrated emulation and likelihood-based inference.

Conclusion: This work demonstrates that diffusion models can be adapted for physical transport problems by using known variance evolution as a calibration clock. The approach successfully captures non-Gaussian distributional structure while enforcing physically meaningful variance dynamics, providing a principled framework for surrogate modeling in stochastic transport without requiring heuristic parameter tuning or intermediate-time data.

Abstract: Stochastic transport describes physical systems in which an initially structured distribution spreads under unresolved forcing, scattering, or heterogeneous media. Useful surrogates for such systems should be probabilistic, time-resolved, and able to represent non-Gaussian distributional structure. Generative diffusion models, which corrupt data with Gaussian noise and learn a reverse flow back to structured states, have these properties. Their noise schedules, however, are usually chosen heuristically: image and audio generation---the canonical use cases---provide no physical clock. In transport, by contrast, the variance, or mean-square displacement, is often known from macroscopic theory or empirical scaling even when the full distribution is not. Here we prescribe the forward noising rate as the time derivative of this variance, turning generative time into a calibrated transport clock. The variance path is enforced by construction, while the learned score field represents how non-Gaussian structure inherited from entrance data is smoothed along that path, requiring no intermediate-time physical transport data. For ballistic-to-diffusive transport in turbulent plasmas, the surrogate matches test-particle distributions, reproduces the laboratory-measured variance scale, and tracks the simulated kurtosis evolution without schedule tuning, enabling calibrated emulation and likelihood-based inference.

</details>


### [134] [RecKAN: Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks with a Learnable Recursive Polynomial Basis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01729)
*Amirhosein Azarpour*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RecKAN introduces a novel KAN variant where the basis functions are defined by a learnable second-order polynomial recurrence, allowing the network to adapt its own basis rather than relying on fixed polynomial families. This approach recovers classical polynomial families as special cases while enabling the learned basis to exceed them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KAN variants use fixed basis functions (B-splines, Chebyshev, wavelets, Jacobi polynomials) and only learn combination weights. This work aims to make the basis itself learnable, enabling the network to discover task-specific basis functions beyond classical choices.

Method: RecKAN defines basis functions via a second-order polynomial recurrence R_{n+1}(x) = (ax²+bx+c)R_n(x) + (dx+e)R_{n-1}(x), with five learnable coefficients (a,b,c,d,e) jointly trained with network weights. The theoretical analysis shows this recurrence recovers classical families (Chebyshev, Fibonacci, Pell, Jacobsthal) and that degree grows linearly in n for the sub-family containing all classical families.

Result: RecKAN outperforms three parameter-matched KAN baselines (Chebyshev, Jacobi, spline) on all classification tasks (image, text, biomedical time series) and achieves lowest MSE on ETTh1 forecasting benchmark. As a classifier head with CNN backbone, it outperforms standard MLP heads on Fashion MNIST, CIFAR-10, and SVHN. On synthetic function fitting, it tracks oscillatory targets that MLP underfits. The learned coefficients are interpretable, particularly moving away from linear degree growth when local structure is needed.

Conclusion: Learning the basis itself rather than just combination weights enables KANs to adapt to task requirements. The learned recurrence coefficients provide interpretability, and the approach demonstrates consistent improvements across diverse benchmarks, suggesting the learned basis can move beyond any fixed classical polynomial family.

Abstract: Kolmogorov--Arnold Networks (KANs) replace the fixed scalar weights of a standard network with learnable univariate functions on each edge, but existing variants still fix the \emph{basis} that those functions are built from: B-splines, Chebyshev polynomials, wavelets, or Jacobi polynomials, and learn only the combination weights over it. We introduce RecKAN, which instead defines the basis itself by a second order polynomial recurrence, $R_{n+1}(x) = (ax^2+bx+c)R_n(x) + (dx+e)R_{n-1}(x)$, whose five coefficients are learned jointly with the network. We show this recurrence recovers several classical polynomial families including both kinds of Chebyshev polynomials, Fibonacci, Pell, and Jacobsthal polynomials as special cases, and prove that its degree grows linearly in $n$ exactly on the sub-family containing all of them, giving a concrete sense in which the learned basis can move beyond any fixed classical choice. Across multiple benchmark datasets spanning image, text, biomedical time series classification, and time series forecasting, RecKAN outperforms three parameter-matched KAN baselines (Chebyshev, Jacobi, and spline based) on all classification tasks and achieves the lowest MSE on the ETTh1 forecasting benchmark. Additionally, when used as a classifier head with a convolutional backbone, RecKAN achieves higher accuracy than standard MLP heads on Fashion MNIST, CIFAR-10, and SVHN. On a synthetic function fitting benchmark it tracks a sharply oscillatory target that a parameter comparable MLP under fits. We further show that the learned recurrence coefficients are interpretable: on the task requiring the most local structure, training moves the basis away from the linear degree growth regime that contains every classical family we identify, consistent with our theoretical analysis of what that structural shift enables.

</details>


### [135] [CAT-Flow: Curvature-Adaptive sTeps for Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01746)
*Qinchan Li,Pedro Cisneros-Velarde,Keru Fu,Samuel Antunes Miranda,Sharan Vaswani,Hao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the efficiency bottleneck in Flow Matching generative models by proposing two training-free algorithms (CAT-OV and CAT-OT) that adapt step-sizes at inference time based on the connection between Flow Matching sampling and gradient flow. These methods reduce generation steps by up to 40% while maintaining image quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow Matching powers state-of-the-art generative models (FLUX, Stable Diffusion 3.5), but requires 20-30 sampling steps due to its ODE-based process. The quality of generated samples is highly sensitive to step-size choices, creating a fundamental efficiency bottleneck. Current models need many steps for good quality, limiting their practical deployment.

Method: The paper proposes two lightweight, training-free algorithms: CAT-OV and CAT-OT. These adapt step-sizes at inference time by leveraging a novel connection between Flow Matching sampling and gradient flow. CAT-OT estimates curvature over time via finite-difference approximation of the time-derivative of the vector field. CAT-OV approximates curvature over the state space via a gradient of the vector field. Both methods avoid requiring additional neural function evaluations, making them computationally efficient.

Result: Both methods have truncation error bounds of constant order under suitable conditions. Empirically, CAT-OV and CAT-OT outperform existing step-size heuristics in image quality metrics across four text-to-image Flow Matching models, reducing the number of generation steps required to reach comparable quality by up to 40%.

Conclusion: The proposed CAT-OV and CAT-OT algorithms provide an effective solution to the efficiency bottleneck in Flow Matching. By adaptively adjusting step-sizes without additional neural function evaluations, they achieve significant reductions (up to 40%) in generation steps while maintaining quality, offering practical improvements for deployment of Flow Matching-based generative models.

Abstract: Flow Matching has emerged as a leading framework for generative modeling, powering state-of-the-art systems such as FLUX and Stable Diffusion 3.5. However, the iterative nature of its ODE-based sampling process creates a fundamental efficiency bottleneck: the quality of generated samples is highly sensitive to the choice of step-sizes, and current models typically require 20 to 30 steps for good quality. In this work, we propose two lightweight, training-free algorithms, CAT-OV and CAT-OT that adapt step-sizes at inference time based on a novel connection between Flow Matching sampling and gradient flow. Our algorithms are computed efficiently by not requiring additional neural function evaluations. Specifically, CAT-OT estimates curvature over time via a finite-difference approximation of the time-derivative of the vector field, while CAT-OV approximates curvature over the state space via a gradient of the vector field. Under suitable conditions, both methods have truncation error bounds of constant order. Empirically, CAT-OV and CAT-OT outperform existing step-size heuristics in image quality metrics across four text- to-image Flow Matching models, reducing the number of generation steps required to reach comparable quality by up to 40%.

</details>


### [136] [A Study of Conditional Diffusion Models for Open-Loop Control under Dry Friction and Stiction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01756)
*Eric Aislan Antonelo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes Action Diffusion, a conditional diffusion model for generating action sequences as open-loop proposals for a point-mass system with dry friction and stiction. The method uses a 1D U-Net to generate bounded control sequences conditioned on initial and target states, outperforming random shooting and CEM in low-sample regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The key challenge is that motion only starts when the applied input exceeds a static-friction threshold (stiction), making effective controls occupy a small and temporally structured subset of the action-sequence space. This requires generating temporally coherent control sequences that can overcome stiction.

Method: A compact conditional 1D U-Net generates bounded control sequences conditioned on initial and target states. The model is trained on a structured dataset prior and serves as an open-loop proposal distribution for planning.

Result: Action Diffusion reduces terminal error and stuck steps compared to uniform random shooting, structured dataset prior random shooting, and the Cross-Entropy Method (CEM), with particularly strong improvements in low-sample regimes.

Conclusion: Conditional diffusion provides an effective mechanism for generating temporally coherent control sequences that overcome stiction by conditioning and recombining structured control primitives from the training prior for state-to-state open-loop control.

Abstract: Diffusion models have recently emerged as expressive generative priors for planning and control. This paper studies Action Diffusion, an action-sequence diffusion formulation used as an open-loop proposal distribution for a point-mass system with dry friction and stiction. In this benchmark, motion starts only when the applied input exceeds a static-friction threshold, so effective controls occupy a small and temporally structured subset of the action-sequence space. A compact conditional 1D U-Net generates bounded control sequences conditioned on initial and target states. We compare it with uniform random shooting, random shooting from the same structured dataset prior, and the Cross-Entropy Method (CEM). Results show that Action Diffusion reduces terminal error and stuck steps, especially in low-sample regimes. These results indicate that conditional diffusion provides an effective mechanism for generating temporally coherent control sequences that overcome stiction by conditioning and recombining structured control primitives from the training prior for state-to-state open-loop control.

</details>


### [137] [Post-Training Language Models for Gold-Medal Performance in Coding Competitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02849)
*Aleksander Ficek,Sean Narenthiran,Mehrzad Samadi,Somshubra Majumdar,Boris Ginsburg*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents an end-to-end pipeline for training large language models to solve competitive programming problems, achieving unprecedented results on IOI 2025 and 2026. The system combines problem curation, synthetic reasoning traces, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning, along with a novel test-time compute strategy called GenCorrect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Competitive programming (IOI/ICPC) represents a key benchmark for testing LLM reasoning capabilities. The goal was to develop an AI system that can exceed human performance on these challenging international programming competitions.

Method: The pipeline combines: (1) Large-scale problem curation with 22,000 problems, (2) Synthetic reasoning traces, (3) Supervised fine-tuning (SFT), (4) Reinforcement learning (RL), and (5) GenCorrect - a feedback-driven iterative solution generation, evaluation, and refinement strategy at test-time.

Result: On IOI 2025: Nano-CC improved from 130 to 291 points after post-training, and to 468 with GenCorrect, exceeding the gold threshold of 438.3. Ultra-CC reached 502 points. On IOI 2026 prospective evaluation: the Ultra-CC system scored 535.4 out of 600, exceeding both the gold threshold (361.12) and the top human score (498.27).

Conclusion: This is the first AI system to outscore the highest-scoring human contestant on an IOI problem set, demonstrating that specialized training pipelines and test-time compute strategies can enable LLMs to surpass human experts in competitive programming.

Abstract: Competitive programming has become a key test of large language model reasoning, with international competitions such as IOI and ICPC representing its most challenging settings. We present an end-to-end specialization pipeline combining large-scale problem curation, synthetic reasoning traces, supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and reinforcement learning (RL). Using 22,000 curated problems, we train Nemotron-3-Nano-CC (30B-A3B) with SFT and RL and Nemotron-3-Ultra-CC (550B-A55B) with SFT alone. We further introduce GenCorrect, a feedback-driven test-time compute strategy that iteratively generates, evaluates, and refines diverse solutions. On IOI 2025, Nano-CC improves from 130 points to 291 after post-training and to 468 with GenCorrect, exceeding the gold threshold of 438.3 while Ultra-CC reaches 502. Guided by these results, we develop a competition-specific Ultra-CC system and evaluate it prospectively during IOI 2026. Under the same time, internet-access, and submission constraints as human contestants, it scores 535.4 out of 600, exceeding both the gold threshold of 361.12 and the top human score of 498.27. To our knowledge, this is the first AI system to outscore the highest-scoring human contestant on an IOI problem set.

</details>


### [138] [Toward Explainable and Policy-Aware AI for Carbon Credit Price Prediction: A Research Framework for Emerging Carbon Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01765)
*Summaiya Unnisa Begum,Mohammed Nadeem Ullah,Mohammed Abdul Ghani Khan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes EPA-CarbonNet, a six-layer deep learning architecture that fuses carbon market time series with policy text via cross-attention, aiming to improve carbon credit price prediction with calibrated intervals and policy-attributed explanations. Testing on 11 years of S&P carbon index data yields mostly negative results: a random walk outperforms the model on 5-day RMSE (0.0365 vs 0.0475), SHAP rankings show moderate stability (rho=0.54), and policy attention fails to align with documented regulatory events. However, the model achieves 58.6% directional accuracy, outperforming all baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current carbon market research clusters on EU and Chinese schemes, relies on sentiment scores from regulatory text, and reports accuracy without proper calibration or explanation stability. The authors identify ten recurring gaps in the literature and propose an impact-feasibility matrix to address these limitations.

Method: The authors propose EPA-CarbonNet, a six-layer architecture that fuses market time series with policy text through cross-attention mechanisms. The model includes calibrated prediction intervals and policy-attributed explanations. They test it on eleven years of daily S&P carbon index data using SHAP for feature importance analysis.

Result: The findings are largely negative: (1) Random walk beats the model on five-day RMSE (0.0365 vs 0.0475); (2) SHAP rankings show moderate agreement (rho=0.54) across resampled backgrounds; (3) Policy attention never coincides with documented regulatory events. However, directional accuracy reaches 58.6%, outperforming all baselines.

Conclusion: Despite sophisticated architecture design, the model fails to outperform simple random walk on price prediction. The study demonstrates that integrating policy text with market data does not necessarily improve forecasting. The only positive finding is marginal directional accuracy improvement. The authors advocate for more rigorous evaluation frameworks in carbon market prediction research.

Abstract: Carbon markets put a price on emissions, yet that price remains hard to forecast. Work in this area clusters on the EU and Chinese schemes, compresses regulatory text into a sentiment score, and reports accuracy without calibration or explanation stability. We distil ten recurring gaps into an impact-feasibility matrix and propose EPA-CarbonNet, a six-layer architecture that fuses market series with policy text by cross-attention and calibrated intervals alongside policy-attributed explanations. We then build and test it on eleven years of daily S and P carbon index data.
