The findings are largely negative, and reported as measured: a random walk beats the model on five-day RMSE (0.0365 against 0.0475), SHAP rankings agree at rho = 0.54 across resampled backgrounds, and policy attention never coincides with documented regulatory events. Directional accuracy, at 58.6 percent, leads every baseline. Code, data documentation and all result artifacts are available at https://github.com/Kimalice/Toward-Explainable-and-Policy-Aware-AI-for-Carbon-Credit-Price-Prediction

</details>


### [139] [Emergence of Fibrations, Compression, and Symmetry Breaking in Artificial Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01768)
*Osvaldo M Velarde,Lucas C Parra,Alireza Hashemi,Hernan A Makse*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper reveals that learning in deep neural networks generates mathematical symmetries called fibrations and coverings from graph theory. These symmetries are stable under stochastic gradient descent and emerge across all major network architectures. The findings enable dramatic model compression (to 17% of original size) and solve the loss of plasticity problem in continual learning, while converting neural networks from opaque black boxes into interpretable colored graphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Artificial neural networks are typically treated as opaque black boxes, making them difficult to interpret, compress, and adapt for lifelong learning. This work seeks to understand the fundamental mathematical structures underlying neural network learning to address these limitations.

Method: The authors prove theoretically that covering symmetries are stable attractors of stochastic gradient descent. They then empirically验证 these symmetries emerge across major architectures (multilayer, convolutional, recurrent, and transformer networks). They exploit these symmetries for model compression and controlled breaking of symmetry to address continual learning problems.

Result: Covering symmetries emerge consistently across all tested network architectures. These symmetries enable reducing networks to 17% of original size without performance loss. Controlled breaking of symmetry overcomes loss of plasticity, achieving state-of-the-art performance in continual learning tasks.

Conclusion: The theoretical framework based on symmetries provides a new foundation for AI systems. These symmetries transform black boxes into interpretable colored graphs and enable more efficient inference and lifelong learning, opening new avenues for understanding and improving neural networks.

Abstract: Artificial neural networks are often regarded as powerful yet opaque black boxes. Here, we demonstrate that learning in deep neural networks generates local symmetries known in graph theory as fibrations and coverings. We prove that covering symmetries are stable attractors of stochastic gradient descent. Consistent with this theory, we report the emergence of covering symmetries across major network architectures, including multilayer, convolutional, recurrent, and transformer networks. Exploiting these symmetries enables drastic model compression - reducing networks to 17% of their original size without sacrificing performance. Furthermore, controlled breaking of covering symmetry overcomes the loss of plasticity, achieving state-of-the-art performance in continual learning. The theoretical results provide a new foundation for AI systems based on symmetries that convert black boxes into interpretable colored graphs and enable more efficient inference and lifelong learning.

</details>


### [140] [D-FROST: Decentralized Federated pRompt-tuning via Optimal tranSporT for Non-IID and Imbalanced Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01802)
*Quan Minh Nguyen,Hoang M. Ngo,Trong Nghia Hoang,My T. Thai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First study of prompt tuning in Decentralized Federated Learning (DFL), proposing D-FROST algorithm that uses optimal transport to merge heterogeneous prompt sets across clients without requiring index-wise alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompt tuning is parameter-efficient for adapting foundation models, making it suitable for DFL where full-model exchange is expensive. However, prompts learned from heterogeneous local data may not be index-wise aligned, making standard decentralized averaging unsuitable. Existing methods lack theoretical guarantees for consensus and convergence in this setting.

Method: Formulated decentralized prompt tuning as a Wasserstein-based optimization problem over prompt measures to capture set-valued structure of prompts. Proposed D-FROST (Decentralized-Federated Prompt Tuning) using optimal transport-based matching to merge neighborhood prompts into compact representative prompt sets through transportation-based matching.

Result: Provided theoretical analysis bounding the Wasserstein consensus error across clients and establishing convergence of the network-level prompt barycenter to a neighborhood of stationarity. Demonstrated effectiveness through experiments under heterogeneous client data.

Conclusion: D-FROST successfully enables prompt tuning in decentralized settings with theoretical guarantees, effectively handling heterogeneous local data through optimal transport-based prompt merging.

Abstract: Prompt tuning provides a parameter-efficient way to adapt foundation models (FMs) by freezing the pretrained backbone and updating only a small set of learnable prompts. This property makes prompt tuning especially suitable for decentralized federated learning (DFL), where exchanging full-model updates can be prohibitively expensive. However, prompt tuning in DFL introduces new challenges. Prompt sets learned from heterogeneous local data may not be index-wise aligned, making standard decentralized averaging unsuitable. In addition, the algorithm should be theoretically guaranteed to achieve consensus and make progress toward the shared objective. In this work, we provide the first study of prompt tuning in DFL. We formulate decentralized prompt tuning as a Wasserstein-based optimization problem over prompt measures, which captures the set-valued structure of prompts. We then propose D-FROST, an optimal-transport-based (OT-based) decentralized prompt-tuning algorithm that merges neighborhood prompts into compact representative prompt sets through transportation-based matching. We further analyze D-FROST by bounding the Wasserstein consensus error across clients, and establishing convergence of the network-level prompt barycenter to a neighborhood of stationarity. Experiments under heterogeneous client data demonstrate the effectiveness of D-FROST for decentralized prompt tuning.

</details>


### [141] [hLLM: Single Pass Decoding for Generative Reranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01807)
*Emil Laftchiev,Prachi Agrawal,Moe Kayali,Bixing Yan,Qi Xu,Zijie Lei,Chen Qiu,Zhi Hua,Ke Li,Luke Simon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: hLLM introduces an O(1) decoding strategy for LLM-based ranking that uses the Hungarian algorithm to compute optimal permutations from prefill hidden states, achieving 64x speed-up (28ms) while maintaining ranking quality through LoRA fine-tuning and teacher distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive decoding in LLMs is inefficient for ranking tasks since it requires one forward pass per emitted token, even though ranking only requires outputting N ordinal values (the ranked order). The goal is to leverage the permutation-structured output format to enable much more efficient decoding.

Method: 1) Uses a lightweight self-attention head to read an N×K item-position score matrix from LLM prefill hidden states; 2) Decodes ordinals as optimal bipartite assignment via Hungarian algorithm (O(1) forward passes); 3) Combines LoRA-based fine-tuning with teacher ranking distillation for training.

Result: Achieves 64× speed-up (28ms end-to-end inference) while maintaining ranking quality on par with the teacher model. Complete ablation shows contributions from architecture, training signal, and backbone adaptation.

Conclusion: hLLM connects generative ranking to combinatorial optimization, enabling O(1)-decode mechanisms for real-time ranking. The approach produces valid permutations by construction rather than through repair, opening pathways for other efficient decoding strategies.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) achieve state-of-the-art generative ranking quality, but the ranking they produce must be decoded, and autoregressive decoding spends one sequential forward pass per emitted token. We observe that the only tokens a ranker must emit are the $N$ ordinal values naming the items in ranked order, and that this narrow, permutation-structured output format admits decoding strategies which are much more efficient than left-to-right generation. We introduce hLLM (Hungarian LLM), a format-specialized decoding strategy that decodes all $N$ ordinals in $O(1)$ forward passes. hLLM reads an $N \times K$ item-position score matrix off the LLM's prefill hidden states with a lightweight self-attention head, then decodes the ordinals as the optimal bipartite assignment of that matrix via the Hungarian algorithm, yielding a valid permutation by construction rather than by repair. Through a systematic study of training signals and backbone adaptation, we show that LoRA-based fine-tuning combined with teacher ranking distillation reaches 28 ms end-to-end inference, a speed-up of $64\times$ while maintaining ranking quality on par with the teacher. We provide a complete ablation decomposing the contributions of architecture, training signal, and backbone adaptation. Our framework connects generative ranking to combinatorial optimization, opening a path toward other $O(1)$-decode mechanisms for real-time ranking.

</details>


### [142] [Import What You Need: Learning When and How to Augment EHR Graphs with External Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01839)
*Chen Chen,Mohsen Nayebi Kerdabadi,Dongjie Wang,Mei Liu,Zijun Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReTA is a reinforcement learning-based framework that dynamically augments electronic health record (EHR) graphs with external knowledge graphs through a per-visit, budget-aware policy, selecting between soft import, hard import, or skip actions to improve longitudinal prediction tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Longitudinal prediction from EHRs is limited by sparsity and irregularity in patient trajectories. Existing KG augmentation methods use fixed, context-agnostic topology, adding the same nodes/edges regardless of patient state, leading to suboptimal knowledge injection.

Method: ReTA employs a policy-based approach with three actions: (1) Soft Import - enriches node features without topology changes, (2) Hard Import - grafts compact KG subgraphs to create message-passing shortcuts, (3) Skip - leaves visit unaugmented when base encoder is confident. Uses a decoupled encoder with separate channels for semantic and structural signals, fused via adaptive gating. Offline refined pool of KG-grounded templates is constructed first.

Result: ReTA consistently outperforms strong baselines on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV across diagnosis prediction, mortality, and readmission tasks. Shows robust gains under sparse supervision, transfers across datasets and knowledge graphs, and yields interpretable augmentation patterns.

Conclusion: Dynamic, budget-aware knowledge graph augmentation significantly improves EHR longitudinal prediction. The per-visit decision-making framework effectively balances accuracy improvement with computational cost containment, demonstrating the advantage of context-aware knowledge import over static augmentation approaches.

Abstract: Longitudinal prediction from electronic health records (EHRs) is limited by the sparsity and irregularity in patient trajectories, and knowledge augmentation with external knowledge graphs (KGs) offers a promising way to alleviate these issues. However, most existing methods perform fixed, context-agnostic topology augmentation by adding the same KG nodes and edges regardless of a patient's evolving state. We propose ReTA, a Reinforcement learning-based dynamic Topology Augmentation framework that casts KG import as a per-visit, budget-aware policy. ReTA first constructs an offline refined pool of KG-grounded templates, then learns a policy to select one augment action per visit from three options: Soft Import, which enriches node features without modifying graph topology, Hard Import, which grafts a compact KG subgraph onto the visit graph to create message-passing shortcuts, and Skip, which leaves the visit unaugmented when the base encoder is already confident. To stabilize learning, ReTA employs a decoupled encoder that processes semantic and structural signals in separate channels and fuses them via adaptive gating. Experiments on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV across diagnosis prediction, mortality, and readmission show that ReTA consistently outperforms strong baselines while remaining efficient, transfers across datasets and knowledge graphs, and yields interpretable augmentation patterns. The robust gains under sparse supervision highlight the advantage of ReTA's dynamic decision to import knowledge, boosting accuracy while curbing costs.

</details>


### [143] [OutageDiT: A Generative Foundation Model for Power Outage Forecasting and Scenario Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01896)
*Yunqin Zhu,Feng Qiu,Yao Xie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OutageDiT is a foundation model for generating seven-day power outage trajectories at quarter-hour resolution, using a condition encoder and shallow flow decoder architecture trained on US-wide outage and weather data. It supports point forecasting, uncertainty quantification, and conditional event simulation within a single deep generative model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Severe power outages are rare events, making it challenging to generate realistic scenarios for planning. Existing data from any single region contains few examples of extreme outage and restoration patterns, requiring methods that can capture uncertainty in magnitude, timing, and duration while preserving temporal dependence.

Method: The model uses a condition encoder that processes historical context and known future covariates once per forecast, combined with a shallow flow decoder that reuses horizon-aligned states to generate complete trajectories. Trained on outage and weather records across the United States.

Result: OutageDiT improves forecast accuracy and scenario quality over strong baselines across outage forecasting benchmarks. It also supports zero-shot transfer to held-out regions, demonstrating generalization capability.

Conclusion: Conditional outage simulation bridges the gap from outage forecasting to operational planning under uncertainty, providing a unified framework for multiple forecasting tasks including point forecasting, uncertainty quantification, and conditional event simulation.

Abstract: Power-outage planning requires scenarios before an event occurs. These scenarios must represent uncertainty in magnitude, timing, and duration while preserving temporal dependence. However, severe events are rare, and data from any single region contain few examples of extreme outage and restoration patterns. To address this challenge, we introduce OutageDiT, a foundation model for generating seven-day outage trajectories at quarter-hour resolution, trained on outage and weather records across the United States. Specifically, a condition encoder processes the historical context and known future covariates once per forecast, and a shallow flow decoder reuses the resulting horizon-aligned states to generate complete trajectories. The resulting samples support point forecasting, uncertainty quantification, and conditional event simulation within one deep generative model. Across outage forecasting benchmarks, OutageDiT improves forecast accuracy and scenario quality over strong baselines and supports zero-shot transfer to held-out regions. Together, these results position conditional outage simulation as a bridge from outage forecasting to operational planning under uncertainty.

</details>


### [144] [CRISP: Cliff-awaRe Input-adaptive Sparse Prefilling with Structural-Mass-Motivated Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01925)
*Huu Huy Nguyen,Chien Van Nguyen,Franck Dernoncourt,Ryan A. Rossi,Linh Ngo Van,Jieyang Chen,Thien Huu Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CRISP is a novel dynamic sparse attention method for long-context LLM inference that addresses computational bottlenecks in the prefilling phase. It replaces costly Jensen-Shannon Divergence routing with a structural proxy (C_struct) and eliminates O(n) background noise accumulation through a sink-aware threshold, achieving up to 5.30x speedup at 512k tokens while matching or exceeding dense attention on retrieval tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The attention prefilling phase of long-context LLM inference scales quadratically, making self-attention a severe computational bottleneck. Existing dynamic sparse attention methods rely on indirect routing proxies with overhead and budget allocation mechanisms that overlook the post-softmax mass hierarchy, leading to inefficient attention computation.

Method: CRISP addresses two structural challenges: (1) Replaces Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) routing with C_struct, a structural proxy that measures mass at Vertical-Slash compatible positions directly from the proxy attention map, eliminating pooled matmul and KL divergence overhead; (2) Formalizes the post-softmax mass cliff and uses a sink-aware threshold grounded in the noise floor to eliminate O(n) background noise accumulation at long contexts.

Result: CRISP is the strongest sparse method overall across InfiniteBench, RULER and LongBench on two model families. It matches or exceeds exact dense attention on retrieval-heavy benchmarks, recovering up to +28.0 pp on retrieval tasks over baselines and achieving up to 5.30x attention speedup at 512k tokens.

Conclusion: CRISP demonstrates that addressing structural challenges in dynamic routing—specifically the routing proxy efficiency and post-softmax mass hierarchy—can achieve significant speedups while maintaining or improving accuracy on long-context tasks, particularly retrieval tasks. The key innovations are the structural proxy C_struct and the sink-aware threshold for noise elimination.

Abstract: The attention prefilling phase of long-context LLM inference scales quadratically, making self-attention a severe computational bottleneck. Traditional sparse attention methods mitigate this through fixed patterns or offline profiling, but lack the flexibility to adapt to input-dependent attention structure. Recent dynamic methods address this by routing heads to sparse patterns in real-time, but rely on indirect routing proxies with overhead and budget allocation mechanisms that overlook the post-softmax mass hierarchy. We present CRISP (Cliff-awaRe Input-adaptive Sparse Prefilling), which identifies and addresses two structural challenges in this dynamic routing paradigm. First, we show that the routing decision can be read directly off the structure of the proxy attention map. We replace the Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) routing with C_struct, a structural proxy that measures mass at Vertical-Slash compatible positions and reproduces JSD's routing decisions while eliminating both the pooled matmul and subsequent KL divergence overhead. Second, we formalize the post-softmax mass cliff and demonstrate theoretically that strictly cumulative coverage thresholds accumulate O(n) background noise at long contexts. CRISP navigates this via a sink-aware threshold grounded in the noise floor. Empirically, across InfiniteBench, RULER and LongBench on two model families, CRISP is the strongest sparse method overall and matches or exceeds exact dense attention on retrieval-heavy benchmarks, recovering up to +28.0 pp on retrieval tasks over baselines and achieving up to a 5.30x attention speedup at 512k tokens, driven primarily by our O(n) noise elimination during selection while preserving structural integrity.

</details>


### [145] [OR-Transformer: Scaling Real-Time Decision-Making to 1,000 Items](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01933)
*Shuze Daniel Liu,David Simchi-Levi,Claire Chen,Chutong Gao,Shangtong Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OR-Transformer is a deep reinforcement learning framework for joint replenishment in large-scale supply chain operations (up to 1,024 items). It uses a permutation-equivariant Transformer architecture with pathwise-gradient training to handle high-dimensional state spaces (>10^4 dimensions) that traditional methods cannot efficiently solve.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern supply chain operations require coordinating replenishment across thousands of heterogeneous items under correlated stochastic demand, heterogeneous lead times, and shared fixed ordering costs. At this scale, traditional rolling-horizon stochastic MILPs become prohibitively slow, and standard RL methods face credit assignment challenges in high-dimensional action spaces.

Method: OR-Transformer combines: (1) an item-permutation-equivariant Transformer architecture that respects symmetry in the inventory problem, and (2) pathwise-gradient training through the inventory dynamics to enable effective learning.

Result: OR-Transformer increasingly outperforms both learning-based and rolling-horizon MILP baselines as problem scale grows (tested up to 1,024 inventory items). It reduces online decision-making time by over 4 million times relative to MILP solvers.

Conclusion: OR-Transformer enables real-time, large-scale deep RL in supply chain operations, demonstrating that properly designed neural architectures can surpass both traditional optimization and existing RL methods at scale.

Abstract: Modern supply chain operations can require coordinating replenishment across thousands of heterogeneous items under correlated stochastic demand, heterogeneous lead times, and shared fixed ordering costs, yielding observation spaces exceeding $10^4$ dimensions. At this scale, rolling-horizon stochastic mixed-integer linear programs (MILPs) become prohibitively slow, while standard reinforcement learning (RL) methods face increasingly challenging credit assignment in high-dimensional action spaces. We introduce OR-Transformer, a deep reinforcement learning framework for joint replenishment under stochastic demand, with an item-permutation-equivariant Transformer architecture and pathwise-gradient training through the inventory dynamics. Across problem sizes up to 1,024 inventory items, OR-Transformer increasingly outperforms learning-based and rolling-horizon MILP baselines as scale grows. It also reduces online decision-making time by over 4 million times relative to MILP solvers, enabling real-time, large-scale deep RL in supply chain operations.

</details>


### [146] [Refining Heuristic-Based Bitcoin Address Clustering with Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01942)
*Hugo Schnoering,Roman Bresson,Michalis Vazirgiannis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a method to refine heuristic-based Bitcoin address clustering by using graph neural network-generated contrastive embeddings, enabling hierarchical analysis and detection of suspicious cluster merges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bitcoin's pseudonymous nature makes it difficult to analyze user-level activity since a single user may control multiple addresses. Existing heuristic-based clustering methods produce flat cluster assignments with limited modularity and are prone to incorrectly merging different users together.

Method: The method uses graph neural networks to generate contrastive embeddings that are consistent with heuristic-based clusters. These embeddings enable hierarchical clustering for finer analysis of heuristic clusters and provide a quantitative criterion for flagging suspicious merges.

Result: The paper releases a publicly available dataset of Bitcoin transaction graphs with a substantial number of clusters, and demonstrates a methodology for learning address embeddings consistent with heuristics backed by theoretical guiding intuitions.

Conclusion: The proposed approach enables finer analysis of heuristic clusters and provides a quantitative criterion for identifying suspicious merges, improving the accuracy of Bitcoin user identification.

Abstract: Bitcoin's pseudonymous nature makes it challenging to analyze user-level activity, since a single user may control multiple identifiers (addresses). Existing heuristic-based methods attempt to identify addresses belonging to the same user, but they often produce flat cluster assignments with limited modularity and are prone to errors such as merging different users together. In this work, we propose a method for refining heuristic-obtained clusters by grounding our clustering on contrastive embeddings yielded by graph neural networks. Our contributions are threefold: (i) we release a publicly available dataset of Bitcoin transaction graphs containing a substantial number of clusters; (ii) we propose a methodology for learning address embeddings consistent with heuristics, and back it up with theoretical guiding intuitions; (iii) through hierarchical clustering, we enable a finer analysis of heuristic clusters and provide a quantitative criterion for flagging suspicious merges.

</details>


### [147] [On-Policy Distillation Meets Off-Policy GRPO: Training Compact Instruction-Following Rerankers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01947)
*Vignesh Prabhakar,Jialing Pan,Anil Babu Ankisettipalli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a two-stage reinforcement learning framework for distilling compact instruction-following rerankers. Stage 1 uses off-policy GRPO to optimize a 4B teacher model, while Stage 2 performs on-policy student distillation where a 1B student receives rewards based on teacher-derived feedback over its own sampled rankings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional reranker distillation uses offline imitation learning, constraining supervision to the teacher's observed ranking space. This limits the student's ability to explore beyond the teacher's distribution and adapt to distribution shift.

Method: Two-stage framework: (1) Stage 1 strengthens a 4B teacher reranker using off-policy GRPO with LLM-judge feedback on 88K instruction-following examples; (2) Stage 2 performs on-policy student distillation where a 1B student samples rankings from its own policy and receives soft teacher-derived rewards on those rankings, coupling student exploration with knowledge transfer.

Result: The 1B student achieves 0.7670 nDCG@6 on MAIR-11 (869-query evaluation), outperforming offline listwise KD by +4.6 points. On MAIR-Full (126 tasks, 9,356 queries), it reaches 0.6808 nDCG@6 and 0.7865 MRR@6. The method exceeds two released 7B RL-trained rerankers on MAIR-11 and consistently improves three architecturally distinct student backbones. On a 9,861-query validation benchmark, it achieves 0.7624 nDCG@6 with favorable quality-efficiency tradeoff.

Conclusion: Reward-based on-policy distillation over student-sampled rankings significantly outperforms offline distillation methods (listwise KD, pairwise RankNet KD) and on-policy GKD. The approach provides a superior quality-efficiency tradeoff for compact reranker deployment.

Abstract: Compact instruction-following rerankers are attractive for deployment, but conventional distillation pipelines typically train students by offline imitation of teacher outputs on a fixed set of examples, constraining supervision to the teacher's observed ranking space. We revisit reranker distillation through the lens of reinforcement learning.
  We propose a two-stage framework combining off-policy teacher optimization with on-policy student distillation. In Stage 1, a 4B teacher reranker is strengthened with off-policy GRPO using LLM-judge feedback on 88K instruction-following examples. In Stage 2, a compact 1B student samples rankings from its own policy and receives soft teacher-derived rewards on those rankings, coupling student exploration with knowledge transfer.
  Our strongest gains appear under distribution shift. On MAIR-11, the original 11-subset, 869-query evaluation, the proposed student reaches 0.7670 nDCG@6, outperforming offline listwise KD by +4.6 points. Controlled comparisons against offline pairwise RankNet KD and on-policy GKD show that neither changing the offline distillation objective nor moving teacher-distribution matching on-policy reproduces the performance of reward-based on-policy distillation over student-sampled rankings. The advantage persists on MAIR-Full: across all 126 tasks and 9,356 queries, the proposed method obtains the highest task-macro point estimates among the evaluated distillation variants, reaching 0.6808 nDCG@6 and 0.7865 MRR@6. It also exceeds two released 7B RL-trained rerankers on the comparable MAIR-11 evaluation, while the same Stage 2 training procedure consistently improves three architecturally distinct alternative student backbones.
  On the 9,861-query validation benchmark, the resulting 1B reranker achieves 0.7624 nDCG@6 while providing a favorable quality-efficiency tradeoff relative to larger alternatives.

</details>


### [148] [Convergence Theory of Knowledge Distillation in Asynchronous P2P Gossip Learning Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01952)
*Lucas Qingyang Fang,Tiyao Liu,Jinhao Jing,Zeji Li,Kaijie Chen,Harikrishna Kuttivelil,Katia Obraczka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses decentralized, serverless learning across heterogeneous architectures where standard decentralized SGD fails due to incompatible model sizes. The authors propose using knowledge distillation (KD) in peer-to-peer settings, replacing weight averaging with soft prediction exchange. They develop convergence theory by treating KD as a geometric contraction operator in logit space, proving O(1/(ηT)) convergence rate to a neighborhood depending on step size (η), task-model distance (B_f), and local heterogeneity (ζ_f). Experiments across homogeneous, width-heterogeneous, and mixed-family networks show 40-61× reduction in function disagreement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to enable decentralized, serverless learning across devices with different neural network architectures—a common scenario in edge computing. Since standard decentralized SGD requires parameter averaging (impossible with different model sizes), and existing KD lacks theoretical convergence guarantees for asynchronous P2P settings, the paper seeks to establish a solid theoretical foundation for decentralized KD.

Method: The authors relocate consensus from parameter space to function (output) space. They treat each KD event as a geometric contraction operator in logit space on the peers' predictive distributions, analyzed in the Hilbert space of predictions on a reference measure. Under standard smoothness/variance assumptions plus two realizability assumptions (bridging SGD to functional step, controlling task/KD alignment), they prove convergence guarantees for time-averaged functional stationarity and function-space disagreement.

Result: Theoretical results: Convergence at rate O(1/(ηT)) to O(η) + O(B_f^2) + O(ζ_f^2) neighborhood. Experimental results: KD contracts function disagreement by 40-61× (vs. isolated training which shows no contraction). Sampled stationarity diagnostic shows late transient exponents 0.99-1.90 on shared-skeleton main runs. Four-point step-size sweep confirms predicted neighborhood tradeoff.

Conclusion: The paper provides the first convergence theory for fully decentralized, asynchronous P2P knowledge distillation. By moving consensus to function space, the approach works across heterogeneous architectures. The theory is validated empirically across diverse network configurations (homogeneous, width-heterogeneous, mixed-family), demonstrating consistent contraction of function disagreement. The neighborhood tradeoff between step size and residual error is confirmed experimentally.

Abstract: Decentralized, serverless learning increasingly connects devices running different architectures, where the standard tool, decentralized SGD, is undefined as models with different parameter counts cannot be averaged. Knowledge distillation (KD) exchanges soft predictions rather than weights and sidesteps this obstacle, yet convergence theory for fully decentralized, asynchronous peer-to-peer (P2P) KD is lacking. We provide one, relocating consensus from parameter space to function (output) space: a KD event is a geometric contraction operator in logit space on the peers' predictive distributions, which we analyse in the Hilbert space of predictions on a reference measure. Under standard smoothness/variance assumptions and two realizability assumptions, one bridging parameter SGD to the functional step and one controlling restricted task/KD alignment, the time-averaged functional stationarity and function-space disagreement converge at rate $O(1/(ηT))$ to an $O(η)+O(B_f^2)+O(ζ_f^2)$ neighbourhood. Here $B_f$ is the distance from the task optimum to the peers' reachable classes and $ζ_f$ measures persistent local-task heterogeneity. Across homogeneous, width-heterogeneous, and mixed-family networks of the experiments, KD contracts function disagreement by $40-61\times$, while isolated training does not. The sampled stationarity diagnostic has late transient exponents $0.99-1.90$ on the shared-skeleton main runs, and the four-point step-size sweep exhibits the predicted transient: neighbourhood tradeoff.

</details>


### [149] [FlashKAN: B-Spline KANs via Truncated Power Form](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01956)
*Naveen Mysore*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlashKAN optimizes Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) by replacing the slow Cox-de Boor recursion (90%+ of forward-pass time) with a truncated power form, implementing it as a single fused GPU kernel with bounded-coordinate stabilization, and providing a production-ready open-source package.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Cox-de Boor recursion for evaluating B-spline activations in KANs is computationally expensive, consuming over 90% of forward-pass time due to k sequential passes for degree-k splines. Additionally, the method historically suffered from catastrophic cancellation issues.

Method: Three technical contributions: (1) torch.compile-fused implementation collapsing operations into a single GPU kernel, eliminating recursion, span lookup, and scatter-gather operations; (2) bounded-coordinate stabilization clamping normalized input to [0, k+1] to prevent catastrophic cancellation; (3) uniform cubic B-spline expressed as five (x)_+^3 terms at shifted knot positions using truncated power form.

Result: A drop-in replacement for existing KAN layers that achieves significant speedup by eliminating the computationally expensive Cox-de Boor recursion through GPU kernel fusion and stabilization techniques.

Conclusion: FlashKAN provides an efficient, production-ready solution for KANs that dramatically reduces computational overhead while maintaining functionality, available as a pip-installable package (pip install flashkan).

Abstract: Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) place learnable B-spline activations on network edges rather than fixed activations on nodes. The standard Cox-de Boor recursion evaluates these activations through k sequential passes for degree-k splines, consuming over 90% of forward-pass time. FlashKAN replaces this recursion with the truncated power form, a classical result from approximation theory that expresses each uniform cubic B-spline as five (x)_+^3 terms at shifted knot positions. This paper makes three contributions: (1) a torch.compile-fused implementation that collapses these operations into a single GPU kernel, eliminating all recursion, span lookup, and scatter-gather operations; (2) a bounded-coordinate stabilization that clamps the normalized input to [0, k+1], preventing the catastrophic cancellation that historically motivated the Cox-de Boor recursion; and (3) a production-ready, open-source package (pip install flashkan) that serves as a drop-in replacement for existing KAN layers.

</details>


### [150] [A Unified Particle Filter LSTM for Data-Driven Process Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01967)
*Parvin Malekzadeh,Opher Baron,Dmitry Krass*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes Unified PF-LSTM, a novel approach that combines particle filtering with LSTM recurrent networks to simulate realistic process trajectories from event logs. The key innovation is maintaining multiple recurrent-state hypotheses (particles) instead of compressing history into a single deterministic state, allowing the model to handle partial observability in event logs. The method predicts next-activity probabilities and activity sojourn time quantiles, trained end-to-end on event data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Event logs provide only partial views of underlying process states, often recording activity completions without service-start times. This creates ambiguity where the same observed history can be consistent with multiple plausible latent process conditions. Standard recurrent models compress each process prefix into a single deterministic state, losing this uncertainty. The motivation is to develop a model that can represent and track this uncertainty through multiple state hypotheses.

Method: The proposed Unified PF-LSTM maintains a weighted set of recurrent-state hypotheses (particles) that are sequentially updated. The particle belief is summarized using its weighted mean and learned features based on the moment-generating function. This representation is used to predict: (1) a categorical distribution over next activities, and (2) conditional quantiles of the current activity's sojourn time. The framework is trained end-to-end from event-log data.

Result: The framework was evaluated on three real-world emergency department datasets. It consistently outperforms considered data-driven baselines in reproducing routing, duration, and system-level behavior across all datasets. Particularly strong gains were observed in settings where complex process dynamics are only partially reflected in the available event logs.

Conclusion: The Unified PF-LSTM successfully addresses the partial observability challenge in process mining by maintaining multiple latent state hypotheses. The particle filtering approach combined with deep sequence learning provides a more accurate representation of process uncertainty, leading to superior performance in process simulation tasks. This is especially valuable for real-world processes where event logs capture incomplete information about underlying dynamics.

Abstract: Data-driven process simulation aims to generate realistic case trajectories from historical event logs without requiring an explicitly specified model of the underlying dynamics. Deep sequence models can capture complex temporal dependencies through next-activity probabilities and conditional time distributions. However, event logs provide only a partial view of the underlying process state, often recording activity completions without the corresponding service-start times. Consequently, the same observed process history may be consistent with multiple plausible latent process conditions, whereas standard recurrent models compress each process prefix into a single deterministic recurrent state. We propose a Unified Particle Filter LSTM (Unified PF-LSTM) that maintains and sequentially updates a weighted set of recurrent-state hypotheses. We summarize this particle belief using its weighted mean and learned features based on the moment-generating function. The resulting representation is used to predict a categorical distribution over the next activity and conditional quantiles of the current activity's sojourn time. The framework is trained end-to-end from event-log data and evaluated on three real-world emergency department datasets. The results show that the proposed framework consistently outperforms the considered data-driven baselines in reproducing routing, duration, and system-level behavior across all datasets, with particularly strong gains in settings where complex process dynamics are only partially reflected in the available event logs.

</details>


### [151] [CAHR-Net: Condition-Adaptive Hysteresis Reconstruction for Compact and Interpretable Magnetic Core Loss Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01991)
*Chunye Gong,Cong Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAHR-Net is a condition-adaptive neural network for magnetic core loss prediction that preserves an interpretable chain from flux density waveform to magnetic field reconstruction, loop-area integration, and power loss estimation. It uses feature-wise linear modulation to inject operating conditions (frequency, temperature, waveform statistics) into the intermediate hysteresis representation, addressing the limitation of existing models that apply conditions only to terminal scalars without preserving intermediate physical representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing magnetic core loss models treat operating conditions (frequency, temperature, waveform shape) only as terminal scalars - empirical equations use fitted exponents and data-driven predictors append them to encoded features. This means no intermediate hysteresis representation remains for conditions to reshape, losing physical interpretability. The paper aims to preserve the interpretable physical chain where conditions can act at the appropriate stage in the reconstruction process.

Method: The proposed CAHR-Net uses feature-wise linear modulation (FiLM) to inject frequency, temperature, and waveform statistics into the intermediate hysteresis reconstruction representation. The network preserves the chain: flux density waveform → magnetic field reconstruction → loop-area integration → power loss estimation. A matched training protocol is used: AdamW optimizer, cosine scheduling, and a staged reconstruction-to-power-loss objective. The model was evaluated on the MagNet final A-E material protocol.

Result: CAHR-Net achieves an average p95 relative error of 6.89% with only 1874 parameters - the lowest among all compared methods. It has a lower worst-material p95 than the strongest black-box solution while using 48x fewer parameters. It reduces the average p95 of the physical reconstruction backbone from 7.47% to 6.89%, and reduces the p95 of material D (the most difficult) from 16.40% to 14.87%. Ablation and condition-slice analyses confirm the improvement comes from coupling physical loop reconstruction, structured condition modulation, and the matched optimization trajectory.

Conclusion: The condition-adaptive approach successfully integrates operating conditions into the intermediate hysteresis representation, achieving superior accuracy with far fewer parameters than black-box alternatives. The method demonstrates that preserving physical interpretability while injecting conditions at the appropriate physical stage can outperform purely data-driven approaches. The matched training protocol is essential for the modulation pathway to take effect.

Abstract: Magnetic core loss originates in the hysteresis loop: the energy dissipated per excitation cycle equals the loop area, and frequency, temperature, and waveform shape set the loss by reshaping the loop geometry. Most existing models let these conditions act only on a terminal scalar - empirical equations fold them into fitted exponents, and data-driven predictors append them to encoded features - so no intermediate hysteresis representation remains for the conditions to reshape. This paper proposes CAHR-Net, a condition-adaptive hysteresis reconstruction network that injects the operating conditions where they physically act. It preserves the interpretable chain from flux density waveform to magnetic field reconstruction, loop-area integration, and power loss estimation, and uses feature-wise linear modulation to inject frequency, temperature, and waveform statistics into the intermediate reconstruction representation. A matched large-batch training protocol based on AdamW, cosine scheduling, and a staged reconstruction-to-power-loss objective is also reported, because the modulation pathway takes effect only within it. On the MagNet final A-E material protocol, CAHR-Net attains an average p95 relative error of 6.89% with only 1874 parameters, the lowest among all compared methods, together with a lower worst-material p95 than the strongest black-box solution at about 48x fewer parameters; it reduces the average p95 of the physical reconstruction backbone from 7.47% to 6.89% and the p95 of material D, the most difficult material, from 16.40% to 14.87%. Ablation and condition-slice analyses attribute the improvement to the coupling of physical loop reconstruction, structured condition modulation, and the matched optimization trajectory.

</details>


### [152] [Train What You Deploy: Closing the MLP Reachability Gap in Low-Rank Clone Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02006)
*Wenhui Chen,Zhifeng Li,Jie Zhou,Navan Preet Singh,Madalina Ciobanu,Chenghua Wang,Qingqing Mao,Ritankar Das*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies a fundamental mismatch in weight inheritance distillation: deployed weights vs. trainable weights. The authors show that LRC (Low-Rank Clone) leaves 62.5-81.4% of each deployed matrix's parameters unreachable during training. They propose "train what you deploy" principle with two methods (Dense-LRC and CORE-LRC) that achieve +2.36 to +10.45 average improvement across three teacher models, with a 1.5B student matching its ~9T-token teacher and a 2.7B student beating Meta's official Llama3.1-8B compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that current weight inheritance distillers (specifically LRC) deploy full-width student MLPs but only train a slice of the weight matrix, leaving 62.5-81.4% of independent linear degrees of freedom unreachable. These "stranded" parameters are paid for at inference but never trainable, representing wasted capacity and inefficiency.

Method: Two mergeable realizations: Dense-LRC and CORE-LRC. Both make the training object the entire deployed matrix with no change in deployed shape, parameter count, or inference FLOPs. They collapse to one deployed weight. The key innovation is training the entire deployed matrix rather than just a teacher-induced slice.

Result: 1) Dense-LRC/CORE-LRC recover stranded capacity with +2.36/+2.71/+10.45 Avg9 over matched-budget plain-LRC baselines across Llama3.2-3B, Llama3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-3B teachers. 2) Largest gain on widest teacher (Qwen), reaching 2x token efficiency. 3) 1.5B student matches ~9T-token teacher's 9-task macro-average within evaluation noise. 4) 2.7B student beats Meta's official Llama3.1-8B compression at ~900x fewer compression tokens.

Conclusion: The principle of "train what you deploy" is validated - enlarging the reachable parameter set recovers stranded capacity without added inference cost. This simple fix significantly improves distillation efficiency and performance, with the largest gains on wider teacher models. The methods achieve remarkable compression results, with a half-parameter student matching its 9T-token teacher and beating Meta's official compression with orders of magnitude fewer tokens.

Abstract: A compressed student has two shapes that need not agree: the weight it deploys at inference and the weight family its training can reach. We show that a state-of-the-art weight-inheritance distiller, Low-Rank Clone (LRC), deploys a full-width student MLP but ties training to a teacher-induced slice, leaving 62.5-81.4% of each deployed matrix's independent linear degrees of freedom unreachable-paid for at inference, never trainable. Our principle is one line: train what you deploy. From the identical LRC warm start, we make the training object the entire deployed matrix, with no change in deployed shape, deployed parameter count, or inference FLOPs, via two mergeable realizations (Dense-LRC and CORE-LRC) that both collapse to one deployed weight. This recovers stranded capacity: taking the stronger realization per teacher, +2.36/+2.71/+10.45 Avg9 over matched-budget plain-LRC baselines across three teachers (Llama3.2-3B, Llama3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-3B), with the largest gain on the widest teacher (Qwen), where it reaches the original recipe's approx. 20B-token accuracy at 10B tokens (2x token efficiency); there the strictly same-lineage arm still recovers +6.39, the fully controlled figure. Controls strongly support attributing the gain to the enlarged reachable set, rather than to added parameters or the recipe. From approx. 10B distillation tokens plus a short SFT, a half-parameter 1.5B student matches its approx. 9T-token teacher's 9-task macro-average, within evaluation noise and with a residual MMLU deficit, and a 2.7B student beats Meta's own official compression of Llama3.1-8B at ~900x fewer compression tokens (a token count under unmatched recipes, not a compute claim). All results are from single-seed runs on the LRC backbone.

</details>


### [153] [Source-Free Class Relearning: Diagnosing Forgetting in Class Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02018)
*Zahra Dehghani,Pablo Piantanida,Mohammadhadi Shateri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether forgotten classes can be recovered from unlearned models in a strictly source-free setting (without access to original training data, auxiliary data, or reference checkpoints). The authors propose SFRA, a white-box method that generates synthetic probe embeddings and uses model-guided confidence filtering to reconstruct classifier decision boundaries for forgotten classes. Experiments on CIFAR-10/100 and TinyImageNet with various architectures (ResNet-18, ViT-B/16, Swin-T) reveal that several unlearning methods exhibit substantial recoverability, with some exceeding retrained reference baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from a critical security concern: low forget accuracy after unlearning does not guarantee the class structure has been erased. Approximate unlearning methods can alter classifier decision boundaries while leaving recoverable representation structures. Prior recovery approaches required real forget/retain samples, auxiliary data, or reference checkpoints—this work explores whether recovery is possible using only the unlearned model itself.

Method: The authors first establish a theoretical analysis showing a sufficient alignment condition under which a single gradient step on synthetic probe sets increases the expected logit margin of the forget class. Building on this, they propose Source-Free Relearning Audit (SFRA), which: (1) generates candidate embeddings in representation space using Gaussian sampling; (2) applies model-guided confidence filtering to construct high-confidence retain probes and low-confidence boundary-adjacent probes relabelled as the forget class; (3) uses Softmax confidence as the default uncertainty criterion. The Relearning Score (RS) is introduced to jointly measure forget-class recovery and retain-accuracy preservation, with class-matched ΔRS reported relative to a retrained reference.

Result: Experiments demonstrate that several unlearning methods exhibit substantial source-free recoverability across CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and TinyImageNet with ResNet-18, ViT-B/16, and Swin-T architectures. Notably, for a subset of methods, the recoverability exceeds the matched retrained reference, indicating that these unlearning methods fail to effectively erase class structure despite achieving low forget accuracy.

Conclusion: The study reveals a significant vulnerability in current class unlearning methods: even in a strictly source-free setting where only the unlearned model is available, forgotten classes can often be recovered through classifier-head updates using synthetic probe sets. This challenges the effectiveness of existing unlearning approaches and highlights the need for more robust methods that truly erase class structure rather than merely adjusting decision boundaries. The SFRA framework provides a practical audit tool for evaluating the true erasure of forgotten classes.

Abstract: Class unlearning aims to remove a model's ability to recognize designated forget classes while preserving performance on retain classes. However, low forget accuracy after unlearning does not necessarily mean the class structure has been erased. Approximate unlearning methods can alter classifier decision boundaries while leaving recoverable structure in the representation. Prior work has shown that forget classes can be recovered, but existing approaches require real forget or retain samples, auxiliary data, or reference checkpoints. We study class relearning in a strictly source-free setting, asking whether a forget class can be recovered through a classifier-head update using only the unlearned model. Our approach rests on a theoretical analysis establishing a sufficient alignment condition under which a single gradient step on a synthetic probe set increases the expected logit margin of the forget class. Building on this, we propose a white-box Source-Free Relearning Audit (SFRA), which generates candidate embeddings in representation space and uses model-guided confidence filtering to construct high-confidence retain probes and low-confidence boundary-adjacent probes that are relabelled as the forget class. Gaussian sampling and Softmax confidence are used by default, while ablations with alternative proposal distributions and uncertainty criteria show that recoverability is not specific to these choices. To quantify recoverability, we introduce the Relearning Score (RS), which jointly measures forget-class recovery and retain-accuracy preservation, and report class-matched $Δ$RS relative to a retrained reference. Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and TinyImageNet with ResNet-18, ViT-B/16, and Swin-T show that several unlearning methods exhibit substantial source-free recoverability, and that for a subset of methods this recoverability exceeds the matched retrained reference.

</details>


### [154] [Act More, Decide Less: Skill-Guided Adaptive Action Chunking for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02042)
*Yanting Yang,Can Jin,Jinman Zhao,Jiahao Wu,Yang Zhou,Zhepeng Wang,Zhendong Wang,Mu Zhou,Dimitris N. Metaxas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SPACE proposes a method for LLM agents to emit variable-length action chunks instead of single primitive actions, addressing inefficiency in long-horizon tasks by learning chunk boundaries through programmatic skill distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ReAct-style LLM agents issue one primitive action per round, which is inefficient for long-horizon tasks involving routine action sequences. Standard RL fails to train variable-length chunk policies due to two failure modes: collapsing to single actions or over-committing to excessively long sequences—both stemming from inability to learn chunk boundaries.

Method: SPACE addresses the chunk-boundary learning problem by: (1) distilling chunk-boundary supervision from trajectory-induced programmatic skills, (2) inducing two-level programmatic skills from successful trajectories where subskill boundaries serve as direct chunk-boundary supervision, and (3) distilling this temporal structure into a primitive-chunk policy via hybrid on-/off-policy optimization with chunk-aware credit assignment.

Result: On ALFWorld and ScienceWorld benchmarks, SPACE improves success rates by 7.0%-31.3% over the strongest baseline in each setting while reducing average LLM decision rounds by up to 78.9%.

Conclusion: By leveraging programmatic skill induction to provide chunk-boundary supervision and using chunk-aware credit assignment for policy optimization, SPACE enables LLM agents to effectively learn variable-length action chunking, significantly improving efficiency and success rates in long-horizon interactive tasks.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents for long-horizon interactive tasks typically follow a ReAct-style protocol, issuing one primitive action per LLM round. While this enables frequent replanning, it is inefficient for long-horizon tasks where many rounds are spent on routine action sequences. A natural alternative is to let the agent emit variable-length action chunks. However, naively training such policies with standard reinforcement learning fails: the agent either collapses to single-action behavior or over-commits to excessively long sequences. Both failures share a common root cause: the inability to learn chunk boundaries. We propose SPACE, which addresses this challenge by distilling chunk-boundary supervision from trajectory-induced programmatic skills. We induce two-level programmatic skills from successful trajectories, where subskill boundaries serve as direct chunk-boundary supervision. This temporal structure is then distilled into a primitive-chunk policy via hybrid on-/off-policy optimization with chunk-aware credit assignment. Experiments on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld show that SPACE improves success rates by 7.0%-31.3% over the strongest baseline in each setting while reducing average LLM decision rounds by up to 78.9%.

</details>


### [155] [The Dynamics of Continuous Mixture Collapse in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02049)
*Ali Backour*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates why pretrained language models fail to preserve latent-state mixtures (continuous reasoning states) rather than discrete tokens. Three sources of failure are identified: (1) transformer architectures distort mixture geometry, amplified by training; (2) softmax readout and autoregressive feedback form a dynamical system that either amplifies differences until one component dominates or contracts mixtures until they become indistinguishable; (3) exact preservation generally requires context-dependent correction with dimensionality growing with number of components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latent-state reasoning methods in LLMs use continuous states (weighted mixtures of token embeddings) to retain multiple reasoning directions, but pretrained language models often fail to preserve these mixtures. Understanding why is crucial for improving continuous reasoning approaches.

Method: The researchers combined theoretical analysis with controlled empirical investigations across various models. They analyzed the dynamical system formed by softmax readout and autoregressive feedback, derived a theoretical threshold for the transition between contraction and amplification, and verified predictions empirically. They also generalized analysis to mixtures of many components.

Result: Three independent, distinct sources of failure were identified: (1) transformer architectures distort mixture geometry, with training substantially amplifying this effect; (2) the softmax-autoregressive feedback dynamical system exhibits either amplification or contraction behavior, with pretrained models predominantly on the amplifying side; (3) exact preservation requires context-dependent correction whose dimensionality grows with number of components. The theoretical threshold for transition between contraction and amplification was verified empirically.

Conclusion: The study reveals fundamental architectural and dynamical limitations in pretrained language models that prevent them from preserving latent-state mixtures. These findings explain why continuous reasoning approaches struggle with pretrained models and suggest that exact preservation of mixtures generally requires context-dependent correction mechanisms.

Abstract: LLMs latent-state reasoning methods replace discrete intermediate tokens with continuous states, such as weighted mixtures of token embeddings, to retain multiple possible reasoning directions rather than committing to one. Yet pretrained language models often fail to preserve these mixtures. We study why through a combination of theoretical analysis and controlled empirical investigations on a variety of models. We identify three independent, distinct sources of failure. First, transformer architectures already distort mixture geometry, and training substantially amplifies this effect. Moreover, the failure can occur even if the model transports mixtures perfectly linearly: the softmax readout and autoregressive feedback form a dynamical system that either amplifies small differences until one component of the mixture dominates or contracts different mixtures until they become indistinguishable. We verify this theoretical prediction empirically: the observed transition between contraction and amplification occurs near the theoretical threshold derived by our analysis, and pretrained-model rollouts lie predominantly on the amplifying side. Finally, we generalize to mixtures of many components and show that exact preservation generally requires context-dependent correction, whose required dimensionality can grow with the number of components.

</details>


### [156] [DynG-Diff: A State-Aware Dynamic Guidance Diffusion Framework for Probabilistic Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02068)
*Zhente Zhang,Zhengwei Ni,Wei Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DynG-Diff is a variable-sensitive dynamic guidance diffusion framework for probabilistic multivariate time series forecasting that addresses information heterogeneity (varying noise levels across variables) through a two-stage training strategy and adaptive state-aware policy network.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion-based methods for MTS forecasting rely on task-specific conditional paradigms that lack flexibility and struggle with "information heterogeneity" - the significantly varying noise levels and evolutionary patterns across different variables in multivariate time series.

Method: The method comprises three key components: (1) A two-stage separated training strategy using an unconditional diffusion backbone to model joint distribution of multivariate time series; (2) A lightweight state-aware policy network that adaptively infers variable reliability from real-time noisy states and one-step denoising estimates, outputting a dynamic guidance strength matrix; (3) Mathematical formulation of dynamic weight as local precision of observation distribution for precise guidance of high-confidence variables while filtering anomalous noise interference.

Result: Extensive experiments on real-world benchmarks demonstrate competitive probabilistic forecasting performance against state-of-the-art conditional diffusion models and improved robustness under severe observation corruption.

Conclusion: DynG-Diff successfully addresses information heterogeneity in multivariate time series forecasting by dynamically assessing variable reliability and providing adaptive guidance, achieving superior performance in probabilistic forecasting and robustness.

Abstract: Probabilistic multivariate time series (MTS) forecasting is crucial for modeling complex dynamical systems. However, existing diffusion-based methods rely on task-specific conditional paradigms that lack flexibility and struggle with inherent "information heterogeneity"--the significantly varying noise levels and evolutionary patterns across variables. To address this, we propose DynG-Diff, a variable-sensitive dynamic guidance diffusion framework for probabilistic multivariate time-series forecasting: (1) DynG-Diff adopts a two-stage separated training strategy and uses an unconditional diffusion backbone to model the joint distribution of multivariate time series. (2) DynG-Diff introduces a lightweight state-aware policy network that adaptively infers variable reliability from real-time noisy states and one-step denoising estimates, outputting a dynamic guidance strength matrix. (3) DynG-Diff mathematically formulates this dynamic weight as the local precision of the observation distribution, enabling precise guidance for high-confidence variables during inference while filtering out interference from anomalous noise. Extensive experiments on real-world benchmarks demonstrate competitive probabilistic forecasting performance against state-of-the-art conditional diffusion models and improved robustness under severe observation corruption.The implementation code is available at: https://github.com/TT-20011031/DynG-Diff

</details>


### [157] [XMerge: Cross-Axis Selection and Reconstructive Layer Merging for LLM Depth Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02083)
*Jundong Hu,Shekar Ramachandran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: XMerge is a post-training method for compressing transformer language models by removing complete layers while preserving quality. It uses cross-axis selection to identify low-impact blocks and local boundary reconstruction to fit adjacent surviving blocks to match original two-block outputs. It introduces no architectural changes or inference-time parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing depth-compression methods for transformer models lose substantial quality when removing complete layers, and the loss varies unpredictably across different models. This creates a need for a more reliable compression method that preserves model quality across diverse backbones.

Method: XMerge consists of two components: (1) Cross-axis selection identifies blocks with low relative-magnitude and angular hidden-state change; (2) Local boundary reconstruction re-fits the adjacent surviving block to match the original two-block output. The method requires no task labels, end-to-end fine-tuning, architectural changes, or additional inference-time parameters.

Result: Across seven Llama and Qwen backbones (0.5B-8B), five baselines, and three layer-reduction levels, XMerge achieves the largest advantage at the most aggressive removal (k=4): ranks first on six of seven backbones on CORE (22-task aggregate) and six of seven on MMLU. It is the only operator that never collapses, ranking top-2 in both zero-shot and in-context regimes. Ablations show local reconstruction provides most of the gain, while cross-axis fusion helps when selection axes disagree. The additional construction cost is recovered after roughly tens of thousands of requests.

Conclusion: XMerge provides an effective post-training depth compression method that preserves model quality across diverse transformer architectures without requiring architectural modifications or fine-tuning. It outperforms existing baselines at aggressive layer removal while maintaining calibration and avoiding quality collapse.

Abstract: Removing complete transformer layers preserves a standard serving architecture, but existing depth-compression methods can lose substantial quality, and the loss varies unpredictably across models. We introduce XMerge, a post-training method with two components. Cross-axis selection identifies a block with low relative-magnitude and angular hidden-state change, and local boundary reconstruction re-fits the adjacent surviving block to match the original two-block output. XMerge uses no task labels or end-to-end fine-tuning, and it introduces neither architectural changes nor additional inference-time parameters. Across seven Llama and Qwen backbones (0.5B-8B), five published baselines, and three layer-reduction levels, its advantage over baselines is largest at the most aggressive removal: at k=4 it ranks first on six of seven backbones on CORE (a 22-task aggregate) and, separately, on six of seven on MMLU (five of seven on both at once), while avoiding the large perplexity increases of several competing operators. In a task-level bootstrap, the 95% confidence intervals for the three largest CORE margins exclude zero; the remaining margins are consistent with ties. Across the 14 (model, regime) cells it is also the only evaluated operator that never collapses, ranking top-2 in both zero-shot and in-context regimes; on a first calibration probe (one backbone) it is the best-calibrated operator. Ablations show that local reconstruction provides most of the gain, while cross-axis fusion helps when the two selection axes disagree. The additional construction cost is recovered through per-token decode savings after roughly tens of thousands of requests.

</details>


### [158] [TC-Next: Zero-Shot Multimodal Cyclone Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02085)
*Zhe Wang,Sijie Chen,Yiming Luo,Daehyun Kim,Chien-Yi Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TC-Next is a multimodal deep learning model that improves tropical cyclone track and intensity forecasting at 6-24 hour leads by combining atmospheric forecast fields from foundation models (GraphCast, Pangu-Weather, IFS HRES) with satellite imagery (GridSat infrared). It reduces track error by 15-44% and intensity error by 3-6x compared to the rule-based tracker TempestExtremes, and shows strong zero-shot generalization across different forecast systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tropical cyclone tracking methods either rely on rule-based algorithms (like TempestExtremes) which may not fully leverage modern forecast data, or lack the ability to effectively combine multiple data modalities (atmospheric fields and satellite imagery). This work aims to create a more accurate and generalizable cyclone forecasting system by integrating foundation model atmospheric forecasts with satellite observations.

Method: TC-Next uses a multimodal deep learning architecture that takes as input: (1) atmospheric kinematic and thermodynamic forecast fields from foundation models (GraphCast), and (2) GridSat infrared satellite imagery. The model was trained on GraphCast forecasts over the Western Pacific region. It was then applied without retraining to other forecast systems (Pangu-Weather, IFS HRES) and to WeatherNext Cyclones for zero-shot evaluation.

Result: Key results: (1) On GraphCast forecasts, TC-Next reduces track error by 15-44% and intensity error by 3-6x compared to TempestExtremes; (2) Applied to Pangu-Weather and IFS HRES without retraining, it outperforms TempestExtremes on both track and intensity; (3) Zero-shot on WeatherNext Cyclones 2025 season achieves lower intensity error at all lead times and lower/comparable track error compared to that model's direct tracker. Ablation studies confirm the multimodal approach improves tracking at all lead times and intensity prediction at longer lead times.

Conclusion: TC-Next demonstrates that a multimodal deep learning approach combining atmospheric forecast fields with satellite imagery can significantly improve tropical cyclone forecasting. The model shows strong cross-system generalization without retraining, suggesting it learns generalizable features of cyclone structure and evolution. The additional satellite modality provides consistent benefits, particularly for intensity prediction at longer lead times.

Abstract: We present TropicalCycloneNext (TC-Next), a multimodal deep learning model that forecasts tropical cyclone track and intensity at $6$-$24$ h leads by leveraging a foundation model's forecast fields of atmospheric kinematic and thermodynamic fields and GridSat infrared satellite imagery. Trained only on GraphCast forecasts over the Western Pacific (WP), yet reliant only on generic atmospheric variables, TC-Next on GraphCast lowers track error by $15$-$44\%$ and intensity error by a factor of $3$-$6$ relative to a conventional, rule-based tracker, TempestExtremes; applied without retraining to the forecast fields of Pangu-Weather and IFS HRES, it stays ahead of TempestExtremes on both. Applied zero-shot to the generic weather fields of WeatherNext Cyclones on the 2025 WP season, TC-Next attains lower intensity error at every lead time, and lower or comparable track error, compared to that model's specialized direct tracker in a deterministic comparison. Our ablation studies show that our multimodal model is able to utilize the additional modality to improve performance in tracking errors at every lead time and in intensity prediction at longer lead times.

</details>


### [159] [Compositional Spectral Prompts for LLM-based Online Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02093)
*Seungyoon Choi,Hyunchul Kim,Jae-Gil Lee,Chanyoung Park*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoSPOT is an LLM-based online time series forecasting framework that uses frozen pre-trained LLMs with compositional spectral prompts grounded in frequency-domain bases for efficient online adaptation, addressing long-term adaptation and generalization to unseen patterns in non-stationary environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory buffer-based retrieval strategies for OTSF struggle with long-term adaptation and fail to generalize to unseen patterns. The motivation is to leverage the strong few-shot capabilities of pre-trained LLMs to address these limitations.

Method: CoSPOT keeps the LLM frozen and employs compositional spectral prompts: (1) decomposes time series into frequency bases, (2) composes corresponding spectral basis prompts according to their amplitudes, allowing unseen patterns to be represented as new combinations of learned basis prompts.

Result: Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate superiority and practicality across challenging online scenarios, including extended online phases and cross-dataset settings with substantial distribution shifts.

Conclusion: CoSPOT effectively addresses long-term adaptation and generalization challenges in online time series forecasting by combining LLM few-shot capabilities with parameter-efficient spectral prompting.

Abstract: To address the sequential and evolving nature of time series, the Online Time Series Forecasting (OTSF) task has been extensively studied in multiple domains. Existing research focuses on adapting to non-stationary environments by employing memory buffer-based retrieval strategies. However, we observe that such frameworks struggle with long-term adaptation and fail to generalize to unseen patterns. To this end, we introduce CoSPOT, an LLM-based online time series forecasting framework that leverages a pre-trained LLM as the backbone online forecaster, motivated by its strong few-shot capabilities. For efficient online adaptation, CoSPOT keeps the LLM frozen and employs compositional spectral prompts grounded in frequency-domain bases to guide the model with the overall distribution of the input, thereby substantially reducing the number of parameters updated during the online phase. Specifically, CoSPOT decomposes time series into frequency bases and composes the corresponding spectral basis prompts according to their amplitudes, allowing unseen patterns to be represented as new combinations of learned basis prompts. Our extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate the superiority and practicality of CoSPOT across challenging online scenarios, including extended online phases and cross-dataset settings with substantial distribution shifts. Our code is available at https://github.com/seungyoon-Choi/CoSPOT.

</details>


### [160] [Federated LoRA Adaptation of BiomedCLIP Across Four International Chest X-Ray Cohorts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02101)
*Sanjaya Poudel,Nirajan Kunwor,Manish Dhakal,Debesh Jha,Sunil Kumar Gaire*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that federated learning with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) enables collaborative fine-tuning of biomedical vision-language models (BiomedCLIP) across heterogeneous institutions without sharing patient data. The approach achieves significant improvements in chest radiograph classification AUC across four geographically distributed cohorts (USA, Vietnam, Spain).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical imaging data are archived behind privacy regulations, and institutions differ widely in scanners, protocols, and compute. This heterogeneity raises questions about how federated LoRA updates should be aggregated, especially as multimodal vision-language models become central to medical image analysis.

Method: The researchers benchmarked federated Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) of BiomedCLIP for chest radiograph classification across four public cohorts on three continents. They compared: (1) Federated LoRA adaptation vs. unadapted backbone, (2) Federated vs. isolated single-cohort training, (3) FlexLoRA's SVD-based product-space aggregation vs. naive factor averaging, and (4) FedProx vs. FedAvg optimizers.

Result: Federated LoRA adaptation improved shared-class AUC on all four cohorts from mean 0.687 to 0.802. Relative to isolated training, federation improved weaker cohorts while preserving the strongest, approaching a centralized reference (0.812). The SVD-based product-space aggregation (FlexLoRA) was essential—naive factor averaging dropped mean AUC by 0.097. FedProx showed no benefit over FedAvg.

Conclusion: Biomedical vision-language models can be adapted collaboratively across heterogeneous, geographically distributed institutions without centralizing data. The FlexLoRA aggregation method is critical for achieving performance gains, while drift-correcting optimizers like FedProx provide no additional benefit due to LoRA's inherent low-rank constraints.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) lets institutions train a shared model without exchanging data, and Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) makes this practical at scale by communicating only compact low-rank updates. Biomedical imaging is a compelling setting for this combination: patient data are archived behind privacy regulations, and institutions differ widely in scanners, protocols, and compute. Such heterogeneity raises the question of how federated LoRA updates should be aggregated, increasingly pressing as multimodal vision-language models become central to medical image analysis. We benchmark federated Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) of BiomedCLIP for chest radiograph classification across four public cohorts on three continents (USA, Vietnam, Spain). Federated LoRA adaptation improves shared-class AUC on all four cohorts over the unadapted BiomedCLIP backbone (mean 0.687 to 0.802), showing that the gains come from federated adaptation rather than from the pretrained model's zero-shot ability. Relative to isolated single-cohort training, federation improves the weaker cohorts while largely preserving the strongest and approaches a centralized reference (0.812) that pools all data. The singular value decomposition (SVD)-based product-space aggregation introduced by FlexLoRA is essential to this gain (naive factor averaging drops mean AUC by 0.097), whereas a drift-correcting optimizer (FedProx) shows no benefit over FedAvg in our single-seed runs, consistent with LoRA's low-rank updates already limiting client drift. Biomedical vision-language models can thus be adapted collaboratively across heterogeneous, geographically distributed institutions without centralizing data.

</details>
