### [161] [A Unified Rate-Distortion Perspective on Vector, Product, and Scalar Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02107)
*Xianghong Fang,Wenlong Mou,Yuan Yuan,Dehan Kong,Tim G. J. Rudner*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a unified rate-distortion framework for understanding and comparing discrete visual tokenization methods (VQ, SQ, PQ). The key insight is that minimizing distortion, not maximizing codebook utilization, is the primary objective for reconstruction fidelity. The authors establish fairness conditions for fair comparison and empirically show that modern VQ methods achieve the lowest distortion under controlled conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete visual tokenization methods (vector quantization, scalar quantization, product quantization) lack a unified conceptual framework for understanding their quantization tradeoffs. This makes it difficult to compare different methods fairly and understand the fundamental principles behind their effectiveness.

Method: The authors propose viewing quantization as lossy compression, characterizing nominal fixed-length coding rate through token count and codebook size, and quantization error as distortion. They establish two critical fairness conditions: (1) controlling latent feature statistics and (2) enforcing identical coding rates for fair comparison.

Result: The paper shows that: (1) minimizing distortion is the primary intrinsic objective for reconstruction fidelity, with direct connection to STE-induced gradient discrepancy; (2) under controlled fairness conditions, the VQ-PQ-SQ distortion hierarchy is recovered; (3) modern VQ methods achieve the lowest distortion among the methods tested.

Conclusion: This work provides a foundational rate-distortion reframing of modern discrete visual tokenization, resolves ambiguities in quantizer evaluation, and offers a controlled framework for isolating intrinsic quantization effectiveness under fixed-rate constraints. It establishes a systematic approach for comparing quantization methods fairly.

Abstract: Discrete visual tokenization, predominantly driven by vector, scalar, and product quantization, lacks a unified conceptual framework for understanding quantization tradeoffs. In this paper, we propose a unified rate--distortion perspective on modern discrete visual tokenization. By viewing quantization as lossy compression, we characterize the nominal fixed-length coding rate through token count and codebook size, and quantization error as the distortion. Within this framework, we resolve three central questions. First, we theoretically and empirically show that minimizing distortion, rather than maximizing codebook utilization, is the primary intrinsic objective for reconstruction fidelity, with a direct connection to the STE-induced gradient discrepancy. Second, we establish two critical fairness conditions for intrinsic quantization comparison: controlling latent feature statistics and enforcing identical coding rates. Third, under these conditions, we recover the VQ--PQ--SQ distortion hierarchy in modern visual tokenization and show empirically that modern VQ methods achieve the lowest distortion. This work provides a foundational rate--distortion reframing of modern discrete visual tokenization, resolves ambiguities in quantizer evaluation, and provides a controlled framework for isolating intrinsic quantization effectiveness under fixed-rate constraints.

</details>


### [162] [A Computational Comparison of Fourier Spectral Differentiation and Spatial Automatic Differentiation in Periodic Physics-Informed Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02110)
*Xilai Liang,Zhao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares spatial automatic differentiation (AD) and Fourier spectral differentiation in periodic physical-space PINNs. Fourier differentiation yields 2.90x-18.52x speedups and reduces GPU memory by 68.7%-94.1%, while maintaining comparable solution accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) use automatic differentiation to evaluate spatial derivatives in PDE residuals, which incurs substantial computational and memory costs when multiple or high-order derivatives are required. This work seeks a more efficient alternative for periodic domains.

Method: Controlled comparison holding constant: neural representation, temporal differentiation, optimizer, sampling procedure, and training schedule. The Fourier variant evaluates network outputs on a uniform periodic grid, transforms to Fourier space, and obtains spatial derivatives through spectral multiplication, reusing Fourier coefficients across derivative orders. Experiments conducted on standard PINNs for Allen-Cahn and KdV equations, and Causal PINNs for Allen-Cahn, KdV, and Kuramoto-Sivashinsky equations.

Result: Fourier differentiation yields mean paired end-to-end training speedups ranging from 2.90x to 18.52x and reduces peak allocated GPU memory by 68.7%--94.1%. Final relative L2 errors remain of the same order, with neither differentiation procedure showing a consistent accuracy advantage.

Conclusion: For 1D periodic benchmarks, Fourier spectral differentiation provides substantially lower training time and memory usage than spatial AD while retaining comparable solution error, at the cost of requiring a uniform structured spatial grid.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) commonly evaluate the spatial derivatives appearing in partial differential equation residuals using automatic differentiation (AD), whose computational and memory costs can become substantial when multiple or high-order derivatives are required. We perform a controlled comparison of spatial AD and Fourier spectral differentiation in periodic physical-space PINNs. Within each paired experiment, the neural representation, temporal differentiation, optimizer, sampling procedure, and training schedule are held fixed, so that the two cases differ only in the spatial differentiation procedure. For the Fourier variant, network outputs are evaluated on a uniform periodic grid and transformed to Fourier space, where spatial derivatives are obtained through spectral multiplication and the same Fourier coefficients are reused across derivative orders. We compare the two procedures in standard PINNs for the Allen--Cahn and Korteweg--de Vries equations and in Causal PINNs for the Allen--Cahn, Korteweg--de Vries, and Kuramoto--Sivashinsky equations. Across these five equation--framework settings, Fourier differentiation yields mean paired end-to-end training speedups ranging from $2.90\times$ to $18.52\times$ and reduces peak allocated graphics processing unit (GPU) memory by $68.7\%$--$94.1\%$. The final relative $L_2$ errors remain of the same order, with neither differentiation procedure showing a consistent accuracy advantage. For the one-dimensional periodic benchmarks considered here, Fourier spectral differentiation therefore provides substantially lower training time and memory usage than spatial AD while retaining comparable solution error, at the cost of requiring a uniform structured spatial grid.

</details>


### [163] [Scalable Bayesian Optimization of Composite Functions for Image-Based Inverse Problems in Materials Characterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02126)
*Dasol Yoon,Poompol Buathong,Chia-Hao Lee,Yujia Zhang,David A. Muller,Peter I. Frazier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SBOCF is a simulation-efficient Bayesian optimization method for estimating physical parameters (specimen thickness and crystal mistilt) from PACBED electron microscopy images, reducing computational cost by representing images with patch-level summaries and two correction terms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for estimating physical parameters from scientific images rely on expensive physics-based simulations. Grid searches scale poorly and neural networks require extensive pretraining that may not transfer to new conditions. There is a need for a simulation-efficient method that can work without task-specific pretraining.

Method: SBOCF exploits the known composite structure of the image-matching objective and intermediate information in simulated images. It represents PACBED images with patch-level summaries and two correction terms, reducing modeled outputs from 24,649 to 11 while preserving the original pixel-wise objective. Bayesian optimization with expected improvement is used to find optimal parameters.

Result: Under 50 simulator evaluations, SBOCF reduced median final SSE by up to 290x compared to standard Bayesian optimization on synthetic SrTiO3 benchmarks (thick and thin specimens). On experimental data, SBOCF produced parameter estimates consistent with previously reported values. For a simulated mistilted specimen, using SBOCF estimates in ptychographic reconstruction recovered sharp atoms that were otherwise blurred.

Conclusion: SBOCF establishes a promising approach for inverse problems involving expensive simulators and high-dimensional structured outputs, enabling parameter estimation without task-specific pretraining while maintaining simulation efficiency.

Abstract: Estimating physical parameters from scientific images is a common inverse problem in materials characterization that often relies on expensive physics-based simulations. In electron microscopy, specimen thickness and crystal mistilt are critical parameters that govern how electrons scatter through the sample, and therefore the accuracy of any atomic-scale structure recovered from it. They are commonly inferred by matching experimental position-averaged convergent-beam electron diffraction (PACBED) patterns to simulated ones, but grid searches scale poorly and neural-network methods require extensive pretraining that may not transfer to new conditions. Here, we propose scalable Bayesian optimization of composite functions (SBOCF), a simulation-efficient method that exploits the known composite structure of the image-matching objective and the intermediate information contained in simulated images. By representing PACBED images with patch-level summaries and two correction terms, SBOCF preserves the original pixel-wise objective while reducing the number of modeled outputs from 24,649 to 11. Under a budget of 50 simulator evaluations, SBOCF outperformed standard Bayesian optimization with expected improvement on synthetic SrTiO3 benchmarks with thick and thin specimens, reducing the median final SSE by up to 290x in the thick-sample case. On experimental data, SBOCF produced parameter estimates consistent with previously reported values without task-specific pretraining. For a simulated mistilted specimen, using the SBOCF estimates in a downstream ptychographic reconstruction recovered sharp atoms that were otherwise blurred. These results establish SBOCF as a promising approach for inverse problems involving expensive simulators and high-dimensional structured outputs.

</details>


### [164] [Online Non-Monotone DR-Submodular Maximization Matching the Offline $0.401$ Factor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02145)
*Vaneet Aggarwal,Yiyang Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper achieves an online approximation factor of 0.401 for maximizing nonnegative, non-monotone DR-submodular functions, matching the best offline guarantee. The algorithm uses a weighted online learner instead of running offline construction on changing objectives, achieving sublinear approximate regret in the post-decision full-information value-oracle model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work achieved 1/e online approximation factor, while the offline best was 0.401. This paper aims to close this gap by achieving the offline 0.401 factor online, addressing the challenge of maintaining approximation quality under adversarial sequence of functions.

Method: The algorithm replaces the offline objective-dependent box step with a weighted online learner that controls residual terms cumulatively. An asymmetric balance theorem preserves offline coefficients despite adversarial variation. The method uses O(dT^{1/4}) oracle calls per round with O(T^{3/4}) regret. General batching achieves O(T^δ) calls per round with O(T^{4/5-δ/5}) regret. Under positive-anchor condition, randomized blocking retains the factor with O(T^{5/6}) one-point bandit regret.

Result: Achieved 0.401 approximation factor online, matching the offline optimum. The algorithm attains sublinear approximate regret when oracle feedback is conditionally unbiased and bounded. Direct implementation: O(T^{3/4}) regret, O(dT^{1/4}) oracle calls per round. Batching variant: O(T^δ) calls per round, O(T^{4/5-δ/5}) regret. One-call endpoint achieves O(T^{4/5}) regret. With positive-anchor condition: O(T^{5/6}) one-point bandit regret.

Conclusion: The 0.401 approximation factor, previously achievable only offline, is also achievable online. This closes the gap between offline and online guarantees for DR-submodular maximization. The approach uses online learning techniques rather than running offline algorithms on changing objectives, with theoretical guarantees on regret and oracle complexity.

Abstract: We study online maximization of nonnegative, non-monotone DR-submodular functions over compact convex down-closed subsets of the $d$-dimensional unit cube. The best known constructive offline approximation factor is $0.401$ under the corresponding meta-solvability assumptions, whereas comparable adversarial online guarantees had remained at $1/e$. We show that this factor is also achievable online. In the post-decision full-information value-oracle model, our algorithm attains factor $0.401$ with sublinear approximate regret when oracle feedback is conditionally unbiased and bounded.
  The online algorithm does not run the offline construction on a changing objective. Instead, it replaces the offline objective-dependent box step by a weighted online learner that controls the required residual terms cumulatively. An exact asymmetric balance theorem preserves the offline coefficients despite adversarial variation. The direct implementation has $O(T^{3/4})$ regret and uses $O(dT^{1/4})$ oracle calls per round. More generally, for every $δ\in[0,1/4]$, batching gives $O(T^δ)$ calls per round and $O(T^{4/5-δ/5})$ regret, including a one-call $O(T^{4/5})$ endpoint. Under a positive-anchor condition, randomized blocking retains factor $0.401$ with $O(T^{5/6})$ one-point bandit regret.

</details>


### [165] [Exact Limits of Random Projections for Preserving Geometry: Distance Recovery, Nearest-Neighbor Rankings, and Covariance Shape in Gaussian Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02155)
*Piyush Sao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) lemma guarantees distance preservation via random projections, but this paper shows the JL bound is uninformative for high-dimensional data because distances concentrate. Even an independent random replacement can satisfy the JL bound. The authors analyze how well any decoder can recover features of squared distances from linear sketches, finding that the k-th singular value scales as (m/d)^(k/2), leading to sharp consequences: only m/d fraction of variance retained, Kendall correlation scales as √(m/d), and nearest-neighbor agreement tends to 1/q.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The JL lemma guarantees that random projections preserve pairwise distances, but in high dimensions, distances concentrate around a baseline. The authors seek to determine whether JL distance preservation actually guarantees useful geometric information for inference tasks.

Method: The authors analyze feature recovery from linear sketches under squared-error loss. They use conditional expectation as the optimal decoder and diagonalize the recovery operator in closed form for isotropic Gaussian data (Σ=σ²I_d). They analyze the singular values in the regime where k is fixed and m,d-m→∞.

Result: For isotropic Gaussian data, the k-th singular value satisfies ℓ_k ≈ (m/d)^(k/2). This yields three consequences: (1) rank-m sketch retains at most m/d fraction of variance; (2) expected Kendall correlation is (2/π)√(m/d) when m→∞ and m/d→0; (3) nearest-neighbor agreement tends to 1/q for fixed q. After removing scale, Haar-averaged retained covariance-shape information is (m/d)².

Conclusion: JL distance preservation does not quantify the geometry available for comparison or inference. One projection can satisfy JL while mean Kendall correlation vanishes when log n ≪ m ≪ d. The key insight is that distance preservation alone is insufficient for downstream tasks like ranking or classification.

Abstract: The Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) lemma guarantees that a random projection of $n$ points to
  $m=O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log n)$ dimensions preserves pairwise squared distances within relative
  error $\varepsilon$ with high probability, and this dimension order is asymptotically
  optimal. In high dimensions, however, distances concentrate around a baseline while key
  geometric information lies in much smaller fluctuations. We show that the JL bound can
  therefore be uninformative about retained geometry: an independent Gaussian replacement map
  can satisfy it even though the replacement cloud is independent of the original data.
  We then ask how well any decoder can recover a feature $f(D)$ of a squared distance $D$ from
  a linear sketch. Under squared-error loss, the optimal decoder is conditional expectation, so
  recovery defines a linear operator whose singular values quantify feature recovery. For
  isotropic Gaussian data ($Σ=σ^2 I_d$), we diagonalize this operator in closed form.
  For fixed $k$ with $m,d-m\to\infty$, its $k$th singular value satisfies $\ell_k\approx(m/
  d)^{k/2}$.
  This yields three sharp consequences. A rank-$m$ sketch retains at most an $m/d$ fraction of
  the variance of any feature of one squared distance. If $m\to\infty$ and $m/d\to0$, the
  expected Kendall correlation is $\frac{2}π\sqrt{m/d}(1+o(1))$; for fixed $q$, nearest-
  neighbor agreement tends to $1/q$. Yet one projection can satisfy the JL bound while mean
  Kendall correlation vanishes when $\log n\ll m\ll d$. After removing scale, Haar-averaged
  retained covariance-shape information is $(m/d)^2$. Thus JL distance preservation does not
  quantify the geometry available for comparison or inference.

</details>


### [166] [GeoSPRINT: Geometric Redundancy-Aware Step Pruning for Inference in Diffusion Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02160)
*Arpita Joshi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GeoSPRINT is a training-free framework that accelerates diffusion model inference by constructing non-uniform sampling schedules from the geometry of denoising trajectories. It uses a hyperplanarity test (via QR factorization) to detect geometrically redundant steps and allocates more steps to high-curvature regions of the trajectory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models achieve high sample quality but remain expensive at inference time because sampling requires many sequential neural function evaluations (NFEs). Existing acceleration methods either use fixed step-skipping schedules, adapt step sizes based on local numerical error, or require additional training. There is a need for a training-free method that can intelligently allocate inference steps based on trajectory geometry.

Method: GeoSPRINT detects geometrically redundant steps using a hyperplanarity test in latent space, implemented efficiently via QR factorization. The resulting redundancy profile is converted into a sampling schedule that allocates more steps to high-curvature regions of the trajectory. Additionally, the trajectory projection score α_traj is introduced as a model-free diagnostic for rectified flow quality, quantifying trajectory straightness via residual variance.

Result: On CIFAR-10 (32×32), GeoSPRINT improves FID by 0.7-1.1 over DDIM across 49-89 NFEs and surpasses DPM-Solver++ at NFE≥30 despite using a first-order DDIM solver. On LSUN Church (256×256), it reduces FID from 1.48 to 1.26 at 52 steps. On Stable Diffusion v1.5 (512×512 latent), it achieves up to 1.93 FID improvement over DDIM.

Conclusion: Trajectory geometry provides a useful global signal for allocating inference steps in diffusion models. Schedule quality can substantially improve diffusion sampling efficiency without retraining, making high-quality image generation more practical.

Abstract: Diffusion models achieve high sample quality but remain expensive at inference time because sampling requires many sequential neural function evaluations (NFEs). Existing acceleration methods either use fixed step-skipping schedules, adapt step sizes based on local numerical error, or require additional training. We introduce GeoSPRINT (Geometric Step Pruning for Inference in Trajectories), a training-free framework for constructing non-uniform sampling schedules from the geometry of denoising trajectories. GeoSPRINT detects geometrically redundant steps using a hyperplanarity test in latent space, implemented efficiently via QR factorization, and converts the resulting redundancy profile into a sampling schedule that allocates more steps to high-curvature regions of the trajectory. In addition, we introduce the trajectory projection score $α_{\mathrm{traj}}$, a residual-variance metric that quantifies trajectory straightness and serves as a model-free diagnostic for rectified flow quality. Across CIFAR-10 ($32{\times}32$), LSUN Church ($256{\times}256$), and Stable Diffusion v1.5 ($512{\times}512$ latent), GeoSPRINT consistently improves over uniform DDIM (Denoising Diffusion Implicit Models) schedules at matched NFE budgets. On CIFAR-10, GeoSPRINT improves FID (Fréchet Inception Distance) by 0.7-1.1 over DDIM across 49-89 NFEs and surpasses DPM-Solver++ at NFE${\geq}30$ despite using a first-order DDIM solver. On LSUN Church, it reduces FID from 1.48 to 1.26 at 52 steps, and on Stable Diffusion v1.5 it achieves up to 1.93 FID improvement over DDIM. These results show that trajectory geometry provides a useful global signal for allocating inference steps and that schedule quality can substantially improve diffusion sampling efficiency without retraining.

</details>


### [167] [DMRL: Document-Mediated Reinforcement Learning for Skill Optimization in Advertising Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02170)
*Wei Zhang,Hongji Li,Song Sun,Peng Yu,Xue Yang,Lei Zhao,Peng Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DMRL is a skill self-evolution framework for advertising recommendation that uses LLM-based skill documents with structured editing actions. It features Dual-Relative Policy Optimization (DRPO) for robust advantage estimation and Long-term Reward Predictor (LRP) for outcome prediction, outperforming baselines on a large-scale short-video ads platform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based skill documents for advertising recommendation lack a principled mechanism to attribute rewards to specific document edits, making skill optimization largely prompt-driven and ineffective for continuous improvement.

Method: DMRL employs a hierarchical agent architecture: an upper-level agent performs controlled document edits while a frozen lower-level task agent evaluates effects via A/B testing. Two key components address credit assignment: (1) DRPO for robust and risk-aware advantage estimation, and (2) LRP using disentangled representation learning and cross-attention transfer to predict long-term outcomes from population heterogeneity.

Result: DMRL was deployed on a large-scale short-video ads platform and demonstrated superior performance over state-of-the-art baselines across key advertising metrics.

Conclusion: The framework successfully enables skill document self-evolution through structured editing actions, with DRPO providing robust advantage estimation and LRP enabling long-term outcome prediction, resulting in improved advertising performance.

Abstract: Advertising recommendation requires continuously tuning complex system parameters while balancing commercial returns and user experience. Recent work has introduced large language models (LLMs) with skill documents to assist this labor-intensive process, but skill optimization remains largely prompt-driven, lacking a principled mechanism to attribute rewards to specific document edits. To address this limitation, we propose Document-Mediated Reinforcement Learning (DMRL), a skill self-evolution framework that models skill document optimization as a sequence of structured editing actions. In DMRL, an upper-level agent performs controlled document edits, while a frozen lower-level task agent evaluates their effects through A/B testing. To address credit assignment and long-term outcomes, we introduce two key components: (1) Dual-Relative Policy Optimization (DRPO), a post-training policy optimization method for robust and risk-aware advantage estimation; and (2) Long-term Reward Predictor (LRP), which estimates long-term outcomes by modeling population heterogeneity with disentangled representation learning and cross-attention transfer. DMRL was deployed on a large-scale short-video ads platform and extensive empirical evaluation shows that DMRL outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across key advertising metrics

</details>


### [168] [Learning the Constitutive Behavior of Materials via Neural Operators and Causal Attention: Case Studies in Plasticity and Damage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02194)
*Rishabh Arora,Lisa Scheunemann,Tim Brepols,Shahed Rezaei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a data-driven constitutive modeling framework that treats material deformation as a functional mapping from strain history to stress response, using causally masked attention, spectral convolutions, and sinusoidal activations to capture path-dependent inelastic behavior without requiring explicit internal state variables. The method achieves accurate predictions of irreversible deformation with resolution invariance and parallel efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional constitutive modeling of path-dependent inelastic materials relies on internal state variables whose evolution equations must be manually postulated based on domain knowledge and calibrated against experimental data. However, in practical settings, these internal variables are typically not directly measurable, forcing researchers to infer constitutive responses entirely from measurable strain-stress data without prior knowledge of the material's internal state.

Method: The paper proposes a data-driven constitutive modeling framework based on the concept of a material operator, which treats a deforming material as a functional mapping from its entire strain history to the corresponding stress response. Key technical innovations include: (1) Training directly on full loading paths as function-to-function mappings (not traditional autoregressive or recurrent formulations), predicting complete stress trajectories in a single parallel forward pass; (2) A causally masked attention mechanism to enforce temporal path dependence by restricting the model's attention to past material states while preserving computational parallelizability; (3) Spectral convolutions for discretization-invariant representations in the frequency domain; (4) Sinusoidal activation functions to resolve strong nonlinear transitions inherent in inelastic regimes.

Result: The framework was evaluated across multidimensional, rate-independent material models exhibiting complex phenomena, with emphasis on nonlinear plasticity and ductile damage accumulation. The results demonstrated accurate and robust predictions of irreversible deformation mechanisms while simultaneously achieving resolution invariance and excellent parallel efficiency.

Conclusion: The proposed material operator framework successfully enables data-driven constitutive modeling without requiring explicit knowledge of internal state variables. The combination of causal attention, spectral convolutions, and sinusoidal activations allows for capturing highly adaptive, non-local history dependence while maintaining computational efficiency. This approach represents a significant advancement in data-driven mechanics, offering resolution invariance and parallel scalability for modeling complex inelastic material behavior.

Abstract: Classical constitutive modeling of path-dependent inelastic materials relies on internal state variables whose evolution equations must be postulated based on domain knowledge and calibrated against experimental data. However, in many practical settings, the relevant internal variables are typically not measurable in experiments, and the constitutive response must be inferred entirely from measured strain-stress data without any prior knowledge of the material's internal state. We propose a data-driven constitutive modeling framework based on the concept of a material operator, which treats a deforming material as a functional mapping from its entire strain history to the corresponding stress response. In contrast to traditional autoregressive or recurrent formulations, the model is trained directly on full loading paths as function-to-function mappings, predicting complete stress trajectories in a single parallel forward pass. Temporal path dependence is enforced through a causally masked attention mechanism embedded within the operator, which restricts the model's attention to past material states while preserving computational parallelizability. Spectral convolutions provide discretization-invariant representations in the frequency domain, while causal attention captures highly adaptive, non-local history dependence. Furthermore, sinusoidal activation functions are used to resolve the strong nonlinear transitions inherent in inelastic regimes. The framework is evaluated across multidimensional, rate-independent material models exhibiting complex phenomena, with an emphasis on nonlinear plasticity and ductile damage accumulation. The results demonstrate accurate and robust predictions of irreversible deformation mechanisms while simultaneously achieving resolution invariance and excellent parallel efficiency.

</details>


### [169] [SMart: A Multi-source Multi-phase Time Series Representation Transfer Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02203)
*Fang He,Wang-chien Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SMart is a novel time series representation learning framework that combines multi-phase recurrence plots recovery tasks with a multi-source dataset selector to improve transfer learning for time series classification and regression tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two limitations exist in current TSRL research: 1) transformer-based frameworks use technically simple self-supervised recovery tasks, and 2) single-source dataset approaches limit representation transfer. This work aims to enhance both aspects through novel mechanisms.

Method: The proposed SMart framework incorporates two novel mechanisms: 1) A multi-phase recurrence plots recovery task in three alternative modes to guide the encoder in embedding time series dynamics into representations; 2) A source dataset selector to choose multiple suitable source datasets to supplement the target dataset for pre-training.

Result: SMart outperforms several state-of-the-art models for time series representation learning, classification and regression on both univariate and multivariate datasets, reducing mean absolute error up to 19.5% for regression and increasing average accuracy up to 1.34% for classification.

Conclusion: The multi-phase recurrence plots recovery task and multi-source dataset selector effectively address the limitations of simple recovery tasks and single-source transfer, demonstrating superior performance across various time series analysis tasks.

Abstract: Time series representation learning (TSRL) has attracted growing research interests in recent years. Two recent explorations in TSRL are: i) exploiting a transformer-based framework to learn time series; ii) instead of using only the targeted dataset, borrowing time series from other datasets to to facilitate representation transfer. While these two explorations are shown effective, the self-supervised time series recovery task in (i) and the single-source dataset used in (ii) are technically simple and thus can be enhanced with new ideas. In this work, we propose a new TSRL framework, namely multi-source multi-phase time series representation transfer (SMart), which has two novel mechanisms to address the aforementioned deficiencies: 1) a multi-phase recurrence plots recovery task, in three alternative modes, for guiding the encoder to embed time series dynamics into the time series representation; and 2) a source dataset selector to select multiple suitable source datasets to supplement the original target dataset for pre-training the TSRL encoder. Experimental results show that SMart outperforms several state-of-the-art models for time series representation learning, classification and regression on both uni-variate and multi-variate time series datasets, reducing mean absolute error up to 19.5% for time series regression, and increasing average accuracy up to 1.34\% for time series classification.

</details>


### [170] [Recursive Value Learning for Long-Horizon Offline Goal-Conditioned RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02237)
*Hyeonseong Jeon,Youngwoon Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DCRL is a novel offline goal-conditioned RL method that uses recursive binary tree decomposition of trajectory segments to address error accumulation in long-horizon tasks, achieving state-of-the-art results on challenging benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling offline GCRL to long-horizon tasks faces two key challenges: (1) long-range value learning depends on shorter-range estimates that may be inaccurate, and (2) max-based value backups amplify overestimation through repeated propagation.

Method: DCRL recursively decomposes each trajectory segment into a balanced binary tree and trains values from leaves to root. Each parent is updated only after its children using exact factorization of observed routes. It jointly propagates values across trajectories to discover shorter routes. The balanced binary tree reduces worst-case bootstrap depth from linear to logarithmic.

Result: DCRL substantially outperforms prior flat offline GCRL methods. On the five most challenging long-horizon OGBench tasks, it improves the best prior average score from 55 to 64, surpassing all flat and hierarchical baselines.

Conclusion: The balanced binary tree structure enables much slower error accumulation due to shorter dependency structure. DCRL successfully addresses the challenge of scaling offline GCRL to long-horizon tasks.

Abstract: Scaling offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning (GCRL) to long-horizon tasks is difficult because (1) long-range value learning depends on shorter-range estimates that may still be inaccurate, and (2) max-based value backups can amplify overestimation through repeated propagation. We propose DCRL (Divide-and-Conquer RL), which recursively decomposes each trajectory segment into a balanced binary tree and trains the values from leaves to root. Each parent is therefore updated only after its children, using an exact factorization of the observed route rather than selecting among noisy alternatives. Since this objective learns values along demonstrated routes that are not necessarily optimal, DCRL jointly propagates values across trajectories to discover shorter routes. Thanks to the balanced binary tree, DCRL reduces worst-case bootstrap depth from linear to logarithmic, and this shorter dependency structure empirically corresponds to much slower error accumulation. Across diverse goal-reaching tasks, DCRL substantially outperforms prior flat offline GCRL methods, and on the five most challenging long-horizon OGBench tasks, it improves the best prior average score from 55 to 64, surpassing all flat and hierarchical baselines.

</details>


### [171] [Similarity-Aware Personalized Federated Learning in Heterogeneous Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02241)
*Arun Kumar A,Sunil Gupta,Dang Ngyuen,Bao Duong,Dat Phan Trong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAPE-FL is a novel personalization framework for Federated Learning that addresses the problem of poor global generalization and degraded local performance caused by distribution mismatch across clients. It uses dual anchoring to both the global model and a similarity-weighted peer averaged model, with dynamic client-specific regularization based on model and output similarity to filter out dissimilar clients and mitigate negative transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In Federated Learning, distribution mismatch across clients leads to poor global generalization and degraded local client-level performance. In heterogeneous settings, some clients' local models may outperform the global model, making collaborative FL less beneficial. The key challenge is balancing global knowledge transfer with local personalization while avoiding negative transfer from dissimilar clients.

Method: SAPE-FL anchors each client's model to both the global model and a similarity-weighted peer averaged model. It uses dynamic, client-specific regularization based on model similarity and output similarity to: 1) adaptively balance global knowledge transfer and peer collaboration, 2) filter out dissimilar clients. This dual anchoring approach mitigates negative transfer and enhances robustness in heterogeneous settings.

Result: SAPE-FL outperforms state-of-the-art methods under high statistical heterogeneity and low client data regimes.

Conclusion: The proposed SAPE-FL framework effectively addresses the challenges of Federated Learning in heterogeneous settings by combining global model knowledge with peer collaboration through similarity-aware personalization. The theoretical convergence guarantees and empirical results demonstrate its effectiveness, particularly in scenarios with high statistical heterogeneity and limited local data.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) allows decentralized clients to train models collaboratively while preserving data privacy. However, distribution mismatch across clients often leads to poor global generalization and degraded local client-level performance. In such scenarios, some of the clients with their local models trained solely on local data may perform better than the globally learnt model, thus nullifying the benefits of collaborative federated learning. To address this, we propose SAPE-FL (Similarity-Aware Personalized Federated Learning), a novel personalization framework that anchors each client's model to both the global model and a similarity-weighted peer averaged model. By incorporating dynamic, client-specific regularization based on both model similarity and output similarity, SAPE-FL adaptively balances global knowledge transfer and peer collaboration while filtering out dissimilar clients. This dual anchoring mitigates negative transfer and enhances robustness in heterogeneous settings. We theoretically analyze our algorithm establishing its convergence guarantees and empirically show that SAPE-FL outperforms state-of-the-art methods under high statistical heterogeneity and low client data regimes.

</details>


### [172] [CAPTURE: Disentangling Preference Drift from Memory Poisoning in Personalized LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02265)
*S M Asif Hossain,Ruksat Khan Shayoni,Md Kishor Morol*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAPTURE is a defense framework for memory-augmented LLM agents that addresses the challenge of distinguishing genuine user preference changes from adversarial memory poisoning, using differential-equation belief tracking, multi-timescale memory, uncertainty-triggered clarification, and counterfactual auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalized language agents use persistent memory to adapt to users, but this creates an attack surface. When new information conflicts with stored preferences, agents must distinguish genuine preference drift from temporary context shifts, ambiguity, or adversarial memory poisoning. Existing rules based only on recency and provenance are insufficient.

Method: CAPTURE employs: (1) a neural differential-equation belief tracker for continuous-time partially observable decision processes over latent user states; (2) a multi-timescale memory ledger; (3) uncertainty-triggered clarification; and (4) counterfactual auditing of cited memories.

Result: On 480 held-out episodes from 96 users, CAPTURE achieves 71.5% win rate (vs. 69.3% baseline and 66.1% strongest heuristic). It limits fixed-policy poisoning to 11.5% while accepting 83.5% of genuine preference updates. Under adaptive attacker with released weights, attack success rises to 24.7%. Zero-shot evaluation on independent benchmark and replay of 40 users' longitudinal interactions (2-3 weeks) show consistent results.

Conclusion: Modeling preference authenticity explicitly can improve both personalization and robustness in memory-augmented LLM agents. However, there's a real adaptation-security tradeoff, as adaptive attackers with access to released weights can increase attack success significantly.

Abstract: Personalized language agents use persistent memory to adapt to users over time, but the same mechanism creates an attack surface. When new information conflicts with stored preferences, an agent must distinguish genuine preference drift from temporary context shifts, ambiguity, or adversarial memory poisoning. We formulate this problem as a continuous-time partially observable decision process over a latent user state and show why rules based only on recency and provenance are insufficient. CAPTURE addresses this ambiguity with a neural differential-equation belief tracker, a multi-timescale memory ledger, uncertainty-triggered clarification, and counterfactual auditing of cited memories. On 480 held-out episodes from 96 users, CAPTURE achieves a 71.5% win rate, compared with 69.3% for an identically supervised baseline and 66.1% for the strongest heuristic baseline. It limits fixed-policy poisoning success to 11.5% while accepting 83.5% of genuine preference updates. Under an adaptive attacker with access to the released weights, attack success rises to 24.7%, exposing a real adaptation-security tradeoff. We further evaluate the frozen system zero-shot on an independently constructed benchmark and replay longitudinal interaction histories from 40 users collected over two to three weeks. These results suggest that modeling preference authenticity explicitly can improve both personalization and robustness in memory-augmented LLM agents.

</details>


### [173] [Entangled Representations Amplify Collateral Damage in Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02285)
*Evžen Wybitul,Tim G. J. Rudner,Christian Schroeder de Witt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides the first controlled experimental test of the long-suspected intuition that representational entanglement (sharing of structure between knowledge domains in neural networks) makes machine unlearning harder. Using Selective Gradient Masking (SGTM) to create 6 language models with varying degrees of disentanglement between biology and non-biology knowledge, the authors apply three unlearning methods and find that more disentangled models consistently achieve better retain-forget trade-offs, with up to 4x lower retain cost at equivalent forgetting levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The intuition that representational entanglement makes unlearning harder has been widely held in interpretability research but never directly tested in a controlled experiment. The authors sought to provide causal evidence for this hypothesis by isolating the effect of model architecture (entanglement level) from data and algorithm effects.

Method: The authors repurposed Selective Gradient Masking (SGTM) to train six 254M-parameter language models on English Wikipedia with graded levels of disentanglement between biology and non-biology knowledge. They then applied three standard unlearning methods to every model in the suite and measured retain-forget trade-offs.

Result: More disentangled models consistently achieve better retain-forget trade-offs. At a fixed level of forgetting, the most disentangled models incur roughly 4x lower retain cost under two of the three unlearning methods, and 1.3x lower under the third method. This provides direct evidence that representational entanglement causes collateral damage in unlearning.

Conclusion: Representational entanglement is one of the causes of collateral damage in unlearning, confirming a long-standing intuition in interpretability research. The authors suggest that a similar experimental design could be used to test other structural claims from interpretability, providing a framework for rigorous hypothesis testing in this field.

Abstract: A long-held intuition in interpretability research is that representational entanglement, the sharing of structure between knowledge domains in a neural network, makes unlearning harder. While the intuition is widespread, it has never been directly tested in a controlled experiment. We present a way to do so: by repurposing Selective Gradient Masking (SGTM), we train a suite of six 254M-parameter language models on English Wikipedia with graded levels of disentanglement between biology and non-biology knowledge. Applying three standard unlearning methods to every model in the suite, we find that more disentangled models consistently achieve better retain-forget trade-offs: at a fixed level of forgetting, the most disentangled models incur roughly $4\times$ lower retain cost under two of the three methods, and $1.3\times$ lower under the third. Because our intervention changes only the model, not the data or the unlearning algorithm, this is direct evidence that representational entanglement is one of the causes of collateral damage in unlearning, as interpretability researchers have long suspected. A similar design could be used to test other structural claims from interpretability.

</details>


### [174] [SEAL: Reinforcing Global Safety in Mixture-of-Experts through Shared Expert ALignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02293)
*Qingyu Meng,Yiwei Zha,Jiahuan Pei,Koen Hindriks,Herbert Bos,Min Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses security vulnerabilities in Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) large language models. The authors identify that sparse routing creates a structural vulnerability where adversaries can manipulate expert selection through jailbreak prompts, malicious fine-tuning, and weight pruning. They propose SEAL and SEAL++, defense methods that leverage shared experts (always-activated components) as router-independent anchors for safety alignment, achieving up to 60% reduction in attack success rate with minimal capability loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MoE architectures power many flagship LLMs but remain vulnerable to adversarial attacks. Existing defenses focus on hardening routers, but routing's nondeterministic nature allows adversaries to bypass or manipulate the routing trajectory. The authors aim to find a router-independent mechanism to enhance safety alignment.

Method: The authors first identify theoretically and empirically that shared experts (always-activated components containing safety-critical neurons) can overcome sparse routing uncertainty. They propose SEAL: a training-time parameter-efficient adapter attached to shared experts. SEAL++ adds an orthogonal constraint to preserve pre-existing safety subspaces during training.

Result: SEAL reduces attack success rate (ASR) by up to 60% across six attack scenarios (combining harmful prompting, jailbreak, malicious fine-tuning with and without neuron pruning), with a capability cost of at most 1.4% on a five-benchmark average.

Conclusion: Shared experts can serve as router-independent anchors for safety alignment. The proposed SEAL and SEAL++ methods provide effective, plug-and-play defenses that integrate seamlessly with router-level defenses, significantly reducing adversarial attack success while maintaining model capability.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) is a scaling architecture for large language models that activates only a small subset of expert modules per token, enabling massive parameter growth with nearly constant computation. Recent Hybrid MoE architecture adds \textit{shared experts} to capture consistently useful representations, further improving stability and generalization. MoE now powers many flagship open-source and commercial models, yet remains vulnerable to adversarial attacks. Specifically, sparse routing introduces a structural vulnerability: MoE safety hinges on which experts are activated, and adversaries can subvert this selection through jailbreak prompts, malicious fine-tuning, and weight-level pruning of safety-critical neurons. Existing defenses primarily focus on hardening the router, but an adversary may still manipulate or bypass the routing trajectory due to the routing process's nondeterministic nature, thereby collapsing the defense. To cope with this problem, we first identify theoretically and empirically that shared expert, an always-activated component containing a small proportion of safety-critical neurons, can overcome the uncertainty of sparsely activated routing path and serve as a router-independent anchor to enhance global safety alignment. Based on this insight, we propose SEAL, a training-time parameter-efficient defense that produces a plug-and-play adapter attached to shared expert, and SEAL++, a variant that adds an orthogonal constraint preserving pre-existing safety subspaces during training. We evaluate SEAL and SEAL++ across six attack scenarios that combine three adversarial inputs (harmful prompting, jailbreak, malicious fine-tuning) with and without neuron pruning. SEAL reduces attack success rate (ASR) by up to 60\%, at a capability cost of at most 1.4\% on a five-benchmark average. Additionally, SEAL can seamlessly integrate with router-level ......

</details>


### [175] [Bayes-Optimal BER and AUC: Estimation and Evaluation of Estimators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02304)
*Ryota Ushio,Takashi Ishida,Masashi Sugiyama*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses estimating optimal performance metrics (BER and AUC) in binary classification when standard accuracy is inadequate. The authors propose soft-label-based estimators that work even with unknown class priors and corrupted soft labels, and extend the FeeBee framework for evaluating such estimators without requiring ground-truth optimal values.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accuracy is a poor performance metric in settings with class imbalance or noisy annotations, where balanced error rate (BER) and AUC are more appropriate. Existing Bayes error estimators only handle accuracy, leaving a gap for these alternative metrics. Additionally, evaluating such estimators is challenging since the optimum is unobservable.

Method: Two complementary contributions: (i) Estimation - propose soft-label-based estimators for optimal BER and AUC, first in a clean setting with known true soft labels and class prior, then extended to a realistic setting with unknown class prior and corrupted soft labels (unknown order-preserving transformation + additive noise). They use isotonic regression with auxiliary hard labels to recover clean soft labels and clipped mean of hard labels to estimate class prior, with finite-sample error bounds. (ii) Evaluation - extend the FeeBee framework to provide practical evaluation scores without requiring knowledge of the optimum.

Result: Experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets validate both the proposed estimators and the evaluation procedure, demonstrating their effectiveness in practical settings.

Conclusion: The proposed methods successfully estimate optimal BER and AUC from soft labels, even under challenging conditions with unknown class priors and corrupted labels. The extended FeeBee framework enables practical evaluation of such estimators without ground-truth optimal values, making this approach broadly applicable.

Abstract: A fundamental quantity in machine learning is the optimal performance achievable by any model on a given task. Estimating this quantity allows us to distinguish the irreducible part of the error from a deficiency of the model, telling us how much room for improvement remains. Recent work has shown that the Bayes error, or equivalently the optimal accuracy, can be estimated from soft labels in binary classification. However, accuracy is often a poor summary of performance in settings with severe class imbalance or noisy annotations, where metrics such as the balanced error rate (BER) and the area under the ROC curve (AUC) are more appropriate. We address this gap with two complementary contributions. (i) Estimation. We propose soft-label-based estimators for the optimal BER and AUC. We first consider the clean setting in which true soft labels and the class prior are known, and then extend the estimators to a more realistic setting in which the class prior is unknown and the observed soft labels are corrupted by an unknown order-preserving transformation, possibly followed by additive noise. In the latter setting, we approximately recover the clean soft labels via isotonic regression with auxiliary hard labels, estimate the class prior with a clipped mean of the hard labels, and derive finite-sample error bounds for the resulting plug-in estimators. (ii) Evaluation. Since the optimum is unobservable on real datasets, evaluating any such estimator is itself nontrivial. We extend the FeeBee framework, originally proposed for evaluating Bayes-error estimators, to the optimal BER and AUC. The resulting procedure provides practical evaluation scores without requiring knowledge of the optimum, and applies to any estimator of the optimal BER or AUC, not only our proposed ones. Experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets validate both the estimators and the evaluation procedure.

</details>


### [176] [What Is Worth Representing? Representational Empowerment for Continual Model Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02322)
*Fei Dai,Hanqi Zhou,Alison Gopnik,Charley Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the fundamental problem of deciding what to represent in model-based learning, framing it as "continual model construction." The authors propose Representational Empowerment (RepEmp) as a principle for scoring candidate representational elements by their future utility for modeling and planning, implemented in a hierarchical Curator-Actor architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that modeling the world requires not just estimating parameters or causal structure, but deciding what should be represented at all. This is a fundamental challenge in bounded-resource settings where agents must decide what to build, retain, and reuse.

Method: RepEmp is proposed as a scoring mechanism that measures "control over internal representations" (contrasted with classic empowerment measuring control over external states). The framework is implemented as a hierarchical Curator-Actor architecture. Three experiments are conducted: (1) human behavioral experiment in closed-vocabulary causal learning, (2) matched simulations, and (3) LLM-augmented open-vocabulary planning domain.

Result: In the human causal-learning task, RepEmp better predicts human abstraction choices than information-gain alternatives (humans prioritize goal reachability over fidelity). Simulations show RepEmp-guided construction contributes more than exploration to structure recovery and cross-task transfer. In the LLM open-vocabulary domain, RepEmp builds more compact symbolic libraries with better generalization. Ablating RepEmp eliminates these benefits.

Conclusion: RepEmp is identified as a key principle for continual model construction under bounded resources, governing what to build, retain, and reuse across environments.

Abstract: The first problem of modeling the world is not just estimating the right parameters or causal structure, but deciding what should be represented at all. We frame this problem as continual model construction: an agent maintains an environment-specific model M of an inaccessible world W and curates a persistent library L of reusable representational elements across environments. We propose Representational Empowerment (RepEmp) to score candidate elements by how much they expand the agent's future capacity to model and plan, complementing the classic definition of empowerment, but redefined as control over internal representations instead of external states. We realize the framework as a hierarchical Curator-Actor architecture and test it across three experiments. In a closed-vocabulary causal-learning task, human participants construct causal models at varying abstraction granularities to maximize goal reachability rather than fidelity to the world, a signature better predicted by RepEmp than by information-gain alternatives. Matched simulations reveal that RepEmp-guided construction contributes more than exploration to sufficient structure recovery and cross-task transfer. Finally, in an open-vocabulary planning domain, an LLM-augmented Curator builds more compact symbolic libraries, which also generalize better than baselines. Ablating RepEmp eliminates these benefits. Together, these results identify RepEmp as a key principle for continual model construction: deciding what to build, retain, and reuse under bounded resources.

</details>


### [177] [AGI Maze Prediction Datasets: A Compact Benchmark for Learning World Dynamics with Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02339)
*Alexey Potapov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces the AGI Maze Prediction Datasets and Benchmark, a controlled testbed for studying world modeling in Transformers using procedurally generated stateful grid worlds. The benchmark includes three prediction tasks, and experiments show that a spatial-memory Transformer outperforms byte-level and unstructured-memory baselines on fixed-horizon tasks and sequential text-trace prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World modeling requires predictive models to maintain internal states for reasoning about action consequences. Existing benchmarks lack controlled settings for testing whether models learn transferable action-conditioned dynamics or simply memorize transitions in familiar layouts. The authors seek to address this gap with a lightweight, structured testbed.

Method: The benchmark derives from procedurally generated, stateful grid worlds with three tasks: per-step transition prediction, fixed-horizon state prediction, and sequential textual-observation prediction. They use source-maze-disjoint train/validation splits and greedy exact-match evaluation. They compare byte-level Transformers with two working-memory-augmented architectures: a generic auxiliary latent-memory Transformer and a pseudo-video spatial-memory Transformer that initializes a 2D latent workspace from input maps and updates it from action history.

Result: The generic auxiliary latent-memory Transformer can fit some training sets perfectly but does not consistently improve held-out performance. The pseudo-video spatial-memory Transformer achieves perfect validation accuracy on selected fixed-horizon tasks where baselines fail, and substantially improves sequential text-trace prediction. Structured, task-aligned working memory proves more useful than additional latent capacity alone.

Conclusion: Structured, task-aligned working memory is more beneficial than additional latent capacity for world modeling. Language grounding is mediated by persistent data structures and computations over them. The benchmark provides a compact setting for testing architectures that couple textual interfaces to learned structured state.

Abstract: World modeling requires a predictive model to maintain and update an internal state adequate for reasoning about the consequences of actions. We introduce the AGI Maze Prediction Datasets and Benchmark, a lightweight controlled testbed for studying this capability in Transformers and other predictive models. Derived from procedurally generated, stateful grid worlds, the benchmark comprises per-step transition prediction, fixed-horizon state prediction, and sequential textual-observation prediction. Source-maze-disjoint training and validation splits, together with greedy exact-match evaluation, distinguish learning transferable action-conditioned dynamics from memorizing transitions in familiar layouts. We establish from-scratch byte-level Transformer baselines and compare them with two working-memory-augmented architectures. A generic auxiliary latent-memory Transformer can fit some training sets perfectly but does not consistently improve held-out performance. In contrast, a pseudo-video spatial-memory Transformer initializes a two-dimensional latent workspace from the input map and updates it from action history without receiving intermediate maps, positions, or state labels. Under the same data, objectives, and evaluation protocol, this model reaches perfect validation accuracy on selected fixed-horizon tasks where the byte and unstructured-memory baselines do not, and substantially improves sequential text-trace prediction. These results suggest that structured, task-aligned working memory can be more useful than additional latent capacity alone. More broadly, we argue that language grounding is mediated by persistent data structures and computations over them; the benchmark offers a compact setting for testing architectures that couple textual interfaces to learned structured state.

</details>


### [178] [Percolation Dynamics in Optimization : Variance Cascades and Discrete Scale Invariance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02373)
*Sai Niranjan Ramachandran,Suvrit Sra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper models SGD dynamics as a percolation process, where architectural symmetries cause subnetworks to merge in discrete blocks, creating variance spikes analogous to phase transitions. This mechanism extends to Adam and AdamW optimizers under heavy-tailed noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how SGD steers deep neural networks toward simpler subnetworks over time, and characterizing the temporal dynamics of this steering mechanism.

Method: Modeled stochastic gradient flow (SGF) as a percolation process; introduced a macroscopic order parameter to detect structural transitions; analyzed Adam and AdamW under an explicit heavy-tailed noise model.

Result: Architectural symmetries force subnetworks to merge in discrete simultaneous blocks rather than gradually; these structural transitions register as variance spikes in the order parameter, echoing physical phase transitions; this trapping mechanism and scaling cascade extend to adaptive optimizers (Adam, AdamW).

Conclusion: The dynamics of SGD can be understood through the lens of percolation theory, with phase-transition-like behavior marking the formation of simpler subnetworks. This framework generalizes to adaptive optimizers under heavy-tailed noise assumptions.

Abstract: We study the dynamics of Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD), which is known to steer deep neural networks toward invariant sets that correspond to simpler subnetworks. How this steering unfolds over time remains poorly understood. We answer this by modeling the stochastic gradient flow (SGF) as a percolation process, in which architectural symmetries force subnetworks to merge in discrete simultaneous blocks rather than one at a time. These structural transitions register as variance spikes in a macroscopic order parameter, echoing physical phase transitions. We further show this trapping mechanism and its associated scaling cascade extend to Adam and AdamW under an explicit heavy-tailed noise model.

</details>


### [179] [Evidence for Shared Routing Geometry and Dynamics in Sparse Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02404)
*Kirill Labzin,Stepan Kulibaba,Artem Dzhalilov,Artem Gorokhov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates the geometric structure underlying routing decisions in sparse mixture-of-experts (MoE) models across multiple layers. The authors discover that routing-relevant states across layers share a common geometric structure that can be aligned using generalized orthogonal Procrustes analysis, revealing that routing evolution follows a reusable process across depth rather than independent layer-specific dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed that routing decisions across depth can be predicted from earlier routing signals, but the structure behind this predictability was unclear. The authors aimed to understand whether routing states across layers share a common geometric structure and whether this structure is specific to routing or reflects generic hidden state evolution.

Method: 1) Isolated control subspaces of each router using orthogonal decomposition
2) Aligned these spaces into a shared canonical representation using generalized orthogonal Procrustes analysis
3) Performed matched-rank comparison to separate routing-specific information from generic cross-layer predictability
4) Tested whether predicted canonical states remain meaningful when used in place of native routing states

Result: 1) After alignment, a single linear transition achieves R²=0.39-0.71 and retains 79-90% of predictive power of layer-specific dynamics
2) Residual representations are often easier to predict across layers, while router-control states preserve expert choices more faithfully
3) Transported states preserve local routing behavior; learned state evolution reduces ΔNLL by 15.7% on OLMoE and 6.2% over 10-router horizon on Phi

Conclusion: Routing-state evolution across layers follows a reusable process with a common geometric structure, distinct from generic hidden state evolution. This shared dynamics is routing-specific and can be exploited to predict and transport routing states, with meaningful performance improvements in downstream tasks.

Abstract: Sparse mixture-of-experts (MoE) models use an independently parameterized router at each sparse layer to select experts for every token. Prior work has shown that routing decisions across depth can often be predicted from earlier routing signals, suggesting that routing is not fully independent across layers. However, the structure behind this predictability remains unclear. In this work, we provide evidence that routing-relevant states across layers share a common geometric structure that is obscured by layer-specific coordinate systems. We isolate the control subspace of each router and align these spaces into a shared canonical representation using generalized orthogonal Procrustes analysis. After alignment, a single linear transition reaches $R^2=0.39$--$0.71$ and retains 79--90\% of the predictive power of separately fitted layer-specific dynamics, indicating that much of routing-state evolution follows a reusable process across depth. We then ask whether this shared dynamics is specific to routing or simply reflects the smooth evolution of hidden representations. A matched-rank comparison shows that residual representations are often easier to predict across layers, while router-control states preserve the model's expert choices much more faithfully. This separates generic cross-layer predictability from routing-specific information. Finally, we test whether the predicted canonical states remain meaningful when used in place of native routing states. The transported states preserve local routing behavior, while learned state evolution reduces $Δ\mathrm{NLL}$ relative to simple persistence by 15.7\% on OLMoE and 6.2\% over a 10-router horizon on Phi.

</details>


### [180] [Coverage, Not Targeting: A Structural Regime in Multi-Turn Agent Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02417)
*Chenyu Zhou,Qiliang Jiang,Shuning Wu,Xu Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates credit assignment in multi-turn agentic RL, arguing that terminal-state verifiers (which only check final outcomes) operate in a low "verifier information density" regime where per-turn credit targeting fails. The key insight is that dense, uniformly distributed rewards outperform sparse binary rewards and targeted credit schemes because success requires coverage of entire action chains (5-8 steps), not just identifying key turns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors identify a fundamental problem in how modern multi-turn agentic RL approaches credit assignment: treating it as a "targeting" problem (identifying which turns mattered) when the structural properties of terminal-state verifiers make this the wrong approach. They seek to understand when targeting works and when it fails.

Method: The authors introduce the concept of "verifier information density" (V_d = k/C), where k is the number of steps whose correctness the verifier exposes and C is the total causal chain length. They conduct controlled experiments on tau^2-bench and BFCL V3 using shared-rollout comparisons that separate reward density from credit geometry. They test: (1) continuous dense reward vs sparse binary reward, (2) concentrated advantage on progress turns vs random turns, and (3) matched-concentration shuffled controls. They also run breadth sweeps and a reward-to-go arm.

Result: Continuous dense rewards uniformly spread outperform sparse binary rewards (net-harmful on 4/5 seeds). Concentrating advantage on progress turns or random turns is equally harmful. Terminal-state verification collapses to a single final-write turn (k=1 in 98% of rollouts) while success requires a 5-8 step chain. The synthetic phase boundary places crossover at V_d* ~ 0.8, but measured V_d is ~0.15 on tau^2-bench and ~0.4 on BFCL V3. The effect reproduces across model families (ToolACE-2-8B: Delta = -0.048 over 32 seeds), and a matched-budget breadth sweep shows a monotone dose-response with deficit vanishing only at full chain coverage. The reward-to-go arm reaches full-coverage parity.

Conclusion: Uniform redistribution is the zero-information coverage default that per-turn targeting schemes must beat. The matched-concentration shuffled control is proposed as a necessary baseline for any targeting claim. The key insight is that when verifier information density is low (V_d << V_d*), coverage matters more than targeting—dense rewards that provide signal across the entire action chain outperform sparse rewards that only verify final outcomes.

Abstract: Multi-turn agentic RL increasingly treats credit assignment as a targeting problem: given a terminal verifiable reward, per-turn methods localize credit onto the turns that mattered. We identify the structural quantity that predicts when this is the right move, the verifier information density V_d = k/C (the fraction of an agent's C-step causal chain whose per-turn correctness the verifier exposes), and show that terminal-state verifiers sit deep in a low-V_d regime where targeting is the wrong axis. In controlled shared-rollout comparisons on tau^2-bench that separate reward density from credit geometry, a continuous dense reward spread uniformly beats the sparse binary outcome reward (net-harmful on 4/5 seeds), while concentrating the same advantage on progress turns or on random turns is equally harmful: targeting is second-order. The mechanism is coverage: terminal-state verification collapses the observable signal to a single final-write turn (k=1 in 98% of rollouts) while success requires a 5-8 step chain of prerequisite tool calls. A synthetic phase boundary places the crossover at V_d* ~ 0.8, whereas measured V_d is ~0.15 on tau^2-bench and ~0.4 on BFCL V3; uniform also wins on BFCL, where a matched-concentration shuffled control is negative on 8/8 seeds. The effect reproduces across model families on ToolACE-2-8B (Delta = -0.048 over 32 pre-registered seeds; an independent 20-seed replication is itself significant), and a pre-registered matched-budget breadth sweep traces a monotone dose-response whose deficit vanishes only at full chain coverage, with a reward-to-go arm reaching full-coverage parity. Uniform redistribution is the zero-information coverage default that per-turn schemes must beat; we contribute the matched-concentration shuffled control that any targeting claim should clear.

</details>


### [181] [IFW-BLS: Dual-Robust Broad Learning System with Intuitionistic Fuzzy Wave Loss](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02422)
*Mushir Akhtar,M. Tanveer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IFW-BLS enhances Broad Learning System robustness by combining wave loss (bounded, asymmetric loss function) for residual-level protection with intuitionistic fuzzy scoring for sample-level credibility control, achieving superior performance on UCI benchmarks and better stability under noise/outlier contamination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard least-squares training in Broad Learning System (BLS) has two key vulnerabilities: (i) large residuals from noise, outliers, or corrupted labels can dominate the objective, and (ii) all samples are treated equally even when some lie in ambiguous or locally conflicting regions. This limits BLS's robustness in real-world scenarios with noisy or unreliable data.

Method: The paper proposes IFW-BLS (Intuitionistic Fuzzy Wave Broad Learning System) with two robustness mechanisms: (1) Residual-level protection using bounded, smooth, and asymmetric wave loss instead of squared loss to prevent extreme residuals from receiving unbounded influence and allow different penalties for positive/negative deviations; (2) Sample-level credibility control using intuitionistic fuzzy scores combining global class-center consistency with local neighborhood conflict. A Nesterov accelerated gradient optimizer is used to solve the objective, avoiding explicit matrix inversion in conventional BLS.

Result: Experiments on UCI benchmark datasets validate the superiority of IFW-BLS over baseline models. Additional corruption experiments show more stable performance than BLS under noise and outlier contamination.

Conclusion: The proposed IFW-BLS successfully addresses both sources of fragility in standard BLS training within a unified optimization model, demonstrating improved robustness and stability compared to baseline approaches.

Abstract: Broad Learning System is an efficient randomized learning model that expands network width through feature and enhancement nodes and estimates the output weights without deep backpropagation. Its standard least-squares training, however, is vulnerable in two different ways: (i) large residuals caused by noise, outliers, or corrupted labels can dominate the objective, and (ii) all samples are treated as equally reliable even when some lie in ambiguous or locally conflicting regions. This paper proposes IFW-BLS, an Intuitionistic Fuzzy Wave Broad Learning System that addresses these two sources of fragility within one optimization model. The first robustness mechanism is residual-level protection, obtained by replacing the squared loss with the bounded, smooth, and asymmetric wave loss. Boundedness prevents extreme residuals from receiving unbounded influence, while asymmetry allows positive and negative deviations to be penalized differently when the dominant error direction varies. The second mechanism is sample-level credibility control, obtained through intuitionistic fuzzy scores that combine global class-center consistency with local neighborhood conflict. The resulting model evaluates the wave loss on credibility-weighted residuals, so unreliable samples are down-weighted before the bounded loss further limits the effect of extreme errors. A Nesterov accelerated gradient based optimizer is used to solve the proposed objective, avoiding the explicit matrix inversion used in conventional BLS. Experiments on UCI benchmark datasets validate the superiority of the proposed IFW-BLS model over the baseline models; additional corruption experiments also show more stable performance than BLS under noise and outlier contamination.

</details>


### [182] [Towards One-for-All Robustness Across a Continuum of Threat Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02440)
*Zhichao Hou,Xiaorui Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A single model (TCN) that achieves robust adversarial defense across a continuum of threat levels using threat-conditional adaptation via Fourier embeddings and channel-wise modulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adversarially robust models typically overfit to specific attack budgets, requiring multiple specialized models for different threat levels. This becomes intractable as the threat space grows, raising the challenge of achieving strong robustness across a continuum of threat levels within a single model.

Method: The proposed Threat Conditional Network (TCN) uses a representation factorization framework that decomposes representation learning into: (1) a threat-invariant shared backbone, and (2) a lightweight threat-conditional adaptor. TCN conditions a single model on the perturbation level via Fourier-based embeddings and channel-wise affine modulation, trained against a distribution over perturbation budgets.

Result: Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny-ImageNet show that TCN matches or surpasses a full ensemble of budget-specialized models with a single set of parameters, generalizes to unseen perturbation budgets, transfers robustly under mismatched threat conditions, with only 4.6% parameter overhead.

Conclusion: TCN provides a promising path toward adaptive and generalizable robustness in dynamic and diverse threat environments, enabling flexible and seamless adaptation across an infinite continuum of threat levels during inference with minimal parameter overhead.

Abstract: Adversarially robust models often overfit to a specific attack budget, necessitating multiple specialized models for diverse and dynamic adversarial environments, a strategy that becomes fundamentally intractable as the threat space grows. This raises an open challenge: can we achieve strong robustness across a continuum of threat levels within a single model? We propose the Threat Conditional Network (TCN), grounded in a representation factorization framework that decomposes representation learning into a threat-invariant shared backbone and a lightweight threat-conditional adaptor. TCN conditions a single model on the perturbation level via Fourier-based embeddings and channel-wise affine modulation, and is trained against a distribution over perturbation budgets, enabling flexible and seamless adaptation across an infinite continuum of threat levels during inference. Extensive experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny-ImageNet show that TCN matches or surpasses a full ensemble of budget-specialized models with a single set of parameters, generalizes to unseen perturbation budgets, and transfers robustly under mismatched threat conditions, with only 4.6\% parameter overhead.
