These contributions chart a promising path toward adaptive and generalizable robustness in dynamic and diverse threat environments.

</details>


### [183] [CACTUS: Mask-Guided Semantic Clean-Label Backdoors in Decentralized Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02450)
*Chao Feng,Burkhard Stiller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CACTUS is a backdoor attack method for decentralized federated learning (DFL) that uses semantic triggers converted into target-directed representation shifts. It achieves 51.2% average attack success rate across nine aggregation rules on Speech Commands with 30% malicious nodes, and demonstrates highest ASR among evaluated attacks on three of four modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses a key challenge in backdoor attacks on DFL: semantic triggers, while less conspicuous than synthetic patches, suffer from sample-dependent placement that weakens backdoor implantation across aggregation rounds. This problem is compounded in DFL where topology-dependent peer aggregation repeatedly mixes local models, making backdoor propagation particularly challenging.

Method: CACTUS converts label-consistent semantic pairs into target-directed representation shifts. It uses mask-guided, modality-specific operators to isolate trigger effects, couple them across samples, and apply the shifts counterfactually to clean non-target embeddings before peer aggregation. The method operates across multiple modalities (speech, text, tabular, image) and adapts to different network topologies in DFL settings.

Result: With 30% malicious nodes, CACTUS achieves a nine-rule mean attack success rate (ASR) of 51.2% on Speech Commands. It attains the highest nine-rule mean ASR among evaluated attacks on three of four modalities (speech, text, tabular). Sensitivity analyses show ASR varies with network topology and increases with the malicious-node ratio.

Conclusion: CACTUS can effectively propagate backdoors through repeated DFL aggregation, demonstrating that semantic trigger-based attacks are viable in decentralized settings. The findings highlight security vulnerabilities in DFL systems, particularly showing that topology and malicious-node ratio significantly impact attack effectiveness.

Abstract: Semantic triggers in federated learning (FL) can be less conspicuous than synthetic patches, but sample-dependent placement may weaken backdoor implantation across aggregation rounds. This challenge is compounded in decentralized FL (DFL), where topology-dependent peer aggregation repeatedly mixes local models. CACTUS converts label-consistent semantic pairs into target-directed representation shifts. Mask-guided, modality-specific operators isolate trigger effects, couple them across samples, and apply the shifts counterfactually to clean non-target embeddings before peer aggregation. Experiments cover speech, text, tabular, and image tasks under nine aggregation rules. With 30\% malicious nodes, CACTUS reaches a nine-rule mean attack success rate (ASR) of 51.2\% on Speech Commands and the highest nine-rule mean ASR among evaluated attacks on three of four modalities. Sensitivity analyses show that ASR varies with network topology and increases with the malicious-node ratio. These results indicate that CACTUS can propagate backdoors through repeated DFL aggregation.

</details>


### [184] [Scalable Kronecker-Fisher Approximation: Efficient Hessian Analysis for Billion-Parameter Language Models Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02451)
*Viacheslav Yusupov,Daria Cherniuk,Evgeny Frolov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a scalable Kronecker-based Hessian approximation method for analyzing billion-parameter neural networks, revealing that value projection layers are the most vulnerable components across multiple model families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full Fisher matrix computation is infeasible for billion-parameter networks, yet understanding second-order derivative information is critical for identifying fragile components for compression and optimization. The authors seek a practical, theoretically grounded approach to enable Hessian analysis at scale.

Method: The method uses a Kronecker-based approximation that captures cross-layer interactions while avoiding storage of the entire Fisher matrix, making Hessian analysis computationally tractable for large-scale models.

Result: The approach reveals consistent vulnerability patterns: value projection layers exhibit the highest sensitivity and strongest cross-layer correlations across multiple model families. These findings strongly correlate with performance degradation and recovery in quantization, sparsification, inter-layer corruption, and post-corruption fine-tuning experiments.

Conclusion: The framework provides a practical tool for identifying fragile components in large models, enabling guided compression strategies including mixed-precision allocation, layer-wise sparsity, and adaptive low-rank decomposition across layers and weight groups.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a scalable Kronecker-based approximation that captures cross-layer interactions without storing the entire Fisher matrix, enabling practical Hessian analysis for billion-parameter networks where full computation is infeasible. Our approach reveals consistent vulnerability patterns: value projection layers exhibit the highest sensitivity and strongest cross-layer correlations across multiple model families, while other components exhibit architecture-specific behaviors. Through extensive experiments on quantization, sparsification, inter-layer corruption, and post-corruption fine-tuning, we demonstrate that our approximation strongly correlates with both performance degradation and recovery. Our framework provides a practical, theoretically grounded tool for identifying fragile components in large models, opening new avenues for guided compression and optimization strategies, such as mixed-precision allocation, layer-wise sparsity, and adaptive low-rank decomposition across layers and even individual weight groups.

</details>


### [185] [DeepAffinity: Long-Term Aspect Preference Prediction in eCommerce using Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02468)
*Yotam Eshel,Guy Hadad,Guy Feigenblat,Yuri M. Brovman,Matt Gearhart,Bracha Shapira*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeepAffinity uses Small Language Models (SLMs) with structured prompts and specialized prediction heads to predict eCommerce user preferences for product aspects (brand, size, color) from their temporal interaction history, outperforming standard fine-tuning methods and general-purpose LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding eCommerce user preferences for product aspects enables fine-grained personalization in recommendation, search, and marketing. Existing methods fail to capture long-term preferences that evolve beyond the current session.

Method: DeepAffinity frames Aspect Affinity as a temporal prediction task, leveraging Small Language Models (SLMs) with structured prompts and specialized prediction heads fine-tuned for forecasting users' future aspect choices from their time-ordered interaction history.

Result: DeepAffinity outperforms standard generative fine-tuning methods. General-purpose open-source LLMs perform poorly without task-specific tuning, highlighting their limits in modeling nuanced behavior. DeepAffinity also enhances recommendation quality on a large-scale multinational eCommerce platform.

Conclusion: Task-specific tuning of SLMs is essential for modeling nuanced user behavior in eCommerce. DeepAffinity successfully captures long-term user preferences and improves recommendation quality in real-world applications.

Abstract: We explore predicting eCommerce user preferences for product aspects such as brand, size, and color - a task we define as Aspect Affinity. Solving this task improves customer understanding and enables fine-grained personalization in recommendation, search, and marketing. We frame Aspect Affinity as a temporal prediction task: forecasting a users future aspect choices from their time-ordered interaction history, capturing long-term preferences that evolve beyond the current session. To this end, we propose DeepAffinity, which leverages Small Language Models (SLMs) with structured prompts and specialized prediction heads fine-tuned for this task. We show DeepAffinity outperforms standard generative fine-tuning methods, while general-purpose open-source LLMs perform poorly without task-specific tuning, highlighting their limits in modeling nuanced behavior. Finally, DeepAffinity enhances recommendation quality on a large-scale multinational eCommerce platform.

</details>


### [186] [RINSE: Robust Target-Time Normality Estimation for Zero-Shot Graph Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02497)
*Taufikur Rahman Fuad,Md Abrar Jahin,Amir Hussain*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RINSE is a gradient-free framework for zero-shot graph anomaly detection that handles domain shift by iteratively estimating target normality without requiring labels, gradients, or per-target tuning. It identifies reliable low-residual nodes, constructs trimmed target-aware normality models, and combines evidence through rank fusion and encoder ensembling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-shot graph anomaly detection faces domain shift when deploying source-trained detectors to unseen target graphs, making source-derived normality notions unreliable. The key challenge is adapting to target graphs without any labels, gradients, or per-target tuning.

Method: RINSE uses a multi-component approach: (1) identifying reliable low-residual target nodes, (2) constructing trimmed target-aware normality models, (3) combining anomaly evidence through reliability-gated rank fusion, and (4) encoder ensembling. The framework is gradient-free and keeps the source-trained detector fixed while sequentially estimating target normality, representation calibration, and evidence reliability.

Result: RINSE achieved the highest average AUPRC across eight unseen target graphs under two separate preprocessing protocols, outperforming evaluated methods. Block ablations and sensitivity analyses supported the combined design.

Conclusion: Robust target-time estimation is a practical approach for generalist graph anomaly detection without target labels, gradients, or per-target tuning. The method successfully addresses domain shift in zero-shot graph anomaly detection.

Abstract: Zero-shot graph anomaly detection seeks to deploy a detector trained on source graphs to unseen, unlabeled targets, yet domain shift can make source-derived notions of normality unreliable. We introduce RINSE (Robust Iterative Normality Self-Estimation), a gradient-free target-time framework that keeps the source-trained detector fixed while sequentially estimating target normality, representation calibration, and evidence reliability from the target graph. Its core idea is to identify a reliable subset of low-residual target nodes, use them to construct a trimmed target-aware normality model, and combine complementary anomaly evidence through reliability-gated rank fusion and encoder ensembling. Across eight unseen target graphs, RINSE achieves the highest average AUPRC among the evaluated methods under two separate preprocessing protocols, while block ablations and sensitivity analyses support the combined design. These results support robust target-time estimation as a practical approach to generalist graph anomaly detection without target labels, gradients, or per-target tuning.

</details>


### [187] [Rethinking the Teacher-Student Framework for Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02507)
*Damian Sójka,Marc Masana,Bartłomiej Twardowski,Sebastian Cygert*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper challenges the common teacher-student framework in Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) by showing that error accumulation still occurs with EMA-based teacher weights, especially in longer sequences. The authors propose using a fixed "intransigent teacher" that doesn't update its weights, which improves performance and hyperparameter robustness across multiple datasets and architectures including semantic segmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The common practice in TTA is to use an exponential moving average (EMA) of student weights as teacher weights, assuming this provides stability. However, the authors observe that error accumulation still occurs, particularly in longer sequences, which is often overlooked in literature that focuses on shorter scenarios.

Method: The authors propose replacing the EMA-based teacher with an "intransigent teacher" - a teacher whose weights remain fixed and never update throughout deployment. This simple change is applied to the teacher-student framework commonly used in TTA methods.

Result: The proposed intransigent teacher approach significantly improves TTA performance on multiple datasets with longer scenarios and provides increased robustness to hyperparameter changes. The method is effective across various architectures and experimental setups, including semantic segmentation.

Conclusion: Using a fixed (non-updating) teacher instead of an EMA-based teacher in the teacher-student TTA framework leads to better performance in longer deployment scenarios and greater robustness to hyperparameter choices. This simple modification can be seamlessly applied to various TTA methods and architectures.

Abstract: Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) has recently emerged as a promising strategy that allows the adaptation of pre-trained models to changing data distributions at deployment time, without access to any labels. To mitigate error accumulation, researchers have widely adopted the teacher-student framework, though its long-term stability is often taken for granted. In this work, we challenge the common strategy of setting the teacher weights to an exponential moving average of the student by showing that error accumulation still occurs, although it is mostly apparent on longer sequences compared to those commonly utilized. We analyze the stability-plasticity trade-off within the teacher-student framework and propose to use an intransigent teacher that does not update its weights. Surprisingly, we show that this simple change allows TTA methods to significantly improve their performance on multiple datasets with longer scenarios and result in increased robustness to changes in hyperparameters. Finally, we show that those changes can be seamlessly and effectively applied to various architectures and experimental setups, including semantic segmentation. The code is available at https://github.com/dmn-sjk/intransigent_teacher.

</details>


### [188] [Spectral Initialization and Scheduled Graph Smoothness for Uncertain Knowledge Graph Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02519)
*Md Abrar Jahin,Taufikur Rahman Fuad,Jay Pujara,Craig A. Knoblock*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QUEST is a method for uncertain knowledge graph completion that initializes entity embeddings using spectral analysis of the confidence-weighted graph Laplacian and applies a Dirichlet energy regularizer, improving confidence prediction and link prediction without adding trainable parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for uncertain knowledge graphs rely on semi-supervised learning but discard the global community and hub structure when initializing entity embeddings, leading to suboptimal performance and training instability, especially on dense graphs.

Method: QUEST uses two key techniques: (1) Initialization using the smallest non-trivial eigenvectors of the confidence-weighted graph Laplacian to incorporate community and hub structure; (2) An unbiased mini-batch Dirichlet energy regularizer to enforce early-stage structural consistency.

Result: On two UKG datasets, QUEST improves confidence prediction and link prediction on 6 out of 8 metric-dataset pairs over prior methods, matches the previous best on the remaining 2, and removes the instability spike observed on dense graphs.

Conclusion: Spectral structural priors combined with a graph Dirichlet energy regularizer improve accuracy, training stability, and checkpoint reliability in uncertain knowledge graph completion.

Abstract: Uncertain knowledge graphs (UKGs) extend knowledge graphs by assigning each triple a continuous confidence score. Since most possible triples lack observed confidences, recent methods rely on semi-supervised learning to generate pseudo-labels. These methods initialize entity embeddings without using the confidence-weighted graph, discarding its global community and hub structure. We introduce QUEST, which adds no trainable parameters to the standard confidence-distribution learning pipeline. First, QUEST initializes entity embeddings using the smallest non-trivial eigenvectors of the confidence-weighted graph Laplacian, incorporating community and hub structure before training. Second, QUEST applies an unbiased mini-batch Dirichlet energy regularizer to enforce early-stage structural consistency. On two UKG datasets, QUEST improves confidence prediction and link prediction on six of eight metric-dataset pairs over prior methods and matches the previous best on the remaining two, while removing the instability spike observed on dense graphs. These results indicate that spectral structural priors combined with a graph Dirichlet energy regularizer improve accuracy, training stability, and checkpoint reliability in UKG completion.

</details>


### [189] [A Comparative Study of Graph Representations for GNN-Based Power Grid Control in L2RPN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02538)
*Adrian Degenkolb,Qiong Huang,Benjamin Schäfer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates graph representation choices in deep reinforcement learning for power grid control, comparing physical topology, electrical-sensitivity, and hybrid graph variants in the L2RPN environment. The key finding is that matching graph complexity to task granularity matters more than representational richness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph construction is a critical but underexamined design choice in deep reinforcement learning for power grid control. The authors aim to address this gap through controlled experimental comparison of different graph representations.

Method: The authors conducted a controlled experimental comparison of different graph representations including physical topology, electrical-sensitivity, and hybrid variants for topology control in the L2RPN (Learning to Run a Power Network) environment.

Result: The findings indicate that matching graph complexity to task granularity is more important than maximizing representational richness. The study highlights the importance of controlled representation studies at scale.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that appropriate graph complexity should be aligned with task requirements rather than simply using the most expressive representation. This emphasizes the need for more controlled representation studies when applying graph-based deep RL to power grid control problems.

Abstract: Graph construction is a critical but underexamined design choice in deep reinforcement learning for power grid control. We present a controlled experimental comparison of different graph representations, including physical topology, electrical-sensitivity, and hybrid variants for topology control in the Learning to Run a Power Network (L2RPN) environment. Our findings indicate that matching graph complexity to task granularity is more important than maximizing representational richness, and highlight the importance of controlled representation studies at scale.

</details>


### [190] [TrajMind: Chaining Role-Specialized LoRAs for Fast-and-Slow Collective Trajectory Anomaly Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02540)
*Jiahao Wu,Zhenqun Yang,Chen Jason Zhang,Qing Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TrajMind is a fast-and-slow framework for diagnosing collective anomalies from urban trajectories. It uses specialized LoRA adapters over a frozen vision-language backbone to provide both efficient screening (fast path) and accurate, evidence-backed diagnosis (slow path), achieving significant improvements over baselines in anomaly typing, localization, and latency reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing detectors efficiently produce scores/labels but lack rich semantics, while vision-language pipelines provide richer semantics but are computationally expensive. Neither couples verifiable diagnosis with low-latency monitoring. The core challenge is recognizing collective patterns and recovering exact event details without running the full diagnostic pipeline for every monitored window.

Method: The framework separates always-on screening from on-demand diagnosis using three role-specialized LoRA adapters over one frozen vision-language backbone. The slow path (TrajMind_slow) chains canvas-based typing, type-conditioned localization over serialized trajectories, and executable verification. The fast path (TrajMind_fast) screens each window in a single text-only pass.

Result: TrajMind_slow outperforms the strongest baselines by at least 15.3 percentage points in anomaly typing and 13.8 percentage points in localization, with gains persisting under cross-city transfer. TrajMind_fast reduces latency by 41.1% and maintains binary balanced accuracy of at least 93.5%.

Conclusion: TrajMind delivers accurate, evidence-backed diagnoses across cities and efficient front-line monitoring by combining a slow path for detailed diagnosis with a fast path for efficient screening, using specialized LoRA adapters over a shared vision-language backbone.

Abstract: Diagnosing collective anomalies from urban trajectories is increasingly important for traffic governance, as it reveals what happened, who was involved, and where and when the event occurred. Existing detectors efficiently produce scores or labels, whereas vision--language pipelines provide richer semantics; neither couples verifiable diagnosis with low-latency monitoring. The central challenge is to recognize collective patterns and recover exact event details from the source trajectories without running the full diagnostic pipeline for every monitored window. We therefore separate always-on screening from on-demand diagnosis: screening raises alerts, while diagnosis releases only source-verified what--who--where--when records. We present TrajMind, a fast-and-slow framework that switches three role-specialized LoRA adapters over one frozen vision--language backbone. Its slow path, \textit{TrajMind$_{\text{slow}}$}, chains canvas-based typing, type-conditioned localization over serialized trajectories, and executable verification, yielding structured, evidence-backed diagnoses. Additionally, the fast path, \textit{TrajMind$_{\text{fast}}$}, screens each window in a single text-only pass, delivering efficient structured alerts. Extensive experiments show that, TrajMind$_{\mathrm{slow}}$ outperforms the strongest baselines by at least $15.3$ percentage points in anomaly typing and $13.8$ percentage points in localization. These gains persist under cross-city transfer, and TrajMind$_{\mathrm{fast}}$ reduces latency by $41.1\%$ and maintains binary balanced accuracy of at least $93.5\%$. Together, TrajMind delivers accurate, evidence-backed diagnoses across cities and efficient front-line monitoring.

</details>


### [191] [Learn from Whoever Is Right: Answer-Verified Multi-Teacher Distillation for Multi-Domain LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02548)
*Xixiang He,Xingming Li,Baiqi Wu,Qiyao Sun,Xuanyu Ji,Ao Cheng,Qingyong Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MT-SDPO is an on-policy distillation method that unifies multiple frozen teacher LLMs into a single student model by dynamically selecting the reliable teacher per sample based on answer verification rather than domain labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs use reinforcement learning for individual domains but struggle to integrate capabilities into a single deployable model. Existing approaches route samples to teachers based on domain labels, but this is suboptimal because domain expertise holds only on average - the matched teacher isn't always correct for every sample, while a teacher from another domain sometimes is. The key insight is that reliable teachers should be identified per sample, not per domain.

Method: MT-SDPO has three components: (1) Self-anchors - where a rollout is supervised by a correct rollout from its own group; (2) Answer-verified eligibility - where a teacher may supervise a sample only if its own answer passes a verifier; (3) Privileged distillation - merges the anchor and all verified feedback into one context that an exponential moving average (EMA) self-teacher reads and the student does not, thereby keeping one policy at deployment.

Result: Across five students from three model families, MT-SDPO lifts the weakest domain of Qwen3-8B by 14.79 points and narrows its domain gap by 74.7%, achieving a better balance than serving one matched teacher per domain.

Conclusion: Verified reliability, not domain membership, should decide who teaches. The key insight is that per-sample teacher selection based on answer verification outperforms traditional domain-based routing approaches.

Abstract: Modern large language models (LLMs) rely on reinforcement learning to build strong capabilities in individual domains, but integrating those capabilities into a single deployable model remains challenging. By routing each sample to the teacher whose domain matches it, existing approaches let a domain label decide which teacher provides supervision. However, domain expertise holds only on average: the matched teacher is not always correct on a given sample, while a teacher from another domain sometimes is. The reliable teacher therefore has to be identified per sample, not per domain. In this paper, we introduce Multi-Teacher Self-Distillation Policy Optimization (MT-SDPO), an on-policy distillation method that unifies several frozen teachers into one student model. MT-SDPO consists of three components: (1) self-anchors, where a rollout is supervised by a correct rollout from its own group; (2) answer-verified eligibility, where a teacher may supervise a sample only if its own answer passes a verifier; and (3) privileged distillation, which merges the anchor and all verified feedback into one context that an exponential moving average self-teacher reads and the student does not, thereby keeping one policy at deployment. Across five students from three model families, MT-SDPO lifts the weakest domain of Qwen3-8B by 14.79 points and narrows its domain gap by 74.7%, a better balance than serving one matched teacher per domain. Verified reliability, not domain membership, should decide who teaches. Code is available at https://github.com/hexixiang/MT-SDPO.

</details>


### [192] [ProbeMatchDTI: Probe-Driven Multi-Scale Biochemical Pattern Matching for Drug-Target Interaction Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02549)
*Quan Hao,Mengyue Fan,Zifan Dong,Youru Li,Jianduo Zhao,Lechuan Xu,Hao Zhang,Fei Xia,Jigang Wang,Chong Qiu,Liguo Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProbeMatchDTI introduces a pattern-probe-driven framework with IterProbe and BindingProbe to address the limitation of passive feature aggregation in existing DTI prediction methods, which suppresses weak binding-relevant signals like functional groups and residue-context patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing biochemical representation learning methods for DTI prediction use passive feature aggregation that tends to favor dominant molecular patterns while suppressing weak yet binding-relevant signals (functional groups, residue-context patterns), limiting multi-scale biochemical correspondence modeling.

Method: ProbeMatchDTI comprises two components: (1) IterProbe - retains contextual states across refinement depths and uses learnable probes to select them at each position before cross-entity matching, preserving weak biochemical patterns and strengthening associations among functional groups, local motifs, and molecular scaffolds; (2) BindingProbe - characterizes cross-entity drug-protein complementarity at local biochemical-unit and whole-pair levels, jointly modeling fine-grained interactions and multi-scale correspondences.

Result: ProbeMatchDTI achieves 2.0% higher AUC-ROC on BindingDB and 0.5% higher on DrugBank compared to baseline methods. Feature-level pattern analyses characterize its probe-driven behavior in cross-scale biochemical pattern matching. Predictions are also connected to an evidence-guided downstream drug-discovery workflow for candidate refinement and validation.

Conclusion: ProbeMatchDTI successfully addresses the suppression of weak binding-relevant signals in DTI prediction through its pattern-probe-driven approach, demonstrating superior performance on benchmark datasets. The integration with downstream drug-discovery workflows shows practical utility for candidate refinement and validation planning.

Abstract: Drug-target interaction (DTI) prediction is an important task in AI-driven drug discovery. Although recent biochemical representation learning methods have improved DTI prediction, their passive feature aggregation tends to favor dominant molecular patterns while suppressing weak yet binding-relevant signals, such as functional groups and residue-context patterns, limiting the modeling of multi-scale biochemical correspondences. To address this issue, we propose ProbeMatchDTI, a pattern-probe-driven framework comprising IterProbe and BindingProbe. IterProbe explicitly retains contextual states across refinement depths and uses learnable probes to select them at each position before cross-entity matching, thereby preserving weak biochemical patterns and strengthening associations among functional groups, local motifs, and molecular scaffolds. BindingProbe then characterizes cross-entity drug-protein complementarity at local biochemical-unit and whole-pair levels, jointly modeling fine-grained interactions and multi-scale correspondences while preserving weaker binding-relevant associations. Extensive experiments demonstrate the superiority of ProbeMatchDTI, achieving 2.0% and 0.5% higher AUC-ROC on BindingDB and DrugBank, respectively. Feature-level pattern analyses further characterize its probe-driven behavior in cross-scale biochemical pattern matching. We further connect ProbeMatchDTI predictions with an evidence-guided downstream drug-discovery workflow, demonstrating their utility for candidate refinement and validation planning. Our code is available at https://github.com/developer-hq/ProbeMatchDTI

</details>


### [193] [Online Reinforcement Learning in the Met Office Unified Model through Distributed Model-Agent Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02566)
*Pritthijit Nath,Sebastian Schemm,Peter Haynes,Emily Shuckburgh,Mark Webb*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates the feasibility of coupling a global weather forecasting model (Met Office Unified Model) with distributed reinforcement learning (DDPG) agents to correct model tendencies through rank-local tensors. The approach uses bounded potential-temperature corrections across 70 vertical atmospheric levels, trained on 10 nudged forecasts and evaluated in non-nudged inference mode, showing significant improvements in Z500 and MSLP forecast accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Numerical weather prediction models require bias correction, but traditional methods lack the ability to adapt to evolving model states while maintaining dynamical consistency and numerical stability. This work aims to address this limitation by using reinforcement learning to learn corrections that adapt to the model state in real-time.

Method: The study couples the Met Office Unified Model (UM) with distributed RL agents through rank-local tensors. A DDPG (Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient) actor shares weights across 70 vertical model levels of each atmospheric column and applies bounded potential-temperature corrections to model tendencies. Training uses 10 nudged forecasts where nudging towards UKMO operational analysis provides a counterfactual target. The frozen policy is then evaluated in non-nudged forecast mode for inference.

Result: The coupled workflow successfully completed training and remained numerically stable. Relative to a matched native UM forecast at +6h, the learned policy reduced Z500 MAE in 4 of 6 latitude bands, with reductions of 45.8% and 40.8% in the northern and southern tropics. MSLP error decreased in 3 bands, with maximum reduction of 27.3% at 0-30°N.

Conclusion: This single-case experiment demonstrates significant promise and feasibility of distributed online learning followed by non-nudged inference. The approach lays the groundwork for RL-based bias correction and parametrisations within operational weather forecasting systems, offering a potential pathway to improve numerical weather prediction through machine-learnt corrections that adapt to evolving model states while preserving dynamical consistency.

Abstract: Machine-learnt corrections can complement numerical weather prediction only if they adapt to the evolving model state while preserving dynamical consistency and numerical stability. To test this within a global forecasting model, we couple the Met Office (UKMO) Unified Model (UM) with distributed RL agents through rank-local tensors. A DDPG actor shares weights across the 70 vertical model levels of each atmospheric column and applies bounded potential-temperature corrections to the model tendencies. Across ten nudged training forecasts, nudging calculations towards the UKMO operational analysis provides an immediate counterfactual target. The frozen policy is then evaluated in a non-nudged forecast for inference. The coupled workflow successfully completes training and remains numerically stable in the evaluated case. Relative to a matched native UM forecast at +6 h, the learnt policy reduces Z$_{500}$ MAE in four of six latitude bands, including reductions of 45.8% and 40.8% in the northern and southern tropics. MSLP error too decreases in three bands, with a maximum reduction of 27.3% at 0-30°N. This single-case experiment demonstrates significant promise and feasibility of distributed online learning followed by non-nudged inference, laying the groundwork for RL-based bias correction and parametrisations within operational systems.

</details>


### [194] [Source Distribution Estimation by Posterior Averaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02622)
*Trung-Dung Hoang,Lisa M. Koch*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes an expectation maximization (EM) approach to solve the source distribution estimation (SDE) problem in simulation-based science, where the goal is to find simulator parameters whose distribution reproduces real observations. Unlike existing methods that train a fixed likelihood surrogate, the proposed EM approach alternates between training an amortized posterior (E-step) and refitting the source distribution (M-step). Two parameterizations are presented: separate source/posterior flows and a shared conditional flow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SDE methods fit the source against a likelihood surrogate trained once from a fixed proposal prior. This is problematic because the objective is stated only in terms of the surrogate (not the true simulator), which can fail in inaccurate regions of parameter space where the surrogate was never trained.

Method: The method uses expectation maximization with two parameterizations: (1) separate source and posterior flows, and (2) a single shared conditional flow. In the E-step, an amortized posterior is trained on fresh simulations from the current source estimate. In the M-step, the source is refitted to the average of that posterior over observed data.

Result: Both proposed methods improve on existing fixed surrogate approaches and iterated variants across three benchmark tasks. On the Lotka-Volterra task, baselines cannot get below 0.96 data-space C2ST (higher is worse), while the proposed methods achieve 0.64-0.68 in three of four initial-prior settings.

Conclusion: The EM-based approach successfully addresses the limitations of fixed surrogate methods by iteratively improving both the source distribution and the likelihood surrogate, achieving better performance especially under misspecified initial priors.

Abstract: Simulation-based science often requires a distribution over simulator parameters whose push-forward reproduces a set of real observations: this is the source distribution estimation (SDE) problem. Existing methods fit the source against a likelihood surrogate trained once from a fixed proposal prior. Their objective is therefore stated only in terms of the surrogate instead of the true simulator, which may fail for inaccurate areas in parameter space where the surrogate was never trained. We instead solve SDE by expectation maximization: an E-step trains an amortized posterior on fresh simulations from the current source estimate, and an M-step refits the source to the average of that posterior over the observed data. We give two parameterizations, (1) separate source and posterior flows and (2) a single shared conditional flow. We evaluate our method on three benchmark tasks under both broad and misspecified initial priors. Both improve on existing fixed surrogate approaches and on iterated variants of each, most clearly on Lotka--Volterra, where no baseline falls below 0.96 data-space C2ST while our methods reach 0.64-0.68 in three of four initial-prior settings.

</details>


### [195] [Oracle, will I ever learn? A study of prediction convergence and complementarity across link prediction models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02638)
*Guillaume Méroué,Fabien Gandon,Pierre Monnin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates how different link prediction models in knowledge graphs capture complementary knowledge, and whether combining multiple models can improve predictions. The authors propose an oracle approach that selects the best prediction for each query from a set of models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Different link prediction models or even different training runs produce substantially different predictions for the same query, suggesting variability in how models capture underlying knowledge. This raises fundamental questions about model complementarity and what can be recovered by combining models.

Method: The authors propose measuring model complementarity through an oracle that selects the best prediction among a considered set of models for each query, providing an upper bound on performance achievable through model combination. They conduct experiments across several architectures and benchmarks.

Result: They find a substantial gap between individual models and their oracle, revealing that different models capture complementary knowledge. However, this complementarity rapidly saturates as more models are added, leaving a persistent subset of queries unsolved even by a large number of models.

Conclusion: The findings reveal both the potential of model complementarity and a fundamental limit to what current link prediction models can collectively recover. This highlights the need for further research to build robust Web applications using knowledge graphs.

Abstract: Knowledge graphs have become an important source of structured knowledge for Web applications, including search, question answering, and recommender systems. In these applications, link prediction can serve either as a prediction task itself or as a means to enrich incomplete knowledge graphs for downstream tasks. Interestingly, different link prediction models, or even different training runs of the same model, can produce substantially different predictions for the same query. This suggests a variability in the capture of the underlying knowledge by models, thus raising a fundamental question: to what extent do different models capture complementary knowledge, and how much of this knowledge could be recovered by combining them? We propose to measure model complementarity through the performance of an oracle that, for each query, selects the best prediction among a considered set of models, hence providing an upper bound on the performance achievable through model combination. Across several architectures and benchmarks, we find a substantial gap between individual models and their oracle, revealing that different models capture complementary knowledge. Yet, this complementarity rapidly saturates as more models are added, leaving a persistent subset of queries unsolved even by a large number of models. These findings reveal both the potential of model complementarity and a fundamental limit to what current link prediction models can collectively recover; thereby highlighting the need for further research to build robust Web applications.

</details>


### [196] [Differentiable Electricity-Market Clearing for Gradient-Based Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02646)
*Luca Mungo,Maarten P. Scholl,Arnau Quera-Bofarull*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses data center planning by treating electricity market clearing as a differentiable optimization layer, enabling gradient-based optimization of load allocation across candidate sites. The method successfully allocates 50 MW across six candidate buses with worst-case objective gaps of 2.3-8.5%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data center planning is challenging because large facilities influence electricity prices through market clearing. While simulation can evaluate a candidate plan, it cannot directly guide improvements. The need is for a method that can optimize planning decisions by understanding how they affect market prices.

Method: The authors treat market clearing as a differentiable optimization layer: forward pass solves the market clearing problem, and reverse-mode automatic differentiation propagates planning costs back through cleared prices to the plan. They validate gradients against finite differences, then apply gradient optimization to allocate 50 MW across six candidate buses in two synthetic networks under fixed active site costs, evaluated over 36 operating states.

Result: Gradient optimization recovers continuous allocations almost exactly compared to exhaustive enumeration, with worst-case objective gaps of 2.3% and 8.5% of the cost difference between best and worst single site. The systematic error occurs near closure costs where smooth relaxation shrinks sites rather than closing them, causing discrete transitions to arrive late.

Conclusion: Differentiable market clearing transforms market-aware planning into an optimizable gradient search problem, enabling planners to directly optimize allocations rather than merely simulate and iterate.

Abstract: Planning a large data center is difficult because a facility big enough to matter changes the electricity prices it will pay. Those prices are set by market clearing, a constrained optimization problem solved anew in every operating condition. However, simulating the market tells a planner how a candidate plan performs but not how to improve it. Here we treat market clearing as a differentiable optimization layer: each forward pass solves the market, and reverse-mode automatic differentiation propagates the planning cost back through the cleared prices to the plan. After validating these gradients against finite differences, we apply them to a concrete problem: allocating 50 MW of data-center load across six candidate buses in two synthetic networks, under a fixed cost per active site, evaluated over 36 operating states. Judged against exhaustive enumeration of all site combinations, gradient optimization recovers the continuous allocations almost exactly, with worst-case objective gaps of 2.3\% and 8.5\% of the cost difference between the best and worst single site. Its one systematic error is instructive: near the costs at which a site should close, the smooth relaxation of the discrete site count shrinks the site rather than closing it, so discrete transitions arrive late. Differentiable market clearing thus turns market-aware planning into a problem gradients can search.

</details>


### [197] [Unfolding the Leech Lattice: Fused Multi-Shell Decoding and VRAM Layouts for 2-Bit LLM Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02652)
*Pier-Jean Malandrino*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper implements a multi-shell decoder for Leech-lattice vector quantization (previously only single-shell existed) and measures its decode-phase GEMV cost. It compares against AWQ (4-bit) and QTIP (2-bit) quantization methods, showing that the trellis kernel outperforms the served layout but with quality trade-offs measured in perplexity and MMLU scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Leech-lattice vector quantization claims the strongest 2-bit quality, but no implementation of the multi-shell decoder (required by the rate) existed. This paper addresses that gap and compares against deployed quantization methods.

Method: Four main contributions: (1) Full 301-class codebook serving path with offline GPU expansion and fused dequantize-plus-matvec kernel without warp divergence; (2) Analysis of in-VRAM rate as design axis, testing binary bit planes vs one-hot masks at various bit widths; (3) Deployment and comparison of AWQ (4-bit) and QTIP (2-bit) GEMV kernels; (4) Validity envelope experiments comparing against no-weights control and FP16 across memory hierarchies.

Result: Binary bit planes beat one-hot masks (4.80 bits/weight, 2.15x FP16). Below 4.3 bits: irregular stream enters; at 3.6 bits: decode becomes non-trivial. Trellis kernel reads 2.40x fewer bytes and runs 2.27x faster than served layout. With identical output head: 1.11x, 1.29x, 1.41x end-to-end gains at 4B, 8B, 14B params. With int8 output head: 4B reaches 87.0 tok/s in 2.60 GB. Quality cost: 1.38x perplexity and 14.7 MMLU points at 4B, shrinking across sizes.

Conclusion: The multi-shell Leech-lattice decoder is feasible but comes with quality trade-offs. The trellis kernel outperforms the served layout due to better memory efficiency, but the quality degradation (perplexity/MMLU) must be weighed against speed gains. The paper establishes that lattice methods fall below FP16 on second memory hierarchy, and provides practical deployment insights for quantization practitioners.

Abstract: Leech-lattice vector quantization holds the strongest reported 2-bit quality under its own evaluation protocol. Its kernel decodes one shell; we found no implementation of the multi-shell decoder the rate requires. This paper supplies one and measures its serving cost for decode-phase GEMV at batch 1. First, a serving path for the full 301-class codebook: an offline expansion into GPU layouts and a fused dequantize-plus-matvec kernel reading them without warp divergence, verified against f64. Second, the in-VRAM rate is a design axis distinct from the on-disk rate. Four bit-exact layouts timed in one process show binary bit planes beating one-hot masks on size and speed at constant bandwidth (4.80 bits per weight, 2.15x FP16). Below 4.3 bits a second, irregular stream enters; at 3.6 the decode stops being shifts and masks. Third, deployed four-bit (AWQ) and two-bit (QTIP) GEMV kernels run in the same process. The trellis kernel reads 2.40x fewer bytes than our served layout and runs 2.27x faster at near-equal fractions of their byte bounds: the time gap tracks the traffic gap, the price of unfolding a codebook too large for a lookup table. Fourth, the validity envelope: the trellis kernel outruns our no-weights control, so our launch geometry sets that floor, and on a second memory hierarchy every lattice arm falls below FP16. With the output head held identical across arms, the kernel-and-format path gains 1.11x, 1.29x and 1.41x end to end at 4B, 8B and 14B; with an int8 output head the served 4B reaches 87.0 tok/s in 2.60 GB. The quality cost, 1.38x perplexity and 14.7 MMLU points at 4B, shrinks across the three sizes measured.

</details>


### [198] [H3DNAS: Hardware-Aware ONNX-Native 3D Point Cloud Model Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02684)
*Anchit Mulye,Rhythm Baghel,Sujay Kumar Ingle,Hardik Jain*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: H3DNAS is a hardware-aware model compression framework that operates directly on ONNX computational graphs without requiring original source code, architecture class definition, or gradient access. It addresses the challenge of deploying 3D point cloud models on edge hardware like NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano by providing the first source-code-free compression pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying 3D point cloud models on edge hardware such as NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano is severely constrained by compute and memory budgets. Existing compression methods require access to the model's original source code, making them inapplicable to ONNX binaries commonly distributed by vendors and model repositories.

Method: The framework makes three contributions: (1) Channel Dependency Graph (CDG) that classifies ONNX operators into four constraint classes and establishes that the free parameter fraction ρ_f is a topological invariant with compression ceiling computable in O(|V|+|E|); (2) Two-Stage Hierarchical Search that prunes candidate architectures by L1-importance channel selection, ranks them by output fidelity as a zero-shot label-free proxy, and applies GhostConv structural mutation to Pareto-optimal candidates; (3) Source-code-free compression pipeline operating entirely via ONNX graph surgery.

Result: On ModelNet40, H3DNAS reduces parameters in PointNet by 65.5%, PointNet++ by 43.2%, and PointMLP by 49.1%, while achieving 1.99×, 1.29×, and 1.67× inference speedups respectively, with negligible accuracy loss.

Conclusion: H3DNAS enables effective compression of 3D point cloud models directly from ONNX binaries without requiring original source code, making it practical for edge deployment scenarios where only compiled model files are available.

Abstract: Deploying 3D point cloud models on edge hardware such as the NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano is severely constrained by compute and memory budgets. Existing compression methods require access to the model's original source code, rendering them inapplicable to the Open Neural Network Exchange (ONNX) binaries commonly distributed by vendors and model repositories. We present \textbf{H3DNAS}, a hardware-aware model compression framework that operates directly on ONNX computational graphs without requiring original source code, architecture class definition, or gradient access during search. H3DNAS makes three contributions: (1) a \textbf{Channel Dependency Graph (CDG)} that classifies ONNX operators into four constraint classes and formally establishes that the free parameter fraction $ρ_f$ is topological invariant, a provable compression ceiling computable in $\mathcal{O}(|V|+|E|)$; (2) a \textbf{Two-Stage Hierarchical Search} that prunes candidate architectures by $L_1$-importance channel selection, ranks them by output fidelity as a zero-shot label-free proxy, and applies GhostConv structural mutation to Pareto-optimal candidates; and (3) the \textbf{first source-code-free compression pipeline for 3D point cloud models}, operating entirely via ONNX graph surgery with no original architecture definition required. On ModelNet40, H3DNAS reduces the number of parameters in PointNet, PointNet++, and PointMLP by $65.5\%$, $43.2\%$, and $49.1\%$, respectively, while achieving $1.99\times$, $1.29\times$, and $1.67\times$ inference speedups with negligible loss in accuracy. The source code is publicly available\footnote{https://github.com/ClarityLab-Org/h3dnas}.

</details>


### [199] [LoRA-TSD: Tangent-Space Spectral Descent for LoRA via Muon-Style Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02734)
*Dmitrii Andriianov,Andrey Veprikov,Aleksandr Beznosikov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LoRA-TSD is a new optimizer for low-rank adaptation that treats each LoRA step as a tangent vector on the fixed-rank matrix manifold, using spectral-norm steepest descent within the tangent space with a cheap retraction operation. It recovers LoRA-Pro as a special case and provides the first global convergence guarantees for LoRA-based optimizers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When LoRA factors are trained independently, the update ignores the geometry of the low-rank weight change. Existing methods either neglect this manifold structure or use expensive operations. The goal is to develop an optimizer that respects the underlying geometry while remaining computationally efficient.

Method: LoRA-TSD treats every LoRA step as a tangent vector of the fixed-rank matrix manifold and takes the spectral-norm steepest-descent step of Muon inside that tangent space. The result is mapped back to the factors through a retraction native to the LoRA parametrization. The tangent-projected gradient is identified as the natural stationarity measure, computable from factor gradients alone.

Result: The retraction is up to 2.8× cheaper than truncated-SVD retraction. LoRA-TSD outperforms every competing LoRA optimizer across six benchmarks (commonsense and NLI) with Llama-3.2-1B, Llama-3.1-8B, and Qwen3-32B, while remaining robust to adapter rank. The Frobenius-norm version recovers LoRA-Pro.

Conclusion: This work provides the first global convergence guarantees for both LoRA-Pro and LoRA-TSD, with rates driving factor-gradient norms to zero. LoRA-TSD achieves superior performance while maintaining computational efficiency through its cheap retraction operation.

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) is the standard way to fine-tune large models, yet when its two factors are trained independently, the update ignores the geometry of the low-rank weight change it induces. We introduce LoRA-TSD, an optimizer that treats every LoRA step as a tangent vector of the fixed-rank matrix manifold and takes the spectral-norm steepest-descent step of Muon inside that tangent space, mapping the result back to the factors through a retraction native to the LoRA parametrization. The step avoids expensive operations on full weight matrices, and its retraction is up to $2.8\times$ cheaper than the truncated-SVD retraction used by prior manifold methods. We prove that the Frobenius-norm version of our surrogate recovers LoRA-Pro, and we identify the tangent-projected gradient, the Riemannian gradient of the manifold, as the stationarity measure natural to LoRA training and computable from the factor gradients alone. Under this measure we give the first global convergence guarantees for both LoRA-Pro and LoRA-TSD, with rates that drive the factor-gradient norms to zero. Across six commonsense and natural-language-inference benchmarks with Llama-3.2-1B, Llama-3.1-8B and Qwen3-32B, LoRA-TSD outperforms every competing LoRA optimizer and stays robust to the adapter rank. Code is available at https://github.com/brain-lab-research/LoRA-TSD.

</details>


### [200] [Do Tabular Foundation Models Know Physics? Contamination, Units, and the Deterministic Limit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02766)
*Wassim Tenachi,Yashar Hezaveh,Laurence Perreault Levasseur,Pierre-Luc Bacon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Tabular Foundation Models (TFMs) are evaluated to determine if they learn physics from training on tabular data. Four TFMs are tested against six baselines on 316 physical equations. While TFMs outperform baselines, they cannot represent noiseless mechanisms or physical units, limiting their ability to act as true physical models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors investigate whether Tabular Foundation Models (TFMs), which learn to fill in tables similarly to how language models fill in text, have learned any physics during training. Since TFMs are Bayesian by construction, the question is what their prior knowledge contains about physical phenomena.

Method: The researchers directly probe the prior of four TFMs (TabPFN-3, TabICLv2, TabDPT, and Real-TabPFN-2.5) by evaluating them against six baselines on datasets sampled from 316 physical equations, testing both in-domain and out-of-domain scenarios.

Result: TFMs dominate the baselines, performing well both out of the box and after tuning. However, the study reveals that their prior cannot represent a noiseless mechanism or physical units, which is why they can interpolate physics but cannot yet function as actual physical models.

Conclusion: While TFMs show superior performance in handling physical equations compared to traditional baselines, they have fundamental limitations. Their Bayesian prior cannot represent noiseless mechanisms or physical units, meaning they interpolate physics effectively but cannot serve as true physical models. This highlights a key distinction between pattern interpolation and genuine physical understanding.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) learn to fill in tables the way language models fill in text, and tables are arguably the format in which most physical measurement arrives. Did they learn any physics in the process? They are Bayesian by construction, so the question is what their prior contains. We probe it directly, evaluating four of them (TabPFN-3, TabICLv2, TabDPT and Real-TabPFN-2.5) against six baselines on datasets sampled from 316 physical equations, in and out of domain. TFMs dominate, out of the box and after tuning. But we show that their prior can represent neither a noiseless mechanism nor physical units, which is why they interpolate physics without yet being able to act as physical models.

</details>


### [201] [Cliff: Learning Process Rewards from the First Mistake](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02817)
*Peixuan Han,Runhui Wang,Ketan Ramaneti,Jie Hao,Gerald Friedland,Chris Kong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cliff is a reward shaping strategy for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) that uses an off-the-shelf LLM teacher to identify the first mistake in reasoning rollouts, converting this into token-level advantages (positive for correct prefix, negative for incorrect suffix).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR relies on coarse outcome rewards that provide limited guidance on intermediate reasoning processes. Existing approaches like process reward modeling and on-policy distillation introduce additional constraints (need for specialized reward model, assumption of identical reasoning patterns). The key insight is that once reasoning goes wrong, evaluating subsequent reasoning provides limited additional information since it's conditioned on an invalid prefix.

Method: Cliff uses an off-the-shelf LLM as a teacher to identify the first mistake in each rollout. This naturally decomposes each rollout into two parts: a correct prefix and an incorrect suffix. The signal is converted into token-level advantages - positive advantages for the correct prefix and negative feedback afterward.

Result: Experiments across 12 different scenarios demonstrate that Cliff consistently improves reasoning performance, outperforming on-policy distillation by 15% and standard GRPO by 7%, even with teachers of modest capability.

Conclusion: Cliff establishes itself as a simple, general, and effective approach for improving RLVR with richer, fine-grained supervision. The paper also analyzes the role of "ground truth" in Cliff and investigates its training dynamics.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for large language model (LLM) post-training, but its reliance on coarse outcome rewards leads to limited guidance on intermediate reasoning processes. Existing approaches such as process reward modeling and on-policy distillation introduce additional constraints, such as reliance on a specialized reward model or assuming identical reasoning patterns between teacher and student. Nevertheless, we observe that once a reasoning process first goes wrong, evaluating the subsequent reasoning provides limited additional information, as it is already conditioned on an invalid prefix. Therefore, we propose Cliff, a reward shaping strategy that utilizes an off-the-shelf LLM as a teacher to identify the first mistake in each rollout. As a result, the rollout is naturally decomposed into two parts: a correct prefix and an incorrect suffix. Cliff then converts this signal into token-level advantages, assigning positive advantages for the correct prefix and negative feedback afterward. Experiments across 12 different scenarios demonstrate that Cliff consistently improves reasoning performance, outperforming on-policy distillation by 15% and standard GRPO by 7%, even with teachers of modest capability. Furthermore, we analyse the role of ``ground truth'' in Cliff and investigate its training dynamics. These results establish Cliff as a simple, general and effective approach for improving RLVR with richer, fine-grained supervision.

</details>


### [202] [UE5M3 FP4 Block Scaling for Stable Language Model Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02846)
*Robert Hu,Carlo Luschi,Paul Balanca*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses challenges in 4-bit floating-point (FP4) pretraining by proposing a simpler recipe using E2M1 payloads with unsigned E5M3 block scales, achieving better performance than NVIDIA's Transformer Engine while being more efficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stable FP4 pretraining is difficult because E2M1 format represents only a narrow range of magnitudes. NVIDIA's Transformer Engine recipe addresses this with complex techniques (current-tensor scaling, randomized Hadamard transform, BF16 final layers) that add overhead outside the FP4 matrix multiplications. The authors seek a simpler approach with better performance.

Method: The proposed method pairs E2M1 payloads with unsigned E5M3 block scales for wider range, applies selective stochastic rounding to backward gradients, omits the randomized Hadamard transform (RHT), and uses FP4 in all eligible internal linears (no BF16 final-block exemption).

Result: Pretrained Nemotron-H 8B for nearly 190 billion tokens. The proposed block-16 recipe achieves lower final-window training loss and lower validation loss (held-out negative log-likelihood) compared to Transformer Engine. Quantized-inference downstream performance is higher on all three reported aggregates. A native execution ablation removing RHT and BF16 final-block exemption increases model-body token throughput by 21.2%.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate end-to-end software-emulated FP4 pretraining with a simpler recipe that outperforms NVIDIA's Transformer Engine. This motivates native support for E5M3 block scaling in hardware.

Abstract: Stable 4-bit floating-point (FP4) pretraining is difficult because the E2M1 payload represents only a narrow range of magnitudes. NVIDIA's Transformer Engine \nv{} recipe addresses this with current-tensor scaling, a randomized Hadamard transform (RHT), and bfloat16 (BF16) final layers, adding work outside the FP4 matrix multiplications. We instead pair E2M1 payloads with unsigned E5M3 (\ue{}) block scales. Their wider range permits periodic tensor scaling, while our recipe applies selective stochastic rounding to backward gradients, omits RHT, and uses FP4 in all eligible internal linears.
  We pretrain a Nemotron-H 8B model for nearly 190 billion tokens. Compared with Transformer Engine \nv{}, the proposed block-16 recipe finishes with lower final-window training loss and, under their respective quantized-inference policies, lower validation loss measured as held-out negative log-likelihood. Its quantized-inference downstream point estimates are also higher on all three reported aggregates. A native \nv{} execution ablation that jointly removes RHT and the BF16 final-block exemption increases measured model-body token throughput by 21.2\%. These results demonstrate end-to-end software-emulated \uefp{} pretraining with a simpler recipe and motivate native support for \ue{} block scaling.

</details>


### [203] [The Implications of Linguistic Illegibility for LLM Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02852)
*James Mickens*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces the concept of "linguistic illegibility" to describe scenarios where LLM externalized outputs or linguistically-probed features fail to accurately represent the model's internal computations. Since LLMs process information through mathematical operations over activation spaces (with lossy translation to natural language), security mechanisms relying on linguistic self-reporting (e.g., chain-of-thought monitoring, activation probing) can never be completely sound. The authors propose taint tracking as a more reliable sandboxing approach, along with robust virtualization and third-party auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to highlight a fundamental limitation in LLM security: the assumption that a model's linguistic outputs reliably reflect its internal reasoning. They seek to demonstrate that security mechanisms dependent on reading a model's linguistic state are inherently unsound and need complementary isolation techniques.

Method: The paper is a conceptual/position paper that introduces the theoretical framework of "linguistic illegibility" and argues for its implications. It analyzes the computational architecture of LLMs (math over activation spaces vs. direct language processing) and evaluates the soundness of various linguistic-based security mechanisms.

Result: The authors conclude that linguistic illegibility is potentially unavoidable in LLMs. Security mechanisms relying on model self-reporting (chain-of-thought monitoring, constitutional self-critique, activation probing) cannot be completely sound. Taint tracking provides a more reliable sandboxing approach by defining a priori which system states should never be influenced by model-produced data, independent of how the model linguistically self-reports.

Conclusion: Effective LLM sandboxing requires isolation techniques whose guarantees do not depend on reading a model's linguistic state. Taint tracking, robust virtualization, and third-party auditing collectively provide a critical safety floor beneath linguistic monitoring and could have mitigated recent sandbox exploits by frontier models.

Abstract: LLMs are trained to generate natural language. However, various strands of evidence indicate that an LLM's externalized linguistic outputs and mechanistically-extracted linguistic features can be an unreliable lens for understanding internal model computation. We introduce the term ``linguistic illegibility'' to broadly refer to scenarios in which an LLM's externalized or mechanistically-probed language artifacts fail to represent how the model actually thinks. We argue that the specter of linguistic illegibility is unavoidable for LLMs whose internal computations are not directly expressed via language, but rather math over activation spaces (with lossy translations between activation spaces and natural language happening at the bookends). If linguistic illegibility is always possible, then security mechanisms that rely on a model's linguistic self-reporting (e.g., chain-of-thought monitoring, constitutional self-critique, activation probing for linguistically-defined feature vectors) can never be completely sound; the model sandbox will always need isolation techniques whose guarantees do not depend on reading a model's linguistic state at all. We argue that observing a model's outputs using taint tracking is a promising approach for an effective sandbox: regardless of how a model linguistically self-reports, a taint tracking policy can define, a priori, various pieces of system state that should never be influenced by model-produced data. We also discuss several additional sandboxing mechanisms (e.g., robust virtualization, third-party auditing of sandboxing configurations) which collectively provide a critical floor beneath linguistic monitoring, and would have mitigated recent sandbox exploits by frontier models.

</details>


### [204] [Graph Machine: Towards Better Pretraining via Edges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02881)
*Lintai Hou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Graph Machine (GM) is a neural network architecture that maintains an O(n)-sized state accessed through sparse, dynamic routing using differentiable "edges" (pointer-like objects). When 75% of dense Transformer layers in Qwen3-0.6B are replaced with GM sparse layers and pretrained on 15.7B tokens, only 2-4 tokens per KV head need to be retrieved from 4,096 total, with minimal loss degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods either use fixed-size states or sparse but static routing. The authors aim to preserve O(n) complexity in sparse layers without restricting the accessible state size to O(1), seeking a more flexible and efficient approach to sparse attention.

Method: GM uses edges - pointer-like objects updated differentiably via a referral mechanism resembling pointer chasing. In each sparse layer, only 2 or 4 tokens (out of 4,096) are retrieved per KV head through dynamic routing, replacing 75% of dense Transformer layers.

Result: With 2 tokens retrieved per KV head, loss degrades only slightly. With 4 tokens, the best model marginally improves loss compared to the baseline.

Conclusion: The Graph Machine demonstrates that sparse, dynamic routing can efficiently replace a large portion of dense Transformer layers with minimal performance degradation, offering a promising direction for efficient large language model architectures.

Abstract: We introduce the Graph Machine (GM), an architecture that maintains an $O(n)$-sized state and accesses it through sparse, dynamic routing. Unlike methods with fixed-size states or sparse but static routing, GM preserves $O(n)$ complexity in its sparse layers without restricting the potentially accessible state size to $O(1)$. Instead, GM uses edges - pointer-like objects updated differentiably by a referral mechanism resembling pointer chasing. We replace 75% of the dense Transformer layers in Qwen3-0.6B with GM sparse layers and pretrain from scratch on 15.7B tokens. With only 2 of 4,096 tokens retrieved per KV head in each sparse layer, loss degrades only slightly; with 4, the best model marginally improves loss.

</details>


### [205] [A Common Measure of Communication for Speech Brain-Computer Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02887)
*Dulhan Jayalath,Benjamin Ballyk,Oiwi Parker Jones*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces open-vocabulary mutual information (OVMI) as a standardized metric to evaluate speech brain-computer interfaces (BCIs), enabling fair comparison across systems that use different vocabularies, recording methods, and datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The speech BCI field lacks a common measure of progress because existing systems use different datasets, recording methods, speech types, and vocabularies, making reported scores incomparable. Additionally, commonly used metrics like accuracy and WER can overstate a system's ability to communicate a user's intended speech.

Method: The authors derive OVMI, an information-theoretic quantity that measures the information conveyed by a decoder relative to a reference distribution over words a user may wish to communicate. This allows capabilities measured under different conditions to be evaluated on a common communication scale.

Result: OVMI reveals that ordinarily reported accuracy and WER can overstate a system's communication capability. The metric exposes trade-offs between vocabulary coverage and decoding accuracy, and demonstrates that vocabulary selection maximizing OVMI yields up to 16.3% relative improvement in accuracy across three speech domains.

Conclusion: OVMI provides the speech BCI community with a principled way to compare heterogeneous systems, improve vocabulary design, and measure progress in the field. It enables meaningful comparisons across different experimental conditions and identifies optimal vocabulary strategies.

Abstract: Speech brain-computer interfaces (speech BCIs) translate neural activity into language, offering a path towards restoring speech for people with paralysis and, more broadly, enabling new forms of natural human-computer interaction. Despite this promise, the field lacks a common measure of progress because systems use different datasets, recording methods, types of speech, and vocabularies, so their reported scores are rarely comparable. Underlying this measurement problem are two unresolved questions: (i) what distribution of words should a speech BCI enable a user to communicate, and (ii) how much information from this distribution can a system convey. We address both by deriving open-vocabulary mutual information (OVMI), an information-theoretic quantity that measures the information conveyed by a decoder relative to a reference distribution over the words a user may wish to communicate. This allows capabilities measured under different conditions, such as distinct vocabularies, to be evaluated on a common communication scale. We show that ordinarily reported accuracy, word error rate (WER), and other metrics computed only over the words a system supports can overstate how much of a user's intended speech the system can communicate. We then use OVMI to compare existing systems, expose trade-offs between how much of the user's language a system supports and how accurately it decodes those words, show that these comparisons depend on what the user is expected to communicate, and demonstrate that selecting a vocabulary to maximise OVMI yields up to 16.3% relative improvement in accuracy across three speech domains. OVMI therefore provides the speech BCI community with a principled way to compare heterogeneous systems, improve vocabulary design, and measure progress in the field.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [206] [Barriers to Using Static Application Security Testing (SAST) Tools: A Literature Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01669)
*Zachary Wadhams,Clemente Izurieta,Ann Marie Reinhold*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper surveys recent literature to understand why developers are reluctant to adopt Static Application Security Testing (SAST) tools and identifies the usability problems they encounter. The study finds both inherent challenges requiring developer investment and tool shortcomings that need addressing by creators. The authors argue that widespread SAST adoption requires effort from both developers (willingness to invest time) and tool creators (simplifying usage).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Software security vulnerabilities are a leading cause of breaches, and while SAST tools can help identify and resolve these issues, many development teams have not adopted them. The researchers aim to uncover the specific reasons for developer apprehension toward SAST tools.

Method: Survey of recent literature to identify and categorize the problems developers face when using SAST tools.

Result: The study identifies a variety of usability problems developers encounter with SAST tools, categorized into: (1) inherent problems requiring some level of developer investment, and (2) tool shortcomings that SAST tool creators must address.

Conclusion: Achieving widespread SAST adoption requires dual effort: developers must accept that some investment is needed, while tool creators must simplify tool usage. Overcoming adoption barriers requires addressing both technical and human factors.

Abstract: Developers face a challenging problem with no clear solution. Modern software breaches can wreak havoc on businesses and individuals alike. With code vulnerabilities being a leading cause, securing applications must be a priority for developers. Static Application Security Testing (SAST) has the potential to harden applications by assisting in the identification and resolution of security vulnerabilities. Despite this, many development teams have not adopted SAST tools into their environment. In this paper, we survey the recent literature to uncover why some developers are apprehensive towards SAST and identify what specific problems they encounter when using it. We found a variety of usability problems developers face when using SAST. Some are inherent of the tool and ultimately require some level of developer investment while others are tool shortcomings that SAST tool creators must address. Ultimately, we argue that in order to drive widespread adoption and consistent SAST usage, developers will need to embrace that some investment is required. Simultaneously, developers will be more likely to integrate SAST tools into their workflows if the creators of SAST tools simplify many aspects related to tool usage. Surmounting the primary obstacles preventing the adoption of SAST requires full consideration of both the technical and human factors.

</details>


### [207] [RosettaBitcoin: An Artifact-Backed Experience Report on Verification Infrastructure for Agent-Assisted Consensus Validators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01702)
*Donavon Guyot*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This experience report analyzes RosettaBitcoin, a single-developer project that built twelve separately implemented Bitcoin testnet4 consensus validators. The study examines a software snapshot to evaluate the project's verification infrastructure, finding that while all twelve ports achieved 45/45 script-corpus proofs and strict 5,000-block baselines, no port had an empty-state-to-tip proof or satisfied the binary full-node gate. The report suggests that explicit failure records, fixtures, and port-owned proofs can improve auditability of agent-assisted systems, though controlled ablations are needed to validate causal improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent-assisted software projects are typically reported through demonstrations or aggregate benchmarks that hide how correctness claims were established. This study aims to provide a transparent, detailed analysis of a real-world project to understand how verification infrastructure works in practice and identify gaps in auditable software development.

Method: The researchers analyzed an immutable software snapshot (DOI 10.5281/zenodo.20738249) from 17 June 2026, examining the tracked SQLite evidence database, curated artifact index, conformance fixtures, validation scripts, blocker records, and version history. They also analyzed a separate diagnostic supplement with a pure-Mojo cryptographic backend validation.

Result: At the snapshot, all twelve ports had port-owned 45/45 script-corpus proofs and strict 5,000-block baselines. Nine had canonical clean validation lanes at 50,000, 100,000, and post-100,000 blocks. Java had one 19.86-second maintenance artifact. No port had empty-state-to-tip proof, and no port satisfied the project's binary full-node gate. Docker and live-node capability gaps remained.
