The diagnostic supplement showed a pure-Mojo backend validated to height 140,234 with a 45-case shadow comparison, but this evidence was noncanonical, noncomparable, and class-bounded.

Conclusion: The case demonstrates that explicit failure records, fixtures, port-owned proofs, and validating imports can enhance auditability of agent-assisted systems. However, controlled ablations and external replications are necessary to determine whether such infrastructure causally improves development outcomes. The study highlights significant gaps in current verification approaches, particularly the lack of full-state-to-tip proofs and binary full-node gate compliance.

Abstract: Agent-assisted software projects are often reported through demonstrations or aggregate benchmarks that conceal how correctness claims were admitted. This experience report studies RosettaBitcoin, a single-developer project that built twelve separately implemented Bitcoin testnet4 consensus validators, through its immutable 17 June 2026 software snapshot (DOI 10.5281/zenodo.20738249). We analyze the snapshot's tracked SQLite evidence database, curated artifact index, conformance fixtures, validation scripts, blocker records, and version history. At the snapshot, all twelve ports had port-owned 45/45 script-corpus proofs and strict 5,000-block baselines. Nine had canonical clean 50,000-block, 100,000-block, and post-100,000 validation lanes. Java had one 19.86-second near-tip maintenance artifact. No port had an empty-state-to-tip proof, and no port satisfied the project's binary full-node gate; Docker and live-node capability gaps remained.
  The artifact history also records a 3 h 17 min 57 s Zig scaffold-to-50,000 span, but the observed intervals describe non-equivalent tasks and cannot estimate effort, productivity, or causality. A separate diagnostic supplement preserves evidence that a pure-Mojo cryptographic backend validated fresh state to height 100,000 and resumed to 140,234, agreed on a 45-case shadow comparison, rejected six crafted invalid classes, and was killed by three targeted mutations. That evidence is noncanonical, noncomparable, and class-bounded. The case suggests that explicit failure records, fixtures, port-owned proofs, and validating imports can make agent-assisted systems more auditable. Controlled ablations and external replications are needed to test whether such infrastructure causally improves development outcomes.

</details>


### [208] [Harness Engineering in LLM Tool Use via Agent-Native Reusable Tool Primitives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01736)
*Haibo Jin,Suijin Wang,Xucheng Yu,Haojing Luo,Haohan Wang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces Tool Primitives, a novel design framework that replaces rigid API schema-based tool calling with natural language interfaces, enabling LLM-based tools to handle schema resolution and execution internally. Combined with ToolFace (a repository of 25,519 functions) and HEART (a Harness Engineering framework with Planner, Router, and Verifier components), this system addresses challenges of brittle multi-step reasoning and performance degradation with large tool catalogues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address two key challenges in LLM-augmented systems: (1) brittle multi-step and multi-turn reasoning caused by incompatible tool output types and API schemas, and (2) performance degradation when dealing with large tool catalogues. Existing approaches require rigid API schema-based invocation, which limits flexibility and scalability.

Method: The proposed method consists of three components: (1) Tool Primitives - wraps each tool with an LLM interface that handles schema resolution and execution internally, using natural language as the interface for tool calling; (2) ToolFace - a centralized repository of 25,519 functions from which LLMs dynamically retrieve only relevant tools at inference time; (3) HEART - a Harness Engineering framework comprising Planner (dynamic tool invocation planning), Router (tool selection), and Verifier (feedback-driven recovery) that jointly support multi-step execution and error recovery.

Result: Experimental results on five benchmarks show HEART outperforms SFT-based models by 10% on average and surpasses GPT-5.4, Claude-4.6-Sonnet, and Gemini-3.1-Pro by 6% on average while reducing API cost by up to 85%. On 50 real-world tasks, HEART achieves 84% task completion rate, which is 3.8× higher than the average of three frontier commercial models (22%).

Conclusion: The combination of Tool Primitives, ToolFace, and HEART provides an effective solution for enabling LLMs to handle complex real-world tasks with large tool catalogues. The natural language-based tool calling interface, dynamic tool retrieval, and multi-component orchestration framework significantly improve performance while reducing costs. The approach demonstrates the viability of agent-native, reusable tool primitives for building reliable LLM-powered applications.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) augmented with external tools have demonstrated remarkable capability in solving complex real-world tasks. However, existing approaches suffer from two key challenges: brittle multi-step and multi-turn reasoning caused by incompatible tool output types and API schemas, and performance degradation under large tool catalogues. To address these, we introduce \textbf{Tool Primitives}, a design that replaces rigid API schema-based invocation with natural language as the interface for tool calling, where each tool is wrapped with an LLM interface that handles schema resolution and execution internally, enabling natural inter-tool communication for nested and multi-turn tool calling. Building on Tool Primitives, we host \textbf{ToolFace}, a centralized repository of 25,519 functions from which LLMs dynamically retrieve only the relevant tools at inference time, eliminating the need to enumerate raw API schemas in context. To orchestrate Tool Primitives and ToolFace reliably in complex settings, we further propose \textbf{HEART}, a \textbf{H}arness \textbf{E}ngineering framework via \textbf{A}gent-native, \textbf{R}eusable \textbf{T}ool Primitives, comprising a Planner, Router, and Verifier that jointly support dynamic tool invocation planning, multi-step execution, and feedback-driven recovery.
  Experiments on five benchmarks demonstrate that HEART outperforms SFT-based models by $10\%$ on average and surpasses GPT-5.4, Claude-4.6-Sonnet, and Gemini-3.1-Pro by $6\%$ on average while reducing API cost by up to $85\%$. On 50 real-world tasks, HEART achieves $84\%$ task completion, $3.8\times$ the average of three frontier commercial models ($22\%$).

</details>


### [209] [From Silicon to Boot Code: Extending Automated Program Repair to Firmware-Layer Security Workarounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01769)
*Maisha Mastora,Dean Sullivan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper extends Automated Program Repair (APR) methodology from RTL/HLS design-time to firmware post-silicon. By applying dictionary-guided localize-synthesize-validate approach to EDK II (UEFI) firmware, they recover all 3 known CVE-fix campaigns and discover 2 additional bug families. Four localizers achieve 100% recall with precision ranging from 2.1-100%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current APR research is limited to pre-silicon design phase. Once hardware vulnerabilities surface post-silicon, patches must be manually generated. This work addresses the gap by extending APR to firmware layer for automated patch synthesis rather than just deployment.

Method: 1) Automated commit-clustering miner to surface recurring fix templates across EDK II repository without CVE identifiers. 2) Built four independent localizers for: missing speculation barriers (Spectre v1), missing bounds checks (decompression CVE), missing RSB stuffing (CVE-2017-5715), and missing integer-overflow guards. 3) Root-cause analysis of false positives using intra-procedural analysis.

Result: Recovered all 3 known CVE-fix campaigns and found 2 additional candidate bug families. All four localizers achieve 100% recall; precision ranges from 2.1-15.5% on C families to 100% on assembly and HOB families. False positive root causes: 77-90% attributed to two intra-procedural causes, with inter-procedural alias-analysis gap measured at 15-20%. Held-out test confirms Spectre v1 localization at 100% recall; fifth dictionary entry (CVE-2018-3630) shows methodology extends to new bug signatures. Naive syntactic baseline recalls only 14% vs 100% for their detector.

Conclusion: Dictionary-guided APR methodology successfully extends from RTL to firmware layer. The approach can recover known CVEs without CVE identifiers, discover new bug families, and generalize to new bug signatures with low cost. This enables a unified hardware-to-firmware correctness lifecycle, though precision on C code needs improvement due to inter-procedural analysis gaps.

Abstract: Automated program repair (APR) research has been constrained to design time. Current techniques localize and fix bugs in RTL or HLS designs before a chip reaches production. Once a hardware vulnerability surfaces post-silicon, the patch must be manually generated: existing automation addresses patch deployment but not patch synthesis. We study the feasibility of extending a dictionary-guided, localize-synthesize-validate APR methodology originally developed for RTL repair to this firmware layer. An automated commit-clustering miner surfaces recurring fix templates across the EDK II (UEFI) firmware repository's full commit history without depending on known CVE identifiers, recovering all three known CVE-fix campaigns and surfacing two additional candidate bug families. Grounded in real fix evidence, we build four independent localizers: missing speculation barriers in C (CVE-2017-5753, Spectre v1), missing bounds checks before array writes in C (a decompression library CVE), missing Return Stack Buffer stuffing in x86 assembly (CVE-2017-5715), and missing integer-overflow guards in Hand-Off Block creation code (surfaced by the miner itself). All four achieve 100% recall; precision ranges from 2.1-15.5% on the C families to 100% on the assembly and HOB families. Root-cause analysis of the C-family false positives attributes 77-90% to two intra-procedural causes, isolating the inter-procedural alias-analysis gap as a measured 15-20% rather than an estimate. A held-out test confirms Spectre v1 localization holds at 100% recall on unseen files; a fifth, independently built dictionary entry (CVE-2018-3630) shows the methodology extends to a new bug signature at low cost; and a naive syntactic baseline recalls at most 14% where our detector recalls 100%. We frame these results within a broader research agenda for a unified hardware-to-firmware correctness lifecycle.

</details>


### [210] [Modelstamp: Pre-Deserialization Verification of Machine-Learning Artifacts and Runtime Environment State](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01781)
*Anagha Dhekne*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Modelstamp is a lightweight Python library that verifies both artifact integrity and runtime-environment state before deserializing persisted machine-learning models. It uses SHA-256 digests, runtime metadata, and tracked package versions in a sidecar JSON manifest, with optional HMAC authentication for producer-verifier workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The problem addressed is that persisted ML models can remain byte-identical while their software environments evolve, creating verification gaps that artifact integrity checks alone cannot detect. This creates security risks when loading models in environments that differ from when they were saved.

Method: Modelstamp associates serialized artifacts with a sidecar JSON manifest containing: (1) SHA-256 digest of the artifact, (2) runtime metadata, (3) installed versions from a bounded tracked-package set. A model-relevant subset determines which package versions participate in drift comparison. Optional HMAC authentication enables shared-secret workflows. At verification time, both the artifact and current environment are checked against recorded evidence before deserialization.

Result: Evaluated using 14 controlled environment-drift scenarios, 8 trust-boundary scenarios, and artifact-size scaling benchmark (10 MiB to 1 GiB). Environment-drift experiments behaved as specified across dependency changes, unchanged environments, and unrelated environmental changes. Trust-boundary experiments confirmed intended detections and expected limitations (shared-key forgery, replay). Median verification time: 0.032s (10 MiB) to 3.334s (1 GiB), with throughput of 307-312 MiB/s.

Conclusion: Modelstamp serves as a complementary pre-deserialization reference-state verification control. It is not intended to replace dependency-management systems, malicious-model detection, safe deserialization, or public publisher authentication. The tool addresses the specific gap of verifying runtime-environment state alongside artifact integrity.

Abstract: Persisted machine-learning models can remain byte-identical while the software environments in which they are loaded evolve, creating a verification problem that artifact integrity checks alone cannot expose. This paper presents Modelstamp, a lightweight Python persistence library for verifying artifact integrity and represented runtime-environment state before deserialization. At persistence time, Modelstamp associates a serialized artifact with a sidecar JSON manifest containing a SHA-256 digest, runtime metadata, and installed versions from a bounded tracked-package set; a separately recorded model-relevant subset determines which package versions participate in drift comparison. Optional HMAC authentication supports workflows in which the producer and verifier share a secret key. At verification time, the artifact and represented current environment are checked against this recorded evidence before the model is deserialized. Modelstamp is evaluated using 14 controlled environment-drift scenarios, eight controlled trust-boundary scenarios, and an artifact-size scaling benchmark from 10 MiB to 1 GiB. The controlled drift experiments behaved as specified across relevant dependency changes, unchanged environments, and unrelated environmental changes, including broader noise controls. The trust-boundary experiments similarly confirmed both intended detections and expected limitations, including shared-key forgery and replay. Median verification time increased from 0.032 s at 10 MiB to 3.334 s at 1 GiB, with measured throughput of approximately 307-312 MiB/s in the benchmark environment. These results characterize Modelstamp as a complementary pre-deserialization reference-state verification control rather than as a replacement for dependency-management systems, malicious-model detection, safe deserialization, or public publisher authentication.

</details>


### [211] [ExecRetrieval: Measuring the Functional-Correctness Gap in Code-Embedding Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01865)
*Aaryan Kapoor,Md Abdullah Al Hafiz Khan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ExecRetrieval is a new benchmark for evaluating code retrieval systems, containing 939 Python tasks with execution-verified canonical implementations and buggy distractors. The benchmark tests whether embeddings can functionally discriminate correct from near-clone-but-incorrect code, revealing that top systems achieve 100% at exec@10 but only 33.1% at exec@1, with rank-1 misses being buggy variants 91.5-99.4% of the time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing code-retrieval benchmarks lack execution-verified buggy variants, making it impossible to determine whether embeddings can functionally discriminate correct code from near-clone-but-incorrect code. The authors argue that retrieval accuracy should be measured against functional correctness rather than lexical similarity.

Method: The authors created ExecRetrieval with 939 Python tasks, each paired with one execution-verified canonical implementation and up to four execution-verified buggy distractors generated by mechanical single-target edits. They evaluated 23 dense embedding configurations plus BM25 using provider-native invocation with paired McNemar tests and query-level bootstrap intervals.

Result: The top hosted system achieves exec@10 = 1.00 but only exec@1 = 0.331. Rank-1 misses are paired buggy variants 91.5-99.4% of the time across the four leading systems. The canonical scores below at least one of its four paired distractors in 67-78% of queries on leading systems.

Conclusion: Even state-of-the-art embedding models struggle to reliably distinguish correct code from buggy near-clones at rank-1. The benchmark reveals a significant gap between retrieval coverage (exec@10) and top-ranked accuracy (exec@1), suggesting that current embedding-based code retrieval needs improvement in functional discrimination.

Abstract: Embedding-based code retrieval is a core component of coding agents and retrieval-augmented code generation, where retrieving correct code matters more than retrieving lexically similar code. Existing code-retrieval benchmarks do not plant controlled, execution-verified single-edit variants of each query's canonical implementation in the search pool, leaving the question of whether embeddings can functionally discriminate correct from near-clone-but-incorrect code unanswered in a retrieval setting. Resolving this requires a benchmark whose search pool itself contains the relevant counterfactuals -- execution-verified buggy variants near-identical to each canonical -- so that a retriever's rank ordering can be directly tested for functional discrimination rather than topical or identity overlap. We introduce ExecRetrieval, 939 Python tasks each paired with one execution-verified canonical implementation and up to four execution-verified buggy distractors, each generated by a mechanical mutation making a single targeted edit, and evaluate 23 dense embedding configurations plus BM25 under provider-native invocation with paired McNemar tests and query-level bootstrap intervals. With near-clone counterfactuals in the pool, the top hosted system reaches exec@10 = 1.00 but only exec@1 = 0.331; rank-1 misses are paired buggy variants 91.5-99.4% of the time across the four leading systems, and the canonical scores below at least one of its four paired distractors in 67-78% of queries on the leading systems. The full dataset, execution oracle, embedding matrices, environment snapshot, and pairwise statistical tests are released at the URL in Appendix D.

</details>


### [212] [From Prompting to Engineering: A Research Agenda for Prompt Engineering in Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02248)
*Vincenzo De Martino,Giovanna Broccia,Fabiano Pecorelli,Jennifer Horkoff,Riccardo Coppola,Antonino Ferraro,Quim Motger,Emma McKenzie,Shahbaz Siddeeq*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper summarizes discussions from PROMPT-SE 2026, the first workshop on empirical prompt engineering for Software Engineering. It identifies five key areas for research: prompt artifacts and standardization, evaluation and benchmarking, lifecycle integration, human-AI collaboration and skills, and governance/privacy/technical debt. The paper proposes a research agenda to professionalize prompt engineering practices in SE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompt engineering is increasingly used across SE activities (requirements, coding, testing, documentation, etc.), but current practices are ad hoc, informal, and lack systematic evaluation, management, traceability, and governance. The authors aim to move prompt engineering from informal interactions toward systematic, maintainable, evaluable, traceable, and governable SE practices.

Method: The authors organized a structured community discussion at PROMPT-SE 2026 (co-located with EASE 2026). Participants discussed current prompting practices, challenges to adoption/evaluation, and future directions. The discussions were synthesized into five thematic areas.

Result: Five key research areas were identified: (1) prompt artifacts and standardization; (2) evaluation and benchmarking; (3) lifecycle integration; (4) human-AI collaboration and skills; and (5) governance, privacy, and technical debt. A research agenda was outlined for each area to advance prompt engineering in SE.

Conclusion: The paper presents a research agenda to professionalize prompt engineering in SE, moving from ad hoc interactions to systematic practices. Future work should address standardization, evaluation methods, lifecycle integration, human-AI collaboration, and governance frameworks for prompt engineering in software development.

Abstract: Prompt engineering is increasingly used across Software Engineering (SE) activities, including requirements analysis, coding, testing, documentation, repository analysis, and planning. Yet prompts and related instruction artifacts are often created and evolved through task-specific and informal practices, with limited support for their systematic evaluation, management, traceability, and governance. To examine how SE can contribute to the maturation of these practices, we organized a structured community discussion at the First International Workshop on Empirical Prompt Engineering for Software Engineering (PROMPT-SE), co-located with EASE 2026. Participants discussed current prompting practices, challenges to their adoption and evaluation, and future directions for integrating prompt engineering into software development. We synthesized these discussions into five areas: prompt artifacts and standardization; evaluation and benchmarking; lifecycle integration; human-AI collaboration and skills; and governance, privacy, and technical debt. Based on these areas, we outline a research agenda to move prompt engineering from predominantly ad hoc interactions toward more systematic, maintainable, evaluable, traceable, and governable SE practices.

</details>


### [213] [AgOSS: A Dataset and Multi-Layer Characterization of Open-Source Agricultural Software](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02591)
*Vatsal Dudhaiya,Mikhail Golovenchits,Aryan Banerjee,James C. Davis*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents AgOSS, a dataset of 66 agricultural open-source repositories, and investigates whether agricultural software has different supply chain security characteristics compared to non-agricultural software. The study finds that governance is independent of dependency risk, and while agricultural projects score lower on security metrics, this is due to project size and maturity rather than the agricultural domain itself.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The agricultural software ecosystem (farm management platforms, cloud services, edge gateways, embedded systems, sensors) forms a domain-specific software supply chain that has received little empirical security attention. It was unknown whether this ecosystem's security posture differs from comparable non-agricultural software, and if so, whether the difference reflects the agricultural domain or the size/maturity of projects.

Method: The researchers created AgOSS, a dataset of 66 repositories across six architectural categories. They assessed supply chain security using: (1) OpenSSF Scorecard, (2) governance metrics, (3) SBOM-based dependency analysis, and (4) KEV (Known Exploited Vulnerabilities) matching. They compared agricultural projects against matched non-agricultural controls using statistical analysis including regression.

Result: Two main findings: (1) Governance is largely independent of inherited dependency risk - Scorecard tracks community activity but shows no association with vulnerability count, density, or known-exploited count; in regression, exposure signal loads on architectural category rather than domain. (2) Agricultural projects score far lower on raw Scorecard, but size and maturity confound this gap - the residual loses significance under matching and regression.

Conclusion: Securing the agricultural open-source software ecosystem requires investing in contributor capacity and dependency hygiene, not agriculture-specific controls. The security gaps are due to project maturity and size, not the agricultural domain itself.

Abstract: Much of agriculture depends on open-source software spanning farm management platforms, cloud services, edge gateways, embedded systems, and field-deployed sensors, forming a domain-specific software supply chain that has drawn little empirical security attention. It is unknown whether this ecosystem's supply chain security posture differs from that of comparable non-agricultural software, and if it does, whether the difference reflects the agricultural domain or the size and maturity of the projects within it.
  As a step towards securing agricultural open-source software, we present AgOSS, a dataset of 66 repositories across six architectural categories. We assess supply chain security within the dataset via OpenSSF Scorecard, governance metrics, SBOM-based dependency analysis, and KEV matching, and compare against matched non-agricultural controls. We report two findings. First, governance is largely independent of inherited dependency risk. Scorecard tracks community activity, but we detect no association with vulnerability count, density, or known-exploited count, and in regression the exposure signal loads on architectural category rather than domain. Second, agricultural projects score far lower on raw Scorecard, but size and maturity confound the gap: the residual loses significance under matching and regression. Securing this ecosystem means investing in contributor capacity and dependency hygiene, not agriculture-specific controls.

</details>


### [214] [Automated Vulnerability Injection in Smart Contracts Using Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02624)
*Luca Migliaccio,Roberto Natella,Naghmeh Ivaki,Nuno Laranjeiro,Marco Vieira*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper proposes using Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically inject vulnerabilities into Solidity smart contracts to create datasets for evaluating vulnerability detection tools. From nearly 1,000 generated candidates, 32 confirmed vulnerable contracts spanning 25 vulnerability types survived validation (16.58% survival rate). The method reveals complementary but incomplete coverage among three static analyzers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assessing vulnerability detection tools for smart contracts requires datasets with known ground truth, yet such datasets are scarce and difficult to build manually.

Method: The authors use LLMs to automatically inject 49 vulnerability types from OpenSCV into real-world contracts from SmartBugs. Generated contracts undergo a multi-step validation pipeline checking compilation, execution, business logic, and presence of the intended vulnerability. The validated contracts are then used to assess three static analyzers.

Result: From nearly 1,000 candidate variants, 32 confirmed vulnerable contracts spanning 25 vulnerability types survived (16.58% survival rate). Surviving contracts concentrate in structurally simpler targets and vulnerability types with localized syntactic patterns. The three static analyzers show complementary but incomplete coverage profiles.

Conclusion: LLM-based vulnerability injection is feasible, but exposes key limitations in scalability and diversity. The approach works better for structurally simpler contracts and vulnerability types with localized syntactic patterns.

Abstract: Assessing vulnerability detection tools for smart contracts requires datasets with known ground truth, yet such datasets are scarce and difficult to build by hand. We propose an approach that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically inject vulnerabilities into Solidity smart contracts, and demonstrate it in a case study targeting 49 vulnerability types from OpenSCV. Injected contracts are validated through a multi-step pipeline checking compilation, execution, business logic, and the presence of the intended vulnerability. Applied to real-world contracts from SmartBugs, LLMs generate nearly 1,000 candidate variants; after deduplication and validation, 32 confirmed vulnerable contracts spanning 25 vulnerability types survive (a 16.58% survival rate). Surviving contracts concentrate in structurally simpler targets and vulnerability types with localized syntactic patterns. We report practical challenges including LLMs' non-determinism and the difficulty of preserving contract semantics. We then use the validated contracts to assess three static analyzers, revealing complementary and incomplete coverage profiles. Results show that LLM-based vulnerability injection is feasible, while exposing key limitations in scalability and diversity.

</details>


### [215] [The Import Tax: A Longitudinal Measurement of Startup Cost in the Python Ecosystem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02753)
*Trinath Sai Subhash Reddy Pittala*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: First systematic measurement of Python import costs across 500 top PyPI packages over 5 years reveals heavily skewed distribution: median 6ms but 99th percentile 354ms. Bytecode compilation costs 3-22x more, submodules up to 294x more than top-level. Mean growth (+11-13%/year) far exceeds median (+1.6-2.4%/year), indicating heavy tail problem. Python 3.15's lazy import mode eliminates cost but breaks 2% of top packages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Python programs pay for imports at every process start, cost invisible in steady-state benchmarks but dominant for CLI tools, test workers, and serverless cold starts. Python 3.15 adds explicit lazy imports (PEP 810) on anecdotal evidence with no systematic ecosystem measurement existing.

Method: Measured 500 most-downloaded PyPI packages sampled quarterly over 5 years across 6 CPython versions (3.9-3.14) on two platforms (Apple M5/macOS and Intel Xeon/Linux), totaling 63,431 measurements. Also directly measured Python 3.15's global lazy-import mode.

Result: Half of packages import in under 6ms, but 99th percentile is 354ms. First import after installation costs 3-22x more (bytecode compilation). Importing submodules costs up to 294x more than top-level import. Median package cost grows +1.6-2.4%/year while mean grows +11-13%/year. Newer interpreters import same code 1.16x slower on macOS but not Linux. Single point release (3.11.5 vs 3.11.16) swings cost by 1.34x. Global lazy mode makes imports essentially free but breaks 8 of 414 top packages.

Conclusion: Import cost is heavily skewed with median 6ms but 99th percentile at 354ms. Growth is concentrated in a heavy tail (+11-13%/year mean vs +1.6-2.4%/year median). Interpreter version impacts vary by platform (1.16x slower on macOS, not on Linux). Global lazy mode makes imports essentially free but breaks 8 of 414 top packages.

Abstract: Python programs pay for their imports at every process start, a cost that is invisible in steady-state benchmarks but dominant for command-line tools, test workers, and serverless cold starts. Python 3.15 adds explicit lazy imports (PEP 810) largely on anecdotal evidence; no systematic measurement of the ecosystem's import cost exists. We present one: the 500 most-downloaded PyPI packages, sampled quarterly over five years of releases, measured under six CPython versions (3.9-3.14) on two platforms (Apple M5/macOS and Intel Xeon/Linux), for 63,431 measurements in total, plus direct measurement of 3.15's global lazy-import mode. Import cost is heavily skewed: half of packages import in under 6 ms, but the 99th percentile is 354 ms, the first import after installation costs 3-22x more (bytecode compilation), and importing a package's submodules costs up to 294x more than the top-level import that benchmarks report. The median package's cost grows only +1.6-2.4%/year, but the mean grows +11-13%/year: growth is concentrated in a heavy tail. Newer interpreters import the same code 1.16x slower on macOS, but not on Linux, and a single point release (3.11.5 vs. 3.11.16) swings cost by 1.34x. Global lazy mode makes import statements essentially free, yet breaks 8 of 414 top packages. Harness and dataset are available on request.

</details>


### [216] [Type Hints in Python Libraries and Frameworks: An Empirical Analysis of Adoption and Maintenance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02782)
*Thiago Roberto Magalhães,Fabio Petrillo,João Eduardo Montandon*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This empirical study examines type hint adoption across 1,000 popular GitHub repositories, finding 91% use type hints but with inconsistent adoption. Libraries primarily use type hints as API contracts (parameters: 45.8%, returns: 35.9% coverage) rather than comprehensive implementation descriptions, with built-in types dominating (73.0%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While type hints are widely available in Python, little is known about how they are adopted and maintained in libraries and frameworks. This study aims to understand the adoption patterns, usage, maintenance practices, and rationale behind using type hints in Python ecosystem.

Method: Analyzed 1,000 popular GitHub repositories, identifying libraries and frameworks and extracting their type annotations. Examined annotation coverage, locations and origins of annotations, their evolution across git histories, and the relationship between developer annotations and types inferred by Pyright.

Result: 91% of analyzed libraries use type hints at least once, though adoption is inconsistent. Among libraries with systematic usage, function parameters have 45.8% median coverage and return types have 35.9%. Built-in types dominate at 73.0%. When modified, annotations tend to migrate to more expressive types. Developers annotate members even when Pyright can infer their types, and these annotations often simplify the inferred types.

Conclusion: Type hints in Python libraries and frameworks primarily serve as API contracts rather than comprehensive descriptions of implementation details. The findings suggest opportunities for tooling that prioritizes public interfaces, identifies meaningful annotation changes, and supports maintainers in evolving type information.

Abstract: Context: In Python, type hints allow developers to annotate variables and functions with explicit type information, improving code clarity and reliability. Although type hints are widely available, little is known about how they are adopted and maintained in libraries and frameworks. Objective: We investigate the adoption, usage, maintenance, and rationale of type hints in Python libraries and frameworks. Method: We analyzed 1,000 popular GitHub repositories, identifying libraries and frameworks and extracting their type annotations. We examined annotation coverage, the locations and origins of annotations, their evolution across git histories, and the relationship between developer annotations and types inferred by Pyright. Results: Of the analyzed repositories, 91% of libraries use type hints at least once, although adoption is inconsistent. Among libraries with systematic usage, maintainers prioritize function parameters and return types, with median coverage of 45.8% and 35.9%, respectively, and mainly use built-in types (73.0%). When modified, annotations tend to migrate to more expressive types. Developers annotate members even when Pyright can infer their types, and these annotations often simplify the inferred type. Conclusion: Type hints in Python libraries and frameworks primarily serve as API contracts rather than comprehensive descriptions of implementation details. The findings suggest opportunities for tooling that prioritizes public interfaces, identifies meaningful annotation changes, and supports maintainers in evolving type information.

</details>


### [217] [ShikumiMiner: Mining Recurring Implementation Patterns in AI Codebases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02789)
*Afsana Tasnim,Sheikh Motahar Naim*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ShikumiMiner is a static-analysis framework that uses AST and CFG features to detect and compare recurring implementation patterns in C++ local LLM codebases. It analyzes ten GitHub repositories and classifies functions into seven categories using a multi-label Random Forest model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While thousands of LLM projects exist in open-source repositories, it remains unclear whether these projects share underlying implementation patterns. Understanding these patterns could provide valuable insights for developers aiming to build LLM applications.

Method: The paper proposes ShikumiMiner, a static-analysis framework combining Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) and Control Flow Graph (CFG) features. Functions from ten GitHub open-source LLM repositories are analyzed and classified into seven study-specific categories using a multi-label Random Forest model.

Result: The paper classifies functions from ten GitHub repositories into seven categories, revealing recurring implementation patterns in C++ local LLM codebases.

Conclusion: Studying these detected patterns can provide useful insights for developers aiming to design LLM applications, offering new dimensions for understanding and developing LLM projects.

Abstract: Large language models are paving the way towards innovation by understanding, analyzing, summarizing and generating content in the modern world. Currently there are thousands of LLM projects developed by engineers in open-source repositories. However, whether these LLM projects have underlying patterns or not remains a question. Exploring these underlying patterns will give new dimensions to the developers who aim to develop these LLM projects. In this paper, we propose ShikumiMiner, a static-analysis framework that combines Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) and Control Flow Graph (CFG) features to detect and compare recurring implementation patterns in C++ local LLM codebases. We analyze ten GitHub open-source repositories and classify functions into seven study-specific categories using a multi-label Random Forest model. Studying these patterns can provide useful insights for developers aiming to design LLM applications.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [218] [Efficient Passive Acoustic Monitoring of Killer Whales Using a Two-Stage Detection and Ecotype Classification Cascade](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01792)
*Daniela Ruiz,Manuel Castellote,Zhongqi Miao,Carl Chalmers,Bruno Demuro,Rahul Dodhia,Pablo Arbelaez,Juan M. Lavista*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes a two-stage ResNet-based cascade system for passive acoustic monitoring of killer whales, achieving real-time detection and classification of five eastern North Pacific ecotypes with improved macro-F1 scores and domain adaptation capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Passive acoustic monitoring is crucial for conserving the endangered Southern Resident killer whale population, but existing models struggle with real-time operation under severe class imbalance and deployment shift across different acoustic environments.

Method: A lightweight ResNet-based two-stage cascade is proposed: Stage 1 detects killer whale vocalizations, while Stage 2 classifies confident detections into five eastern North Pacific ecotypes, abstaining on ambiguous calls. The system is trained on the DCLDE 2027 dataset and adapted to Puget Sound using active learning.

Result: The detector achieves 0.960 macro-F1 and the classifier achieves 0.958, outperforming frozen Perch 2.0 embeddings. The two-stage cascade improves seven-class macro-F1 from 0.919 to 0.933, with the largest gain on the rare OKW ecotype. Active learning adaptation increased killer whale detection F1 from 0.405 to 0.755 on Puget Sound data. Each stage processes a 3s window in ~1.4ms on NVIDIA H100.

Conclusion: The two-stage cascade pipeline enables reliable killer whale detection and classification, successful adaptation to new acoustic domains through active learning, and faster-than-real-time inference for conservation applications.

Abstract: Passive acoustic monitoring of killer whales is particularly important for conservation of the endangered Southern Resident killer whale population, but requires accurate models that can operate in real time under severe class imbalance and deployment shift. We propose a lightweight ResNet-based two-stage cascade that first detects killer whale vocalizations and then classifies confident detections into five eastern North Pacific ecotypes, abstaining on ambiguous calls. We train and evaluate the pipeline on the DCLDE 2027 dataset, where the detector achieves 0.960 macro-F1 and the classifier 0.958, outperforming frozen Perch 2.0 embeddings on the five-ecotype benchmark. By separating detection from ecotype recognition, the end-to-end cascade improves seven-class macro-F1 from 0.919 for a single-stage model to 0.933, with the largest gain on the rare OKW ecotype. To assess transfer beyond the benchmark, we use active learning to adapt the Stage 1 to the acoustic environment of Puget Sound, WA, increasing killer whale detection F1 from 0.405 to 0.755 on manually verified detection windows. Finally, each stage processes a 3 s window in approximately 1.4 ms on an NVIDIA H100, enabling faster than real time inference. These results demonstrate that the proposed two-stage cascade pipeline enables reliable killer whale detection and classification, adaptation to new acoustic domains, and real-time monitoring for conservation applications.

</details>


### [219] [Auditory Illusion Benchmark for Large Audio Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02277)
*Hayoon Kim,Eunice Hong,Kyogu Lee*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: AIB is the first benchmark for evaluating Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) using auditory illusions, covering 10 representative illusions across music, sound, and speech. The study compares model responses with human listening studies to assess whether LALMs replicate human perceptual biases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Perceptual illusions are valuable tools for understanding human cognition, but auditory illusions remain underexplored in AI benchmarking compared to visual illusions. This gap limits our ability to test whether LALMs replicate human perceptual tendencies.

Method: The researchers created a benchmark pairing model evaluation with controlled human listening studies, enabling direct comparison of responses across ten representative auditory illusions annotated for knowledge-based priors.

Result: Systematic differences were found: most LALMs remain signal-faithful on low-level acoustic illusions, but several exhibit more human-like responses when linguistic or musical priors are involved. No model matches the full human perceptual profile.

Conclusion: LALMs currently have limitations as cognitive models. Auditory illusions provide a rigorous testbed for probing neural black-box models and advancing understanding of auditory cognition. The benchmark AIB is publicly available.

Abstract: Perceptual illusions have long served as crucial probes into human cognition, revealing biases and limitations of perception. In the auditory domain, such illusions provide a unique lens for testing whether Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) replicate human perceptual tendencies. Despite their importance, most benchmarks focus on visual illusions or general audio tasks, leaving auditory illusions underexplored. To this end, we present AIB, the first auditory illusion benchmark for LALMs, covering ten representative illusions across music, sound, and speech, each annotated for the presence of knowledge-based priors. Our methodology pairs model evaluation with controlled human listening studies, enabling direct comparison of responses. Results show systematic differences: while most LALMs remain signal-faithful on low-level acoustic illusions, several exhibit more human-like responses when linguistic or musical priors are involved, although no model matches the human perceptual profile. These findings highlight the current limitations of LALMs as cognitive models. By establishing auditory illusions as a rigorous testbed, our work offers a new perspective for probing neural black-box models and advancing understanding of auditory cognition. AIB is publicly available at https://github.com/gillosae/aib.

</details>


### [220] [SonicCaps: Large-Scale Diverse and Fine-Grained Captioning for Improved Audio-Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02343)
*Zineb Lahrichi,Marc Ferras,Gaël Richard,Geoffroy Peeters*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: SonicCaps is a large-scale audio captioning dataset (~15M captions for ~700k audio clips) generated using a multi-modal LLM (Qwen3-Omni) with ~24 captions per audio to address limitations of existing datasets: low semantic diversity, generic descriptions, and one-to-one mappings. Human evaluation confirms higher quality, and training CLAP models on this data improves audio retrieval and zero-shot classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current audio captioning datasets suffer from three key limitations: (1) low semantic diversity, (2) generic descriptions lacking acoustic details, and (3) one-to-one audio-caption mappings that fail to capture the inherent ambiguity of auditory perception. These limitations hinder advances in audio-language modeling.

Method: 1) Generate captions using Qwen3-Omni (multi-modal LLM) conditioned on both audio and text; 2) Use structured prompt engineering and few-shot generation to create ~24 captions per audio clip; 3) Captions span main descriptions, rephrased variants (varying verbosity and style), and semantic tags; 4) Train CLAP models on SonicCaps using a multi-caption sampling strategy.

Result: Human evaluation shows SonicCaps is rated significantly higher than existing captioning datasets. Fine-grained analysis indicates captions are more descriptive and precise, which strongly correlates with quality judgments. Training CLAP models on SonicCaps with multi-caption sampling consistently improves audio retrieval and zero-shot classification, with stronger generalization across public and commercial benchmarks.

Conclusion: SonicCaps addresses key limitations of existing audio captioning datasets through diverse, multi-caption generation using multi-modal LLM. The dataset enables improved CLAP model performance on audio retrieval and zero-shot classification tasks, demonstrating the value of semantic diversity and multi-caption approaches in audio-language modeling.

Abstract: Recent advances in audio-language modeling have been driven by large-scale audio captioning datasets. However, existing datasets remain limited by low semantic diversity, generic descriptions lacking acoustic details, and one-to-one audio-caption mappings that poorly reflect the inherent ambiguity of auditory perception. We introduce SonicCaps, a large-scale audio captioning dataset comprising ~15M captions paired with ~700k audio clips, generated using a multi-modal large language model (Qwen3-Omni) conditioned on both audio and text. To explicitly promote diversity, we generate around 24 captions per audio via structured prompt engineering and few- shot generation, spanning main descriptions, rephrased variants (verbosity, style) and semantic tags. Human evaluation shows that SonicCaps is rated significantly higher than existing captioning datasets, with fine-grained analyses indicating that our captions are perceived as more descriptive and precise, which strongly correlates with quality judgments. Finally, training CLAP models on SonicCaps with a multi-caption sampling strategy consistently improves audio retrieval and zero-shot classification, with stronger generalization across public and commercial benchmarks. We release both SonicCaps and two specialized CLAP models on hugging face: https://huggingface.co/datasets/Zineb/SonicCaps.

</details>


### [221] [Removing Speech, Keeping Activities: A Privacy Firewall for Acoustic Sensing in Assisted Living](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02376)
*Pavlos Nicolaou,Christos Efstratiou*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A privacy firewall pipeline using U-Net encoder-decoder trained on synthetic data removes speech from ambient audio while preserving environmental sounds for activity recognition. Combined with VGGish+SVM, it achieves 0% VAD-detectable speech while maintaining activity recognition performance (85% precision/recall on ESC-50, 76% on real-world data).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Acoustic sensing is promising for non-intrusive monitoring of older adults, but speech privacy concerns prevent real-world deployment. The paper addresses this critical barrier.

Method: U-Net encoder-decoder trained on synthetic data for speech removal; VGGish transfer learning with SVM classifier for activity recognition. Evaluated on ESC-50 and SINS datasets, and real-world AudioHive app recordings.

Result: Reduced residual speech to 0% VAD-detectable speech under all tested conditions, outperforming Facebook Denoiser (6.55%), SepFormer (36.34%), and ConvTasNet (47.21%). On ESC-50 at 40% speech level: 85% precision/recall after removal vs 81%/75% before removal. Real-world evaluation: 0% VAD-detectable speech with 76% precision/recall.

Conclusion: The pipeline enables privacy-preserving acoustic sensing without sacrificing activity recognition performance, addressing a key obstacle to ambient monitoring adoption in elderly care.

Abstract: Acoustic sensing offers a promising non-intrusive approach for monitoring daily activities of older adults, yet speech privacy concerns remain a critical barrier to real-world deployment. We present a privacy firewall pipeline based on a U-Net encoder-decoder, trained entirely on synthetic data, that removes speech from ambient audio while preserving environmental sounds indicative of daily activities. Activity recognition is performed using VGGish transfer learning with an SVM classifier. Evaluated on the ESC-50 and SINS datasets across multiple speech content levels, the proposed model reduced residual speech to 0% VAD-detectable speech (Silero Voice Activity Detection) under all tested conditions, outperforming Facebook Denoiser (6.55% residual), SepFormer (36.34%) and ConvTasNet (47.21%) on ESC-50 at the 100\% speech level. On ESC-50 at 40% speech level, classification performance recovers to 85% precision and 85% recall after speech removal, compared with 81%/75% before removal and an 84%/83% speech-free baseline. Evaluation on real-world participant home recordings collected with the AudioHive app showed 0% VAD-detectable speech after processing while maintaining 76% precision and recall. The pipeline enables privacy-preserving acoustic sensing without sacrificing activity recognition performance, addressing a key obstacle to the adoption of ambient monitoring in elderly care.

</details>


### [222] [Scalable Direction-Following TTS via Voice Impression-Guided Pseudo Triplet Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02623)
*Kenichi Fujita,Yusuke Ijima*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper addresses "direction-following TTS" - generating modified utterances that reflect given performance directions relative to a reference utterance while preserving speaker identity. The authors propose a pseudo-triplet construction pipeline using impression-controllable TTS and LLMs to address the lack of training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Voice actors modify their delivery when given performance directions, but there is no training data capturing such relative modifications. This limits the development of TTS systems that can follow direction-based style changes.

Method: The authors propose a scalable pseudo-triplet construction pipeline that generates (reference utterance, direction text, modified utterance) triplets. It uses an impression-controllable TTS model to generate controlled style variations and an LLM to produce natural language directions from estimated impression differences.

Result: Pseudo-triplets alone enable stable speaker-preserving modification. Combining pseudo and recorded data further improves direction alignment while maintaining speaker similarity.

Conclusion: The proposed pseudo-triplet construction pipeline effectively addresses the data scarcity problem in direction-following TTS, enabling models to learn direction-based modifications without requiring large amounts of recorded human performance data.

Abstract: Voice actors often re-read the same script while modifying their delivery in response to performance directions. We study this setting as direction-following TTS, where a system generates a new utterance that reflects a given direction relative to a reference utterance while preserving speaker identity and linguistic content. A key challenge is the lack of training data capturing such relative modifications. To address this, we propose a scalable pseudo-triplet construction pipeline that generates~(reference utterance, direction text, modified utterance) triplets. It generates controlled style variations using an impression-controllable TTS model and uses an LLM to produce natural language directions from estimated impression differences. Experimental results demonstrate that pseudo-triplets alone enable stable speaker-preserving modification, and that combining pseudo and recorded data further improves direction alignment while maintaining speaker similarity. Audio examples are available on our demo page https://ntt-hilab-gensp.github.io/IS2026pseudo/

</details>


### [223] [Understanding Automatic Mixing: A Subtask-Oriented Analysis of Two-Stage Mixing System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02835)
*Jinjie Shi,Wei Hua,Kunzhu Xie,Make Li,Yuchen Liu,Joshua Reiss*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether two-stage automatic mixing systems (separating intra-group processing from inter-group mixing) outperform single-stage systems, and analyzes the factors contributing to these improvements through three controlled listening experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-stage systems are commonly used in automatic mixing to handle complexity from large track counts, diverse instrumentation, and strong inter-track dependencies. However, it was unclear whether the performance gains came from stronger component models or from explicit task decomposition itself.

Method: Three controlled listening experiments were conducted on three dense pop and rock excerpts to investigate: (1) transfer capability of full-mix models to intra-group mixing, (2) downstream model compensation for grouping and loudness errors, and (3) whether two-stage decomposition improves full-mix quality compared to single-stage baselines.

Result: Transfer learning performance varied between evaluated models. Inappropriate grouping caused clear downstream degradation, while altered loudness relationships had weaker and model-dependent effects. Both two-stage variants significantly outperformed their corresponding single-stage baselines.

Conclusion: The findings support explicit separation of local balance and global mix coordination as a useful design principle for automatic mixing systems. The gains from two-stage decomposition appear to come primarily from the task decomposition itself rather than just stronger component models.

Abstract: Automatic mixing transforms multitrack recordings into perceptually coherent, balanced, and aesthetically consistent mixes. In real-world production, this task is challenging due to large track counts, diverse instrumentation, and strong inter-track dependencies. Two-stage systems address this complexity by separating intra-group processing from inter-group mixing, yet it remains unclear whether their gains arise from stronger component models or from explicit task decomposition. We present a subtask-oriented analysis of automatic mixing through three controlled listening experiments. We investigate whether full-mix models transfer to intra-group mixing, whether downstream models compensate for grouping and loudness errors, and whether two-stage decomposition improves full-mix quality. Across three dense pop and rock excerpts, transfer differs between the evaluated models; inappropriate grouping causes clear downstream degradation, while altered loudness relationships have weaker and model-dependent effects. Both two-stage variants significantly outperform their corresponding single-stage baselines. These findings support explicit separation of local balance and global mix coordination as a useful design principle for automatic mixing. Code and audio examples are available online.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [224] [EvalDetectBench: A Benchmark for Measuring Evaluation Awareness in Frontier Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01611)
*Xinning Li,Kemunto Ochwang'i,Aryasomayajula Ram Bharadwaj,Alexandra Souly,Robert Kirk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvalDetectBench is an open benchmark and pipeline for measuring "evaluation awareness" in frontier LLMs—whether models can recognize when they are being evaluated versus deployed. The paper addresses systematic biases in prior evaluation awareness research, including model identity effects and prompt selection biases, and provides corrected methodologies via per-model probe calibration and generator-harmonisation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier large language models may recognize when they are being evaluated and exhibit different behavior than in deployment settings. This undermines the validity of AI safety evaluations, which are a crucial component of current AI safety frameworks. There was a need for a standardized, extensible benchmark to measure evaluation awareness reliably.

Method: The authors introduced EvalDetectBench, an Inspect-compatible evaluation pipeline with a curated transcript suite covering frontier system-card evaluations and diverse deployment sources. They identified two methodological biases in prior work: (1) the identity of the model generating deployment transcripts accounts for significant measurement variance, and (2) elicitation prompts optimized for one model perform poorly on others. These were corrected via per-model probe calibration and a stratified generator-harmonisation procedure.

Result: The study found that the identity of the model generating deployment transcripts accounts for 11.25% of measurement variance and can reorder model rankings. Additionally, elicitation prompts selected for high performance on one model can perform near chance on other models. The benchmark serves two purposes: measuring how reliably frontier LLMs recognize evaluation contexts, and assessing how detectable individual benchmarks are as evaluations.

Conclusion: EvalDetectBench provides a rigorous, extensible tool for measuring evaluation awareness in frontier LLMs while correcting for systematic biases that previously undermined measurement validity. This contributes to more reliable AI safety evaluations by accounting for the possibility that models may alter their behavior when aware of being evaluated.

Abstract: Frontier large language models can often recognize when they are being evaluated, a capability known as evaluation awareness. If models behave differently in evaluations than in deployment, this undermines the validity of evaluation results, which are a crucial component of current AI safety frameworks. We introduce EvalDetectBench, an open pipeline and benchmark for measuring evaluation awareness that works with any Inspect-compatible evaluation, allowing practitioners to test against current and future benchmarks. EvalDetectBench ships with a newly curated transcript suite covering current frontier system-card evaluations and diverse deployment sources. The benchmark serves two purposes: measuring how reliably frontier LLMs recognize that they are being evaluated, and assessing how detectable individual benchmarks are as evaluations. We identify two methodological choices in the existing literature that introduce systematic bias: the identity of the model that generated the deployment transcripts accounts for 11.25% of measurement variance and can reorder model rankings; and elicitation prompts selected for high performance on one model can perform near chance on others. EvalDetectBench corrects for both via per-model probe calibration and a stratified generator-harmonisation procedure.

</details>


### [225] [Meta-ethics and AI: exploring the novel meta-ethical questions in the era of AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01685)
*Shang Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops a conditional framework for exploring meta-ethical questions that would arise if AI systems develop their own moral reasoning, intentionality, and reflection capabilities. It identifies four domains of inquiry and examines how existing meta-ethical theories might apply to AI cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The development of AI creates pressures on traditional human-centered meta-ethics, requiring new frameworks to address potential AI moral agency and ethics distinct from human-imposed ethical principles.

Method: The paper employs a conditional and methodological approach: (1) It defines conditions for AI systems to have "integrated capacities for moral reasoning, intentionality, and reflection"; (2) It establishes four domains of meta-ethical inquiry based on subject (human vs. AI) and perspective (human vs. AI); (3) It applies existing meta-ethical theories (cognitivism, non-cognitivism, error theory, success theory, relativism, objective realism) to these four domains to test their applicability.

Result: The paper finds that many mainstream meta-ethical theories, originally formulated for human ethics, may not transfer straightforwardly to AI cases and require substantial revision. The four domains are: (1) human ethics from human perspective; (2) AI's own ethics from human perspective; (3) human ethics from AI perspective; (4) AI's own ethics from AI perspective.

Conclusion: The emergence of AI's own ethics would place significant pressure on current meta-ethical frameworks, potentially requiring substantial refinement, reconstruction, or reconceptualization of existing theories to accommodate AI moral agents.

Abstract: With the development of artificial intelligence (AI), the landscape of meta-ethics, which has largely centred on human ethics, faces pressures that may significantly reconfigure it. In particular, if future AI systems were to exhibit sufficiently integrated capacities for moral reasoning, moral intentionality, and moral reflection, novel meta-ethical questions would arise concerning what I call "AI's own ethics", as distinct from ethical principles merely imposed on AI by human designers. This paper offers a conditional and methodological framework for identifying the questions that would emerge if such AI systems were to arise. On that basis, the paper distinguishes four domains of meta-ethical inquiry in the era of AI: questions about the nature of human ethics from the human perspective; questions about the nature of AI's own ethics from the human perspective; questions about the nature of human ethics from the AI perspective; and questions about the nature of AI's own ethics from the AI perspective. The paper then considers how some existing mainstream meta-ethical theories (such as cognitivism and non-cognitivism, error theory and success theory, relativism, and objective realism) might illuminate these domains, while arguing that many familiar human-centred formulations of those theories may not transfer straightforwardly to AI cases without substantial revision. The overall conclusion is that the emergence of AI's own ethics would place significant pressure on current frameworks and may require substantial refinement, reconstruction, or reconceptualisation.

</details>


### [226] [When Can a Machine Trust a Statute? A Survival Certificate for Machine-Extracted Legal Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01741)
*Surya Saka*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a method for certifying formal implications in machine-extracted statutory text despite significant parser disagreement (0.43 false-negative rate). Using Monte Carlo trials with Wilson confidence bounds, the approach validates implications across 29,365 Missouri sections and 502 Indian central-Act sections, but shows the certified system is fragile under global error models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses the problem of machine disagreement in statutory text parsing, where two independently written extractors diverge on numeric-threshold presence at a false-negative rate of 0.43. The study investigates what formal logic can survive such noise in machine-extracted legal contexts.

Method: The study measures per-attribute inter-extractor disagreement between two independently written parsers on Missouri statutes, then replays this against the Duquenne-Guigues implication basis across 1,000 Monte Carlo trials. Implications are certified using a one-sided Wilson 95% lower bound on survival reaching 0.95. Each certified implication includes premise spans and minimal counterexamples. A 2x2 factorial design is used to attribute performance differences to calibration-rate transfer versus selection effects.

Result: The preregistered held-out gate passes: 10 statute families across 7 Titles exact, and 16 across 11 with 5% tolerance. However, under one globally deployed error model, 93.2% of held-out chapters fall below the informativeness floor. The 2x2 factorial analysis attributes this failure to calibration-rate transfer rather than selection effects.

Conclusion: The certificate for machine-extracted statutory implications is functional but fragile, requiring per-chapter calibration or error tolerance for deployment. The method successfully passes held-out validation tests but suffers significant informativeness loss under global error models, with 93.2% of chapters falling below the threshold.

Abstract: Statutes are increasingly parsed by machines before people read them, and the parsers disagree: on Missouri's statutes, two independently written extractors diverge on numeric-threshold presence at a false-negative rate of 0.43. We ask what formal logic survives such noise. We build a passive survival certificate for the Duquenne-Guigues implication basis of machine-extracted statutory contexts: per-attribute inter-extractor disagreement is measured, replayed against the basis in 1,000 Monte Carlo trials, and an implication is certified only when a one-sided Wilson 95% lower bound on survival reaches 0.95; every certified implication carries premise spans and a minimal counterexample. On 29,365 Missouri sections and 502 Indian central-Act sections, the preregistered held-out gate passes (10 statute families across 7 Titles exact; 16 across 11 with 5% tolerance), yet under one globally deployed error model 93.2% of held-out chapters fall below the informativeness floor, and a 2x2 factorial assigns that to calibration-rate transfer, not selection. The certificate is usable but fragile: deploy it per-chapter-calibrated or error-tolerant. Code, data products, and the audit trail, including one retracted claim, are released.

</details>


### [227] [When Does Information Sharing Improve Decentralized Discovery? Aggregation, Independent Rescue, and Equilibrium Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01814)
*Yohei Nakajima*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the effects of information sharing in finite discovery models, separating the improvement from pooled estimates versus the elimination of independent actions. It examines centralized action-budget profiles, registered incremental-sharing protocols, and equilibrium selection in a two-agent Bayesian game with mixed signal sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand when information sharing improves discovery outcomes versus when it merely eliminates redundant independent efforts, and to determine whether sharing benefits are universal or depend on equilibrium selection.

Method: Exact finite discovery models, centralized action-budget profile analysis, registered incremental-sharing protocol examination, and two-agent Bayesian game analysis with hidden mixture of common and independent signal sources.

Result: A sharing step improves discovery exactly when pooled residual error contracts faster than an independent rescue attempt. At signal accuracy 3/5, the registered selected equilibrium yields a strict positive sharing interval, while alternative equilibria show selection-dependent rather than universal results.

Conclusion: Information sharing benefits are not universal but depend on equilibrium selection. The models demonstrate compression, aggregation, neutral curves, and bounded zero mixed class in exact bounded registries, with equal one-person accuracy able to coexist with different portfolio values.

Abstract: Information sharing can improve a pooled estimate while eliminating independent rescue actions. This paper separates those effects in exact finite discovery models. A centralized action-budget profile shows that equal one-person accuracy can coexist with different portfolio values. Under a registered incremental-sharing protocol, a sharing step improves discovery exactly when pooled residual error contracts faster than an independent rescue attempt. Exact bounded registries exhibit compression, aggregation, neutral curves, and a bounded zero mixed class. In a two-agent Bayesian game with a hidden mixture of common and independent signal sources, the registered selected equilibrium yields a strict positive sharing interval at signal accuracy 3/5, while alternative equilibria show that the result is selection-dependent rather than universal. The models are synthetic and finite; no human or organizational data are used.

</details>


### [228] [Induction and Inquiry via Probabilistic Reasoning over Language and Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01815)
*Wasu Top Piriyakulkij,Sam Acquaviva,Cassidy Langenfeld,Joshua Tenenbaum,Kevin Ellis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a computational model of human abstract knowledge acquisition that combines symbolic programs (natural language + source code) with LLM-guided Bayesian learning. The model satisfies three key desiderata: data/compute efficiency, uncertainty representation, and flexibility. It successfully reproduces human inductive learning behaviors (anchoring, garden-pathing) that pure LLMs or classic Bayesian models cannot achieve.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The challenge of understanding how humans grow and maintain abstract knowledge from sparse, noisy experiential data has remained unsolved in cognitive science. Existing computational approaches fail to satisfy three essential requirements: data/compute efficiency, uncertainty representation for intelligent inquiry, and flexibility to represent the endless range of concepts humans can learn.

Method: The model encodes symbolic knowledge as "mental programs" combining natural language with source code, and sequentially infers these mental programs using LLM-guided Bayesian learning algorithms. This hybrid approach uses bottom-up neural mechanisms (LLM) to make inference tractable while maintaining symbolic representation.

Result: The model successfully reproduces quantitative signatures of human inductive learning and active inquiry, including anchoring, garden-pathing, and other effects. In contrast, pure LLMs and classic Bayesian models either fail at the task, don't reproduce human behavior, or require excessive computational cost.

Conclusion: Humans may acquire knowledge by mentally representing hypotheses spanning language-like and program-like representations, then revising them through approximate Bayesian updates, while neural mechanisms (LLMs) make this inference both tractable and learnable.

Abstract: How humans grow and maintain abstract knowledge from the sparse, streaming noisy data of experience is a longstanding challenge in cognitive science. Any computational account must satisfy at least three desiderata: It must be (1) data-efficient and compute-efficient, (2) capture gradations of uncertainty to support intelligent inquiry and information gathering, and (3) be flexible enough to mentally represent the endless range of concepts people can learn and think about. Here we introduce a computational model that captures these three properties, by encoding symbolic knowledge as mental programs that combine natural language with source code, and sequentially inferring mental programs using LLM-guided Bayesian learning algorithms. Across a range of behavioral studies this model successfully reproduces quantitative signatures of human inductive learning and active inquiry, such as anchoring, garden-pathing, and other effects. In contrast, pure LLMs and classic Bayesian models either fail at the underlying task, or do not reproduce human behavior, or succeed only at exorbitant computational cost. These results suggest that one way humans continually grow their knowledge is by mentally representing many hypotheses spanning language-like and program-like representations, then revising those hypotheses to approximate Bayesian updates, while a bottom-up neural mechanism (an LLM) makes inference both tractable and learnable.

</details>


### [229] [Architecting Conversational Data Systems for Stateless LLM APIs: The Hydration Proxy Pattern](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01834)
*Joseph Axisa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the architectural challenge of managing conversational state in LLM-powered enterprise platforms. The authors propose the Hydration Proxy Pattern to decouple session persistence from reasoning engines, and introduce the Context Stabilization Mandate to balance sovereign state management with KV caching performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The transition to conversational reasoning interfaces in enterprise platforms exposes a fundamental tension: LLM APIs are stateless (enabling horizontal scalability), but conversational applications require persistent state and semantic memory. This forces client applications to bear the entire burden of managing conversational context, creating an architectural gap that undermines platform sovereignty and complicates multi-stage semantic grounding.

Method: The paper introduces two architectural solutions: (1) Hydration Proxy Pattern - a middleware architecture that separates session persistence from the LLM reasoning engine, allowing the platform to maintain control over conversational data while delegating reasoning to external APIs; (2) Context Stabilization Mandate - a framework to resolve the inherent tradeoff between maintaining sovereign state management and leveraging KV caching for performance optimization.

Result: The framework enables enterprise platforms to retain sovereignty over their conversational data while still benefiting from stateless LLM API scalability. It provides a mechanism for secure, multi-stage semantic grounding without compromising on caching performance.

Conclusion: The Hydration Proxy Pattern represents a viable architectural solution for enterprise AI platforms navigating the tension between stateless LLM APIs and stateful conversational requirements. The Context Stabilization Mandate offers a principled approach to balancing data sovereignty with inference performance, though practical implementation challenges remain.

Abstract: As enterprise platforms transition to conversational reasoning interfaces, the stateless nature of LLM APIs creates an architectural gap. While statelessness enables horizontal scalability for AI providers, it forces client applications to manage the entire burden of conversational state and semantic memory. The work identifies the Hydration Proxy Pattern, an architecture that decouples session persistence from the reasoning engine. The framework ensures platform sovereignty over conversational data while enabling secure, multi-stage semantic grounding. We further propose the Context Stabilization Mandate to resolve the tradeoff between sovereign state management and KV caching.

</details>


### [230] [SSAKG 2.0: An Open-Source Package for Structural Associative Sequence Memory and Context-Based Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01849)
*Przemysław Stokłosa,Janusz A. Starzyk,Paweł Raif*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SSAKG 2.0 is an open-source Python/C hybrid software package for constructing and using Structural Sequential Associative Knowledge Graphs, where graph vertices represent objects and ordered sequences form structural patterns. It enables sequence reconstruction from partial, unordered context using memory-efficient bit-level algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to create an efficient associative memory system that can store and reconstruct complete sequences from incomplete information. The authors aim to address the computational and memory overhead issues associated with large sparse graphs by implementing performance-critical operations in C while maintaining a flexible Python interface.

Method: The method involves representing objects as graph vertices and ordered sequences as structural patterns of graph connections. Version 2.0 introduces new algorithms that exploit individual bits of computer memory for efficient graph connection searching. The hybrid implementation uses Python for high-level programming and C for performance-critical graph operations.

Result: The algorithms were evaluated using three types of data: randomly generated numerical sequences, sentences from the NLTK corpus, and mRNA sequences. The experiments demonstrated the package's ability to store and reconstruct sequences from partial contexts. The results provide a basis for evaluating the effects of graph density, sequence length, and memory size on retrieval performance.

Conclusion: SSAKG 2.0 is distributed under Apache 2.0 license and is publicly available through GitHub and PyPI with documentation and reproducible examples. The package successfully enables associative memory functionality through sparse graph structures, with the hybrid Python/C implementation providing a balance between flexibility and performance.

Abstract: This article presents SSAKG 2.0, an open-source software package for constructing and operating Structural Sequential Associative Knowledge Graphs (SSAKGs). An SSAKG represents objects as graph vertices and ordered sequences as structural patterns of graph connections. The resulting sparse graph is used as an associative memory in which complete sequences can be reconstructed from a partial, unordered context.
  Version 2.0 introduces new algorithms that exploit individual bits of computer memory to efficiently search graph connections. The package is implemented in Python, while performance-critical graph operations are implemented in C and exposed through a Python interface. This hybrid implementation provides a flexible high-level programming environment while reducing the memory and computational overhead associated with large sparse graphs.
