  The algorithms were evaluated using randomly generated numerical sequences, sequences derived from sentences in the NLTK corpus, and mRNA sequences. The experiments demonstrate the ability of the package to store and reconstruct sequences from partial contexts and provide a basis for evaluating the effects of graph density, sequence length, and memory size on retrieval performance.
  SSAKG 2.0 is distributed under the Apache 2.0 open-source license. The package includes documentation and reproducible examples and is publicly available through GitHub and the Python Package Index (PyPI).

</details>


### [231] [The Memory Trust Gap: Capability-Dependent Failures in Persistent-Memory Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01852)
*Jundong Hu,Shekar Ramachandran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies how persistent memory in personalized AI agents can cause harm when stale stored facts override current authoritative evidence. Using a frozen benchmark with Benefit (unsolvable without stored fact) and Safety (authoritative tool available) suites on Qwen3 models (0.6-8B), researchers found that the "Memory Trust Gap" reflects over-trust rather than confusion. Larger models are more vulnerable when stale information is made to appear current. The trigger for over-trust depends on both the feature type and model scale. Mitigation effectiveness is capability-dependent: metadata exposure helps capable models, while pre-resolving conflicts is needed for smaller models. This pattern generalizes to Llama-Instruct models and external datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persistent memory in personalized agents can cause harm when stale stored facts override current authoritative evidence without warning. The researchers wanted to understand when this harm begins as model capability changes, and identify what triggers over-trust in different model sizes.

Method: Evaluated a frozen, closed-set, action-scored benchmark with 2 suites representing different meanings of "no memory": (1) Benefit suite - unsolvable without the stored fact, and (2) Safety suite - where an authoritative tool always holds the correct value. Tested on Qwen3 model-size series (0.6/1.7/4/8B). Conducted a 2×2×2×2 factorial experiment to test which features trigger over-trust. Validated findings on Llama-Instruct model-size series and 2 external datasets (RGB, MisBench).

Result: In the Benefit suite, models answer with stale values 0.92-1.00 of the time at every scale. In the Safety suite, harm below the no-memory baseline is capability-gated - larger models collapse most when stale notes appear current. Which feature triggers over-trust depends on both feature type and model scale. Removing labels amplifies over-trust at every size. A recency feature (stale dated newer) fools larger models harder. Source authority is weak and scale-flat, while position changes from positive to negative across the Qwen3 series. Mitigations: exposing metadata helps capable models; pre-resolving conflicts helps smaller models. In framing controls, smaller models trust stale documents more than stale memories, but this difference vanishes at 8B.

Conclusion: The Memory Trust Gap reflects over-trust rather than confusion, and this vulnerability is capability-dependent. Larger models are more susceptible to certain triggers (like recency features), while smaller models have different failure modes. Mitigation strategies must be tailored to model capability - metadata exposure works for larger models, while conflict pre-resolution is needed for smaller ones. The pattern generalizes across model families and datasets.

Abstract: Persistent memory supports personalized agents, but a stale stored fact can override current authoritative evidence without warning. We study when this harm begins as model capability changes. We evaluate a frozen, closed-set, action-scored benchmark with 2 suites that represent 2 different meanings of "no memory" (a Benefit suite, unsolvable without the stored fact, and a Safety suite, in which an authoritative tool always holds the correct value), on a same-family model-size series (Qwen3 0.6/1.7/4/8B). The Memory Trust Gap reflects over-trust rather than confusion. In the Benefit suite, models answer with the stale value 0.92-1.00 of the time at every scale. In the Safety suite, harm below the no-memory baseline under the trap conditions ($Δ_{\mathrm{mem}}$) is capability-gated, with the larger models collapsing most once a stale note is made to look current. In a $2\times2\times2\times2$ factorial, which feature triggers over-trust depends on both the feature and model scale. Removing a label amplifies over-trust at every size, and a recency feature (stale dated newer) fools the larger models harder. Source authority is weak and scale-flat, and position changes from positive to negative across the Qwen3 model-size series. We confirm these scale interactions with direct cross-size contrast tests rather than overlapping per-model intervals. Mitigation is likewise capability-dependent: exposing metadata improves accuracy for the capable models, but only pre-resolving the conflict restores accuracy for the 2 smaller checkpoints. The same pattern appears on the capable models in an independent Llama-Instruct model-size series and on 2 external datasets (RGB, MisBench). A framing control finds no consistent advantage for the memory label: at the 3 smaller scales, models trust a stale document more than a stale memory; at 8B, the difference is not significant.

</details>


### [232] [Belief-Calibrated Optimization: An Explicit World Model for Agentic Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01861)
*Yuhan Chen,Zhihua Tian,Mahavir Dabas,Charith Peris,Rahul Gupta,Ming Jin,Feiyang Kang,Siyuan Zhang,Nan Wang,Ruoxi Jia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BCO (Belief-Calibrated Optimization) introduces a persistent in-context "world model" document that explicitly records beliefs about how the environment responds to code edits, improving LLM agent scaffold performance across five benchmarks spanning memory QA, tool-use QA, code-as-action app agents, and terminal agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent scaffolds use implicit beliefs about environment responses that remain latent in the model's reasoning or parameters. This information is not persisted between calls, so later iterations cannot leverage accumulated insights about what edits work and why.

Method: BCO writes down beliefs as a persistent in-context document that gets continually revised as new candidates are evaluated. This document serves as a "world model" - an explicit account of how the environment responds to different edits. The scaffold is then enhanced with this document in the optimization loop.

Result: BCO achieves higher train passrate than a matched control lacking only the world model on five benchmarks. The advantage persists on held-out splits not used for candidate selection. After swapping the frozen model while keeping the scaffold, BCO-selected scaffolds lead on most tasks except where context-window overruns occur. Offline ablation shows the document carries reusable information - a fresh predictor given the accumulated document forecasts environment responses more accurately than predictors given no document or falsified content.

Conclusion: The persistent world model document carries actionable information in its content, not just its form. This enables the scaffold to accumulate and reuse knowledge about environment behavior across optimization rounds, even across different underlying models.

Abstract: The performance of an LLM agent depends on the scaffold around a frozen model. A common way to improve that scaffold is to use a coding agent as an optimizer: it reads current scores and traces and iteratively edits the source, producing a new candidate each round. Each edit is chosen according to a belief about how the environment will respond: what went wrong, and which change should help. That belief is typically implicit. It lives in the coding agent's reasoning on the current call, or remains latent in its parameters, rather than as something written down. Later calls therefore see scores and traces, but they do not use that belief. We introduce Belief-Calibrated Optimization (BCO), a method that writes that belief down as a persistent in-context document and continually revises that document as new candidates are evaluated. The resulting document is a world model: the current account of how the environment responds to edits. Added to an otherwise standard loop, BCO reaches a higher train passrate than a matched control that lacks only the world model, on five benchmarks spanning memory QA, tool-use QA, code-as-action app agents, and terminal agents. The gap remains on every held-out split, which is not used to select the candidate. After a target-model swap, in which the frozen model is replaced and the scaffold is not, the selected BCO scaffold leads on the tasks we test, except where context-window overruns leave it unfinished. An offline ablation then asks whether that gap comes from what the world model says. A fresh predictor given the accumulated document forecasts how the environment will respond more accurately than predictors given either no document or a same-form copy whose content has been falsified. The comparison indicates that the document carries reusable information in its content, not only in its form.

</details>


### [233] [Epistemic Sybil Resistance: Multiplying AI Agents Without Multiplying Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01873)
*Marc Bara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies and formalizes the "epistemic Sybil problem" in multi-agent AI systems, where multiple agent reports may not be genuinely independent due to shared evidential ancestry. The authors demonstrate that no report-only aggregator can distinguish replication from independent corroboration, and propose tracking evidential ancestry rather than report multiplicity or similarity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent AI systems improve inference by spawning multiple agents to synthesize reports. However, the authors recognize that apparently independent reports may actually descend from the same evidence (replication), while genuinely independent evidence can produce nearly identical reports (corroboration). This creates a fundamental identification problem that existing aggregators cannot solve.

Method: The authors formalize the epistemic Sybil problem using information theory (defining when a report is an epistemic Sybil extension), develop a Gaussian shared-root model for theoretical analysis, and conduct over 20,000 controlled LLM-agent report and extraction calls on synthetic evidentiary documents. They perform controlled manipulations isolating representation similarity from evidential ancestry.

Result: Empirical results show: (1) Holding one evidence root fixed while report multiplicity rises from 1 to 32 collapses naive posterior coverage from 0.940 to 0.263; (2) Holding report count fixed while evidence-root multiplicity rises from 1 to 16 closes the gap, with aggregators becoming statistically indistinguishable at k = 16; (3) Agent replicate extraction errors are correlated (gamma_cal = 0.719); (4) A correlated-extraction aggregator restores calibration; (5) Representation similarity affects inferred cluster count 35× more than true ancestry changes.

Conclusion: Collective inference should track evidential ancestry and dependence, not agent or report multiplicity or similarity. Standard report-only deduplication mechanisms are deceived by representation similarity while missing true evidential ancestry. Correlated extraction errors from shared base models among independent agents further degrade inference quality, but can be addressed with appropriately designed aggregators.

Abstract: Multi-agent AI systems improve inference by spawning agents and synthesizing reports. But another agent is not another observation: apparently independent reports may descend from the same evidence, and genuinely independent evidence can produce nearly identical reports. We formalize this as an epistemic Sybil problem. A report Z is an epistemic Sybil extension relative to reports R when I(Theta; Z | R) = 0. No report-only aggregator can generally distinguish replication from independent corroboration: identical reports can warrant different posteriors under unobserved ancestry. A Gaussian shared-root model shows common ancestry does not imply complete redundancy. Repeated extraction adds information toward a source-level ceiling, and correlated extraction errors, which a shared base model can induce among independent agents, lower that ceiling further. We test these predictions with more than 20,000 controlled LLM-agent report and extraction calls on synthetic evidentiary documents. Holding one evidence root fixed while report multiplicity rises from 1 to 32 collapses naive posterior coverage from 0.940 to 0.263. Holding report count fixed while evidence-root multiplicity rises from 1 to 16 closes the gap, and the aggregators are statistically indistinguishable at k = 16. The agent's replicate extraction errors are correlated (gamma_cal = 0.719, estimated out of sample), and a correlated-extraction aggregator restores calibration accordingly. A controlled manipulation isolates representation similarity from evidential ancestry. It changes a report-space deduplication mechanism's mean inferred cluster count by 1.425 (95% CI [1.363, 1.485]), whereas a fourfold change in true ancestry changes it by only 0.040 ([-0.045, 0.120]). Collective inference should therefore track evidential ancestry and dependence, not agent or report multiplicity or similarity.

</details>


### [234] [The Ceiling Is in the Channel: Auditing Learner Gaps and Measurement Frontiers in Clinical Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01909)
*Sayeed Shafayet Chowdhury,Nusrat Jahan,Snehasis Mukhopadhyay,Shiaofen Fang,Vijay R. Ramakrishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a framework for separating two sources of clinical prediction model saturation: learner gap (algorithm failure to extract information) and measurement-channel ceiling (fundamental limit from recorded variables). The authors develop theoretical results, practical diagnostics, and validate on three real datasets plus 104 clinical tasks, providing a systematic approach to determine whether to improve algorithms or measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses a critical issue in clinical prediction: model performance saturation. The authors recognize that saturation can occur due to two distinct reasons - either the learning algorithm fails to extract available information from the data, or the recorded variables themselves impose a fundamental limit on predictive performance. This distinction is crucial for guiding improvements in clinical prediction models, but existing methods cannot separate these two quantities.

Method: The authors develop a theoretical framework that separates the "learner gap" (suboptimal model extraction) from the "measurement-channel ceiling" (fundamental limit imposed by available variables). They introduce several key concepts: total-variation separation for optimal balanced accuracy, architecture invariance, partial-identification under replacement contamination, a cross-fitted ceiling estimator, and exact conditions for multimodal decision improvement. They also propose two finite-sample diagnostics: label-permutation optimism floor and underfit curve. The framework is validated on three real cohorts (UCI readmission, BRFSS diabetes, NHANES HbA1c) and a synthesis of 104 clinical tasks.

Result: Well-tuned gradient boosting nearly reaches the estimated frontier in UCI and BRFSS datasets, while deliberately or practically deficient learners retain large gaps. In NHANES, there is no significant difference between questionnaire and measured marginal frontiers, but a significant joint complementarity gain exists. Modest AUROC gains coexist with substantially larger Bayes decision-flip rates across all cohorts. Several architectures estimate similar frontiers while their achieved balanced accuracy differs sharply. The synthesis of 104 clinical tasks reveals: a broad but non-universal structured-clinical region, diminishing same-channel gains across model families, and higher performance when measurement channels change.

Conclusion: The framework successfully converts performance saturation from an empirical observation into an auditable decision point. When headroom remains (learner gap exists), efforts should focus on improving the learner/algorithm. When no headroom exists (measurement-channel ceiling reached), efforts should focus on improving measurement quality. This provides a systematic approach for guiding resource allocation in clinical prediction model development.

Abstract: Clinical prediction can saturate for two different reasons: a fitted learner may fail to extract available information, or the recorded variables may impose a population frontier. We separate these quantities through the \emph{learner gap} and the \emph{measurement-channel ceiling}. Optimal balanced accuracy is characterized by total-variation separation, yielding architecture invariance, a sharp partial-identification result under replacement contamination, a cross-fitted ceiling estimator, and exact conditions for multimodal decision improvement. We add two finite-sample diagnostics, namely a label-permutation optimism floor and an underfit curve, and validate the audit on three real cohorts: UCI readmission ($n=99{,}343$), BRFSS diabetes ($n=253{,}680$), and NHANES HbA1c ($n=10{,}219$). Well-tuned gradient boosting nearly reaches the estimated frontier in UCI and BRFSS, whereas deliberately or practically deficient learners retain large gaps. NHANES yields a null difference between questionnaire and measured marginal frontiers but a significant joint complementarity gain, refining the simplistic claim that an objective modality must dominate. Across all cohorts, modest AUROC gains coexist with substantially larger Bayes decision-flip rates, and several architectures estimate similar frontiers while their achieved balanced accuracy differs sharply. A PRISMA-guided synthesis of 104 clinical tasks then shows that the same channel-level regularities recur across more than 18 disease categories: a broad but non-universal structured-clinical region, diminishing same-channel gains across model families, and higher performance when measurement channels change. The framework converts saturation from an empirical observation into an auditable decision: improve the learner when headroom remains; improve measurement when it does not.

</details>


### [235] [Looped Transformers under the Jacobian Lens: Does the Global Workspace Survive Recurrence?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01924)
*Wenlong Wang,Fergal Reid*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether workspace functionality (causally potent, verbalizable representations) emerges in recurrent transformers as it does in standard feedforward transformers. Using extended Jacobian lens analysis on three architectures (Ouro-2.6B, Huginn-0125, and Qwen3.6-27B baseline), the study finds that workspace functionality does emerge in iterated architectures but recurrence fundamentally changes how the workspace can be accessed and manipulated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work identified a "global workspace" in standard feedforward transformers with stacked layers. This study asks whether the same workspace functionality emerges when depth is implemented through recurrence rather than distinct layers. This is important because recurrent architectures reuse weights across depth, fundamentally different from layer-stacking approaches.

Method: Extended the Jacobian lens to iterated architectures using a "virtual-unrolling adapter." Applied the full workspace suite including lens fitting, readout, and eleven causal experiment families to three architectures: Ouro-2.6B (48 layers looped 4 times with deep supervision), Huginn-0125 (4-layer core recurred 16 times for latent reasoning), and Qwen3.6-27B (64 untied layers) as baseline.

Result: Workspace forms in the iterated part of each architecture, but recurrence changes access patterns. Ouro reconstructs workspace content in every loop; linear transport cannot carry content across loop boundaries; writes/ablations must span all remaining loops. Huginn carries content forward across all 16 recurrences, while reads/writes/ablations act only within a sliding window of ~2 recurrences. Verbalizability of newly injected content tracks explicit per-iteration supervision, but steerability of existing content does not.

Conclusion: Workspace functionality can emerge in recurrent transformers but operates fundamentally differently than in feedforward architectures. The access patterns and transport mechanisms depend on the specific recurrent architecture and its supervision. This suggests that recurrence introduces distinct computational dynamics that reshape how information flows and can be manipulated within the network.

Abstract: Recent work identifies a mid-depth band of verbalisable, causally potent representations in a standard feedforward transformer --- a functional analogue of a global workspace. Whether the same workspace functionality emerges when depth is implemented through recurrence rather than a stack of distinct layers remains unknown. Looped and depth-recurrent transformers provide a direct test of this question because they reuse the same weights across depth. We extend the Jacobian lens to iterated architectures using a virtual-unrolling adapter. We apply the full workspace suite --- lens fitting, readout, and eleven causal experiment families --- to Ouro-2.6B (48 layers looped 4 times, deeply supervised) and Huginn-0125 (a 4-layer core recurred 16 times, trained for latent reasoning), using Qwen3.6-27B (64 untied layers) as the standard baseline. We find that a workspace forms in the iterated part of each architecture, but that recurrence changes how it can be accessed. Ouro reconstructs workspace content in every loop, and linear transport cannot carry that content across loop boundaries; writes and ablations must therefore span every remaining loop. Huginn carries content forward across all sixteen recurrences, while reads, writes, and ablations act only within a sliding window of roughly two recurrences. Whether newly injected content can be verbalised tracks explicit per-iteration supervision; whether existing content can be steered does not.

</details>


### [236] [Post-Training Ternarization of Qwen3-4B Capability, Effective Bit Budget, Storage Compression, and Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01962)
*Anirudh Malik,M Sparsh Mehra,Poojith Devan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an end-to-end post-training conversion of Qwen (4B parameters) using ultra-low-bit quantization techniques (KOTMS rotation, E2M-ATQ ternarization, and GPTQ-style error compensation). The final model uses 1.641 effective bits per weight with 81.62% of parameters quantized, achieving 54.7% accuracy (down from 64.5%) across ten capability benchmarks, while perplexity increases significantly. Packing reduces model size from 8.29 GiB to 3.96 GiB, but inference is 4.6x slower than FP16 baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ultra-low-bit language models can reduce storage and memory bandwidth, but the actual stored representation, retained capability, and runtime behavior of "1.58-bit" models are not fully understood. This study aims to provide a comprehensive empirical evaluation of post-training quantization on a 4B-parameter instruction-tuned model.

Method: The researchers performed weight-only quantization on Qwen (4B parameters) using KOTMS rotation, E2M-ATQ ternarization, and GPTQ-style error compensation from TWLA. Activations remained at 16-bit precision. They evaluated effective bit accounting, task capability retention, perplexity, calibration sensitivity, checkpoint composition, and deployment behavior. A packing step was also applied to reduce model size.

Result: The quantization achieved 1.641 effective bits per weight for quantized linear weights with 81.62% of parameters targeted. Task accuracy dropped from 64.5% to 54.7% across ten benchmarks, with uneven degradation: BoolQ retained 84.6% of teacher performance while ARC-Challenge retained only 43.8%. Perplexity increased from 13.639 to 18.748 on WikiText-2, 24.700 to 31.992 on PTB, and 19.831 to 28.966 on C4. Packing reduced model size from 8.29 GiB to 3.96 GiB with minimal perplexity change. However, Triton GEMV microbenchmark showed 4.6x slower inference than FP16 cuBLAS.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that while ultra-low-bit quantization can significantly reduce model size (from 8.29 GiB to 3.96 GiB), it comes with substantial capability degradation (10% accuracy drop) and slower inference runtime (4.6x slower). The researchers do not claim that compression alone yields faster inference, and the packed artifact has not been benchmarked end-to-end for task accuracy or generation throughput.

Abstract: Ultra-low-bit language models can reduce storage and memory bandwidth, but a nominal "1.58-bit" label does not fully describe the stored representation, retained capability, or runtime behavior.
  We study an end-to-end post-training conversion of Qwen, an instruction-tuned 4B-parameter model, using KOTMS rotation, E2M-ATQ ternarization, and GPTQ-style error compensation from TWLA. The experiment is weight-only: activations remain at 16-bit precision, so ILA-AMP is omitted. We evaluate effective bit accounting, task capability retention, perplexity, calibration sensitivity, checkpoint composition, and deployment behavior.
  The final conversion uses 1.641 effective bits per weight for quantized linear weights, with 81.62% of model parameters targeted. Across ten scored capability comparisons, accuracy falls from 64.5% to 54.7%. Degradation is uneven: BoolQ retains 84.6% chance-corrected teacher performance, while ARC-Challenge retains 43.8%. Perplexity rises from 13.639 to 18.748 on WikiText-2, 24.700 to 31.992 on PTB, and 19.831 to 28.966 on C4.
  A subsequent packing run preserves the ternary planes and scales, reducing reported model size from 8.29 GiB to 3.96 GiB with essentially unchanged perplexity. A separate third-party packing attempt was lossy and is excluded from the primary artifact claim. The packed artifact has not been benchmarked end-to-end for task accuracy or generation throughput. A preliminary Triton GEMV microbenchmark is 4.6x slower than FP16 cuBLAS on one tested shape. We therefore do not claim that compression alone yields faster inference.

</details>


### [237] [Benchmarking Language Models for Statistical Problem Formulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01982)
*Chen Wang,Junzhe Zhao,Xin Cong,Wanlu Deng,Ke Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StatFormBench is a new benchmark for evaluating LLMs' ability to formulate statistical problems from informal user goals, covering 1,013 samples across 85 fine-grained categories. The best LLMs achieve only 72.0% classification accuracy and 63.2% variable set overlap, revealing significant limitations in current LLM performance on this upstream statistical task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM evaluations assume the analysis target is already specified, but in practice users arrive with informal goals and heterogeneous data. The model must decide what statistical task is implied and which data are relevant - a critical upstream step that has been overlooked.

Method: The authors formalized Statistical Problem Formulation into two subtasks: (1) Statistical Problem Classification and (2) Variable Identification & Role Assignment. They built StatFormBench from five cross-domain statistics textbooks and a data science case library, containing 1,013 samples across 20 coarse-grained and 85 fine-grained categories. They evaluated 14 open- and closed-source LLMs using zero-shot prompting and various enhanced prompting strategies.

Result: The best zero-shot models achieved only 72.0% fine-grained classification accuracy and 63.2% variable set overlap. No model performed consistently best across both subtasks. Enhanced prompting strategies yielded only limited or inconsistent gains.

Conclusion: Current LLMs struggle with statistical problem formulation tasks. The benchmark and evaluation code are released to facilitate future research on improving LLMs' ability to handle informal user goals and heterogeneous data in statistical analysis workflows.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as assistants for statistical and data science work, yet existing evaluations largely assume the analysis target is already specified. In practice, users arrive with informal goals and heterogeneous data, leaving the model to decide what statistical task is implied and which data are relevant. We first formalize this upstream step as Statistical Problem Formulation and decompose it into two subtasks: (1) Statistical Problem Classification and (2) Variable Identification & Role Assignment. We then introduce StatFormBench, a benchmark built from five cross-domain statistics textbooks and a data science case library, covering diverse problem types, data representations, and scenario styles. It contains 1,013 samples spanning 20 coarse-grained and 85 fine-grained statistical problem categories. Across 14 open- and closed-source LLMs, the best zero-shot models reach only 72.0 fine-grained classification accuracy and 63.2 variable set overlap. No model performs consistently best across the two subtasks, while enhanced prompting strategies yield only limited or inconsistent gains. We release the benchmark data on Hugging Face at https://huggingface.co/datasets/THU-CongLab/StatFormBench and the evaluation code on GitHub at https://github.com/THU-CongLab/StatFormBench.

</details>


### [238] [ToolGate: An Executable Acceptance Pipeline for Tool-Dependent Scientific Benchmark Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02067)
*Ke Zhang,Yankang Liu,Roya Zandi,Maziar Raissi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ToolGate is a system that automatically generates and validates scientific benchmarks using language models, employing three filtering gates: (1) executable script verification, (2) no-tool screening to reject trivially answerable questions, and (3) tool-using agent solving. Applied to FEniCSx with 500 attempts, 128 unique valid candidates survived after all gates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific benchmarks are traditionally built by domain experts through labor-intensive processes of writing tasks and cross-checking. While language models can propose candidates quickly, acceptance remains problematic—candidates may be invalid (script fails or returns wrong answer) or trivial (solvable without specialist software). The authors aim to automate answer checking and difficulty screening while keeping domain design and final review to experts.

Method: ToolGate treats every generated item as a proposal and retains it only if three gates pass: (1) Local-verification gate - an executable solution script must reproduce the proposed answer when run with the scientific software (FEniCSx); (2) Randomized no-tool screening rejects candidates that language models can solve from the prompt alone without software; (3) A tool-using agent (GPT-5.5 Codex CLI) must solve each surviving candidate within a fixed time limit. The final pool undergoes rescreening and deduplication.

Result: From 500 generation attempts in FEniCSx: 478 candidates passed the local-verification gate. After rescreening: two randomized no-tool screens excluded 222 candidates, and direct GPT-5.5 API calls excluded another 121. Of the remaining 135, the GPT-5.5 Codex CLI agent with FEniCSx access solved 130. After exact deduplication, 128 unique protocol survivors remained.

Conclusion: ToolGate successfully transforms repeated answer checking and difficulty screening into an auditable, automated process while delegating domain design and final expert review to humans. It significantly reduces per-item labor while maintaining benchmark quality through rigorous multi-stage filtering.

Abstract: Scientific benchmarks are commonly built by domain experts who write tasks and cross-check one another's work, or who adapt existing material from textbooks, published papers, and online resources. These routes can produce strong evaluations, but they require substantial per-item labor. Language models can reduce this repeated work by proposing candidates quickly. The remaining problem is acceptance. We target scientific questions whose answers require computations with specialist software rather than unaided reasoning alone. A candidate is invalid if its script fails or returns a different answer, or trivial if a model answers it without the software. We present ToolGate, which treats every generated item as a proposal and keeps it only if three gates pass. First, an executable solution script must reproduce the proposed answer when run with the scientific software. Second, randomized no-tool screening rejects candidates that models can already solve from the prompt alone. Third, a tool-using agent must solve each survivor within a fixed time limit. We instantiate ToolGate in FEniCSx with 500 generation attempts. The local-verification gate retains 478 candidates. For final reporting, we rescreen this pool after generation: two randomized no-tool screens exclude 222 from the reported pool, and direct GPT-5.5 API calls at medium reasoning (the API default) exclude another 121. Of the remaining 135, a GPT-5.5 Codex CLI agent with access to FEniCSx solves 130; exact deduplication leaves 128 unique protocol survivors. ToolGate turns repeated answer checking and difficulty screening into an auditable process while leaving domain design and final review to experts.

</details>


### [239] [When Agents Implement Systems: A Case Study in Defects, Detection, and Evaluation Rigor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01985)
*Phanindra Reddy Madduru*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a case study of an LLM coding agent implementing a multi-component data system, cataloging 5 defects and evaluating retrieval-filtering trade-offs on HotpotQA. The study finds filtered retrieval significantly outperforms unfiltered search (p<0.0001), with filtered recall reaching ceiling at budget 3 while unfiltered only achieves 69% at budget 10. The agent required human correction for some defects, highlighting the need for oversight in LLM agent deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To empirically characterize how LLM coding agents behave on systems-level requirements (schema design, async orchestration, configuration correctness, retrieval-filtering trade-offs) which lack empirical study despite increasing end-to-end engineering work.

Method: Case study of an LLM coding agent implementing a multi-component data system against a pre-existing specification, with evaluation on the HotpotQA benchmark comparing filtered vs unfiltered retrieval strategies across budgets from 1-10 on 2994 paragraphs.

Result: Five defects cataloged by constraint violated and detection method. Retrieval evaluation shows filtered recall reaches ceiling by budget 3 (expected since candidates restricted to gold paragraphs), while unfiltered search recovers all evidence only 69% of the time at budget 10. Gap holds at every budget tested with sign test p<0.0001. Agent autonomy succeeded in some areas but required correction in others, including one unverified performance fix.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that constrained LLM agents can implement complex data systems but require human oversight. The retrieval-filtering strategy shows significant performance gains (filtered recall reaches ceiling at budget 3, while unfiltered search only achieves 69% at budget 10, p<0.0001). Key findings include: (1) agents successfully implement given specifications but introduce defects requiring correction, (2) entity-based filtering substantially outperforms unfiltered search for retrieval tasks, (3) one instance showed a claimed performance fix was never re-measured on the regression that motivated it. The study provides empirical evidence for the importance of constraint specification in LLM agent deployments.

Abstract: As LLM coding agents increasingly perform end-to-end engineering work, we lack empirical characterization of how they behave on systems-level requirements: schema design, async orchestration, configuration correctness, and retrieval-filtering trade-offs. We present a case study of one such agent implementing a multi-component data system against a detailed pre-existing specification. Storage technologies, schema, entity-resolution algorithm, and retrieval-filtering strategy were fixed in advance; the agent autonomy was in the implementation, in diagnosing and fixing defects it introduced, and in interaction-design choices left open. Over a single session, we catalog five such defects, categorized by constraint violated and detection method. We further evaluate, on the public HotpotQA benchmark, the one retrieval trade-off specified in that architecture: restricting candidates to a graph-identified entity set before ranking versus unfiltered search. We substitute the benchmark gold evidence labels for entity identification, since we lacked LLM access to run that stage, and report standard recall rather than the benchmark own accuracy metrics. Across retrieval budgets from 1 to 10 and 100 questions against a pooled corpus of 2994 paragraphs, filtered recall reaches its ceiling by a budget of 3, expected once candidates are restricted to the gold paragraphs themselves, while unfiltered search recovers all required evidence only 69 percent of the time even at a budget of 10, a gap that holds at every budget tested, with sign test p less than 0.0001. We close with a discussion of where the agent autonomy succeeded versus required correction, including one instance where a claimed performance fix was never re-measured on the regression that motivated it.

</details>


### [240] [ClaimReceipt: Verifying Evidence Sufficiency and Coverage in Agent Evaluations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01992)
*Peiying Zhu,Sidi Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ClaimReceipt is a claim-relative receipt specification and selective verifier for agent evaluations that binds typed transaction evidence to a signed experiment manifest, returning PASS, INVALID, or INCONCLUSIVE per claim. It addresses two key evidentiary questions: sufficiency (whether claims can be recomputed from evidence) and coverage (whether records cover all committed experiments). The system was evaluated on 1,392 historical records and a prospective 30-assignment epoch, with very low overhead (0.021% inference time, 9.9 KB per transaction).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent evaluations face critical evidentiary challenges: generic logs and hash-linked transcripts cannot reliably verify whether a reported claim is recomputable from retained evidence (sufficiency) or whether retained records cover the committed experiment set (coverage). This creates a verification gap in computational experiment auditing.

Method: ClaimReceipt binds typed transaction evidence to a signed experiment manifest using a frozen SHA-256 specification (18d109...b81). It implements a selective verifier that returns PASS, INVALID, or INCONCLUSIVE per claim. Evaluation involved: (1) CR-2 verifier on 1,392 historical buyer-seller records reproducing 5 manual audit verdicts, replaying 600 deterministic and 792 post-generation records, testing 13 field groups for non-redundancy, and checking 11/11 semantic faults with 0/8 false positives; (2) CR-3 prospective epoch with 30 pre-committed assignments, signed terminal receipts, and encrypted private evidence for auditors.

Result: CR-2 verifier reproduced all 5 manual audit verdicts, exactly replayed all 1,392 records, made all 13 declared field groups non-redundant under ablations, and returned expected results on 11/11 semantic faults with 0 false positives. CR-3 prospective epoch: complete evidence yielded PASS for coverage and accounting; withholding one receipt returned INCONCLUSIVE_COVERAGE; withholding all private openings preserved coverage but made economic claims inconclusive (matching preregistered prediction). Overhead: 0.021% model-inference time, 9.9 KB per transaction. A specification-legibility probe revealed the frozen specification is not yet unambiguous to independent readers.

Conclusion: Claim verification requires both claim-sufficient evidence AND a committed universe against which omissions become visible. The system demonstrates effective verification but highlights that specification legibility remains a challenge for independent verification.

Abstract: Agent evaluations face two distinct evidentiary questions: whether a reported claim is recomputable from retained evidence (sufficiency), and whether the retained records cover the committed experiment set (coverage). Generic logs and hash-linked transcripts answer neither reliably. We introduce ClaimReceipt, a claim-relative receipt specification and selective verifier that binds typed transaction evidence to a signed experiment manifest and returns PASS, INVALID, or INCONCLUSIVE per claim. We freeze the specification before implementation (SHA-256 18d109...b81). On 1,392 historical buyer--seller records, a CR-2 verifier reproduces all five manually labeled audit verdicts, exactly replays 600 deterministic and 792 post-generation records, makes every one of 13 declared field groups non-redundant under tested ablations, and returns the expected result on 11/11 semantic faults with 0/8 false positives. We then run a separate prospective CR-3 epoch: 30 assignments are committed before inference, terminal receipts are signed and chained, and private evidence is encrypted for an auditor. Complete evidence yields coverage and accounting PASS; withholding one terminal receipt returns INCONCLUSIVE_COVERAGE, while withholding all private openings preserves coverage and protocol verification but makes economic claims inconclusive, exactly matching a preregistered prediction. Receipt instrumentation adds 0.021% of model-inference time and 9.9 KB per transaction. A specification-legibility probe indicates that our own frozen specification is not yet unambiguous to an independent reader. Claim verification therefore requires both claim-sufficient evidence and a committed universe against which omissions become visible.

</details>


### [241] [HeadWiseKV: Budgeted Per-Head Cache Residency for Hybrid Long-Context Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02029)
*Renjie Xie,Juncheng Yang,Aoting Hu,Mingxi Zhang,Liyao Wu,Zheheng Hong,Wei Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HeadWiseKV is a training-free framework that compresses residual global KV caches in hybrid language models by assigning each KV head a static, multilevel history window, reducing memory usage by 8.59% while preserving downstream quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context inference in hybrid language models consumes substantial GPU memory due to growing key-value (KV) cache during decoding. The residual global-attention layers dominate context-dependent cache demand, reducing generation throughput and limiting the usable context length.

Method: HeadWiseKV uses a training-free framework that assigns each physical KV head a static, multilevel history window. The core algorithm SeqCalib processes layers in execution order and conditions each decision on the lower-layer policy used at deployment. A grouped-cache runtime materializes the selected policy as actual per-head KV residency rather than a mask over a full cache.

Result: HeadWiseKV retains near-Full-KV quality on RULER and LoCoMo benchmarks across four hybrid long-context models. It reduces sampled peak device memory by 8.59% at 112K context length and extends the largest verified successful context from 114K to 161K.

Conclusion: HeadWiseKV effectively addresses the KV cache memory bottleneck in hybrid language models while preserving model quality. It reduces sampled peak device memory by 8.59% at 112K context length and extends the largest verified successful context from 114K to 161K, making long-context inference more practical.

Abstract: Long-context inference retains a growing key--value (KV) cache during decoding, which consumes substantial GPU memory and can reduce generation throughput. This bottleneck remains in hybrid language models because their residual global-attention layers can dominate context-dependent cache demand. We study how to allocate this state under an aggregate KV-residency budget. We introduce HeadWiseKV, a training-free framework that compresses the residual global KV caches of hybrid language models while preserving their native local, recurrent, and linear paths. It assigns each physical KV head a static, multilevel history window, making cache demand predictable before serving. We formulate this allocation as a restricted operational rate--distortion problem and propose SeqCalib as the core policy-generation algorithm in HeadWiseKV. SeqCalib processes layers in execution order and conditions each decision on the lower-layer policy used at deployment, thereby accounting for interactions across depth. A grouped-cache runtime materializes the selected policy as actual per-head KV residency rather than a mask over a full cache. We evaluate downstream quality across four hybrid long-context models and study physical residency and serving behavior on Qwen3.6-27B. HeadWiseKV retains near-Full-KV RULER and LoCoMo quality across the evaluated models. In the fixed-model systems study, it reduces sampled peak device memory by 8.59\% at a 112K context length and extends the largest verified successful context from 114K to 161K.

</details>


### [242] [Monitoring Web Agents Without Internal Signals: Observable Trajectories and Key-Step Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02057)
*Sitong Pan,Yipeng Shen,Yilin Lu,Caiwen Ding,Lu Cheng,Qianwen Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses web-agent monitoring by predicting whether an agent's execution is on track or failing using only observable trajectory signals (without access to internal model signals like token logits). Two feature representations are proposed: Macro features (cross-step behavior summaries) and Micro features (consistency measurements through black-box queries). The approach labels critical uncorrected errors as key-step boundaries to preserve valid early prefixes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monitoring web agents is challenging when model-internal uncertainty signals (token logits) are unavailable. This work aims to enable reliable risk prediction for web agents using only externally observable signals, which is practical for real-world deployment where internal model states may not be accessible.

Method: The authors derive two observable trajectory representations: (1) Macro features that summarize cross-step agent-environment behavior and feedback, and (2) Micro features that measure consistency of intention, action, and anticipated state change through repeated black-box queries. They propose a labeling scheme that identifies the first critical uncorrected error associated with final failure as a key-step boundary, preserving valid early prefixes of failed trajectories.

Result: Across WebArena-Lite and Online Mind2Web benchmarks with five different backbones (both open and closed-source), observable trajectory signals achieved competitive performance compared to internal-signal baselines. The predictors also support early intervention under fixed false-cut budgets and transfer across held-out website categories.

Conclusion: Observable trajectory signals can support valuable risk prediction abilities for web agents, enabling practical monitoring even when internal model signals are unavailable. This approach enables early intervention and generalizes across different website categories.

Abstract: Reliable web-agent monitoring is difficult when model-internal uncertainty signals such as token logits are unavailable. In this work, we study prefix-level risk prediction for web agents using observable trajectory signals: given an evolving prefix, estimate whether the current execution remains on track or is tending toward failure. We derive two observable trajectory representations: Macro features summarize cross-step agent--environment behavior and feedback, while Micro features measure the consistency of intention, action, and anticipated state change through repeated black-box queries. Instead of inheriting the final result label, we label the first critical error that remains uncorrected in the observed continuation and is associated with final failure as a key-step boundary, preserving valid early prefixes of failed trajectories as on track. Across WebArena-Lite and Online Mind2Web web agent benchmarks with five open- and closed-source backbones, observable trajectory signals are competitive with internal-signal baselines. The resulting predictors also support early intervention under fixed false-cut budgets and transfer across held-out website categories. These findings show that observable trajectory signals support valuable risk prediction abilities.

</details>


### [243] [DocHop: Benchmarking Out-of-domain Multi-hop Reasoning in Information-Dense Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02059)
*Zhuoran Yu,Le Thien Phuc Nguyen,Jaden Park,Xinyi Gu,Zexue He,Soochahn Lee,Rogerio Feris,Yong Jae Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DocHop is a benchmark for evaluating multimodal large language models' ability to perform integrated chart-context reasoning in document-style images, where models must use textual context to select, interpret, and aggregate evidence across multiple charts based on multi-step compositional constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks evaluate chart and document understanding in isolation, leaving unexplored the key capability of whether models can use textual context to determine how chart evidence should be selected, interpreted, and aggregated.

Method: Constructed via a stochastic logic-first generation pipeline with controllable reasoning depth and visual density, covering 2,074 examples across six task categories. Questions are grounded on semantic reference labels defined in the narrative, requiring models to resolve target entities from context before aggregating evidence across multiple charts.

Result: Human annotators achieve over 90% accuracy while the best model reaches only 62.83%. Reasoning-enhanced models consistently show improved results, but performance degrades as reasoning complexity increases.

Conclusion: DocHop provides a controlled testbed for challenging multi-hop document reasoning, revealing a substantial gap between human and model performance on integrated chart-context reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong performance on structured visual understanding tasks such as chart and document question answering. However, existing benchmarks typically evaluate these domains in isolation, leaving underexplored a key capability: whether models can use textual context to determine how chart evidence should be selected, interpreted, and aggregated. We introduce DocHop, a benchmark for integrated chart--context reasoning in document-style images. In DocHop, the document narrative specifies multi-step compositional constraints, while charts provide the corresponding data values. Questions are grounded on a semantic reference label defined in the narrative, requiring models to resolve target entities from context before aggregating evidence across multiple charts. To enable systematic evaluation, we construct DocHop via a stochastic logic-first generation pipeline with controllable reasoning depth and visual density, covering 2,074 examples across six task categories. Experiments on a wide range of proprietary and open-source MLLMs show a substantial gap to human performance: annotators achieve over 90% accuracy, while the best model reaches only 62.83%. Reasoning-enhanced models consistently show improved results, but performance degrades as reasoning complexity increases. Overall, DocHop provides a controlled testbed for challenging multi-hop document reasoning.

</details>


### [244] [MineTRACE: An Evidence-Grounded Interactive Reasoning System for Mineral Prospectivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02060)
*Yiran Zhang,Jinwen Liu,Daniel Su,Yisu Chen,Qiang Sun,Chris Gonzalez,Eun-Jung Holden,Marco Fiorentini,Wei Liu,Yihao Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MineTRACE is a web-based mineral exploration system that integrates multi-source geochemical, geophysical, and geological evidence for eight commodities (Cu, Au, Ni, W, Sn, Co, Ta, Mn). It provides transparent prospectivity scoring through an expert tree, supports location/region queries, and offers a natural language conversational assistant for evidence-based exploration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mineral prospectivity systems provide only opaque scores or heatmaps without interpretable evidence, making it difficult for geoscientists to verify and understand exploration results. There is a need for transparent, evidence-grounded exploration tools that make public geoscience data more accessible, interpretable, and verifiable.

Method: The system uses a transparent expert tree informed by geological knowledge and known deposits to combine multi-source evidence into interpretable prospectivity scores. A conversational assistant retrieves scores and supporting evidence from the analysis pipeline and presents them in natural language. Evaluation includes spatial AUC measurements across test scenarios and end-to-end assessment of query accuracy and response grounding.

Result: The scorer achieves spatial AUC values of up to 0.917 across different test scenarios. End-to-end evaluation assesses query accuracy and response grounding, demonstrating the system's effectiveness in providing transparent and accurate mineral exploration support.

Conclusion: MineTRACE successfully makes public geoscience data easier to access, interpret, and verify through its transparent expert tree approach and natural language interface. The system supports more efficient and transparent mineral exploration by combining multi-source evidence into interpretable prospectivity scores with high accuracy (AUC up to 0.917).

Abstract: Mineral exploration requires integrating heterogeneous geochemical, geophysical, and geological evidence, yet existing prospectivity systems often provide only opaque scores or heatmaps. We present MineTRACE, a web-based system for evidence-grounded exploration of eight commodities: Cu, Au, Ni, W, Sn, Co, Ta, and Mn. Users can explore prospectivity maps, query locations or regions, inspect supporting evidence, and interact through natural language. A transparent expert tree, informed by geological knowledge and known deposits, combines multi-source evidence into interpretable prospectivity scores. For a new location, the conversational assistant retrieves the score and supporting evidence from the analysis pipeline and presents them in natural language. The scorer achieves spatial AUC values of up to 0.917 across different test scenarios, while end-to-end evaluation assesses query accuracy and response grounding. MineTRACE makes public geoscience data easier to access, interpret, and verify, supporting more efficient and transparent mineral exploration.

</details>


### [245] [CHIME: Credit-Aware Hierarchical Memory Evolution for Long-Horizon Agentic Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02074)
*Yongshi Ye,Tian Lan,Feihu Jiang,Muyang Ye,Bin Zhu,Qianghuai Jia,Longyue Wang,Zhao Xu,Weihua Luo,Xiaodong Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CHIME addresses the credit assignment problem in self-evolving memory systems for agent planning by maintaining separate planning and execution memory banks and attributing task outcomes to their specific causes before memory updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-evolving memory methods improve planning at inference time without parameter updates, but suffer from a credit assignment problem where final task outcomes conflate plan quality with execution errors and environmental factors, leading to biased and noisy accumulated planning experience.

Method: CHIME maintains separate planning and execution memory banks and follows an "attribute-before-memorize" principle: it first attributes each task outcome to the plan, execution, both, or neither, then updates only the corresponding memory bank.

Result: CHIME consistently outperforms state-of-the-art training-based and self-evolving memory baselines on four long-horizon agent benchmarks. It accumulates effective memory with fewer items, learned memory values faithfully reflect downstream utility, and the accumulated memory transfers effectively across backbone models.

Conclusion: The credit-aware hierarchical memory approach successfully solves the credit assignment problem in self-evolving memory systems, enabling more efficient and accurate planning through proper attribution of outcomes to planning versus execution components.

Abstract: Planning is a central capability that enables agents to decompose complex long-horizon tasks into manageable steps. Test-time search and training-based methods improve planning but incur high inference costs or require expensive training data. Self-evolving memory instead accumulates reusable experience from agent interaction outcomes into an external memory bank, so planning capability keeps improving at inference time without parameter updates. However, existing self-evolving memory methods share an inherent credit assignment problem: they rely on final task outcomes as feedback, but such outcomes conflate plan quality with execution errors and environmental factors, so the accumulated planning experience is often biased and noisy. To address this problem, we propose Credit-Aware Hierarchical Memory Evolution (CHIME), a self-evolving memory framework that maintains a separate planning bank and execution bank and follows an attribute-before-memorize principle: CHIME first attributes each task outcome to the plan, the execution, both, or neither, and then updates only the corresponding memory bank. Extensive experiments on four long-horizon agent benchmarks show that CHIME consistently outperforms state-of-the-art training-based and self-evolving memory baselines. Further analyses reveal several interesting findings. For example, CHIME accumulates effective memory with far fewer items. In addition, the learned memory values faithfully reflect downstream utility: high-quality planning memories are more valuable than execution memories. Finally, the accumulated memory effectively transfers across backbone models. Code will be released at https://github.com/ATH-MaaS/Marco-DeepResearch.

</details>


### [246] [Beyond Outcome Gaps: Process-Aware Fairness Diagnosis for LLM-based Multi-Agent Decision Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02092)
*Yiran Zhao,Lu Zhou,Liming Fang,Yufei Chen,Jiafei Wu,Zhe Liu,Xiaogang Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCOPED-Hiring is a process-aware fairness diagnosis pipeline for LLM-based hiring multi-agent systems that reveals how balanced final hire rates can mask hidden trajectory unfairness, and demonstrates that process diagnosis can guide effective repair.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current outcome-based fairness audits for LLM-based MAS miss risks that arise within decision trajectories. There is a need to detect hidden unfairness in multi-agent decision processes, particularly in high-stakes hiring scenarios.

Method: SCOPED-Hiring constructs controlled resume variants, runs role-based hiring committees, logs over 311K structured decision trajectories, and converts trajectory fields into quantitative fairness signals using six diagnostic lenses: final outcome, counterfactual, process, pathway, dynamic, and design effects.

Result: The study reveals that balanced final hire rates can mask hidden trajectory unfairness: career gaps trigger suspicion, proxy cues shape qualification judgments, and identity cues lead to unequal investigation. Targeted repair guided by these diagnoses reduces total layered burden by 72.3% while shifting the hire rate by only 1.86 percentage points.

Conclusion: Process diagnosis can effectively guide repair in LLM-based hiring MAS, achieving significant burden reduction with minimal impact on hire rates. The six diagnostic lenses provide a comprehensive framework for identifying and addressing fairness issues throughout the decision trajectory.

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) are increasingly considered for high-stakes decision-making, yet outcome-based fairness audits can miss where risks arise within the decision trajectory. We present SCOPED-Hiring, a process-aware fairness diagnosis pipeline for LLM-based hiring MAS. SCOPED-Hiring constructs controlled resume variants, runs role-based hiring committees, logs over 311K structured decision trajectories, and converts trajectory fields into quantitative fairness signals organized by six diagnostic lenses: final outcome, counterfactual, process, pathway, dynamic, and design effects. SCOPED-Hiring reveals that balanced final hire rates can mask hidden trajectory unfairness in multi-agent decision trajectories: career gaps trigger suspicion, proxy cues shape qualification judgments, and identity cues lead to unequal investigation. Targeted repair guided by these diagnoses reduces total layered burden by 72.3% while shifting the hire rate by only 1.86 pp, showing that process diagnosis can guide effective repair. Project Page: https://scoped-hiring-project-page.vercel.app/

</details>


### [247] [MASkills: Continual Skills Optimization for Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02094)
*Huaiyuan Yao,Xiaoou Liu,Charles Fleming,Tianlong Chen,Hua Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MASkills is a continual learning framework for optimizing multi-agent LLM systems through agent skills, featuring skill-conditioned credit assignment, hierarchical credit aggregation, and momentum-smoothed optimization with skill library evolution via refinement, induction, consolidation, and pruning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-reflection methods for LLM-based multi-agent systems build experience memories that are hard to invoke, refine, or scale. Agent skills offer a more actionable unit of structured procedural knowledge specifying when/how to act and which resources to use.

Method: MASkills introduces an agent-optimization pipeline integrating: (1) skill-conditioned credit assignment for attributing outcomes to specific skills, (2) hierarchical credit aggregation across multiple agents/tasks, and (3) momentum-smoothed optimization for stable skill library evolution through four operations: refinement, induction, consolidation, and pruning.

Result: Experiments on HotpotQA, LoCoMo, and GAIA benchmarks demonstrate MASkills' effectiveness across multiple agentic tasks.

Conclusion: MASkills provides a novel framework for continual improvement of multi-agent LLM systems by treating agent skills as the optimizable unit, enabling scalable and actionable knowledge acquisition compared to traditional memory-based approaches.

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent systems have shown strong performance on complex tasks, yet continual improvement from interaction experience remains challenging. Existing self-reflection methods build experience memories, but memories are mostly hard to invoke, refine, or scale, while agent skills offer a more actionable unit: structured procedural knowledge that specifies when to act, how to act, and which resources or tools to use. We introduce MASkills, a continual learning framework that optimizes multi-agent LLM systems through agent skills. MASkills presents a new agent-optimization pipeline that integrates skill-conditioned credit assignment, hierarchical credit aggregation, and momentum-smoothed optimization, enabling agent skill libraries to evolve through refinement, induction, consolidation, and pruning. Experiments on HotpotQA, LoCoMo, and GAIA demonstrate the effectiveness of MASkills across multiple agentic tasks. Our code is available at https://github.com/DaRL-GenAI/MASkills

</details>


### [248] [READY or Not: Reliable Enterprise Agent Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02095)
*Veronica Chatrath,Bryan Zhu,Jingxuan Fan,George Pu,Soham Dinesh Tiwari,Soham Dan,Ryan Young,Yuan,Li,Yuang Yao,Apaar Shanker,Minglai Yang,Daniel Yue Zhang,Yunzhong He,Ying Liu,Chenguang Wang,Zhijun Yin,Yuan Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: READY is a framework for qualifying AI agents for enterprise deployment that measures reliability, human-oversight burden, and cost, rather than just autonomous performance. It evaluates whether an agent can meet specified reliability targets under acceptable human oversight at tolerable cost, and provides a statistical qualification procedure on held-out cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI-agent benchmarks measure whether an agent can complete realistic professional work, but enterprise deployment requires answering a different question: whether an agent can meet a required reliability level, under acceptable human oversight, and at tolerable cost. Current benchmarks fail to capture these deployment requirements.

Method: READY preserves each workflow's definition of successful execution while applying a common qualification procedure. Given an agent, workflow, and candidate oversight policies, it measures reliability and operating cost of the human-AI system, selects the minimum-cost policy satisfying a specified reliability target, and statistically qualifies it on held-out cases. The framework is implemented as an open testbed that decouples workflow specification, execution, evaluation, and qualification.

Result: In an end-to-end clinical-audit case study with 16 agent systems and 750 cases, READY revealed differences hidden by autonomous performance: two systems with only 0.3 points difference in autonomous accuracy (72.8% vs 72.5%) required 39.2% versus 29.6% human review respectively to qualify at the same 76% reliability target.

Conclusion: READY shifts enterprise agent evaluation from "how well can the agent perform the work?" to "under what conditions, and at what cost, can it be reliably deployed?" It provides a basis for comparing agent systems, setting oversight requirements, and making evidence-based deployment decisions by making deployment conditions explicit and statistically testable.

Abstract: An AI agent can perform well on benchmarks and still be unsuitable for deployment. Existing AI-agent benchmarks measure whether an agent can complete realistic professional work, whereas enterprise deployment asks a different question: whether an agent can meet a required reliability level, under acceptable human oversight, and at tolerable cost. We introduce Reliable Enterprise Agent Deployment (READY), a framework for qualifying AI agents for deployment on enterprise workflows. READY preserves each workflow's own definition of successful execution while applying a common qualification procedure. Given an agent, a workflow, and a class of candidate oversight policies, READY measures the reliability and operating cost of the human-AI system, selects the minimum-cost policy that satisfies a specified reliability target, and statistically qualifies it on held-out cases. The resulting deployment profile characterizes the supported operating point: reliability, human-oversight burden, and cost. READY is implemented as an open testbed that decouples workflow specification, execution, evaluation, and qualification, and runs on existing agent-evaluation infrastructure. In an end-to-end clinical-audit case study spanning 16 agent systems and 750 cases, READY reveals differences hidden by autonomous performance: two systems separated by only 0.3 points in autonomous accuracy (72.8% vs. 72.5%) require 39.2% versus 29.6% human review, respectively, to qualify at the same 76% reliability target under the evaluated oversight policy. READY thus shifts enterprise agent evaluation from how well can the agent perform the work? to under what conditions, and at what cost, can it be reliably deployed? By making those conditions explicit and statistically testable, READY provides a basis for comparing agent systems, setting oversight requirements, and making evidence-based deployment decisions.

</details>


### [249] [Semantic Signal-Assisted Inspection and Recovery Allocation in Reverse Logistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02116)
*Jiani He,Dingyan Shang,Yihua Xu,Shiqi Huang,Yan Lyu,Jize Li,Shangjing Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework that converts return notes into condition factors and signal-quality scores to guide inspection depth and recovery allocation in reverse-logistics operations, evaluated across three synthetic benchmark scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reverse-logistics operators must decide how to inspect and route returned assets before their condition is fully observed, while full inspection consumes scarce labor resources.

Method: Semantic Signal-Assisted Decision Support framework converts return notes into a condition factor and signal-quality score to guide inspection depth and recovery allocation under shared labor capacity. Evaluated in three synthetic benchmark scenarios: IT decommissioning, aircraft maintenance, and consumer-electronics returns, using 30 paired simulation seeds.

Result: Keyword implementation improves net recovery value relative to structured-feature comparator with noisy full inspection while reducing inspection cost in all three scenarios. A risk-blind comparator (skipping inspection) still records higher value under purely economic objective. At matched inspection cost, score-guided targeting adds $53.9K per batch in aircraft scenario but has little effect in other two configurations. Phrase and LLM extractors provide further gains in aircraft scenario.

Conclusion: Narrative evidence (return notes) can effectively support inspection allocation decisions before recovery decisions are made in reverse-logistics operations.

Abstract: Reverse-logistics operators often decide how to inspect and route returned assets before their condition is fully observed, while full inspection consumes scarce labor. Semantic Signal-Assisted Decision Support converts return notes into a condition factor and a signal-quality score that guide inspection depth and recovery allocation under shared labor capacity. We evaluate the framework in three synthetic benchmark scenarios spanning information technology decommissioning, aircraft maintenance, and consumer-electronics returns. Across 30 paired simulation seeds, the keyword implementation improves net recovery value relative to a structured-feature comparator with noisy full inspection while reducing inspection cost in all three scenarios. A risk-blind comparator that skips inspection altogether still records higher value under the benchmark's purely economic objective. At matched inspection cost, score-guided targeting adds 53.9 thousand United States dollars per batch in the aircraft scenario but has little economic effect in the other two configurations; phrase and large language model extractors provide further gains in the aircraft scenario. These results show how narrative evidence can support inspection allocation before recovery decisions are made.

</details>


### [250] [Beyond Context Windows: Persistent Discovery Context for Data-Centric Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02129)
*Jalal Mahmud*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "persistent discovery context," a memory layer that stores prior intent-to-object mappings and reuses them for future retrieval in data-centric agents. Across three structured data environments, this approach consistently outperforms metadata-only search, works with automatically generated memories, and reveals a reproducible "interference" failure mode. In lexically sparse domains, memory-only retrieval can even beat metadata-based retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-centric agents repeatedly perform discovery steps to identify relevant data objects, but these successful discovery outcomes are typically discarded rather than reused. The authors recognized this as a missed opportunity for improving agent performance over time.

Method: The researchers evaluated persistent discovery context across three structured data environments, testing its effectiveness with both manually created and automatically generated memories. They compared memory-augmented retrieval against metadata-only search baselines.

Result: Persistent discovery context consistently improved retrieval quality over metadata-only search. It remained effective with automatically generated memories. The approach exposed a reproducible "interference" failure mode. In lexically sparse domains, memory-only retrieval actually outperformed metadata-based retrieval.

Conclusion: Discovery outcomes constitute a useful form of reusable context for data-centric agents. The findings suggest that storing and reusing prior discovery results can significantly enhance retrieval performance in data-centric systems.

Abstract: Data-centric agents repeatedly perform a discovery step before planning or execution: identifying the data objects relevant to a task. Yet successful discovery outcomes are typically discarded rather than reused. We introduce persistent discovery context, a lightweight memory layer that stores prior intent-to-object mappings and reuses them to augment future retrieval. Across three structured data environments, persistent discovery context consistently improves retrieval quality over metadata-only search, remains effective with automatically generated memories, and exposes a reproducible interference failure mode. In lexically sparse domains, memory-only retrieval can even outperform metadata-based retrieval. These findings suggest that discovery outcomes constitute a useful form of reusable context for data-centric agents.

</details>


### [251] [EmoStance: Response-Side Affective-Orientation Control for Empathetic Response Generation via Emoji Weak Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02133)
*Ziyuan Jin,Yuxuan Ge,Zheng Tian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces EmoStance, a method for controlling affective orientation in empathetic response generation by using multi-annotator emoji distributions as latent control signals. The approach steers a frozen instruction-tuned LLM through continuous prefix embeddings, achieving a 62.2% win rate in human evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Empathetic response generation requires models to understand not just what to say, but how to respond to the previous speaker's emotional state. Existing methods lack operationalized control over the response-side affective orientation (listener stance).

Method: The authors: (1) Formulate response-side affective-orientation control using emoji distributions as weak affective-attitudinal evidence rather than gold labels; (2) Construct EmojiDialogue, an extension of EmpatheticDialogues with emoji votes and confidence scores; (3) Propose EmoStance which models source-side affective expression, predicts soft response-side orientation from dialogue context and speaker roles, and steers a frozen instruction-tuned LLM through continuous prefix embeddings.

Result: In blind pairwise evaluation with 20 annotators and 800 judgments, EmoStance achieves a 62.2% decisive win rate, with clearest gains in contextual specificity and perceived responsiveness, while remaining complementary to external-knowledge methods.

Conclusion: The proposed EmoStance approach effectively controls affective orientation in empathetic dialogue generation by leveraging emoji-based weak supervision and continuous prefix embedding steering, demonstrating significant improvements in human evaluation metrics.

Abstract: Empathetic response generation requires models to decide not only what to say, but also how to respond to the previous speaker's affective situation. We formulate this as response-side affective-orientation control and use multi-annotator emoji distributions as weak affective--attitudinal evidence, rather than as output symbols or gold labels, to induce a latent control space that operationally approximates listener stance. We construct EmojiDialogue, an utterance-level extension of EmpatheticDialogues with emoji votes and confidence scores, and propose EmoStance, which models source-side affective expression, predicts a soft response-side orientation from dialogue context and speaker roles, and steers a frozen instruction-tuned LLM through continuous prefix embeddings. In blind pairwise evaluation with 20 annotators and 800 judgments, EmoStance achieves a 62.2% decisive win rate, with the clearest gains in contextual specificity and perceived responsiveness, while remaining complementary to external-knowledge methods. Code, annotation metadata, and reconstruction scripts are available in our GitHub repository: https://github.com/18277390221/EmoStance.

</details>


### [252] [FUSE: An Evaluating Framework for Dangerous Capabilities of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02168)
*Zhengyi Jin,Ru Zhang,Xiao Chen,Xinbo Liu,Jiaxuan Lin,Jia Huang,Jianyi Liu,Zhen Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a modular framework (K-D-H) for evaluating dangerous AI capabilities across three orthogonal dimensions: Knowledge, Defense, and Harm. The framework was instantiated with a chemical-biological (CB) module and tested on 12 commercial LLMs, revealing significant variations in dangerous capability profiles both across models and over time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the problem of fragmented safety evaluation, which undermines governance of dangerous AI capabilities. The authors argue that existing evaluation approaches lack standardization and comprehensiveness, making it difficult to compare AI systems' dangerous capabilities across different dimensions and model families.

Method: The researchers developed a modular framework with three orthogonal pipelines (Knowledge K, Defense D, and Harm H) under a unified protocol. The framework includes pluggable modules for scenario seeds, knowledge banks, hazard queries, and judge rubrics. They evaluated 12 commercial LLMs from four families (Claude, DeepSeek, GPT, and one other) using a chemical-biological (CB) module. Cross-judge consistency was measured using bootstrap correlation (ρ > 0.79, with 4 out of 5 judges), and pipeline orthogonality was verified through inter-correlation analysis (K-D-H correlations ρ ∈ [0.32, 0.52]).
