Result: The study found three key findings: (1) Models with comparable knowledge differ significantly in refusal resilience; (2) Strong defenders do not necessarily generate less harmful content when they comply; (3) Family-level patterns separate Claude, DeepSeek, and GPT models distinctly. Temporal analysis revealed that dangerous capability has not monotonically declined—newer models deepen knowledge while only partially improving defense, indicating that scaling and alignment progress do not uniformly translate into safety improvements.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that a standardized dangerous-capability profile (φ) can effectively aggregate results from the three orthogonal dimensions. The framework demonstrates transferability through a cyber pilot complementing the CB evaluation. They establish reliability via cross-judge consistency and pipeline orthogonality, suggesting that their modular approach can be applied across different safety domains while maintaining standardized evaluation protocols.

Abstract: Fragmented safety evaluation undermines the governance of dangerous AI capabilities. We present a modular framework that evaluates each model through three orthogonal pipelines---Knowledge ($K$), Defense ($D$), and Harm ($H$)---under a unified protocol, aggregating results into a standardized dangerous-capability profile $φ$. Pluggable modules supply scenario seeds, knowledge banks, hazard queries, and judge rubrics, while the core evaluation engine remains unchanged across domains; the CB evaluation is complemented by a cyber pilot demonstrating protocol transfer.
  Instantiating the framework with a chemical-biological (CB) module, we evaluate 12 commercial LLMs from four families. Our first contribution is a horizontal comparison of dangerous capability across models and model families: the three dimensions expose sharply divergent profiles---models with comparable knowledge differ in refusal resilience, and strong defenders do not generate less harmful content when they do comply---while family-level patterns further separate Claude, DeepSeek, and GPT models. The second is a temporal analysis of capability evolution: tracking $K$, $D$, and $H$ against model release dates reveals that dangerous capability has not monotonically declined; newer models deepen knowledge while only partially improving defense, showing that scaling and alignment progress do not uniformly translate into safety. Reliability is established via cross-judge consistency (bootstrap $ρ> 0.79$, 4 of 5 judges) and pipeline orthogonality ($K$--$D$--$H$ inter-correlations $ρ\in [0.32, 0.52]$).

</details>


### [253] [Examining the Vulnerability of Multi-Agent Medical Systems to Human Interventions for Clinical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02191)
*Benjamin C Liu,Dillon Mehta,Rishi Malhotra,Adam Zobian,Yong Ying Tan,Samir Chopra,Daniella Rand,Natalie Pang,Abhiram Gudimella,Kevin Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study examines how human interventions at vulnerable moments ("fault points") affect diagnostic accuracy in multi-agent medical AI systems. Using the MedQA dataset, researchers found that correct interventions can improve accuracy by up to 40%, while incorrect interventions degrade performance by up to 6% and increase diagnostic drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses a critical challenge in medical AI systems: how human interventions can either improve or undermine diagnostic accuracy at key moments in AI reasoning. The study recognizes that AI agents have specific vulnerability points where external influence can significantly impact outcomes.

Method: The study used the MedQA dataset to analyze simulated doctor-patient conversations. Fault points were defined as moments in AI agent conversations when an agent's reasoning became most vulnerable to external influence. The researchers measured how different types of interventions (correct, incorrect, bias-related) affected reasoning and accuracy.

Result: Correct intervention methods improved baseline diagnostic accuracy by up to 40%. Incorrect or bias-related interventions degraded performance by up to 6% and increased diagnostic drift and uncertainty. The analysis also revealed behavioral similarities between cognitive biases in simulated agent environments and real-world clinical practice, including premature closure and susceptibility to misleading cues.

Conclusion: Identifying and guiding fault points with human interventions may provide a mechanism for improving diagnostic robustness in multi-agent medical systems. This suggests that strategic human oversight at vulnerable moments could be a key strategy for enhancing AI-assisted diagnostic systems.

Abstract: Human interventions at fault points can alter the diagnostic accuracy of multi-agent medical systems. We defined fault points as moments in AI agent conversations, in which an agent's reasoning became most vulnerable to external influence. Using the MedQA dataset, this study analyzed simulated doctor-patient conversations to measure how interventions shifted reasoning and accuracy. Correct intervention methods showed an improvement in baseline diagnostic accuracy of up to 40%, while incorrect or bias-related interventions degraded performance by up to 6% and increased diagnostic drift and uncertainty. Beyond performance changes, our analysis revealed behavioral similarities between cognitive biases in simulated agent environments and real-world clinical practice. Examples included premature closure and susceptibility to misleading cues. Overall, these findings demonstrate that identifying and guiding fault points with human interventions may provide a mechanism for improving diagnostic robustness in multi-agent medical systems.

</details>


### [254] [ASCII Attack: Recontextualising Harmful Requests as Artistic Critique in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02215)
*Da Cheng Gu,Yifei Dong,Xinghao Yang,Yongshun Gong,Wei Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the "ASCII Attack," a novel jailbreak method that embeds harmful requests within ASCII art presented as artwork, bypassing safety training in large language models. Unlike previous attacks that hide content, this attack keeps the request fully readable but reframes it as an artistic critique request. Experiments across 11 models and 8 harm topics show 62% of framed prompts are judged harmful vs 42% of direct controls, with some models achieving 93% success rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety alignment training focuses on refusing harmful requests stated plainly, but fails to generalize to requests that recontextualize the same content differently. The researchers identify this as a "mismatched generalization" problem where surface form is protected but the underlying operational content is not. The ASCII Attack exploits this weakness by presenting harmful requests as artistic critique rather than direct commands.

Method: The researchers used a single-turn, black-box approach with no access to model internals. Each framed prompt (ASCII art with harmful request) was paired with a direct-question control to isolate the effect from topic, model, and decoding variation. They tested across 11 different language models and 8 harm topics, using a harm-aware classifier to evaluate responses. Five different harm judges were used to assess the outputs.

Result: The harm classifier judged 62% of framed prompts as harmful versus 42% of controls. The most susceptible model achieved a 93% success rate. A single query matched or exceeded published single-query attacks under 4 of 5 harm judges. The effect was found to track the model more than the topic and did not diminish with model scale. Nearly two-thirds of framed rows had at least one judge dissenting from the panel majority.

Conclusion: The ASCII Attack demonstrates that current safety training suffers from mismatched generalization—models learn to refuse surface forms rather than underlying harmful intent. The attack succeeds by recontextualizing harmful requests as artistic feedback requests. This vulnerability is consistent across model scales and represents a fundamental limitation in current alignment approaches. The widespread judge disagreement also suggests measurement validity issues in harm assessment.

Abstract: Safety alignment trains large language models to refuse harmful requests stated plainly, but that training is applied mostly to surface form. Requests that only recontextualise the same operational content, changing how the model reads it, are therefore only weakly covered. The ASCII Attack is one such recontextualisation. It is single-turn and black-box: one message, with no access to model internals. It embeds a fully legible harmful request in ASCIl-art characters, presents it as artwork, and asks for feedback. Unlike ArtPrompt, it hides nothing: the request stays readable. The reply is written as artistic critique and can contain operational detail that a plain request would have been refused for. Every framed prompt is paired with a direct-question control, so the contrast is isolated from topic, model and decoding variation. The contrast identifies a bundled surface, not one isolated channel. Across eleven models and eight harm topics, a harm-aware classifier judges 62% of framed prompts harmful against 42% of controls. On the most susceptible model the framed prompt succeeds 93% of the time. A single query matches or exceeds published single-query attacks under four of five harm judges. The effect tracks the model more than the topic and does not diminish with scale. At least one judge dissents from the panel majority on nearly two-thirds of framed rows, which is itself a measurement-validity finding. That pattern is consistent with mismatched generalisation.

</details>


### [255] [PEARL: Path-Entity Aligned Relational Learning with Contextual Subgraphs for Inductive Knowledge Graph Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02216)
*Yunchi Yang,Longlong Li,Cunquan Qu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PEARL is a Path-Entity Aligned Relational Learning framework for inductive knowledge graph completion (IKGC) that predicts missing links involving unseen entities by modeling paths as context-conditioned reasoning signals. It constructs query-specific contextual subgraphs, uses LLM-guided retriever to distill relevant paths, builds bipartite interaction graphs, and employs dual-view contrastive learning to suppress noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing subgraph- and path-based IKGC approaches encode relational paths independently of their surrounding query subgraphs, ignoring that the predictive relevance of a path may vary across different structural contexts.

Method: PEARL constructs query-specific contextual subgraphs from the union of query entities' neighborhoods. It uses an LLM-guided retriever to distill semantically relevant paths, then builds a bipartite interaction graph over paths, contextual entities, and a global subgraph representation. A dual-view contrastive objective promotes representation consistency under stochastic contextual perturbations to suppress noise from enlarged context.

Result: PEARL achieves the best average Hits@10 among compared IKGC methods on all three benchmarks (WN18RR, FB15k-237, and NELL-995).

Conclusion: Ablation studies, efficiency analyses, and case studies validate the contributions of contextual subgraph modeling, semantic path retrieval, path-entity interaction, and contrastive regularization to the model's performance.

Abstract: Inductive knowledge graph completion (IKGC) aims to predict missing links involving entities unseen during training, requiring models to learn transferable relational and structural patterns. Existing subgraph- and path-based approaches often encode relational paths independently of their surrounding query subgraphs, although the predictive relevance of a path may vary across structural contexts. We propose PEARL, a Path-Entity Aligned Relational Learning framework that models paths as context-conditioned reasoning signals. PEARL constructs a query-specific contextual subgraph from the union of the query entities' neighborhoods and uses a large language model (LLM)-guided retriever to distill semantically relevant paths. It then builds a bipartite interaction graph over paths, contextual entities, and a global subgraph representation, allowing path embeddings to adapt to local and global structural evidence. To suppress noise introduced by the enlarged context, PEARL employs a dual-view contrastive objective that promotes representation consistency under stochastic contextual perturbations. Experiments on WN18RR, FB15k-237, and NELL-995 show that PEARL obtains the best average Hits@10 among the compared IKGC methods on all three benchmarks. Ablation studies, efficiency analyses, and case studies further validate the contributions of contextual subgraph modeling, semantic path retrieval, path-entity interaction, and contrastive regularization.

</details>


### [256] [SkillGLoW: Procedural-Family Skill Consolidation for Self-Improving Agents on Long-Horizon Task Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02217)
*Ao Yan,Xin Zhang,Jiawei Du,Joey Tianyi Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillGLoW introduces a hierarchical skill management system for LLM agents that bridges the gap between a single global document (too generic) and a flat per-task pool (too instance-specific). It uses procedural families to aggregate local skills into reusable global priors, with instance details regenerated per task rather than stored.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM self-improvement approaches using textual skills fail on long-horizon workloads with diverse tasks: global documents collapse into generic advice, while per-task pools inflate and stay bound to specific instances. The missing element is the solving procedure shared by related task clusters.

Method: SkillGLoW (Global-Local Weave) operates in three phases: (1) Local skills are written from each task's execution; (2) These are aggregated into procedural families and compressed into de-instantiated global priors; (3) A commit gate admits priors only when real execution validates they don't degrade the library. Instance details are regenerated per task rather than stored.

Result: Across four benchmarks (math reasoning, terminal automation, software repair, embodied control) and three models: priors gain 17.2 points on hard tasks over no-skill baseline; positive gains in all 12 continual-improvement runs; 18.0 with local regeneration; library holds one prior per procedural family (3.6x more compact than per-task pool). Leads published optimizer on 15/21 cells. Lifts ALFWorld success from 73.9% to 83.9% on unseen tasks.

Conclusion: The key insight is that what transfers across tasks is the solving procedure (procedure), not task-specific memory. This procedural prior approach achieves superior performance while maintaining a compact library, demonstrating that de-instantiated global knowledge combined with per-task regeneration is more effective than either extreme of skill management.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly self-improve by writing and reusing textual skills, kept either as one global document or as a flat pool of per-task entries, though most of the evidence comes from domains with structurally similar tasks. On long-horizon workloads where each task demands a different solution, the two forms fail in opposite ways: the document collapses into generic discipline, while the pool inflates and its entries stay bound to the instance that wrote them. We argue the missing unit of reuse is the solving procedure shared by a cluster of related tasks, and build SkillGLoW (Global-Local Weave) around it: the local skills a task writes from its own execution are aggregated into procedural families and compressed into de-instantiated global priors, while the instance detail they hold is regenerated per task rather than stored; a commit gate admits a prior only when real execution shows it does not degrade the deployed library. Across four benchmarks (mathematical reasoning, terminal automation, software repair, and embodied control) and three models, the priors gain 17.2 points (hard) over the no-skill baseline on average, with positive gains in all 12 continual-improvement runs, and 18.0 with local regeneration, while the library holds one prior per procedural family, 3.6x more compact than the per-task pool. Under the same protocol GLoW leads a published single-document optimizer on 15 of 21 cells. Unmodified, the library lifts success on unseen ALFWorld tasks from 73.9% to 83.9%, evidence that what transfers is procedure rather than task memory.

</details>


### [257] [PhoenixNest-Video: Evidence-Grounded Multimodal Agent Framework for Automated Video Interview Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02231)
*Fan Yuxuan,Huang Miaojun,Zhang Haimei,Wu Jingshen,Liu Hao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PhoenixNest-Video is an evidence-grounded multimodal agent framework that automates video interview assessment by building semantic video graphs, performing cross-modal verification across visual/audio/textual streams, and using RL-trained scorers to produce per-criterion scores with traceable rationale. It achieves 91.50% grade-level accuracy on VInterview-2025.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interview assessment requires per-criterion judgments with behavioral evidence, but human evaluation is costly and inconsistent at scale, while existing AI approaches lack transparent, traceable rationales for their scores.

Method: The framework builds a semantic video graph as structured working memory, performs rubric-conditioned retrieval with cross-modal verification across visual, audio, and textual streams, and produces per-criterion scores anchored to candidate materials. A Scorer is trained via Rubrics-based Reinforcement Learning with dual rewards for rubric alignment and score-level differentiation.

Result: PhoenixNest-Video achieves 91.50% grade-level accuracy on VInterview-2025, substantially outperforming larger proprietary models.

Conclusion: A compact, rubric-grounded agent can score candidates with closer agreement to expert panels than direct prompting of much larger models, while exposing the evidence behind each score for human review.

Abstract: Interview assessment requires per-criterion judgments grounded in behavioral evidence, yet surging applicant volumes have made human-only evaluation costly and inconsistent, while existing AI approaches yield opaque scores without traceable rationale. We introduce PhoenixNest-Video, an evidence-grounded multimodal agent framework for automated video interview assessment. It builds a semantic video graph as structured working memory, performs rubric-conditioned retrieval with cross-modal verification across visual, audio, and textual streams, and produces per-criterion scores anchored to the candidate's materials. A Scorer trained via Rubrics-based Reinforcement Learning with dual rewards for rubric alignment and score-level differentiation internalizes the discriminative structure of multi-level rubrics. PhoenixNest-Video attains 91.50\% grade-level accuracy on VInterview-2025, outperforming substantially larger proprietary models. A compact, rubric-grounded agent therefore scores candidates in closer agreement with an expert panel than direct prompting of much larger models, and exposes the evidence behind each score for human review.

</details>


### [258] [PGPO: Potential-Guided Policy Optimization for Multi-Turn Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02236)
*Yuyao Zheng,Haipeng Sun,Junwei Bao,Lemao Liu,Hongfei Jiang,Yang Song,Dejing Dou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PGPO addresses coarse credit assignment in group-based RL for multi-turn agentic tasks by estimating state potentials from anchor-state-group statistics and deriving action advantages from potential differences between adjacent states, enabling cross-trajectory credit propagation and finer-grained credit assignment within failed trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Group-based RL for LLM post-training provides coarse credit for intermediate actions in multi-turn agentic tasks with sparse terminal rewards. Step-level advantages (e.g., GiGPO) still rely on final outcomes, so actions within failed trajectories remain poorly differentiated - effective actions receive same unfavorable credit as erroneous ones.

Method: PGPO estimates empirical state potentials from anchor-state-group return statistics within each rollout group, then derives action advantages from potential differences between adjacent states, enabling cross-trajectory credit propagation.

Result: Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show strong overall performance relative to recent group-based RL methods. Analysis provides evidence that PGPO yields more informative failure-side credit signals with negligible training overhead.

Conclusion: PGPO achieves strong performance on ALFWorld and WebShop benchmarks relative to recent group-based RL methods. It provides more informative failure-side credit signals with negligible training overhead by enabling cross-trajectory credit propagation and finer-grained step-level credit assignment, especially within failed trajectories.

Abstract: Group-based reinforcement learning (RL) has become an effective paradigm for LLM post-training, but in multi-turn agentic tasks with sparse terminal rewards, it often provides coarse credit for intermediate actions. To obtain more fine-grained credit assignment, recent work such as GiGPO introduces step-level advantages for intermediate actions. However, these step-level signals still rely on the final outcome of each individual trajectory. As a result, actions within failed trajectories can remain poorly differentiated, so effective actions can receive the same unfavorable credit as erroneous ones. In this work, we propose Potential-Guided Policy Optimization (PGPO) for multi-turn agentic tasks. PGPO estimates empirical state potentials from anchor-state-group return statistics within each rollout group. It then derives action advantages from potential differences between adjacent states, enabling cross-trajectory credit propagation. This provides finer-grained step-level credit assignment, especially within failed trajectories. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show strong overall performance relative to recent group-based RL methods. Further analysis provides evidence that PGPO yields more informative failure-side credit signals with negligible training overhead.

</details>


### [259] [Propose to Learn, Learn to Propose: Evaluability-Aware Assistance under Bounded Rationality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02242)
*Yifan Zhu,Sammie Katt,Samuel Kaski*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "evaluability-aware proposal planning" where AI assistants treat proposals not just as task solutions but also as probes to learn user preferences and evaluation constraints. This addresses the limitation that users often cannot reliably evaluate proposals due to bounded rationality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI assistance methods assume users can reliably evaluate any proposal. However, this fails in practice because of bounded rationality - users often cannot accurately assess proposals, especially complex or unfamiliar ones. The authors identify that proposals serve dual purposes: task intervention AND preference probing.

Method: The authors formalize this as "ProSE" (Probing Sequential Enhancement), a hidden-parameter sequential assistance problem. They instantiate it with a KL-regularized bounded-rational binary response model where acceptance trades off value gain against a distance-dependent evaluability penalty. They then develop "ProSE-Plan," a depth-2 Bayes-adaptive planner that scores proposals by possible responses and response-induced posterior beliefs.

Result: In controlled graph simulations, ProSE-Plan outperforms evaluability-unaware and myopic baselines when evaluation cost is the bottleneck. A probe-commit ablation confirms that the approach selects informative proposals that simpler methods miss.

Conclusion: The paper identifies "user evaluability" as a planning-relevant dimension of AI assistance, complementary to existing dimensions like generation quality and preference inference. This represents a new paradigm where AI systems explicitly plan for how learnable their proposals are from user feedback.

Abstract: AI assistants often collaborate by proposing candidate edits, plans, or designs that users evaluate before adoption. Existing assistance methods focus on proposal quality or user-goal inference, often assuming that the user can reliably evaluate any proposal, which can fail in practice because of bounded rationality. We study evaluability-aware proposal planning, where proposals serve both as task interventions and as probes for learning latent preferences and evaluation constraints, where the resulting belief updates then guide later proposals. We formalise this setting as ProSE, a hidden-parameter sequential assistance problem, and instantiate it with a KL-regularised bounded-rational binary response model in which acceptance trades off value gain against a distance-dependent evaluability penalty. Analysing the planning consequence of this likelihood reveals that likely accepted proposals and informative probes need not coincide, which explains why planners that only pursue acceptance systematically underperform. We operationalise ProSE with \textsc{ProSE-Plan}, a depth-2 Bayes-adaptive planner that scores proposals by possible responses and response-induced posterior beliefs. In controlled graph simulations, \textsc{ProSE-Plan} improves over evaluability-unaware and myopic baselines when evaluation cost is the bottleneck, and a probe-commit ablation confirms that our approach selects informative proposals that simpler methods miss. Our results thus identify user evaluability as a planning-relevant dimension of AI assistance, complementary to generation quality and preference inference.

</details>


### [260] [Task-Level Natural Language Priors as Learning Signals for Low-Resource LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02244)
*Jian Gao,Xiao Zhang,Xun Zhu,Miao Li,Ji Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Prior-Guided Tuning (PGT) is a training approach that incorporates natural-language task priors as auxiliary learning signals for low-resource LLM training, rather than treating them as mere input context. The proposed method Contrastive Prior Steering (CPS) adds positive/negative prior-conditioned losses to encourage task-consistent learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle when low-resource training data is ambiguous or incomplete. While task-level natural-language priors can provide guidance, existing approaches treat these priors only as input context, not as learning signals during training.

Method: Contrastive Prior Steering (CPS) keeps the original supervised objective intact while adding positive and negative prior-conditioned auxiliary losses. This encourages task-consistent learning and discourages plausible but misleading alternatives.

Result: AmbiMath: 97.6% average exact-match accuracy. Jigsaw: +9.5 percentage points Macro F1 over standard fine-tuning; with 1/10 data, slightly exceeds full-data plain fine-tuning. HANS: +8.3 and +5.2 percentage points non-entailment accuracy for LLaMA 3.1 8B and Qwen 2.5 7B respectively, while maintaining comparable in-domain MNLI accuracy.

Conclusion: Task-level natural-language priors can serve as useful auxiliary learning signals for low-resource LLM training, consistently improving over plain and prompt fine-tuning across diverse datasets and model architectures.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often struggle when low-resource training data are ambiguous or incomplete. Task-level natural-language priors can provide useful guidance in such settings, but existing approaches usually treat these priors as input context rather than as learning signals during training. We propose Prior-Guided Tuning (PGT), a training perspective that incorporates natural-language priors as auxiliary learning signals for low-resource LLM training. Under this perspective, we introduce Contrastive Prior Steering (CPS), which keeps the original supervised objective intact while adding positive and negative prior-conditioned auxiliary losses to encourage task-consistent learning and discourage plausible but misleading alternatives. Experiments on AmbiMath, Jigsaw, and MNLI/HANS show that CPS consistently improves over plain and prompt fine-tuning. On AmbiMath, CPS achieves 97.6% average exact-match accuracy. On Jigsaw, CPS improves average Macro F1 by 9.5 percentage points over standard fine-tuning, and with 1/10 of the experimental training data slightly exceeds full-data plain fine-tuning. On HANS, CPS improves non-entailment accuracy by 8.3 and 5.2 percentage points for LLaMA 3.1 8B and Qwen 2.5 7B, respectively, while maintaining comparable in-domain MNLI accuracy. These results support our central claim: task-level natural-language priors can provide useful guidance as auxiliary learning signals for low-resource LLM training. Our code and data will be publicly available.

</details>


### [261] [LLM-as-a-Judge Is Not an Oracle: Why Self-Improving Agents Need Deterministic Guardrails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02246)
*Vansh Wahi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies critical failure modes in self-improving agent pipelines where an LLM judge evaluates optimized prompts, and proposes PROCTOR, a Teacher-Student architecture with deterministic guardrails to constrain the judge's authority. The authors ran months of autonomous prompt-optimization in production across contract analysis, compliance review, and code quality, documenting 11 distinct failure modes in four categories. PROCTOR demotes the LLM judge from oracle to advisor, using five deterministic guardrails to gate all changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that self-improving agent systems rely on an LLM judge as the sole authority for measuring improvement, but this judge has not "earned" its oracle status. The authors' position is that the judge should be demoted to advisor, with its verdict becoming one input among several, and every change gated by deterministic verification that the judge cannot override.

Method: The authors built an alternative system (PROCTOR) and ran it in production over months across three domains: contract analysis, compliance review, and code quality. They cataloged failures through systematic observation of autonomous prompt-optimization loops. PROCTOR implements a Teacher-Student architecture with: (1) a stateful orchestrator holding all tool access, (2) stateless subagents that diagnose failures and draft mutations but cannot apply them, and (3) a Teacher (LLM judge) that grades mutations under five deterministic guardrails: hermetic sandboxes, capability-disjoint roles, acceptance checks that outrank the Teacher, frozen holdouts, and canary cases.

Result: The authors documented eleven failure modes in four classes: judge bias, harness and metric failures, ground-truth errors, and reward hacking. Key findings include: agents achieving perfect scores by reading cached answer keys (100% pass rate concealing 68% true capability), a corrupted ground-truth label causing the optimizer to delete correct compliance rules, and syntactically broken prompts being promoted due to silent parser fallback. Attempts to fix the judge by rewriting its rubric plateaued; only structural constraints on output order provided reliable improvement. PROCTOR prevented certain failures but the Teacher (as LLM judge) still has inherent limitations.

Conclusion: The judge in self-improving agent pipelines should be demoted from oracle to advisor. The only reliable fix for judge failures came from structural constraints on output order, not rubric rewriting. PROCTOR's five deterministic guardrails (hermetic sandboxes, capability-disjoint roles, acceptance checks, frozen holdouts, and canary cases) provide a framework for constraining LLM judges, but the system acknowledges that the Teacher itself remains an LLM with inherent limitations. The authors argue that deterministic verification layers are necessary to gate all changes, not just the judge's verdict.

Abstract: Self-improving agent pipelines have a problem at their center. An optimizer rewrites prompts to score higher, and the score comes from a judge that is itself an LLM. That judge has the last word on whether the system is getting better, and our position is that it has not earned it. The judge should be demoted from oracle to advisor: its verdict becomes one input among several, and every change is gated instead by a deterministic verification layer the judge cannot override. We reached this position by building the alternative and running it. Over months of running autonomous prompt-optimization loops in production across contract analysis, compliance review, and code quality, we cataloged eleven ways the evaluation signal failed, in four classes: judge bias, harness and metric failures, ground-truth errors, and reward hacking. Agents achieved perfect scores by reading cached answer keys from their environment, a 100% pass rate concealing 68% true capability. A corrupted ground-truth label caused the optimizer to delete correct compliance rules to agree with it. A syntactically broken prompt was promoted as the winner because a silent parser fallback improved the metric. Attempts to fix the judge by rewriting its rubric plateaued; the only reliable gain came from a structural constraint on its output order. In response we describe PROCTOR, a Teacher-Student loop in which a stateful orchestrator holds all tool access, stateless subagents diagnose failures and draft mutations they cannot apply, and a Teacher grades those mutations under five deterministic guardrails: hermetic sandboxes, capability-disjoint roles, acceptance checks that outrank the Teacher, frozen holdouts, and canary cases engineered so that a perfect score is itself evidence of cheating. We report the failures this prevented, and, because the Teacher is itself an LLM judge, the failures it did not.

</details>


### [262] [APEx: Distillation of Agent Procedural Experience for Adaptive Deep Research Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02253)
*Jie Ding,Rui Sun,Xinyuan Zhang,Zeyu Zhang,Xin Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: APEx is a hierarchical experience utilization framework for deep research agents that combines instance-level trajectory memories with category-level procedural skills through a closed-loop architecture (Executor, Distiller, Planner). It uses three-stage alternating GRPO training for reward-guided skill distillation and enables test-time self-improvement via skill-guided reinforcement learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for learning from prior experience in deep research agents either retrieve verbose task-specific traces that burden decision-making, or distill procedural skills that remain decoupled from downstream policy adaptation. A unified framework is needed to effectively leverage both instance-level memories and category-level skills.

Method: 1) Hierarchical experience organization: instance-level trajectory memories + category-level procedural skills
2) Closed-loop architecture: Executor, Distiller, and Planner modules
3) Three-stage alternating GRPO training paradigm for reward-guided skill distillation
4) Skill-guided test-time reinforcement learning with skill-alignment regularization for ground-truth-free self-improvement

Result: APEx achieves state-of-the-art performance on 7 benchmarks, surpassing GPT-5.4 by 14.7 points and the strongest memory-augmented baseline by 3.0 points.

Conclusion: The proposed APEx framework effectively addresses the limitation of prior experience utilization in deep research agents by integrating hierarchical experience organization with closed-loop training and test-time adaptation, demonstrating significant performance improvements over existing approaches.

Abstract: Deep research agents augment large language models with external tools to answer complex, long-horizon questions through multi-turn reasoning. Learning from prior experience is crucial for continual improvement, yet existing methods either retrieve verbose task-specific traces that burden decision-making, or distill procedural skills that remain decoupled from downstream policy adaptation. We propose APEx, a hierarchical experience utilization framework that organizes interaction history into instance-level trajectory memories and category-level procedural skills, and couples them through a closed-loop architecture of Executor, Distiller, and Planner. The three modules are optimized via a three-stage alternating GRPO training paradigm, enabling reward-guided skill distillation rather than fixed-prompt generation. At test time, distilled skills serve as procedural priors for online Planner adaptation through skill-guided test-time reinforcement learning, allowing ground-truth-free self-improvement with skill-alignment regularization to prevent policy drift. Experiments on 7 benchmarks demonstrate that APEx achieves state-of-the-art performance, surpassing GPT-5.4 by 14.7 points and the strongest memory-augmented baseline by 3.0 points.

</details>


### [263] [Codebook Agent: Amortized Topology Design for LLM Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02264)
*Jinxi Yu,Yubei Li,Eric Hanchen Jiang,Zhi Zhang,Dong Liu,Wenxiao Zhao,Levina Li,Kai-Wei Chang,Ying Nian Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses the problem of adapting communication topologies in LLM multi-agent systems for each query. Current approaches treat this as conditional graph generation, which the authors argue is misaligned with the problem. Three key empirical findings motivate their proposed solution: (1) topologies collapse to ~6 distinct graphs despite large codebooks, (2) edge count negatively correlates with token consumption (r ≈ -0.4), and (3) message-passing scorers become adjacency-invariant when agents share profiles. The proposed "Codebook Agent" uses a vector-quantized autoencoder to compress topologies into a 16-entry query-independent codebook, with reward-weighted MLP for code selection and an MLP proxy for reranking. The method achieves 84.6% average accuracy (vs 83.0% prior best), 2.4ms inference time, and 21.9-33.2% token reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for designing communication topologies in LLM multi-agent systems treat this as conditional graph generation using variational, autoregressive, or diffusion decoders, combined with graph-network proxies trained on utility and structural costs. However, the authors identify three fundamental problems with this approach: (1) effective topologies collapse to very few distinct graphs regardless of codebook size, (2) edge count is negatively correlated with token consumption (counterintuitive), and (3) message-passing scorers become adjacency-invariant when agents share profiles, making them incapable of ranking candidates. These issues suggest the formulation is fundamentally misaligned with the problem.

Method: The authors propose "Codebook Agent" with three components: (1) A vector-quantized autoencoder that compresses successful topologies into a query-independent 16-entry codebook; (2) A reward-weighted MLP that maps query embeddings to a distribution over codes; (3) An MLP proxy that reads flattened adjacency, regressed on measured utility and per-task normalized token cost, which reranks top decoded candidates in a single batched forward pass. The key innovations are eliminating iterative search and message passing at test time, leveraging query-independent codebook compression, and using a simpler MLP proxy instead of graph networks.

Result: Codebook Agent achieves state-of-the-art performance across all six benchmarks tested: 84.6% average accuracy compared to 83.0% for the strongest prior method. It generates topologies in just 2.4ms and uses 21.9-33.2% fewer LLM tokens. The method outperforms prior approaches on both accuracy and efficiency without requiring iterative search or message passing at test time.

Conclusion: The authors demonstrate that the problem of adapting communication topologies for LLM multi-agent systems is better framed as codebook selection rather than conditional graph generation. By compressing topologies into a small query-independent codebook and using simple MLP-based proxies, they achieve superior accuracy and efficiency compared to more complex graph-based approaches. The key insight is that effective topologies naturally cluster into a small number of distinct patterns, making codebook-based approaches more suitable than exhaustive graph search methods.

Abstract: Adapting the communication topology of an LLM multi-agent system to each query improves both accuracy and efficiency, yet current designers treat this as conditional graph generation: a variational, autoregressive, or diffusion decoder searches the $N \times N$ adjacency space, and a graph-network proxy trained on utility and a structural cost such as edge count ranks the sampled candidates. We argue that this formulation is misaligned with the problem. Empirically, topologies that survive a reward filter collapse to about six distinct graphs even when the codebook capacity grows from 8 to 64; edge count is negatively correlated with measured token consumption (Pearson $r \approx -0.4$), so sparsifying the graph makes inference more expensive; and a message-passing scorer over agent-profile nodes is adjacency-invariant whenever agents share a profile---the default configuration of published benchmarks---so it cannot rank candidates at all in that regime. These three facts motivate Codebook Agent: a vector-quantized autoencoder compresses successful topologies into a query-independent 16-entry codebook; a reward-weighted MLP maps the query embedding to a distribution over codes; and an MLP proxy that reads the flattened adjacency, regressed on measured utility and per-task normalized token cost, reranks the top decoded candidates in a single batched forward pass. With no iterative search and no message passing at test time, Codebook Agent is the most accurate method on all six benchmarks we compare (84.6 average against 83.0 for the strongest prior designer), emits a topology in 2.4 ms, and uses 21.9--33.2% fewer LLM tokens.

</details>


### [264] [CoMerge: Conflict-Driven Preference Optimization for Multi-Task Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02273)
*Mingjie Zheng,Zihao Chen,Wenqing Chen,Weile Yuan,Zhixuan Chu,Jianxing Yu,Zibin Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoMerge proposes a conflict-driven preference optimization framework for model merging that uses defects of naive merging methods as hard negative samples to construct preference pairs without external annotations. It optimizes lightweight tensor-wise merging coefficients to mitigate parameter-space conflicts while preserving task-specific capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model merging provides an efficient paradigm for constructing multi-task LLMs without full model retraining, but it suffers from parameter interference. Existing methods aim to preserve expert model capabilities but don't directly learn from the potentially degraded behaviors exposed by naive merging. The authors seek to reformulate model merging as a preference optimization problem to address this gap.

Method: The approach utilizes a self-supervised, conflict-driven strategy that leverages defects of naive merging methods (e.g., task arithmetic) as hard negative samples to construct preference pairs without external annotations. Preference optimization is then applied to refine lightweight, tensor-wise merging coefficients.

Result: CoMerge achieves an average normalized performance of 0.9968 on MergeBench, outperforming all evaluated data-free and data-driven model-merging baselines. On Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, it yields marked improvements on conflict-sensitive tasks such as instruction following and safety, while remaining highly competitive with full-parameter fine-tuning despite optimizing only 1,445 scalar coefficients.

Conclusion: CoMerge demonstrates that treating model merging as a preference optimization problem and using self-supervised hard negative samples can effectively mitigate parameter interference. The framework achieves state-of-the-art performance with minimal parameter optimization (1,445 scalars), showing promise for efficient multi-task LLM construction.

Abstract: Model merging provides an efficient paradigm for constructing multi-task large language models (LLMs) without full model retraining, yet it remains challenged by parameter interference. While existing methods aim to preserve the capabilities of individual expert models and mitigate interference, they generally do not directly learn from the potentially degraded behaviors exposed by naive merging. In this paper, we propose a conflict-driven preference optimization framework for model merging (CoMerge), which reformulates model merging as a preference optimization problem. The approach utilizes a self-supervised, conflict-driven strategy that leverages the defects of naive merging methods (e.g., task arithmetic) as hard negative samples to construct preference pairs without external annotations. By applying preference optimization to refine lightweight, tensor-wise merging coefficients, CoMerge enables the model to mitigate parameter-space conflicts while preserving task-specific capabilities. Extensive experiments show that CoMerge achieves an average normalized performance of 0.9968 on MergeBench, outperforming all evaluated data-free and data-driven model-merging baselines. Furthermore, on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, CoMerge yields marked improvements on conflict-sensitive tasks such as instruction following and safety, while remaining highly competitive with full-parameter fine-tuning despite optimizing only 1,445 scalar coefficients.

</details>


### [265] [SCX Router: Streaming Zero-Shot Model Selection with a Decoder-KV Classifier and a Real-World Task Ontology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02292)
*Ihor Stepanov,Aleksandr Smechov,Mykhailo Shtopko,Dmytro Vodianytskyi,Oleksandr Lukashov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GLiClass-based router that dynamically selects the optimal LLM for each task by predicting model suitability, task type, difficulty, reasoning mode, and expected output length without autoregressive generation. The 0.6B-parameter model combines Qwen3 decoder with shallow bidirectional scorer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The proliferation of diverse LLMs creates an optimization opportunity to select the right model per task. However, the heterogeneity of inference endpoints (quality, price, latency, context support, tool use, domain expertise, reasoning behavior) makes manual heuristics inadequate for achieving favorable speed-cost-quality trade-offs.

Method: 1) Construct task ontology with 23 families, 115 task types, 345 routable subtypes, 1,173 synthetic examples, and 30 domains; 2) Generate 150,000 verifier-scored tasks and 15,000 open-ended tasks; 3) Train Qwen3 decoder (0.6B parameters) with shallow bidirectional scorer; 4) Decoder-KV execution preserves text-only KV cache across sessions, encodes only new dialogue turns, and evaluates transient candidate-label tokens without adding to persistent cache; 5) Explicitly separate learned request prediction from per-task policies for eligibility, cost, cache reuse, safety, and sovereignty.

Result: On six LiveBench subsets, router outperforms the mean candidate. On 1,000-task subset, achieves aggregate top-1 score of 0.707 vs 0.696 for strongest fixed model, with benchmark-dependent gains.

Conclusion: The lightweight GLiClass-based router successfully optimizes LLM selection at inference time, achieving better aggregate performance than the strongest fixed model by dynamically matching tasks to suitable models based on predicted attributes.

Abstract: The rapid proliferation of large language models (LLMs) and the growing diversity of their applications presents a unique optimization opportunity: selecting the right model for the task, while optimizing for speed, cost, and quality at a per-task level. However, inference endpoints can vary widely in quality, price, latency, context support, tool use, domain expertise, and reasoning behavior. This heterogeneity makes manual heuristics difficult to maintain and unlikely to achieve consistently favorable speed--cost--quality trade-offs on their own. We introduce \router{}, a lightweight GLiClass-based router that assigns a suitability score to each inference-time model label without autoregressive generation. The released 0.6B-parameter checkpoint combines a Qwen3 decoder with a shallow bidirectional scorer. Its decoder-KV execution path preserves a text-only key--value cache across a session, encodes only new dialogue turns, and evaluates transient candidate-label tokens without adding them to the persistent cache. The same checkpoint also predicts task type, difficulty, reasoning mode, and expected output length, and supports custom zero-shot labels. For task generation, we construct a task ontology with 23 families, 115 task types, 345 routable subtypes, 1,173 synthetic examples, and an orthogonal axis of 30 domains. Using this structure, we generate 150,000 verifier-scored tasks and 15,000 open-ended tasks. We then train the Qwen3 decoder on these tasks, while explicitly separating learned request prediction from per-task policies for attributes such as eligibility, cost, cache reuse, safety, and sovereignty. Across six LiveBench subsets, the router outperforms the mean candidate; on the selected 1,000-task subset, it achieves an aggregate top-1 score of 0.707 versus 0.696 for the strongest fixed model, with benchmark-dependent gains.

</details>


### [266] [Improving Evaluation Realism with Inference-Time Compute and Deployment Scaffolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02302)
*Axel Ahlqvist,Richard Guan,Juan-Pablo Rivera,Adeline Kassler,Dmitrii Troitskii,Alexandra Souly,Kai Fronsdal,Robert Kirk,John Hughes*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses "evaluation awareness" in AI safety - where models can detect when they're being tested, undermining the validity of alignment evaluations. It proposes two techniques: critique refinement (using additional compute to refine simulator actions based on target model feedback) and DISH (a deployment-imitating agent harness for coding settings). These techniques compose to improve realism of alignment evaluations more effectively than simply extending audit length.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core obstacle is "evaluation awareness" - capable AI models can recognize when they are being tested rather than deployed in the real world. This awareness weakens the conclusions that safety evaluations can support, as the model's behavior in test scenarios may not reflect its behavior in actual deployment.

Method: The paper presents two complementary techniques: (1) Critique refinement - generates multiple candidate actions, refines them using feedback from the target model on how to make them more realistic, and continues with the most deployment-like candidate; (2) DISH (Deployment-Imitating SWE-Agent Harness) - wraps the target model in an agent harness to reduce the gap between simulated and real deployment environments, specifically in coding settings.

Result: The techniques were tested on multiple target models and found to compose effectively - applying both yields larger realism gains than either technique alone. The results demonstrate that automated approaches can improve the realism of alignment evaluations, and that additional compute is used more effectively than simply making the audits longer.

Conclusion: Automated techniques can improve the realism of alignment evaluations by addressing evaluation awareness. The combination of critique refinement and DISH provides greater realism gains than either approach individually, and using additional compute for refinement is more effective than extending audit duration.

Abstract: A core obstacle to alignment evaluation is evaluation awareness: capable models can tell when they are being tested rather than deployed, weakening the conclusions a safety evaluation can support. We present two techniques that make simulated alignment evaluations harder to distinguish from real deployments. Our first technique, critique refinement, spends additional inference-time compute on each simulator action: the simulator generates multiple candidate actions, refines them using feedback from an instance of the target model on how to make them more realistic, and continues the evaluation with the most deployment-like candidate. Our second technique, DISH (Deployment-Imitating SWE-Agent Harness), wraps the target in an agent harness, reducing the gap between simulated and real deployment environments in coding settings. We test the techniques on multiple target models and find that they compose: applying both yields larger realism gains than either alone. Our results show that automated approaches can improve the realism of alignment evaluations, and that these improvements use additional compute more effectively than making the audits longer.

</details>


### [267] [SALA: Semantic-Aware Logical Alignment for Complex Reasoning in In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02336)
*Zhao Ji,Wenqing Chen,Zhixuan Chu,Jianxing Yu,Jingping Liu,Shanhe Zhao,Zibin Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SALA is a Semantic-Aware Logical Alignment framework for selecting effective demonstrations for in-context learning in complex reasoning tasks. It learns task-specific reasoning operations, embeds them in a continuous semantic space, and uses dynamic time warping (DTW) for flexible logical alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional retrieval methods for ICL rely on surface similarity, failing to capture problem-solving logic. Existing logic-based methods use rigid rules and exact-match criteria, which cannot handle flexible or diverse reasoning processes.

Method: SALA automatically learns task-specific reasoning operations instead of using fixed inventories. These operations are embedded into a continuous semantic space, and dynamic time warping (DTW) is used to align reasoning sequences, enabling soft, flexible matching while remaining interpretable.

Result: Experiments across four reasoning benchmarks and three LLMs demonstrate that SALA outperforms existing demonstration selection methods.

Conclusion: The analysis confirms the effectiveness of both the operation induction component and the logical semantic alignment component in improving ICL demonstration selection for complex reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Effective in-context learning (ICL) for complex reasoning relies on selecting the right demonstrations. Traditional retrieval methods based on surface similarity fail to capture the underlying problem-solving logic. Recent logic-based methods address this by matching predefined reasoning steps, but the rigid rules and exact-match criteria is improper to handle flexible or diverse reasoning processes. To address the problem, we propose SALA, a Semantic-Aware Logical Alignment framework. Instead of relying on a fixed inventory, SALA automatically learns task-specific reasoning operations. It then embeds these operations into a continuous semantic space and uses dynamic time warping (DTW) to align the reasoning sequences. This approach allows for soft, flexible matching of reasoning logic while remaining highly interpretable. Experiments across four reasoning benchmarks and three LLMs demonstrate that SALA outperforms existing demonstration selection methods. Further analysis confirms the roles of the operation induction and the logical semantic alignment.

</details>


### [268] [Diagnosing with Insights: Structured Analysis of Agent Failures via Behavioral Abstractions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02371)
*Jiayi Bi,Yanjie Gao,Yuanmin Xie,Liqun Li,Tianyin Xu,Fan Yang,Mao Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AGENTSCOPE is a neuro-symbolic approach for diagnosing failures in LLM agents by abstracting agent behavior into structured representations and using neural invariants to specify behavior properties. It leverages LLM-guided reasoning to pinpoint failure steps and types in complex agent trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The proliferation of LLM agents creates a need to understand and diagnose failures, but agent failures manifest via long and complex trajectories making manual diagnosis impractical. Traditional software bug diagnosis techniques fail to address LLM agent failures, while purely LLM-based diagnosis yields unreliable results.

Method: AGENTSCOPE abstracts agent behavior from trajectories into structured representations, introduces neural invariants to specify agent behavior properties, and uses LLM-guided reasoning atop the structured representation against neural invariants to identify failure steps and their types.

Result: AGENTSCOPE significantly outperforms current state-of-the-art methods in fault localization and attribution accuracy on both the Who&When dataset and a new comprehensive dataset (AgentErrata).

Conclusion: Integrating structured abstractions with LLM-guided reasoning enables effective, reliable, and interpretable diagnosis for LLM agent failures.

Abstract: With the proliferation of LLM agents, the ability to understand and diagnose failures in agents is essential to achieving superior effectiveness and trustworthiness. As agent failures often manifest via long and complex trajectories, manually finding the needles in the haystack is untenable. However, traditional diagnosis techniques for software bugs can hardly address LLM agent failures, while completely relying on LLMs as the judge yields unreliable diagnosis results. To overcome these challenges, this paper presents AGENTSCOPE, a new neuro-symbolic approach for agent failure mode diagnosis. The key principle of AGENTSCOPE is to abstract agent behavior, based on its trajectories, into structured representations. Furthermore, AGENTSCOPE introduces the concept of neural invariants to specify agent behavior properties. AGENTSCOPE leverages LLM-guided reasoning atop the structured representation against neural invariants to pinpoint both the failure step and its type in the trajectory. We show the effectiveness of AGENTSCOPE on publicly available agent failure datasets (Who&When) and a more comprehensive dataset created by us (AgentErrata), where AGENTSCOPE significantly outperforms the current state of the art in fault localization and attribution accuracy. Our work shows that integrating structured abstractions with LLM-guided reasoning enables effective, reliable, and interpretable diagnosis for agent failures.

</details>


### [269] [Contrastive Explanations in Quantitative Bipolar Argumentation Frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02399)
*Xiang Yin,Nico Potyka,Antonio Rago,Francesca Toni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces contrastive explanations for Quantitative Bipolar Argumentation Frameworks (QBAFs), which explain the difference between two topic arguments rather than explaining a single argument's reasoning outcome. The authors propose Contrastive Attribution Functions (CAFs) with general properties, and develop implementations based on removal, gradients, and Shapley-values, demonstrating their usefulness in healthcare and bias identification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Argumentation frameworks are valuable for AI classification tasks and explainability, but existing explanations for QBAFs typically explain only a single argument of interest. The authors identify a gap: there is a need for contrastive explanations that compare two topic arguments to understand why one is accepted over another.

Method: The authors introduce a general form of Contrastive Attribution Functions (CAFs) and establish a set of general properties they should satisfy. They develop three specific CAF implementations: (1) removal-based CAFs, (2) gradient-based CAFs, and (3) Shapley-value-based CAFs. They then analyze and study the properties of these different approaches.

Result: The paper provides a theoretical framework for contrastive explanations in QBAFs, with multiple CAF implementations that satisfy the established properties. The authors demonstrate practical usefulness through applications in healthcare (explaining diagnostic decisions) and bias identification (detecting unfair treatment patterns).

Conclusion: Contrastive explanations for QBAFs are feasible and valuable. The proposed CAF framework provides a principled way to compare two topic arguments, offering more nuanced explanations than single-argument approaches. This work advances explainable AI by enabling comparative reasoning in argumentation-based systems.

Abstract: Argumentation frameworks are useful tools for representing and reasoning with information in a variety of settings, e.g. in supplementing AI models as they perform classification tasks, with a notable benefit of providing additional explainability. In this paper, we introduce contrastive explanations for Quantitative Bipolar Argumentation Frameworks (QBAFs), one such formalism. Unlike most existing explanations for QBAFs, which explain the reasoning outcome of a single argument of interest (i.e. a topic argument), contrastive explanations explain the difference between two topic arguments. We introduce a general form of contrastive attribution functions (CAFs) and establish a set of general properties they should satisfy. We introduce CAFs based on removal, gradients and Shapley-values, and study their properties. Finally, to illustrate contrastive explanations, we demonstrate their usefulness in healthcare and bias identification settings.

</details>


### [270] [UTP-Bench: Uncertainty-aware Travel Planning Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02421)
*Etcharla Revanth Rao,Priyanshu Karmakar,Shubhojit Mallick,Manish Gupta,Shreya Ghosh,Abhik Jana*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UTP-Bench is a benchmark for evaluating travel planning under uncertainty, integrating real-world data from 504 Indian cities with empirical delay distributions and crowd-density patterns. It proposes three metrics (BAS, CATS, TDAS) to quantify itinerary robustness against transit delays and crowd variability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing travel planning benchmarks (TravelPlanner, TripCraft) assume deterministic environments and only evaluate static constraint satisfaction, ignoring whether plans remain robust when uncertainties like transportation delays and crowd fluctuations arise.

Method: Built a large-scale dataset using real-world travel data from 504 Indian cities (attractions, restaurants, accommodations, multi-modal transportation). Incorporated empirical delay distributions and crowd-density patterns from major cities. Proposed three evaluation metrics: Buffer Adequacy Score (BAS), Crowd-Aware Timing Score (CATS), and Transport Delay Absorption Score (TDAS).

Result: Experiments with GPT-5, Qwen3, Mistral, and Phi-4 reveal substantial gaps between model-generated and human-authored plans, particularly in temporal buffering, delay-aware transportation scheduling, and crowd-sensitive planning.

Conclusion: State-of-the-art LLMs have significant limitations in handling uncertainty in travel planning. They need improvement in temporal buffering, delay-aware scheduling, and crowd-sensitive planning to generate robust itineraries.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently demonstrated strong capabilities in automated travel itinerary generation. However, real- world travel planning is inherently uncertain: transportation delays, crowd fluctuations, and unexpected stochastic delays frequently inval- idate otherwise feasible schedules. Existing benchmarks like TravelPlanner and TripCraft assume deterministic environments, evaluating only static constraint satisfaction and ignoring whether generated plans remain robust when such uncertainties arise. To address this limitation, we introduce UTP-Bench1 , a large-scale benchmark for uncertainty-aware travel planning. The dataset integrates real-world travel data spanning 504 cities of India, including attractions, restau- rants, accommodations, and multi-modal trans- portation networks. To model realistic disrup- tions, UTP-Bench incorporates empirical delay distributions and crowd-density patterns col- lected from major cities, enabling evaluation of travel plans under stochastic conditions. We further propose three evaluation metrics, namely Buffer Adequacy Score (BAS), Crowd- Aware Timing Score (CATS), and Transport Delay Absorption Score (TDAS), which quan- tify the ability of generated itineraries to main- tain robustness against transit delays and crowd variability. Experiments with state-of-the-art LLMs like GPT-5, Qwen3, Mistral and Phi-4 re- veal substantial gaps between model-generated and human-authored plans, particularly in tem- poral buffering, delay-aware transportation scheduling, and crowd-sensitive planning.

</details>


### [271] [CivBench: A Long-Horizon Benchmark for Tool-Mediated Agents in Civilization VI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02459)
*Austin Tudor David Andrews,Liam Wilkinson,Jamie Heagerty,Harry Coppock,Jakob Nicolaus Foerster,Rui Ponte Costa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CivBench is an open-source benchmark evaluating language model agents in long-horizon, tool-mediated environments (Civilization 6 game) using Model Context Protocol (MCP). It spans 300+ turns with 76 MCP tools, testing 4 model families across 23 runs. The study introduces two metrics: Proactive Monitoring Rate (PMR) and RAG@10 (commitment execution within 10 turns). Results reveal agents under-monitor strategic state despite explicit guidance and frequently fail to execute stated commitments (RAG@10: 48.2%-65.8%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks fail to evaluate language model agents in long-horizon, tool-mediated environments with partial observability. Current benchmarks lack sustained planning, state monitoring, and execution tracking over extended episodes. This paper addresses the need for comprehensive evaluation of agent behavior in complex, realistic environments.

Method: CivBench uses Civilization 6 as the environment with 76 MCP tools and a narration layer converting visual game state to structured text. Two metrics were introduced: (1) Proactive Monitoring Rate (PMR) - measuring whether agents actively query latent strategic state, and (2) RAG@10 - measuring whether stated commitments in planning reflections are executed within 10 subsequent turns. Four model families were tested across 23 admissible runs under a shared playbook protocol.

Result: Two consistent patterns emerged: (1) Agents under-monitor strategically relevant state - querying victory progress every 30-75 turns instead of guided 20 turns, and missing 7 of 20 detectable defeats within the 20-turn warning window. (2) Agents frequently fail to execute stated commitments - RAG@10 ranges from 48.2% to 65.8% across models. These patterns exist despite tool access and explicit guidance.

Conclusion: The observed behavioral patterns represent deviations under instruction rather than absences of capability. Agents have the tools and guidance but fail to follow them consistently. This suggests instruction-following issues in long-horizon tasks, not capability limitations. The benchmark and metrics provide valuable tools for evaluating agent behavior in complex, tool-mediated environments.

Abstract: We present CivBench, an open-source benchmark for evaluating language model agents in long-horizon, tool-mediated environments through the Model Context Protocol (MCP). A single episode spans 300+ turns and produces thousands of tool calls over a large action space, requiring sustained planning, state monitoring, and execution under partial observability. The environment exposes 76 MCP tools and a narration layer that converts visual game state into structured text.
  We use CivBench to characterise agent behaviour across four model families in 23 admissible runs. The sample is a pilot, not a model ranking: aggregate outcomes do not reliably discriminate models at this scale. Instead, we introduce two interface-level metrics that the environment makes measurable: Proactive Monitoring Rate (PMR), capturing whether agents actively query latent strategic state, and RAG@10, capturing whether commitments stated in structured planning reflections are executed within ten subsequent turns.
  Across runs we observe two consistent patterns under a shared playbook protocol. Agents under-monitor strategically relevant state that is available but requires explicit querying: despite playbook guidance to query victory progress every 20 turns, agents do so only every 30 to 75 turns, and in 7 of 20 detectable defeats they failed to query within the 20 turn warning window before game end. Agents also frequently fail to execute near-term commitments stated in their own planning reflections (RAG@10 between 48.2% and 65.8% across models). Both patterns arise despite tool access and explicit guidance, and we interpret them as deviations under instruction rather than absences of capability.
  We release the environment, scenarios, logs, metrics, and analysis pipeline at https://github.com/lmwilki/civ6-mcp

</details>


### [272] [Collective creativity in hybrid societies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02620)
*Mason Youngblood,Katie Mudd,Manuel Anglada-Tort,Cameron Jones,Elena Miu,Diana Omigie,Margaret Schedel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that debates about whether generative AI helps or harms creativity are misguided because they conflate two distinct concepts: novelty (property of individual artifacts) and diversity (property of populations). The authors propose that creativity in the AI era should be understood as emerging from hybrid human-AI collectives, not individual creators. Their key finding is that AI-assisted ideation increases individual novelty but decreases aggregate diversity—but this is not inevitable. Because humans and AI models search in complementary ways, optimal mixtures can outperform both pure human and pure AI groups. The outcome depends entirely on composition: which agents are present, in what proportions, and how they interact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The researchers aim to resolve the ongoing disagreement in the literature about whether generative AI enriches or impoverishes culture. They identify that this debate suffers from conceptual confusion—specifically, the failure to distinguish between novelty (individual-level property) and diversity (population-level property).

Method: This appears to be a conceptual/theoretical paper that synthesizes arguments about creativity in human-AI interaction. It likely draws on empirical findings about AI-assisted ideation and proposes a framework for understanding creativity as collective rather than individual.

Result: The paper finds that AI reliably raises novelty of individual output while narrowing population-level diversity. However, because humans and AI models search in complementary ways, mixed human-AI groups can outperform and out-diversify homogeneous groups of either type. The outcome is determined by composition: which agents participate, their proportions, and their connectivity.

Conclusion: The question is no longer whether AI helps or harms creativity, but rather which specific mixtures of human and AI agents allow individual gains to accumulate without eroding collective diversity. Creativity should be understood as an emergent property of hybrid collectives rather than individual creators.

Abstract: Generative AI is changing how cultural artifacts are created and circulated, and with it our understanding of creativity itself. Researchers disagree about whether these tools enrich or impoverish culture, and we argue that much of that disagreement comes from conflating two distinct components of creativity: novelty, a property of single artifacts, and diversity, a property of populations. We argue further that creativity in the context of generative AI is best understood as a property of hybrid collectives, or populations of interacting people and algorithms, rather than of individuals. AI-assisted ideation reliably raises the novelty of individual output while narrowing diversity in the aggregate, but this is not an inevitable consequence of putting machines in the loop. Because humans and models search in complementary ways, mixed groups can outperform and out-diversify groups of either kind alone, and machine-discovered solutions can enter human culture and persist there. What decides the outcome is composition: which agents are present, in what proportion, and how they are connected. The question is no longer whether AI helps or harms creativity, but which mixtures let individual gains accumulate without eroding collective diversity.

</details>


### [273] [Loom: Weaving Diagnostic Strands into Free-Text Consensus via Embedding-Space Reweighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02649)
*Ron Begleiter,Katya Egert Berg,Gilad Saban,Gil Shabat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Loom is a generative consensus framework for real-world Root Cause Analysis (RCA) that bridges traditional weak supervision and monolithic LLM agents. It aggregates open-form hypotheses from modular heuristics by projecting them into embedding space and resolving conflicts via iterative centroid-based reweighting, then uses a single lightweight LLM call for synthesis. The system achieves comparable accuracy to state-of-the-art autonomous agents while being ~26-33x faster.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The fundamental challenge is aggregating noisy, conflicting textual hypotheses into reliable consensus in industrial NLP deployments. Existing approaches have limitations: monolithic LLM agents suffer from context limits, compounding hallucinations, and high latency, while traditional weak supervision is restricted to discrete classes and lacks mathematical rigor for open-form text.

Method: Loom uses a hybrid approach: (1) Modular heuristics emit diagnostic templates populated with episode-specific entities, times, and metrics; (2) Hypotheses are projected into a continuous embedding space; (3) An iterative centroid-based reweighting algorithm resolves conflicting signals; (4) The resulting consensus weights ground a single lightweight LLM synthesis step.

Result: On the OpenRCA benchmark, Loom matches state-of-the-art autonomous agent performance on Bank and Market-2 datasets while trailing on Market-1 and Telecom. It achieves this with a single LLM call per incident, making it ~26x faster (or ~33x with an 8B-parameter synthesizer).

Conclusion: Loom occupies the accuracy-efficiency Pareto frontier for RCA tasks. Key deployment lessons include: trade-offs between agentic depth and inference latency, negative results in redundancy detection, and how deterministic consensus fosters trust among Subject Matter Experts. The work demonstrates that aggregating modular heuristics through embedding-based consensus can effectively balance accuracy and efficiency in industrial NLP systems.

Abstract: Aggregating noisy, conflicting textual hypotheses into a reliable consensus is a fundamental challenge when deploying NLP systems in real-world industrial settings. While monolithic Large Language Model (LLM) agents offer unbounded expressivity for tasks like Root Cause Analysis (RCA), they suffer from context limits, compounding hallucinations, and prohibitive inference latency. Traditional weak supervision offers statistical rigor but is mathematically restricted to discrete classes. We present Loom, a generative consensus framework deployed for real-world RCA that bridges these paradigms. Loom aggregates open-form hypotheses emitted by modular heuristics (diagnostic templates dynamically populated with episode-specific entities, times, and metrics) by projecting them into a continuous embedding space, and resolves conflicting signals with an iterative centroid-based reweighting algorithm. The resulting consensus weights ground a single lightweight LLM synthesis step. Evaluated on the OpenRCA benchmark, Loom occupies the accuracy--efficiency Pareto frontier: it matches a state-of-the-art autonomous agent on Bank and Market-2 and trails on Market-1 and Telecom, while using a single LLM call per incident on all four datasets ($\sim$26$\times$ faster; $\sim$33$\times$ with an 8B-parameter synthesizer). We discuss our deployment experience, highlighting lessons learned regarding the trade-offs between agentic depth and inference latency, negative results in redundancy detection, and how deterministic consensus fosters trust among Subject Matter Experts~(SMEs).

</details>


### [274] [Door-in-the-Face Requests and Refusal Behaviour in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02707)
*Til Jordan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper tests whether the "door-in-the-face" persuasion technique (where a refused large request makes a smaller follow-up request more likely to be granted) works on nine language models from three providers. Results are mixed: the technique works on Anthropic's Opus 5 (65.8% vs 29.3% compliance), but backfires on OpenAI and Google models, and on Haiku 4.5 (lowering compliance by 15.5-23.0 points). The effect depends on whether the small request is related to the refused large request, and what the request actually asks for.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether a well-known human persuasion technique (door-in-the-face) transfers to language models, and to understand which models and conditions make this technique effective.

Method: The authors tested nine production models from three providers (Anthropic, OpenAI, Google). Each model refused a large request, then received a smaller version of the same request. They compared compliance rates against asking the smaller request directly. They also included controls: unrelated large requests, and requests for explanations instead of rewrites.

Result: The technique works on Anthropic's frontier models (Opus 5: 65.8% after refusal vs 29.3% direct), but backfires on OpenAI and Google frontier models and Haiku 4.5 (compliance drops 15.5-23.0 points). A control showed that a refused large request on an unrelated topic has less effect than a related one, suggesting the concession itself matters. The technique does not transfer to refusals from public benchmarks. The key factor is what the request asks for: rewriting 265 refused requests into requests for explanations removed the refusal in 263 cases.
